Jul 6.1933 

As Young as You Wish to Be

Nature's Elixir is practically the only medicine that the modern person does not take for preserving health or curing or preventing disease. Yet it is the most agreeable medicine to take, and the most efficacious, especially for preventing sickness, improving health and prolonging the span of life.

Distinguished physicians say that we are killing ourselves by our reckless manner of living, and the only assurance against mental and physical breakdown is Nature's Elixir and Individual Hygiene. 

Health can be preserved and the ageing process counteracted. The most trustworthy scientific authorities say so. 

But physicians and surgeons can do little more than they are doing, and they advise each of us to practice Preventive 

Medicine, using Nature's Elixir. 

Challenge your Ageing Process, or slow self-destruction- 

While You Can-Before It Is Too Late. At 70 years of age and older, it may be Retarded, at 49 to 70 Stopped, at 

28 to 49 Reversed. Your own Common Sense should assure you that there is More Than a Hope of succeeding. 

Inasmuch as you are to be your own doctor of preventive medicine, practicing individual hygiene (John Everyman, D. P. M., doctor of preventive medicine), you should have some good scientific information and not be a quack to yourself. You must know where you belong in the universal scheme and what you are in it, and what you need in order to preserve your health. 

Read, in the following book, proved suggestions, based on explained facts, for counteracting the ageing process and preserving prime health and expanding the life-span. Facts are presented in everyday terms, which every one can understand.

DEDICATED TO MY BROTHER FRANK VICTOR HAMMAR

PARK G. HAMMAR

W 

BOOK ONE CHAPTER I. 

E have been reveling in so giddy a jazz of artificialized selfishness, I fear, as to believe that syncopation of every ideal, activity and objective is a toning indispensable to success or satisfaction, and a flashy display of the ultimate of the possible in complex civilization. We live, I fear, as if we expect a jubilation in discordances of sordidness, greed and ribaldry to blend into a rolling euphony of serenity of conscience, peace of mind, glow of robust health and purr of soft luxury. 

Will you come with me, therefore, on a happy-golucky jaunt in the green and sunny wonderland of Nature, and there rhapsodize over the grand simplicity of the diverse beauties of life and harmony, and feel the rapturous awe of the mysteries of commonplace miracles in the grand scheme? We shall take with us smatterings of the genius of science in order that we may, in either understanding or mystification, appreciate the spellbinding marvels which delight the senses and exalt reason to sublime contemplation. 

Civilization Not Bad at That 

I am not a grouchy misanthropist. The young, I feel, should be merry, and the elders, from the age of common sense on to that of senility, happy. Our civilization, in my opinion, excels that of any previous generation, and I could not be convinced that the numerous facilities which we have for comfortable existence are Page 3

detrimental to health or morals, or that honor and virtue cannot survive in the wider dissemination of enlightenment and the broader distribution of the opportunities of individual freedom and wealth. 

Civilization would be of incomplete beneficial human consequence unless it extended and increased the prospects of happiness for every person. Ours tends more and more to this result. The average American lives more sumptuously than a king did twenty-five years ago, more sumptuously than some of the royalties who still wear the crown and personify the majesty of the state; and mankind will be better off when modern living conveniences ramify out to the rickety hutch in the tree and the dark cave in the rock.

Progress Makes Us Quite Dizzy 

The facilities for easy living and business efficiency are products of the human mind, and practically all have been invented in the past century and a half. Business and the mode of living have been revolutionized by rapid graduation in approximately two decades, and it is not amazing, therefore, that the machines, and the system and routine, and the conventions, styles and habits, of advanced and complex civilization should rule the intelligence which originated and fashioned them. 

If never-equaled advantages for work, leisure and culture were of themselves assurances of happiness and prosperity, we who at present inhabit the United States and other progressive countries should be fully contented. But it seems to all thinkers that the social, economic and political development has been so speedy and transitional that we, in frenzy to keep up with it, chas- Page 4

ing gold and pursuing pleasure, have not paused to differentiate between the proper use in one respect and the injurious misuse and disuse in the other of our great privileges.

Our public and private affairs have been jazzed for years, and chiefly because of procrastination in adjusting our principles and benefits in a rational mode. Our public and private affairs are still syncopated. A war which was fought for the ideals of individual rights culminated in a peace of hypocrisy, suspicion, avarice and oppression, and our extravaganza of self-gratification and profligacy whirled us into a reaction gloomy with unemployment, stagnation and poverty. With foodstuffs rotting in fields and warehouses, and monetary gold stacked up in depositaries mountain-high, the world is starving and penniless, and we witness on the street the deep contrast of furs and jewels stepping high past slouching bundles of trudging rags. 

In times such as these it is impossible for one not to be as sarcastic in reviewing the glaring paradoxes, as sad in beholding the distressing anomalies, or as optimistic and confident in considering the resourcefulness and adaptability of human nature. The folly of today is the wisdom of tomorrow, as the misery of this is the prodigality and debauchery of yesteryear. Human nature never has been able to obtain philosophy except by experience. 

Surest Way to Become Wise

Experience, quite hard and worrisome, is leading us into the epoch of common sense, by which, with selfdiscipline, we shall avoid excesses and use the advantages of civilization for health, prosperity and happiness. We Page 5

could not reasonably condemn and dispense with the benefits of our progress, for they are desirable and serviceable advantages. It would be more logical to perfect our civilization by a crowning feat in ingenuity and metamorphosize the complex into the simple without losing variety in our tangible and intangible goods. 

With the thought that our next progress will be in working out from experience, need and comfort, an adjustment between civilization and the living mode, I have the pleasure of tendering to you a few suggestions based on obvious and permanent fundamentals. If you will pardon a little sarcasm and levity here and there, which, I fear, our common imbecility merits, you will, I trust, get some enjoyment and profit from the ideas. 

The earth as it is at this time is man's world. It is no longer the earth, but the world. The earth is something which science brings to our attention once in a while, and which is depicted in geographies and represented by terrestrial globes, which are used, with decorative lamps, as ornaments in private libraries. We have almost forgotten about the earth and cosmic nature in our eager, concentrated interest in the world, which we made, and the environment in which we exist and struggle for happiness and prosperity is almost wholly artificial; consequently we are living like animated automatons in a denatured complexness of machines, standardization and hobbies. 

Man Has Left the Earth There

The earth remains underneath and the sun above the world, and man, though abiding in his world, continues to subsist on the sun and earth. Civilization has not suspended natural law or annulled cosmic principle, and Page 6

man cannot defy either without suffering a penalty for his presumption. 

You may imagine, as we ramble along in nature's wonderland and through realms of inquisitive science, that the route is long and devious and the visions complex. But this, if you fancied it, would be only a panoramic illusion. When we roam into the mysterious, whither we are going now, we see naught but the simple, for such universal nature is. 

In leading you to the delights of the universal harmony, where the elements of nature rejoice continuously in a great paean, I have in mind that we may join in the all-pervading symphony by attuning ourselves to the active principles. As we desire happiness in a long life of robust health, we see that nature proffers it to us on the sole condition of living conformably to the cosmic rhythm.

Nature in the universal, collective magnitude is simple, and the law of reason for man is just common sense. 

The majority should have that, at least. And on the pattern of common sense, I suggest, as we look into the admirable orderliness of the cosmos, that it is possible for us to adopt these principles to our own manner of living and be assured of constant health, with concomitant happiness, and a prolonged span of life expectancy. 

We could well eliminate the discords of the jazz in our living mode and retain the melody. If we do not, we shall continue to syncopate the natural rhythm to the dissipation of health and happiness, and be turned over to the undertaker at the age when we should begin to live and reap the rewards of accelerated activity.

Page 7

Renaturing Dissonances of Jazz 

The ageing process can be reversed by reconciling our habits to nature's requirements. While you can, you should challenge the ageing which rash living-virtually gradual, but rapid self-destruction-is causing. Common sense impresses on you the fact there is more than a hope of adding several years to your span of life and from now on having continuous prime health. 

Nature demonstrates my concept that the span of life can be prolonged. It is only necessary to reverse the ageing process by taking up a common sense manner of living. Retard! Stop! Reverse! Instead of antagonizing nature by jazzy customs and activities, by which health is wrecked in or before mid-life, or existence is cut short, you are obliged only to comply with nature's easy requirements. You should feel this truth keenly as a premonition.

CHAPTER II. 

Your wish is your law. Under the conditions which exist would it not be foolish of you to believe otherwise or to act to the contrary? You think so. Whatever you wish for you obtain, and as if by magic. You are in the habit of expecting your desires to be fulfilled. 

You get want you want, not by the command of strong will or physical force, but with less effort and power. 

You see your yearnings realized almost by the mere signal of the lips or the hand. Only a gesture or a word, or a nod, finger-touch, or glance, and what you crave is yours. All persons about you and all things about you cater to you and at your slightest indication serve you instantly. You naturally feel, from custom,

Page 8

if not reason, that you will enjoy life to the fullest of opportunity if you are only solicitous as to yourself. 

Why should you not have intrenched convictions regarding the rights which you hold inherent in your will? Are they not vested rights, conferred on you by endowment? Nature herself performs wonders for your pleasure and comfort; science almost outmarvels

Nature in achievements to please you. So it is a fact that you not only receive all that you wish for, but also that numerous boons which you had not even imagined are purveyed to you without so much on your part as a casual idea or fleeting thought, begetting for you fresh and varied desires and expectations. 

Always and everywhere favor pursues you. 

Why Work? Only to Finish It?

You work, when you work, or as you work, for money; but money is just an incident to the exaggeration of your delights. Your work is a game. Work is only an indispensable pastime to relieve the monotony of the surfeit of enjoyment; it is diversion by which, with your money, that you may not earn, by a dull contrast brightens your pleasure. Your work, such as it is, is mechanized or routined, and simplified, systematized and facilitated so as to minimize the labor of the brain or hand. Paradoxically, you save labor with work, and you work and think in order to dispense with productive employment and intellectual activity, to do away with working and thinking. 

Another Way to Get Money your

Money has ceased to be simply the medium of exchange. Instead it is the lustered disc of gold or the Page 9

color-embellished rag which electrifies your wish, the presto!-symbol of the enchantment in which you thrive. You have discovered that money itself, aye, fortune! may be attracted to you, as to a magnet, in greater quantity by the wiles of an artful mind than by the obsolete methods of industrious application. 

You procure your financial revenue and capital by the quick and easy expedient of sagacious indolence, perhaps respectable chicanery. 

What, let us consider, have you at your peremptory disposal? How do you live? How is your day scheduled out? What work do you perform, and how? 

What are your personal habits? Have you cultural interests; and, if so, do they help you? What is the nature of your employment, and is it adapted to your talents, and do you like it? What are your social proclivities and what kinds of entertainment do you seek? 

Is your time regulated by the clock? 

How Wonderful You Are!

You are the paragon among living beings, and the absolute master over them and all inanimate things. 

You have the superiority of intellect which makes you "the lord of all you survey" and more. All beings and all things are subject to your actuated conclusions. You have such quick and comprehensive control over the units and complexities in your natural environment that they respond in obedience to your decision. As a result you have at your dictation the replete possibilities for health and happiness. You are so constructed in the advantage of intelligence and the supremacy of will that you are capable of ruling yourself, too.

Page 10

The elements are your ready valets and the distant orbs and fiery nebulae in the far outer space your serfs. 

You wear their service in your clothes; you use their service in your abode. They aid you in your business or profession, and, through the advancement of science, in the preservation of your health.

You inhale the air as the breath of life, and science pulls nitrogen out of it and with this ingredient in the soil provides fertility for the growth of foods for your sustenance. Besides the light and warmth of the sun, which are meted to you free by Nature through the changing seasons, research and experimentation offer to you other beams and rays for better health. Chemistry treats water for you so that it contributes more effectually to your benefit than in the rough state. 

Fire, you eat and drink. Each of many elements is refined in several forms and means to improve and preserve your health, enhance your comfort and convenience, and give you the facilities for happiness. 

Our Old Fogeyish Forefathers 

Your immediate forefathers had virtually none of the material advantages for health and happiness which the combination of inventive intelligence and the forces of 

Nature has developed for you. Whether you use them for your good is a question to be weighed seriously, and subsequently we shall inquire into that. 

In the sane utilization of the power which is ours in the universal domain we have made no progress in two thousand years and more, although we have gone forward in approximately a hundred years, or about three human generations, in realizing wonderful results in commerce and science.

Page 11

CHAPTER III. 

Hah! Gold Is Discovered!

The modern situation of society in business, government and the mode of living is attributed by economists and historians to the discovery of gold in America by the Spaniards and the distribution of the yellow metal throughout Europe in the sixteenth century. Why this precious ore spurred mankind to the far-reaching and prolonged transformation which we witness is impertinent to our subject, except to remember that the world then and thereby obtained an adequate supply of material well suited to monetary purposes, that is, a medium of exchange with which to carry on commerce. 

The distribution of gold was a stimulus to enterprise. 

Invention followed, but not until the discovery of electricity and steam; and the civilized state which now prevails, which still astounds even us who have viewed its onward course, is a comparatively recent attainment. The important facts are that it exists, affording us every opportunity to live as comfortably and happily as we wish, and that it was produced by our intelligence and will. 

The Plain Unvarnished Truth

You might profitably ponder three points in regard to this widespread transformation. The first is that the gold, in the form of money or the medium of exchange, was the lure which induced men to strive for reward. 

The second is that the incentive of gain led them to plan and contrive. The third is that change and progress in business and advancement in science were the sequence of the designing and making of machines and Page 12

tools and instruments. The advancement has been a mechanical and scientific, in brief, an inventive, evolution.

Research and invention really had their inception in the thirteenth century. Discoveries were made then and things conceived of and made which had not been thought of before. They had not been dreamed of or wrought in the preceding scores of centuries, and prior to demonstration by scientists and artisans the world would have scoffed at the new ideas. When, however, there was a prospect of gain, the discoveries and products were tried, and proved, and accepted. 

You use the marvels which emanate from Nature and human intelligence, yet not with particular appraisement of their complete value and serviceability. They have become part of your habit and routine, and to you are as commonplace as dawn and dusk, eating and sleeping. Those which you were not born to, were slipped in, and you scarcely noticed the multiplication of your living advantages. 

And Personal Appearance, Too! 

Clothes? You take into account price, style and quality. Do you know what the material in your suit is, or whence it came? 

The same inquiry you may make as to your hat, your necktie, your collar, your gloves, your shoes, and your shirt and your underwear. The same inquiry would disclose interesting facts as to the things in your house, such as the rugs, the spreads, the furniture, the musical instruments and the ornaments. The foodstuffs come from different parts of the world, and some of them were altered by industrial process. Page 13

Make a Wish, and Presto!

Now, look at the public service conveniences which are ever susceptible to your whims. Press a button, and you have light. Turn a cock or a knob, and you have gas. Move a lever, and water flows from a faucet. 

Lift to your mouth and ear a synthetic handbar and you may exchange words with some one thousands of miles distant on land or at sea, or you may telephone a message to a telegraph office and have it transmitted to the remotest point in civilization. You may speak by radio to a relative or friend on a ship at sea or in a foreign land.

All these facilities are comparatively new. In mediaeval and ancient times the sole accommodations which the masses had in public utilities were furnished in aqueducts, constructed of brick, stone or cement, which conveyed water from highland sources by gravitation to communities. Men and women were obliged to carry the water to their dwellings in jugs and pitchers from central places of supply. But in these times we have light, gas and water in our homes, brought there by wire or in pipes. 

Hot and Other Air Served You

The gas may be piped a hundred or a thousand miles. 

The water may flow through immense mains from a huge impounding reservoir, or a lake or river, perhaps a few hundred miles away, or it may be pumped from an adjacent lake or river into the mains. The electricity may be generated by the power of a lake, river or waterfall, also, perhaps, a hundred miles off. Each service entails an extensive distribution service, which enters into thousands of homes in a community. Page 14

You also have in your home facilities for your pleasure and interest, such as the radio and phonograph. 

Each of these is as easy to operate as the public service adjuncts. You may listen to the description of a base ball or football game, or hear a concert, lecture or play, or an address by the President, a king or distinguished personage. The phonograph will produce for you, if you wish, the voice or music of some one long since dead.

Near your door you may enter a motor bus or street car and alight near your work place. Or you may walk a few steps to your garage and drive forth in your own automobile, and ride where you will. 

The Boss Appreciates You

You have similar ease in your occupation. The air in the office, store or factory is cleaned and either cooled or warmed. You have tools or instruments which do your work for you, and accessories which relieve you of effort and contribute to your comfort.

Talking and motion pictures are among the creations of genius which add to your pleasure.

When you travel, you have the luxury of steam train cars or motor buses, the latter being the modern substitute for the picturesque, but uncomfortable stage coach, and you tarry in hotels more sumptuous than palaces. You may cross the ocean in a floating hotel and playhouse, or fly in an airplane and annihilate time and distance in the voyage. 

You do not need money in order to purchase your luxuries or necessities. You may have them installed and delivered by merely signing documents by which Page 15

you promise to pay for them over an extended term of several months or a few years. Credit for the masses is an invention of the age, and it is so popular that much of our retail business is done on the installment plan. 

And you are not only recipient of all the foregoing courtesies, not excepting that of deferred liquidation of financial obligations. Your work is facilitated and smoothed to such an extent that it is simply a mechanical or routine operation. You also are exempted from thinking.

CHAPTER IV. 

How You Don't Think

It may not have occurred to you that your thinking is cut down by several kinds of standardization and orderliness to the point at which you may, in most of your activities, let your brain function involuntarily and exercise your will scarcely any. Upon cursory reflection you will agree that this is a fact, surprising though it may be to you.

Let us go to a movie or talkie theater. If we ride in a street car or motor bus our thinking is limited to precautionary alertness in entering and alighting, paying the fare and avoiding vehicles as we rush to and from the safety zones. If we ride in an automobile, it practically runs itself; we just start, steer and stop it. A series The picture is thrust on the silver screen. of legends introduces the title and cast of characters and presents information about the play and players, writers, photographers and directors. The persons in the play are not described to us, but pictured; the de- Page 16

velopment of the plot is visualized, and titles and subtitles at spaces clarify, each in a few words, the scenes which are shown on the silver screen. We do not use our will at all. Our ideas follow the pictures. We do not think, for, as it were, the thoughts are pictures, the pictures thoughts. 

You had not considered this? You are in similarly favorable circumstances as to your living and working. 

The review which we have drawn of the unprecedented benefits which are at your service at home and in your business place outlines for our scrutiny an array which would be extraordinary were it not common to all and individual to each. 

You, as well as all the rest of us, are fastidious in your needs. Luxuries of other years are necessities at the present time. Your senses must be pleased as your wants are filled. You have cultivated a nice taste, which we might call a materialistic refinement; it is like the semblance of refinement with which men and women invest themselves with the accumulation of wealth, and it is not unjustified, for, with the exception of the rare and costly things, the average man is as well off in his living and working conveniences as the multimillionaire. 

Luxuries are Stale and Flat

Every additional convenience causes you to be more finicky. You must have it; you cannot do without it, and it must conform in its appearance and quality to the refinement of taste which you have fostered by selfindulgence. You take this attitude concerning your food, also; it must please the eye and palate while it sates your hunger. You would be pleased besides being

Page 17

served, and your desires are as illimitable as your needs, as with you they are the same. 

Is this not the feeling of every one whom you know? 

Do not all your friends and acquaintances live like you, at least up to their social status (whatever they believe it to be) and at least up to their income? The mode of living consists of all the luxuries obtainable, with all the imperatively requisite necessities, and the luxuries are classified in the same category with the needs. 

We allude to these obvious facts to show that there is practically no caste in the mode of living. The rich, the well-to-do and the poor are of mental equality as to luxury and convenience. Members of each class go as far as their money and credit allow them to go. The opulent alone have masterpieces of art, but members of other groups satisfy their culture or curiosity in public museums. 

Too Much Is Not Enough

Nothing is too good for you, as nothing is too good for everybody else. The facilities never are fully satisfying, as, with new ones offered and the old ones improved, your needs and desires increase. Inventors cannot keep up with you; you cannot keep up with them; yet capital and credit seem to be inexhaustible in production and consumption.

Let us consider again the illustration of the motion or talking picture relieving us of the necessity of thinking. Thinking is not so much as a luxury. It is hardly a diversion. You resort it it now and then to break the monotony of not thinking, and to vary your regular routine, and to destroy any doubt which you may have Page 18

that you are human. If there is ever compulsion for you to think, it is only when bills come in or past due installments are stressed to you by the importunity of creditors, who also are adhering to a system and a routine. Should disaster befall you, to think would be urgent.

You learn in school, college and university how to avoid thinking. Teaching students this is a vital principle in the curriculum. As success in occupation depends on one's adjusting himself to rules and methods of standardization, students are instructed thoroughly towards familiarizing themselves with professional and business routine. Knowledge is catalogued and indexed and students are shown where, when and how to find it when (or if) they have use for it. Students are synchronized mentally to reference pamphlets. 

Brain More Useless Than Appendix

The system, routine, machine, tools, instruments and regulations of your business or profession enable you to work mechanically, and you are not obliged to think more than when viewing a picture play. You are expected to do your work according to the standards allotted to you as your line of duty, and, if you thought, you might fail in obeying the strict command of your employer.

Your state in your home is just as congenial to suspended reasoning. You live there by habit. You have an unwritten scheme for every day. The schedule is unchanged from Sunday to Sunday. You ride to and from work daily by the same means of transit and the same route; your work is fixed; you eat in the same Page 19

restaurant, you meet the same persons. In all probability the domestic menu is repeated week after week. You have the same sorts of entertainment in the same places every week. Why should you take the trouble to think? 

Very Exasperating Questions 

Then, what are you? Who are you? To what purpose are you living and working? Are you in quest only of money and pleasure? Have you other objectives? Have you meditated whether you are utilizing your facilities as benefits and whether the results are worth while to you and those whom you love and who love you? These, if to think is comprised among your wishes, are serious things to think about; and should you delay too long in thinking about them, it will be too late to think of them, and your thinking then would be vain.

The foregoing are sensible questions, questions which you should not neglect to answer to yourself. Unless you do, the results of your indifference very likely would be still more detrimental than your motives, and when you arrive at the end of your time there would be but one question left, which would be none of these. 

This would be, what have I done? and you would be unable to answer it except with complete regret, perchance remorse.

The ancients, with fewer facilities than you, propounded these questions to themselves and tried to live to the logic of their conclusions. With no advantages and very few conveniences, unworthy of being compared to yours, they made deliberate efforts towards self-profit, and the futility of their attempts cannot Page 20

detract from the soundness of their thinking and the earnestness of their trials. 

Progress Is Less Than Zero

The questions have remained unasked or unanswered for two or three thousand years. Mankind in this long stretch of time, in which existed sixty to ninety generations, has not progressed towards happiness and health; if the truth must be admitted, we have retrograded. That we should achieve one material triumph after another, and stagger our imaginations with the wonders which we have made from mere ideas, yet misuse them through carelessness, appears to be as anomalous as that the ancients got benefits from lack of conveniences. 

Do you ask yourself why we have not centered our thought on the reason of our standing still for twenty or thirty centuries with regard to the proper utilization of our great advantages? The answer is not in the inventions and machines, in the material evolution, but in ourselves. 

A Little Sarcasm Now and Then

You scarcely could have missed the sarcastic implication in most of the preceding review of the advantages of modern society. The truth is that you are no exception among the millions who have given up genuine thinking and become foolhardy libertines in a rigid system of routine. You have read that you are a human machine which you adjusted, or allowed to be adjusted, precisely to the ruts of business and the conventionalized habits of frenzied society. You degraded yourself to such a degree as to let the projection lantern think Page 21

for you and self-chosen arbiters of style and custom, no better or more studious than yourself, design your wearing apparel and order your conduct.

Perchance, you flushed with pride at the reminder of the advantages which science, invention and the machine had given you, and the luxuries which you classified as benefits. You were vainly elated, perhaps, with the idea that you had progressed so far that you had invented and made machines to substitute for you in thinking, and the benefits which assured you comfort and ease were joined with unlimited individual freedom. 

You rejoiced that you had greater liberty and more conveniences than your forefathers of any era, and you felt superior, as if all shackles had been broken and all beliefs and restrictions destroyed. 

You should be able to see that you have no freedom at all, and the license which you take in a reckless manner of living is no more than personal excess within the bounds arbitrarily prescribed for you by the System, which regulates every phase of your being. You fancy that you can and do do as you please, whereas you do nothing that you please except what you should not. 

Scientist Jabs at Science

You boast of the progress which we have made in the past few centuries, especially in your day, and gaze at the past and its deficiencies with contempt. Have we made good progress? If we have, have we used it right? Is it not possible, or, we should say, probable, that the progress is toward moral and physical degeneration? Instead of being contemptuous of common sense and simplicity, you should, with more reputable propriety, feel humiliated.

Page 22

Most of us brag of the strides made by science and invention. Yet authorities whose opinion is worth while assert with positive conviction that scientists are mainly in the wrong in their discoveries and conclusions. Prof. Calvin Page said publicly in Chicago on 

June 29, 1931, that scientists are wrong in practically every one of their theories concerning the universe, gravity, electricity and other natural phenomena. Dr. 

Page won the Nobel prize in 1924 for his outstanding work on the atomic theory, and his caustic comment cannot be waved aside as of no significance; he is not a charlatan, visionary or reactionary, but a distinguished scholar. A scientist, mingling with scientists, and in sympathy and touch with them, he is qualified to pass judgment deserving of close attention. In making his assertion regarding the implausibility of scientific theories, he announced his discovery of the rex atom. "The rex atom", he said, "is a repellant force. When two of these atoms come together they repel themselves and fly apart. All other atoms cohere to rex atoms".

He added that electricity consists of rex atoms flowing in currents.

It is too bad that we cannot here exhibit motion pictures to think for us and reveal the fallacy of many of the theories of science. If you ever take the pains to exercise your mind at reading, you would be compelled to note that the theories propounded by scientists a year or week ago are disproved by new theories announced by others today. Nevertheless, you, being a human machine, with the part in your skull functioning mechanically, believe every new theory submitted, and thrust out your chest in self-assurant approval.

Page 23

Minds with Attics and Basements On the one hand, scientific advancement is repudiated by conservative savants having dimensional intellect, that is, minds with breadth, as well as depth and height, without, we may be pardoned for saying this, sub-debasement; and on the other hand, inventive evolution throwing millions of workmen out of employment, making business ramshackle, unsettling society, causing pandemonium in politics, and intensifying your discontent at home and in your place of occupation. 

So-called technical progress and the overcoming of the resistance of matter have not added to the sum of human happiness beyond that of the past, because, for one reason, at each step forward the more do wants increase. Thus, with the increase in inventions the sum of happiness decreases in proportion. You cannot deride this as a theory; it is, as we shall see, an obvious fact to the majority of human beings and in their common experience. 

You may sit back complacently, perhaps with a curl in your lips and a sneer on your face, musing that it is presumptuous for one whom you automatically index in your brain box as an amateur in research and philosophy to criticize your idols and their conjectures.

But we again have authority to support us, and present to you Dr. Glenn Frank, president of the University of Wisconsin. 

Opinionated Man "In the Red" 

Dr. Frank says in an article of the McClure Newspaper Syndicate, Inc., that the heaviest liability to society is the opinionated man who is certain that a Page 24

thing cannot be done. You and the rest of us, according to his view, are tradition-bound; we have grooved our ideas to fixed convictions, and our insight and vision are warped. He alludes to a statement by an executive of the General Motors Research Corporation to the effect that experts who are called in state what cannot be done. The executive explains that the experts insist that something cannot be done, and the conference breaks up then, and the workers disperse and go ahead and sometimes do what the experts maintain could not be done.

You assume that you are not tradition-bound or activated by each and all of the innumerable chains of habit and routine, but we shall look into the subject and try to find out whether you are tightly manacled and cannot either think or do without going in the line marked out for you. The freedom which you fancy that you have is no more than a kind of self-deception. You flatter yourself by insisting that you are free, in the same manner that you flatter yourself that you are superior to those who have lived before you. 

1001 Plagues of the U. S. A.

As to the luxuries which are included among your advantages, let us contend, too, that they conduce to tie you fast into the system, and they are not luxuries, but plagues. Every convenience which you enjoy is a nuisance. It is a preventive to your peace, your happiness, your health. As you add to your facilities for soft living, you add to your discomfort, and, as has just been said, every addition to them engenders in you a desire for more.

Each new convenience is another souring element in your discontent; it makes you more morose Page 25

and exacting, and you are irritated both with the luxuries which you possess and the craving for more. 

You are tradition-bound and custom-bound. You are habit-bound and opinion-bound. You are pridebound and luxury-bound. You are ruled by the petty whims of self-interest and the small vanities of friends and acquaintances. You are operated as an automaton by scores of rules and contrivances of organized business and irrational society. 

Do these charges irritate you? Or do you approve them? Before proceeding, let us examine into the state of your mind. Your first mental inclination (not thought, mind you) is to resent and denounce them.

This is not surprising, as your brain is functioning mechanically. You long ago quit reasoning. Part of the twenty-four-hour day the routine of your business controls you and during the other part the routine of society and the habit of your pleasure. You would be forced spontaneously by the system which dominates your every idea and act to deny the charges. "Pine Appling" the Brain and Nerves

It would shock you to be told that you are realizing no satisfaction from the advantages offered to you by science and invention, and, on the contrary, you are living in misery and to no salutary purpose. In order to emphasize this point, and try to liven you to resume thinking, your attention is called to the statement which Dr. George W. Crile of the Cleveland Clinic, made in an address to the annual convention of the 

American College of Surgeons in Atlantic City, October 14, 1931.

Page 26

He declared categorically that we of today are shattering our brains and nervous systems and wrecking our bodies by our follies and excesses in our domestic, social and business affairs. We are working and indulging ourselves, he said, to insane asylums, hospitals and cemeteries. Dr. Charles H. Mayo, of the Mayo Clinic, Rochester, Minn., in an interview made similar statements.

CHAPTER V. 

The observations that the passion and emotion identified with our living and working modes are choking the span of life at 58 years were stressed by Dr. Crile. 

Both doctors, Crile and Mayo, said that the tendency toward prolonging the span had resulted from security against plagues and epidemics and from preventive measures studied out and applied by medical and allied sciences, whereas a new phase had been attained now, which necessitated the individual's actively interesting himself in his health and longer life by soliciting and following the advice of the physician. 

Too Much Up-to-Dateness? "Adult diseases are caused mostly by infections and emotions", Dr. Crile stated. "Nature combats infections by speeding the body's chemical activity. This increased speed causes strains. The body as a whole may be damaged by the chemical defense. "The emotions, worry, fear, hate and jealousy affect every cell in the body. Some organs are stimulated; some inhibited; all are disturbed; waste products are increased; foundations of certain characteristic human diseases are laid.

Page 27

"This is particularly true of those who have made civilization what it is, and who now bear the burden, who have the greatest capacity and urge for work and worry".

Dr. Crile described the diseases and ailments coming from the emotions as "the wound stripes of civilization". The principal diseases of this kind, he said, are exophthalmic goitre, neurocirculatory asthenia, diabetes, peptic ulcer, high blood pressure and heart disease. "Man cannot fear", he said; "he cannot hate, he cannot worry intellectually-he fears with all his organs.

There is not one group of phenomena for the president of a bank in a financial crash and another for the criminal who suddenly faces the naked probability of the penitentiary, or for a mother who watches over the child. 

Doctor Sees "Keep Off My Grass" "I feel justified in the prophecy that before many decades the role of the physician will have changed so much that his profession will seem an entirely new one". 

Dr. Mayo declared that it was almost useless to offer advice to old persons, but there was some hope in the future in giving advice to the young. Counsel to preserve health, avoid health dangers and prolong life was more important now than ever, as the individual would have to fight his own health battles. "All the mass destroying diseases have largely come under control", said Dr. Mayo, "and now each adult must fight his own individual battle; usually he does not brook any interference with his own mode of living. Page 28

"I like to talk to young people. The remarks I make need to fall on young soil. Old people either do not need advice, or, if they do, they are beyond help. 

Newspapers Do the Impossible "Newspapers are the greatest educating, thoughtmolding enterprise in the world, and I desire to pay tribute to them for the part they have played in the subject under discussion. Our great daily newspapers believe in rational medicine, and almost every edition has a column from a physician concerning health and its preservation and disease or its prevention. "Newspapers stand as the bulwarks in the support of scientific medicine as represented by the American Medical Association and by special societies, such as the American College of Surgeons and the American College of Physicians".

Dr. William H. Mayo, of the Mayo Clinic, in an interview at the same convention warned the individual not to look for new treatment. The life span, he said, should be increased, and will be, but only if the individual takes care of his health. If the individual will do this, the average span should be extended from fiftyeight to seventy years. "Man tends to degenerate", Dr. Mayo said. "He must take care of himself between the ages of thirty and fifty in order to live to be seventy. "The idea prevails that if one gets a good doctor when one is about 58 years old, he has a good chance of keeping his health and going on living for years. Health, however, should be studied and taken care of at all times.

Page 29

That Human Rarity-Common Sense "No new method of treatment exists today which can benefit the mass of humanity, but by this care of health, the individual learning to co-operate more intelligently with the physician, I believe that within the next twenty-five years man may come to the age of 70 as the average expectancy. I believe this can come through his own efforts and interest in his health if manifested earlier in life".

You will admit that these three doctors-Crile and the two Mayos-are reputable authorities, whose aim is to benefit persons by advice and instruction quite as much as by medicine and treatment. 

Dr. Crile says that we are ruining our health and opposing all the efforts of science to prolong the life span by our recklessness and indifference, and the Mayos warn us that physicians cannot help us beyond a certain point; we must, they urge, help ourselves. 

You notice that Dr. William H. Mayo expresses the opinion that 12 years can be added to the life span expectancy by general care of health during two or three decades. You notice that he emphasizes the belief that the individual must give more attention to health. 

A Fortune for a Missing Tooth

Whatever science accomplishes in preventing disease and physicians achieve in curing and controlling it is being done. But the physicians cannot prolong the life line if individuals continue to wreck mind and body irresponsibly, as Dr. Crile illustrated. The progress made in prolonging human life resulted from scientific Page 30

discovery and skill, and then, as Dr. Crile showed, we checked the expansion ourselves with strains on every part of the body and on the mind. 

Mr. Julius Rosenwald, the great Jewish philanthropist and merchant of Chicago, who died recently, was an authority on human affairs. His hundreds of millions of dollars could not buy back his health, he said, if he lost it. One of his aphorisms was: "The millions that came to me at 50 could not restore the tooth I lost at 30". Here, in a few words, he elucidated the contention which we are considering, which is that we cannot keep our health unless we take care of it, and we cannot rely on money, any more than on science, to do the impossible; it is impossible to buy late in life some physical asset that was lost earlier.

The suicidal living habits of the present age were denounced recently by another prominent medical authority, Dr. Theodore B. Appel, Secretary of Health of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. He stated in a signed article in the United States Daily of Dec. 15, 1931, that physicians, surgeons and public health departments have done their utmost in efforts to prolong the span of human life, and henceforth results must be looked for in individual hygiene. "As a matter of fact", he says, "it can be safely said that the great scourges to which humanity less than a half century ago was a target, have, thanks to preventive medicine and modern scientific research, been laid low. Therefore, the big factor yet to be discovered by the average person is that health and long life at the present time are to a large extent a performance problem on the part of the individual.

Page 31

"Nature's rules, though strict, are not harsh. It follows that a little less affection for present-day killing conduct and more personal attention to the basic rules of health is urgently required.

Limit for Public Health Officers 

"Public health officials have done and will continue to do their part. However, long, healthy and vigorous, life in the final analysis demands personal, intelligent and daily attention. Death rates of the middle-aged and older persons will unfortunately be maintained unless this fact can be sufficiently impressed upon the consciousness of every one". 

Dr. Appel avers that we are killing ourselves by riotous and extravagant living. Disease and death he attributes largely to recklessness and indifference. "The great pity about many of the deaths occurring these days", he states, "is that they need not have happened if the victims had been more considerate of their health and lives than they were. Indeed, the great barrier which has been erected against public health officials and modern preventive medicine is the personal conduct one; unfortunately, this can only be removed by the people themselves. "High-speed living, excessive use of stimulants, lack of sufficient rest, inadequate or immoderate food intake and absence of proper exercise all demand their eventual toll against the human system. Based upon performance, the present-day stresses and strains evidently were not anticipated when the original (and unchangeable) model for the body was determined upon".

Page 32

An Ill Wind Blowing No Good 

Estimating that the economic loss in the United States from sickness is approximately $6,000,000,000 a year, Congress in 1931 established the National Health Institute to preserve the health of the citizens and prolong the life span. The estimated loss is a tentative, substantial calculation of the loss in terms of money; it does not, as, of course it could not, indicate the more crying losses, that is, the capital losses, due to deterioration or breakdown of individual health.

It is an attempt of the national government to help physicians and scientists generally and the people to combat disease and conserve health. "It will", says the managing director, Joseph E. Ransdell, in an instructive feature article published and circulated by Universal Service, "marshal the nation's army of experts in the sciences of medicine, surgery, psychiatry, dentistry, chemistry, physics, biology, bacteriology, pharmacology and allied professions in a concerted drive to prevent disease by ascertaining its cause and applying preventive measures in advance of its outbreak. "Every available facility will be provided to aid and encourage scientists to combat sickness; solve the remaining mysteries of disease; discover all the laws governing life, and increase the years of man. It is the greatest health conservation project every undertaken by any government".

Director Ransdell continues that the "American government now is actively seeking the secret of longer life".

Page 33

Government Also Chases a Secret

We have made astounding progress, in his opinion, in fighting pestilence. Science has conquered yellow fever, smallpox, malaria, typhus, hookworm and other infectious diseases. However, he comments that we have been "almost niggardly by comparison in combating and controlling the noncommunicable diseases that each year afflict thousands of citizens, exacting a more distressing toll".

He accentuates the preceding remark with the statement that "it is pathetic that most of these noncommunicable diseases are just as fatal today as they were a century ago".

His conclusions are as alarming as Dr. Crile's concerning the specifications of the progress made toward prolonging life. They are to the same effect, which is that the gains are limited to preventive work done by physicians and allied scientists and to work centered on diseases suffered by infants. "Private initiative", says Director Ransdell, "through the great foundations and institutions which have been founded for scientific research, and through the progress that has been made in sanitation and hygiene, have increased the average length of life in the United States from forty-nine to fifty-six years in the last twenty-five or thirty years, but this has been accomplished largely by study of child diseases and the consequent reduction in infant mortality. "Virtually nothing has been done to give man a greater life expectancy during his most productive years. Nor has much been done in alleviating the suf- Page 34

ferings incident to the sicknesses which attack man in his mature years".

Human Heart Is Battered and Bruised 

Director Ransdell says that diseases of the heart and circulatory systems have increased from seventy per 100,000 in 1870 to more than 300 in 1930. In 1870 

Bright's disease caused fifty deaths in 100,000, whereas at present it causes practically twice as many. The statistics thus confirm the warning declared by Dr. Crile. 

Virtually nothing is known about the common cold, which, physicians state, is by no means a trifle, and which, from the economic viewpoint, is one of the principle causes of loss from time and efficiency. "Others", Director Ransdell says, "are influenza, before which modern science remains impotent; measles, the offending organisms of which have not yet been definitely proven; pneumonia, which is still unconquered; tuberculosis and cancer, which baffle the skill of scientists; childbed sickness, so fatal to mothers, and infantile paralysis, which remains a curse to childhood. "There are also Brights disease and diabetes, which are so prevalent among adult men; mouth infections, which are productive of so many general maladies, anemia, mental troubles, heart disease and venereal troubles, all of which take heavy toll of life, the greater number during the most productive periods; and leprosy, life's greatest tragedy, which is only slowly being subdued". 

Decline Is On the Increase

Mark this assertion of his: "Not only have the death rates of these degenerative diseases characteristic of the Page 35

middle or later years of life, failed to decline—they have actually increased at a rather rapid rate”. 

Director Ransdell calls attention to the preponderance of interest in communicable diseases which tended to occur in epidemic form. It was with these, in the field of preventive medicine, that the greatest progress was made. In fact, you have the statements of some of the foremost physicians in the United States that practically the whole sum of progress is in preventive medicine due immediately to the labors of physicians and allied scientists. "The reason for this deviation of interest is apparent", he continues, alluding to the conquest of communicable diseases of epidemic form. "For the first time in the history of science methods had been devised and placed at the disposal of investigators for discovering the microscopic agents which caused these diseases. "Compensation of Interest" "Before this time the diagnosis and treatment of these maladies had necessarily been more or less empirical. 

Now they are in the way of becoming both accurate and rational. Research agencies were naturally attracted by this immensely and immediately productive field of work and threw the bulk of their energies in this direction. "This compensation of interest does not occur without a compensatory neglect of non-communicable diseases which showed no tendency to occur in epidemic form, although the aggregate of cases might far outnumber that of some of the acute epidemic diseases". 

In other words, there is another deviation in medical Page 36

practice. This is to medical practice by the lay individual. These diseases that lead to the insane asylum and the grave cannot be prevented by the physicians and in most instances cannot be cured by the physicians. Generally speaking, they cannot be cured at all. 

The only security against them is by prevention, and this is a precaution which the individual has to take himself by following nature's laws.

CHAPTER VI. 

And So Your Mind Is-Free?

Now, it may be hoped, your mind is liberated sufficiently to look into the subject with diminished prejudice. These eminent physicians and surgeons do not wish to insult you, but to warn you against committing suicide as a robot. 

Clergymen caution you that you are abandoning moral principles and scrapping spiritual ideals, and your higher faculties are desecrated by regular addiction to pleasure. They accuse you of being weak and sick in the spirit and the doctors of medicine and surgery of impairing your mind and wracking your body. These thinkers base their conclusions on real and indisputable evidence.

Each of us, we assume, longs for the same generic objectives. Each desires health; each desires happiness. 

These are the supreme satisfactions. 

All others are secondary, contributory, sequential, correlative. Fundamentally, health and happiness are sufficient. You are getting neither, and through your own fault.

You probably have not discriminated between pleasure and happiness, believing them to be synonymous, Page 37

and you are engaged in the quest of pleasure instead of the pursuit of happiness. Pleasure cannot bring you happiness; on the contrary it brings you to the condition which the wisest man, Solomon, defined as "Vanity! Vanity! All is vanity!" He indulged in all the pleasures procurable, yet with ever-increasing dissatisfaction, until, at length, he denounced them all as empty and pernicious.

Home Not in Universal Cosmos? 

You seek pleasure with more facilities than Solomon had, and your entertainment and luxuries are more varied. Modern facilities simplify the quest of pleasure. Were there happiness in pleasure, you should be happy; but there is not and cannot be, for pleasure is vanity.

To what extent, do you think, have your conveniences and comforts contributed to your happiness?

The truth unnerves you. Your well-appointed house or apartment is only a refuge, and not a home; you repair to it when you revolt against the annoyances of the combined Babel and Babylon in which you frolic. The appointments in it, which are supposed to afford you ease or pleasure, distress you, and it is as formal as the other places which you visit. It is not a sanctum; there is no peace in it, or happiness. You had added one convenience after another, and one after another palled on you, and added to your discomfort rather than your peace and ease. Every one of them requires time and labor of you, and all together fret you.

Nothing is easier to feel nor harder to understand than the discontent, tribulation and wear and tear consequent to the modes which govern us in our living Page 38

and occupation. Every new convenience should, it seems, increase happiness and safeguard health, and tend toward establishing a permanent state of contentful ease. Yet your experience, as is that of every one else, is the reverse.

The fault is partly in the modes, but chiefly in yourself. You, like every person whom you know, have submitted to system, routine and habit, whereas you should rule them to your benefit. As Dr. Crile says, the modes are driving mankind to madness, illness, decrepitude and immature senility. The sorry significance of this is that body and mind are debilitated in or prior to the prime of life, and there ensue a few years of acute physical and mental suffering in the dark mood of despair, and one cannot regret, in the circumstances, that angry Nature prematurely terminates this existence. In the finality, your controversion of your advantages drags you down to the depths. 

In Action, But Doing Nothing 

Wherein, you consider, are you to blame more than the modes for your failure at living? First, your motives are wrong, and last, your objectives. You live and work in prescribed or habituated action. You feel that you must ever be in motion, whether you are accomplishing anything or not. 

You turn on the radio simply because you are used to doing so. You may pay no particular attention to the broadcast; you probably do not; but you are conscious of hearing something. You are conforming to habit. You go out motoring; although you have no special destination, you are doing something, and again you conform to habit. You might look into every Page 39

thing that you say and do from morning to night and day to day, and you would be amazed at the fact that you adhere strictly to habit or routine. 

You have accumulated a mass of responsibilitiesresponsibilities in facilities, luxuries, pleasures and wild dreams, even to obligations in the future for conveniences which you are using at present. Your heart, your nerves, every part of your body, your brain-all are synchronized in unguided functioning to the destructive compulsions of the mode. And so it is, as the experts have proved by evidence from experience, that you discarded opportunities for the better favors subject to your will, and substituted the bothersome distractions which rob you of happiness and health and shorten your life. 

If You Do Even Think- 

Have you ever thought profoundly concerning the best goods which it is possible for you to acquire? This is the question to which all that has been written here leads. It is the question among all the big questions which confront you and everybody else. What do you live for? What do you realize of benefit to yourself from the modes by which you live and work? What are you most desirous to have?

In all probability you have not looked into the relationship which exists between yourself and your comprehensive environment. The idea has gleamed in your mind at intervals, but only for an instant. Now and then you have felt a poignant regret that, with all that you have, you have nothing. But you have not gone further with your pondering. You have always dismissed the subject abruptly. Page 40

Let us try to find the answer to the first question. I say, Health and Happiness. And let us see whether this is the right answer. 

You might, temporarily, have happiness without health, or health without happiness. But over a large portion of your life the two would be interdependent. 

Impairment of health would tend ultimately to the dissipation of happiness; the loss of happiness eventually would wear out health. There is a continuous countereffect, alternately important or trivial, between them, and it is indisputable that the possession of both is the perfect natural state for any individual.

The Long and Short of It The most satisfactory and most inclusive fulfillment of your highest and broadest desire is happiness. For you to get more than this out of life would be impossible. Happiness is the best condition that you can realize, and, therefore, it connotes practically all that you can get. It is the most that can be realized in a long or short life, for whoever has happiness has all that it is possible for him to obtain. Happiness is everything; lack of it, nothing.

The average person confuses happiness with pleasure. 

Pleasure, he believes, is happiness. This is why, for one reason, he seeks pleasure. Existence will be pleasant, he believes, if he has all the conveniences of comfort and luxury, and if he accumulates money, and if he gratifies his emotions and passions. With the enjoyments as many as he can buy or create, he would, he fancies, be happy.

The luxuries, enjoyments and comforts are some of Page 41

the pleasures. They generally tend to mitigate happiness, as in the indulgence there are stimulating or enervating consequences detrimental to the nerves, the body and the brain. They excite or depress, either of which effect produces results destructive to happiness; and they engender more and new desires for additional luxuries and enjoyments, which, as we have already considered, absolutely preclude the prospect of happiness. 

It is the mistaken opinion that happiness lies in the pursuit and achievement of pleasure that causes most persons to miss true happiness. Most strive for pleasure and find disappointment. They could obtain the maximum of realizable pleasure in the attainment of happiness, but happiness cannot be had in pleasure. If you want pleasure, do not expect happiness.

The Chromatic Bubble Bursts 

The complexities of our material progress have multiplied our pleasures, and the growth and increase of allurements have generated a myriad of cares which turn our pleasures into unrest and sap our vitality. The physicians tell us that we are ageing prematurely in wasted health and clouded minds, and you are aware, as every one else is, that not only has happiness not been realized, but pleasure also has not.

Perfunctory reflection suffices to indicate that pleasure could not be agreeable except in the fast-passing moment during which its fascination prevails. As soon as its delight vanishes, which is suddenly, an unpleasant reaction sets in, and this is the opposite to happiness, the proof within us that the pursuit of pleasure is the wrong way toward happiness.

Page 42

In happiness there is peace and contentment. Those among us who have taken the great benefit which they can easily get-happiness-could desire nothing else. 

They possess the only treasure, which, with health, makes living a joy. 

Health and happiness! Are there any substitutes for them? Having them, what more could be wished for? 

Now, you are competent to answer that yourself.

You would, favored with them, have but one more hope, but one more wish. This would be that you might retain them as long as possible, that is, as you would regard their preciousness, you would desire to expand your life as far into the future as they would endure. So, your desire, described in one term, would be: A long life with health and happiness.

Inasmuch as the consummate wish of every human being is to live in happiness and health, his correlative desire is to preserve health and happiness indefinitely.

He longs to retain the natural beatitude with a perfect state of mind and body for as many years as he can stretch his time into, and thus own the only satisfactions which assure virtual contentment. He would like to have a long life in happiness and health. 

Looking Back Into the Future 

Longevity is a mathematical problem in which the future and present have a vital connection with the past, and it is a proposition of extreme importance to all mankind in the direct and personal concern shown actively in it by the individual. The average duration of human life is computed from varying conditions and circumstances among all races and nations, and a high Page 43

death rate for one people counteracts the encouraging reports of another with a low death rate. 

CHAPTER VII. 

"The Span of Life", the curve of the years of human existence is called. And so far it has been no more than a span. The reach of years resembles the spread of the fingers of the hand, being but a span. As the distance marked out by the hand is short, so the period of time, which is life, is brief. The allusion is impressively descriptive.

The span of life on the average has not lengthened in thousands of years. To all practical purposes, it is the same today as it was in the earliest recorded era. 

The child born today comes into the world with a life expectancy, based on average experience, of fifty-eight years, and in this respect he is not as well off as one born in Ur of the Chaldees in the days of Abraham. 

Ripe Old Age Is Unmellowed

The promise of threescore and ten, the ripe old age mentioned with gusto in the Old Testament, is the substance of hope for the few. But it is the exception. 

The outlook for the majority is, as it has been for centuries, less than threescore. Five decades and eight years constitute the small average for what is to most of us the normal span of life. 

You might say to yourself that the span of life has not lengthened in the latter millenium because the normal average of fifty-eight years represents the sum of possible effort.

Your passive acquiescence in this subconscious ver- Page 44

dict is not sustained by the studied opinion of scientists whose reasoning is free from the restraints and bounds to investigation and thought which tradition, routine and dictum have fixed. 

The average span of life has been determined without experience, not with experience. People have just lived. 

This is experience. But they have not tried to see how long they could live, and this is want of experience. They have been indifferent to the means at their command to protract the curve of existence. Unless and until individuals and governments, with the advice of physicians, surgeons and other qualified scientists, put to use all available measures for lengthening the span of life, it cannot be held that fifty-eight years, the present estimate, is the last possibility or the final evidence of experience. Obviously, experience, to be full, must embrace every resource and expedient for improving health, preventing disease and postponing the end.

Your Other Relations-The External 

The scientific theory which should apply to the duration of human life has been stated by Dr. Eugene L. 

Fisk, an executive officer of the Life Extension Insti tute of New York. "There is no law of mortality”, he says. "There is no law of mortality"! That is, he declares in effect, there is no specifically definable obstruction in the sense of advanced science to an extension of the average life-line beyond a point staggering to imagination. "There is no law of mortality", continuing with Dr. 

Fisk's views, "shocking as this may seem to some actuarial minds, insistent that the curve must end at Page 45

ninety-five, because an actuary discovered that it now ends there ***. Life is an internal response to ex ternal relations, and not a period of activity preordained to cease in a given time. "Life has been extended for the young, but there is good reason to believe that it has been shortened for the elderly. In the United States our falling death rate is a reflection of gains in the years below fifty chiefly". 

Dr. Fisk by incidental implication calls attention in this assertion to the common fault, even among the learned and inquisitive, of taking for granted whatever happens to be. We look upon the average of fifty-eight years as the arbitrary normal merely because some actuary figured it out. He, too, confirms the argument heretofore presented to you that it is an illogical assumption to think that something cannot be done because it has not been done.

Darwin laid down the concept that human life is an internal interaction with external relations. The fact of it is so obvious as not to call for the least additional comment. 

If Tried It Might Be Done

Nothing noble, great or stupendous would be attempted by the most ambitious and daring against the deterrent attitude that "it can't be done". Most of the innovations and successes of exceptional importance are things that the tradition-bound or the habit-bound coldly said could not be done, and many enterprises, now resting in embryo because their progress has been stifled by arbitrary opinion, will be pushed to completion when thinkers who are not intimidated by custom give them the test.

Page 46

Lengthening the span of life has not been tried except by a few experts and in a limited number of methods. 

Others are cowed by the rut-idea that "it can't be done". To maintain that something "can't be done" that has not been tried is absurd. It would be almost equally unreasonable and fearful to say that something "can't be done" because a few trials were without results.

Many ventures, we know from our familiarity with commercial history, have failed several times and still have at last been managed to success. We are aware that this experience is common in science, the arts, and, in fact, in all activities. Generally, a minor detail or a mistake of judgment, or an omission of some kind, or frailty of confidence, would explain why there might be numerous failures before something could be condemned as impossible. However, this point does not apply to the prolongation of the life span, as the question is not one of failure, but of neglect, for no general measures have been taken by the public and individuals singly or in co-operation to find out from actual experience how long the human being might, on the average, live. 

Expectation vs. the Adding Machine

Really, nobody has said regarding prolongation of the Span of Life that the hope is vain; nobody has said that "this can't be done". With the exception of the disease-preventive measures taken by physicians and surgeons and the work of public health officials and of a few societies, the probability of extending the average life limitation has been ignored. Thus, it can be held on the testimony of fact and such experience as we Page 47

have that we have ignored one of our most vital problems as if it were rubbish.

Mankind has been living a certain number of years on the average, and an actuary discovered, as Dr. Fisk comments, that the normal span is fifty-eight years; and so, without allowing the matter to interfere with our pleasure and routine, we have complacently adopted, informally and unquestionably, the casual suggestion that the span consists of fifty-eight years. 

In view of what Dr. Fisk, who is an acknowledged authority, says, what, in your opinion, would be the ultimate terminus possible to the span of life? Dr. 

Fisk states that "the ultimate attainable lifetime may be as many million years as the duration of habitable conditions on the earth". His assertion restricts the span by a sole qualification, which is that the "attainable lifetime" is measurable at the maximum to the "duration of habitable conditions".

According to the strict, scientific interpretation of his opinion, you could have a lifetime corresponding to the existence of the earth, provided "habitable conditions" would prevail so long; and, inasmuch as your lifetime could be "as many million years" as human life could be sustained on the earth, it logically follows in the technical sense that the average span could be that long. "Life", says Dr. Fisk, "is an internal response to external conditions", and he says further that it is "a continuous adjustment of internal relations to external relations". He is very careful, as you notice, to conform to the reasonable sequences suggested by the laws of nature in the light of scientific knowledge. His Page 48

statement is logical as a scientific deduction. Life, he explains, is within you, and, therefore, it is the internal relations; and the rest of nature is outside of you, this comprises the external relations. 

To Many, a Very High Compliment and

Consequently, there is "a continuous adjustment of internal relations to external relations". Your life, as personified by you, has effects on the external relations, and the latter exert influences over you. You are a living unit in nature, the exclusive living unit having higher intelligence, and this higher intelligence cannot function in rational harmony with the external relations unless it accepts the philosophy forced on you by the evidences of nature that life is too brief only because you and others have not relied on these evidences to lengthen it. In other words, few of us have given a single thought to the material evidences of physics for a continuous adjustment to stretch out the span of life.

Now, let us analyze to the last conclusion the scientific import of Dr. Fisk's conviction. He refers to the "duration of habitable conditions on the earth", saying that human life could last as long as they last. The "duration of habitable conditions" is the exclusive qualification that he declares to the possible duration of the lifetime. Obviously, he means by "habitable conditions" the conditions which are necessary for generating or preserving life.

The internal relations are you. Life is within you. 

The external relations are the natural things and conditions outside of you; they are the soil and the air, Page 49

the heat and the cold, the changing seasons, food and drink, climate, altitude, the sun and other planets; that is, all of nature outside of you with which you have the least direct or indirect contact. We should include among the external relations the other human beings who come and go in your work and play, and all persons, things, forces and conditions in your environment, including your economic circumstances and the luxuries and conveniences at your command. 

Your life, then, is your whole life-the life within you and the world outside of you, the internal and external relations. There is, then, a continuous adjustment going on of the internal relations with the external. 

A Physical Possibility-the Sesquicentenarian Offhand, we feel that it is ridiculous to believe that the lifetime could be a million years, or 500 years, or 200 or 100 years, but this is so because we are used to thinking of fifty-eight years as the average for the span, and the idea of more strikes us as being preposterous. A centenarian is, from our standpoint, a rare specimen, and a sesquicentenarian would be an ancient and a freak.

The severed portion of the heart of a chicken embryo which the celebrated Dr. Alexis Carrel placed in a test tube in the Rockefeller Institute of Medical Research in 1912 is reported to be still active. Dr. Carrel thinks that the only things which prevent the life of man from being perpetual are the brain and nervous system. 

These, he thinks, are not possessed of the properties to assure corporeal immortality. 

Dr. Morris Fishbein, of Chicago, editor of the American Medical Journal, one of the best known scientists Page 50

in the world, declared, in an address to the Temple Club, in Chicago, that work would promise to the average man the Biblical span of threescore and ten years.

In order to live long, he advised his auditors to keep on working continually.

Among prominent men who attained the Biblical span and with whom work has been a pleasure he mentioned the following: Andrew Mellon, 76, Secretary of the Treasury; George W. Wickersham, 73, distinguished in government and law; Samuel Insull, 74, public utility magnate; Edwin Markham, 79, poet; Ralph Modjeski, 71, engineer; former United States Senator James 

A. Reed, 70; Jane Addams, 71, sociologist; Clarence 

Darrow, 74, lawyer, and Lorado Taft, 71, sculptor.

Picking up the Old Testament, reading at random you will run across in the chronicles of persons conspicuous in the early history of mankind reliable information as to individual life spans ranging from 400 to 

969 years. Methuselah and Noah lived nearly a thousand years, and several of the patriarchs mentioned prospered for 400 years.

External conditions may have been different at that remote time from what they are today. There may have been only two seasons, and both clement. The days in all likelihood were not shorter, but in all probability were longer; and, were this the case, the lifetimes reported as 400 years could have been 800 or more and it is possible that Methuselah, according to our reckoning, may have lived 2,000 years. We do not know what the external natural conditions were that long ago, when the earth was young. A day might have been the equivalent of a thousand years. Page 51

Dispensing with speculation, we are certain that many persons lived 400 to nearly 1,000 years, and in these facts we have historical support for the scientific theory propounded by Dr. Fisk. It is reasonable to conclude that as many men have lived 400 years and more, other men can do so, and that the average span could be lengthened indefinitely. Scientific theory and natural proof agree.

CHAPTER VIII. 

The Chemist Knows What You Are 

What is this visible and invisible state that is life? 

Physically, what are you? Life is the vitality within you, the vitality imbued in flesh, blood and bone. Life is the active condition of a material body of regular form and appearance by which the body exists, functions and thinks. The flesh, blood and bone are living matter; they are inanimate matter, which, by natural process, are merged and blended into the shape, position and state which results in active existence. 

The human body is composed of material substances, products and variations of chemical transition which are of the earth. The human body is of the earth, and, therefore, earthy.
