It is as earthy as the glass in your window, the vase on your table, the gold of your watch, the diamond in your ring, the fabric of which your clothes are made, the birds of the air, or the snake that crawls on the ground. It is as earthy as the air that you breathe, the water that you drink, the food that you eat. 

Everything that you eat and wear, every machine or tool that you use, everything that you see, is earthy.

Page 52

The fine painting and the canvas on which it is produced by the skill of man are earthy. You are as earthy as everything about you, and everything about you is as earthy as you.

You as an entity of substance are an individualized chemical compound. As a body you consist of liquids, minerals, and gases, that is, of elements which exist in inanimate nature. Chemists have determined the human body to be earthy in this combination: Oxygen, 72 percent; carbon, 13.50 percent; hydrogen, 9.10 percent; nitrogen, 2.50 percent; calcium, 1.30 percent; phosphorus, 1.15 percent; sulphur, .01476 percent; sodium, .10 percent; chlorine, .085 percent; fluorine, .08 percent; potassium, .026 percent; magnesium, .012 percent; iron, .01 percent; silicon, .0002 percent, and traces of aluminum, iodine, copper and lead.

Still Another Relation-the Pumpkin 

In the same way that iron or coal is a part of nature, or that a fish is part of nature, or a bird or a dog is part of nature, or that a tree, a flower, or a pumpkin is a part of nature, you are a part of nature. The ingredients of your body are derived from the external conditions from which you are born and in which you exist. By physical origin you are of the earth, you live on the earth, you live of the earth, you are continuously part of the earth, a material body imbued with the spark of life, an active and thinking material body, a human chemical compound. 

I emphasize preventive measures. Once one's health is broken down from the speed, madness, worry and excesses due to misuse and disuse of civilization's advantages, it cannot be renewed. The physicians state Page 53

this. The only security is in prevention, and prevention is the personal duty of the individual to himself and his family.

As I write this caution, I think of an article by the celebrated Mr. Hendrik Willem Van Loon in the New

York Herald-Tribune of January 3, 1932. 

"I can sit by and smile pleasantly when the world is passing through this crisis of imbecility", he says, "because history has taught me that we have not yet begun to live, that we are still cave-men driving about in highpowered cars, that our famous leaders thus far have never yet risen above the intellectual and spiritual level of those painted and tattooed savages who thought that they could enrich themselves by stealing their neighbor's property, that speaking by and large we are still brethren under the skin of those troglodytes who occupied the Western world in the wake of the retreating glaciers and who resembled us in everything except their superior ability to describe animal life in a pictorial form".

His ideas are gratifyingly identical with ones which I have written in this book for your entertainment and benefit. He writes of "a profound and unshakable feeling for the universality and the absolute oneness of all created substance". The following excerpt recalls exact thoughts which I have written here:

Van Loon Discovers the Raccoon 

"They (who observe the laws) will be able to feel the kinship", he says, "between man and the tree in his backyard, to greet the funny-nosed raccoon and the ponderous bear as his distant cousins, to learn to regard Page 54

all living matter as highly diversified expressions of that universal energy which has neither beginning nor end, and to which the extinction of a star or an entire planetary system means no more than the destruction of an anthill by a careless wanderer-a mere temporary transfer of a few sparks of energy from one container to another".

Man from his beginning has been a living unit in nature and a living unit of nature, and so you, from your birth, have been a being emanating from nature, and now, as your eyes follow these words, transmitting them to your brain, which transmutes them into thought, you are still a part of nature. You have changed with the progression of the years and with the action and interaction of internal and external relations; but compositely you are in body as earthen as you were in the helpless and green periods of your life. 

Life, we think in earnest appreciation, is our most precious endowment. It is a real gratuity. We had no participation whatever in its generation. We cannot think of it as other than a gift; and even if we valued it commensurately for itself alone, for the state of active being, none of us could prize it less than in the desire and by the endeavor to use it to personal and common benefit and preserve it in the healthy and happy state to as great a length as he can. 

What treasure has one that is so priceless as life?

And this, the greatest boon, is a bestowal obtained without an initial price! All the extraordinary gifts which are ours, all the advantages which are necessary normally for sound health and true happiness, are received free, and all are connected with life. Page 55

Can You Imagine It? Life at Its Worst!

Life at its real or conceivable worst remains the most valuable and useful asset of each of us. "While there is life there is hope". No contrariety could be so unnatural, so unreasonable, so despicable, as a willingness to relinquish this treasure, or to destroy it. The very obligation of appreciation entails at least the readiness in the first place to retain the gift and in the second place to make the most of it. The selfish point of view should impel one to treasure life appropriately. neglect to strive to use life in health and toward happiness, to disregard health and waste life, is an offense against nature and conscience almost as grave as hatred of life. Both are measures of self-destruction, the most abominable of natural breaches. To 

Although life is realized free, being a gratuity, you cannot escape paying a price for it. Conditional to the gift is the duty of making the proper use of it, and treasuring and preserving it, and this requirement is also a selfish benefit in that by the proper use of it you achieve the objectives which make life worth whilehealth and happiness. 

Do not deceive yourself with the fallacy that because you got life free you got it for nothing and do not have to pay for it, or with the other fallacy that the terms of compensation are exacting and burdensome. You are obliged to pay a price for everything, a price in money, service, sacrifice, or some other measure. When you pay the price you procure the goods. But if you do not pay the price, a penalty is demanded of you, to the consequence that the benefits which you might realize are neutralized by the punitive inflictions. Page 56

As You Suspected, There Is a Price

However, the price which you must pay for enjoyment of life is unlike the price of anything else. It is equivalent to a reward—a reward for employing the gratuity according to the natural regulations. You receive life to use properly and thereby obtain health and happiness, and in paying the price by proper use you realize the rewards or satisfactions. On the other hand, the duty of proper use is a price in the ordinary sense, because, unless you do pay it, you cannot have health and happiness and a long span of life.

The earth, the air, the water, light and all the elements of which the earth consists existed before man. 

Had we not the authority of history for this opinion, we could demonstrate it on natural grounds. The fact that man is a chemical compound of ingredients which are derived from the earth proves conclusively that the substances of which he was made were there before he was. It would not be reasonable to say that the earth originated from man; this deduction would be ludicrous.

Man is a unit in the universal cosmos. His life is a rhythm in the universal harmony. He is in tune with the rapturing paean of the perfect diapason of all things and beings, seen and unseen, far and near. He breathes, pulsates and thinks to the measures of the great melody which thrills in all objects from the interior of the earth to the core of the most distant ball or mass of gases in outer space. A material unit in the vast encompassment of nature is man, and a melodic strain in the perennial music of the spheres is his life. Page 57

Opportunity for the Mentally Capable

The feeling and understanding of the universal cosmos which man has are magnified by free intelligence, and we exclusively have the ability to realize keenly the exquisiteness of the rhapsody or the option to clash with it. Life to us offers in happiness so much as our intelligence takes by wish.

The sensory estimation of the free gift of life is shown in an entrancing manner by animals. Picture a spotted leopard at repose in a high, cool, but safe, clearing in a jungle fastness. The form of the wild beast is fascinatingly graceful, and its mottled fur, soft and velvety, and glowing with the natural sheen in the light shifted here and there on the supple body by the play of the wind in the foliage, is an elegant garment that the most beautiful woman would envy. The fine head is poised in contentment. The eyes are pleased with the vision unfolded. The nostrils inhale the pure air and the aroma of luxuriant vegetation in delight. The tail flourishes as a gesture of satisfaction with the pervading harmony. This is a veracious description of an act, which we know to be common, of natural appreciation of the favor of mere living. 

You would witness a similar act of life-appreciation in the bottom of the sea, or in the utter darkness of the depths of the earth, or in the nests up in the tree. 

You would witness something like it on the farm and in the zoo, and on the mountain and prairie. 

If the lower species by their instinct find life so agreeable in the natural synchrony, how much more should we prize it who are more capable in our higher intelligence of appraising it!

Page 58

CHAPTER IX. 

The Kitchen Cat and the Gold Fish 

Naturally, in the simple sense, the lion is our cousin, and the kitchen cat, the mouse and the elephant, the worm and the rat, the eagle and the goldfish. They are relatives of ours. There is natural bonds of more or less kindred matter between us and the tree, the head of cabbage, the rose and the weed. There is natural ties somewhat like substantial relationship between us and a lump of coal, a bed of salt or a deposit of petroleum. 

Some of these are living and some are inanimate, some are animal and some are vegetable, but each and all are, like man, of the earth.

Of course, there is some difference between man and every other animal, and there are variations in constituency among the animals themselves. The difference is in each instance probably a triviality in natural chemistry, although the difference between species and units is considerable. But it is undeniable that this extensive relationship does prevail among natural objects as a phase of the grand cosmos.

The theory of Dr. Assar Hadding, the celebrated 

Swedish geologist, that life originated in a muddy pool by chemical action, is not new, but his assumption that the first protoplasms were formed thus in muddy, saline pools in the Polar regions is a new attempt to localize the chemical action on the earth. His concept, which was announced through the Smithsonian Institution in Washington, in December, 1931, is of immediate interest to us, as it describes, almost as if by repetition, the kindred relationship between all entities and parts of universal nature as I have pictured it to you. Page 59

"Despite the myriad kinds of life on the earth, ranging from the simplest bacteria to the most complex of mammals", said Mr. G. Edward Pendray in the New 

York Herald-Tribune of Dec. 20, 1931, explaining the

Hadding concept, "there is only one life-stuff-the socalled protoplasm, an almost colorless, jelly-like substance, which either contains the living matter or is the living matter of all plants and animals. 

Hadding Concept Comes from Sweden 

"The discovery of this fact, now amply proved, shows conclusively that all living forms are related to all others. Man is related to the amoeba and to the elm tree, as well as to every man and creature on the earth, in somewhat the same sense that the palace is related to the stone hut, since both are constructed of the same primary units or bricks. In addition, his relationship is even closer, since it is possible that every creature today descended from the same first cell, and therefore partakes of the same protoplasmic origin. "The material of the cell contains living matter and also substances which cannot be considered as living. 

The latter may be minute globules of food substances about to be absorbed by the cell, or minute particles of waste products, the end result of the cell's activities, about to be thrown off. In addition, there is a good deal of water, usually bearing a definite concentration of certain salts, including common salt. "The inorganic, or non-living part, of a cell is known as the metaplasm, while the whole cell-substance is known as the cytoplasm. If the metaplasm could be subtracted from the cytoplasm, and from the substance Page 60

of the nucleus, or neucleplasm, the remaining material would be protoplasm. "If this seems complicated, it is only necessary to think of ultimate substance of life as being a kind of living jelly-a jelly capable of reacting to its environment, of growing, of absorbing food and of transforming it into energy and continuing the process of life. "Living organisms feed, breathe, grow and reproduce.

The most characteristic property of living matter is its ability to undergo continual change-a fundamental process called metabolism. This process involves a perpetual transformation of certain complex chemical substances, with the liberation of heat and energy, which are absorbed by the protoplasm. 

Man Is But a Protoplasm "From the point of view of the chemist, protoplasm consists of substances known in the order of their chemical complexity as carbohydrates, fats and proteins. 

These substances, through oxidation and other processes, are the ones which undergo changes in the cell, by virtue of which their chemical structure is changed, energy meanwhile being absorbed from them. To keep the process going, it is necessary that a new supply of carbohydrates, fats and proteins be continually fed into living material, and that the waste products produced by the processes be carried away".

All opinions as to the origin of life are scientific deductions. All that we know to a certainty concerning life is that it is present in the protoplasm as the unit in element and principle by chemical action. It is evident that the active unit is the same in all forms of animal Page 61

and vegetable life. However, to hold that all forms of life originated from the same cell or a few of the same cells is merely an assumption. I do not say that all forms of life had the same origin, or emanated from the same cell or cells; I content myself with maintaining that life is the same in all forms of living substance. 

Dr. Hadding confirms to you my contention that man is a cousin to the weed and the hog, the sun and the block of granite or pile of garbage. Some matter is living, some is dead, but living and dead substances consist of the same stuff. 

You smile a little at the somewhat strange idea that we are related to the grasshopper and June bug, the brook and the ocean, the diamond and a sheet of tin, the hummingbird and the condor, the skunk and the peacock, the lizard and the phosphor-worm, the grass and the desert sand. But we and they are much the same in a general sense. There are variations in chemical composition; however the most important differences are in the order of intelligence, running from lifelessness in some things to life without higher faculties in others, to life and instinct in others, and up to life with intelligence in man. 

Science Can't Be Wrong in This

We, with the animals, plants and inanimate matter, are regulated components of the universal order and concord. We are not only in the universe, but also of it, and of all the wonders which exist in matter and space, we, as human beings with the gift of mind, are the most striking.

Science as yet has not reached definite conclusions as Page 62

to the origin of man and of the earth, but it has elaborated by research and speculation plausible bases for further study and inquiry. It has no doubt, however, that mankind is an entity of the stupendous cosmos and that he is constructed of earth. 

CHAPTER X. 

The earth had not in the beginning the virtually round form which it now has. The shape at first was irregular and the orbit probably was oval, or, perhaps, more eccentric. The physical laws operating in space worked out an evolution which consolidated the earth and brought about regularity of form and motion. 

Presumably, the evolution took millions of years, during which great length of time the apparent chaos evolved into symmetry, and the earth at length became the orb which it is today. 

Science Is Quite Flattering 

Man, too, according to the theory of scientific investigators, went through a course of evolution from a primitive being. In him as a body composed of matter, a chemical combination, life was born, as it was born in animals of a lower class than he. Nothing is known as to the principle of life in man and other animals from the scientific viewpoint. Scientists have conducted experiments in the hope of reproducing life from bodies and matter, but the best that they have been able to do by chemical process was to cause motion; although the subjects gave off the simulation of life, the experiments could not be pronounced successful. So far, therefore, life has not been reproduced except by the natural procreation method. Page 63

Science undertakes to demonstrate that man originated as a being of a lower order, like, yet higher, than, the brutes, and he went through evolution during thousands, perchance millions of years, until the present stage of physical and cultural development was reached. 

The evolutionary theory is accepted by practically all scientists and not rejected by many leading theologians.

Many prominent churchmen have approved this theory, and the only point on which enlightened theologians insist is the Biblical assertion in the Old Testament that 

God breathed into man an immortal soul.

Man's Brain in Accordance with Surmise 

All scholars take the evolutionary theory as the most scientific and credible suggested by serious investigators. 

The progression of the apparent primary chaos gradually into universal cosmos fits well with the evolutionary theory, or, rather, this theory is suited to the universal growth of the all-comprehensive cosmos. 

It is as

The brain of man is, of course, matter. earthen as any other part of the body. Recent research indicates that the brain consists of innumerable cells and reservoirs of chemicals. In brief this is the scientific explanation of the composition of the brain. But the mind and the will and the spiritual aspirations remain beyond demonstration or even speculation by science. 

The intangibles in the human economy continue to be puzzles.

For our purposes it is enough to know to a certainty, as we do, that man is the human entity of the great universal cosmos, and his structure consists wholly of natural matter, and he is by his very substance, exist- Page 64

ence and environment attuned to the cosmos. Man, we know, is in and of nature, and is subject to the general natural laws to which all animate bodies, animal and vegetable, are subject.

The physical evolution of the earth proceeded during one thousand million to five thousand million of years, as indicated by results of investigation in the several branches of science within whose provinces the age of the earth could logically be a question determinable by natural evidences. The divisions of time and of the seasons doubtless were different during the evolutionary period from the present day. The days, for instance, may have been much longer. But in reckoning the lapse of time in the early stages science is able to decide upon equivalents in natural results; that is, with certain known physical and chemical action requiring certain known conditions and time for visible results, the lapse of time is computable from these results, and by equivalents science has estimated the age of the earth. 

Facts a Woman Can't Understand 

While astronomers, adopting the stellar theory of the formation of the earth, place the age of the earth at one thousand million to five thousand million years, geologists figure it at 2,000-million years. Scholars in the one branch make their deductions from observations in the heavens, and in the other from deposits and changes in the earth itself. Investigations have been made in their separate lines by physicists, chemists, biologists and paleontologists. The conclusions vary from 1,000-million to 5,000-million years, with a reasonable compromise at 3,000-million years. 

Zoroaster declared that this world began at 13,000

Page 65

B. C. The popular idea places the beginning at about 

4,004 B. C. However, these statements or speculations are not inconsistent with the others. They may apply to the birth of man on and of the earth as soon as habitable conditions evolved, or to the time at which man's evolution had been completed to the extent that he became "the lord of creation" and capable of exercising mastery over the earth and the beings on it. 

We must not be daunted by the incalculable immensity of 3,000-million years. To us a 1,000-million years is unintelligible, even unimaginable. 

We can scarcely even realize the time measured in 5900 years. 

But in the universe a few thousand-million years cannot mean more in progression than our age-span means at this moment to us. Time is an important element in change or transition, and some change or transition cannot be produced except in stretches of much longer time than we are used to; and it is reasonable to suppose that the physical evolutionary process required a quantity of time inconceivable by us.

Many Moderns Have Not Found It Out There is a limit to every mundane thing or condition. In the natural situation nothing is infinite. This is a positive physical law. The mind of man cannot so much as fancy the infinite, for the reason that the mere idea paralyzes thought. Space may be infinite, and probably is; but time is not. Yet we cannot think in a practical way of 3,000-million years, for we have no mental or objective formula whereby we measure so much time.

Time is deceptive with each of us, anyhow. As we peer into the future, five or ten or twenty years, that Page 66

time appears to be far away; but as we glance back, twenty or forty years ago seems to have been yesterday.

The life-span is naught as we review the time that has faded into the past, yet every year that may be added to our own time in the future is, therefore, the more precious. Through the hasty and imperceptible flight of time we have learned the value of time. 

Great redwood trees in California have stood sentinel on the mountain heights for thousands of years. Many of them were alive in their kind of life at the beginning of the Christian era. When imperial Rome conquered and ruled the world, and flourished in power and glory, these tall giants towered over a pristine territory on land. They were sturdy then, these redwoods, and they may be fresh and strong a thousand or two thousand years hence.

Relatively, it is possible for us to obtain an idea of longevity from these trees. They convey to us the purport of ten to twenty centuries. We compare historical events with the designated time, and parallel the deeds of man with the racing of time, and by putting one equivalent beside the other we are able to create a concept of ten or twenty centuries. 

Why They Are Thick-Skinned

Among the animal species turtles and elephants have life-spans comparable with those of the redwood trees. 

Turtles are known to have lived a hundred years, and elephants 400 and more. No doubt, there are mammoths deep in the sea which have lived as long. 

If instinctive animal and vegetable growth live 400 to thousands of years, is it an exaggerated desire that Page 67

the human being should prolong the span of life, first a few years beyond the present normal of fifty-eight, and subsequently to one hundred and later on to several hundred? If Methuselah and Noah each lived to about one thousand years, cannot men of this era live as long?

CHAPTER XI. 

Would you say that it would be impossible for man to live thousands of years or millions? Would you say that it would be impossible for other men than Methuselah and Noah to live a thousand years? As they lived each approximately a thousand years, others, it is plain, could live as long; they did live this long; therefore, to live a thousand years is possible to man. If a number of individuals could string life out to 400, 600 and 1,000 years, it is within the range of possibility to raise the average span to 70, as Dr. Mayo suggested, and to 

100, and subsequently a vastly longer time.

Obviously, mankind retrograded in the care of health throughout something like thirty to fifty centuries. 

When natural conditions in the world were primitive, and man was not ravaged by diseases, cares and worries of his own making, such as those to which Dr. Crile alluded, the span of life was much longer than it is now or has been for several thousands of years. Take, even, a parallel case in ancient times, that of Job. Here was a man who was afflicted with adversity and scores of maladies and tribulations, a man whose trials were somewhat in nature like those which are quite common today, more common than we care to admit. The rich man has troubles peculiar to his riches and the poor man to his incapacity, but they are alike. Job had a long life.

Page 68

Reliable Attestation

You may assure yourself of the fact that long life once was the rule by glancing through the Old Testament. Men then lived more in conformity with the ordinary laws of nature and took care of their health. 

Their existence was as simple as nature. They lacked the benefits which science has provided for us in the prevention and control of disease; consequently, their longevity must have been due largely to their own vigilance and care; they reconciled their modes to nature.

We think, as Dr. Mayo pointed out, that we can be cured and gain a few more years by going to a physician after we have run down our health. We trust him beyond a reasonable or fair confidence because he has warded off disease by preventive measures. We persist in the quest of pleasure and the chase for money, thinking that a competent physician can neutralize the consequences of our excesses. We grow old, as Dr. Crile indicated, by our own folly. The physician cannot relieve us of the penalties of waste of health. As the medical profession protected our health by preventive methods, so it is compulsory on us to adopt preventive measures of our own against our injurious modes. We do not live as long as we should because we do not try to; our health is not as good as it should be, because we neglect and ruin it. 

Zoroaster's report that the world had been started about 13,000 B. C. would mean, we might reasonably infer, that the earth was by that year habitable and more or less populated and settled, and mankind was, in a sense, organized along lines of civilization. The Page 69

earth, in that case, has been inhabited by man in a progressive human state for nearly 15,000 years. History dates back definitely more than 5900 years, and the extensive work which archaeologists are doing in most ancient lands may unearth testimony going back still farther.

Really, the Earth Was Once Less Habitable We must bear in mind that there are two divisions of age with regard to the earth, and apparently only one with regard to man. The first period of the earth's age was the formative or constructive, when, in all likelihood, habitable conditions did not exist; it was the period or course of time during which the apparent chaos was emerging into cosmos; it was the period of the primary stage of evolution. Man, we may assume, was not born then. The second period began with the establishment of natural order and law on the earth, with habitable conditions and fundamentals favorable for the higher evolutionary progression in which man participated.

The reason for presenting the age of the earth and the age of man together is to illustrate emphatically the fact that time is an element of the first importance in nature, and, as the human life is an individual unit of time, we cannot afford to underestimate the value of time. Life is existence in the measurement of tine, the time-span of the individual in vital and thinking matter.

Life and time being one, it is strange that we ignore the actual and connotive value of both. Not until we feel in growing fear that our indifference and neglect, even our detrimental recklessness, are impairing our health do we begin to think seriously of the personal Page 70

treasures which life and time are. We are as extravagant with health as we are with time, and there is scarcely a law of nature bearing directly on these endowments that we do not contumaciously break. 

Let us try to get an idea of the human value of time as a standard, in order that we may see how long life is and how short. 

Is It Possible That You're Overpaid? 

You do not actually employ more than fourteen of every twenty-four hours in useful or profitable application. Therefore, you put to use about 5110 hours in a year. The other ten hours in the day you consume in sleeping, attending to your toilet and other things of an ordinary sort.

Assuming your life-span to be the average, you would have, in the time allotted to you, about 260,610 hours for valuable use. It is estimated that of the fifty-eight years you would be capable of employing about fiftyone.

You probably have not measured life on the basis of time, notwithstanding that life and time are, as we might say, contemporaneous, so far as the individual is concerned. Life has been simply a general idea with you. So has time. You have not given serious thought to either; had you, the span of life would be, as it should, a matter which you would consider in terms of good health.

A second is the smallest unit of time. Has it ever occurred to you to reduce this mathematically to employment or action? That is, have you figured out how much you could do in a second?

Page 71

A second of time is really comparable to a wink of the eye; a wink takes approximately one second. Now, in illustrating the value of time we do not care to suppose that somebody's wink, taking a second, governed the rest of your life thereafter; nevertheless, the example would be appropriate, for it happens many thousands of times in a year that the good impression obtained by a glance is the best or the worst happening to the persons concerned. An exchange of friendly impressions is often fraught with great importance. 

Everybody is aware of this fact. 

Human Excels the Man-made Machine In actual work you could not do much in a second. 

In fact, fifteen seconds were consumed in composing and typing the eleven words in the preceding sentence, the first of this paragraph. You outstretch your hand and arm in a second. You take a long step in a second. 

You turn your head about in a second, or you change your eye-focus. The equivalent of what you could do in a second with your eye, your hands, your legs, or your body is the value of the smallest unit of time. 

You could lose your life in a second in a hazard by not doing what you could or should do, or you could save it with the suitable decision and action. Or what you would not or would do in a second might result in failure or success to you, as it has to many others. 

In result by active work the length of a second in quantity of substance or product is exemplified in modern machine operation. In up-to-date industrial operation the machine gives high value to the work contained in a second. Thus it is, as shown by the unit, that labor-saving is time-saving. Mechanics utilizes the Page 72

full measure and produces the full value of time to results equally efficient, as, for example, including the full monetary value.

The human machine portrays similar, yet more dramatic, achievements in the wink of time in athletics and sports and physical exercise. In these occupations and diversions the trained human machine functions at its best. A pugilist strikes several hard blows in a second. A football player grabs the ball and makes a decisive play. By fleetness of foot a baseball player may win a game in a second. Sports statistics are obtainable which would show you in detail what great athletes realize from a second. 

The Talents of the Precious Few

But it is in ideation and idealism that the greatest and also the most durable value of the time unit is exemplified in results. In this category are included what we call inspiration, and noble ideas, constructive thought, invention and intellectual initiative and accomplishment in general. The spiritual and material value of ideas, thoughts and decisions to mankind is beyond calculation, as the range of the dispersion of the benefits and their complete effectiveness cannot be discerned.

CHAPTER XII. 

Hon. Ramsay MacDonald, Prime Minister of Great

Britain, a statesman who rose from the bottom to the top in public affairs, gave an apt description of the human worth of the gossamer creations of the brain in an address which he delivered to the Council on For- Page 73

eign Relations in New York City in October, 1929. 

He said: "The fact of the matter is, if you take any great human cause that has triumphed for the benefit of the world, you will find that it originally comes down from the clear, blue sky of idealism, down, down, down, through experiment after experiment that has failed, until at last it touches the earth; by almost a magical transformation of its creative power, it begins to grow up and up by physical means and by successive additions, until, at last, it establishes itself as one of the great achievements of the intelligent human will".

The wink of time is, however, only a pulsation, only a breath, only a gesture and signal; it is just a beat in the grand jubilation of the cosmic symphony. Between this wee spacing in the uncounted centuries gone and the countless centuries to come and the invisible molecule which in the bulk you see as matter there is a sympathetic relativity, and time and motion roll on with life and substance to purposes which are fixed specifically by the co-ordination of all things in the universal unison.

Harmony in Mathematical Tempo 

The one perfect science, mathematics, is the means by which we divine the spirit and mettle of the allcomprehending concord. Mathematics is perfect because it is a synchrony of computation which was designed from the things, forces and conditions of nature. 

It is not mathematics which produces for us the order and connection in life, time and things, but that of the latter in their inter-relationships we constructed the Page 74

system of measurement and calculation by which we have understanding in our natural observations. 

That we, having higher intelligence, would discover the device of the metre of the cosmos was inevitable. 

The relativity among beings and things alike or kindred in components was an obvious fact which the human mind could not resist pursuing to the last phase of knowledge. In mathematics we made the key. 

So, we must note each second as the beginning and end of sections in the continuity of time. It marks off certain time that has past for you and every one else, and uncertain time starting. The second is made up, then, of culminations of ideas, deeds, works, conditions and so on which the individual has been thinking and moving toward, consciously and unconsciously, throughout his prior years, and of conditions which have developed since the very beginning of time. You might go farther, and say that comprised in the unit is some cumulation of causes having beginning prior to the beginning of time. For the earth existed before there was a division of light and darkness and seasons, and, if you contend, as you might reasonably do, that there was no time until there was light, then the effects of causes in the embryo of matter would have their effects in this present moment. The other section of time which begins in this immediate second, in which you scan these words and fret over this idea, comprises, besides the accretions of the past, the new ideas and works and causes of all sorts which extend onward into the future. Several of the very thoughts which are submitted to you in this paragraph probably belong in the latter category; at least, as far as I know, they had not been previously advanced.

Page 75

Relativity Among the Relations 

The unit of time is a mathematical fraction of all that has past and all that is to take place in the future. 

This must be so, for the mathematical concepts which we took from the cosmos prove the prevalence of relativity among all beings, things and conditions in nature. 

We might divert for a few moments to interest ourselves, from such knowledge as we have gathered, in the displacement of space by matter and the rotation of this displacement. Space existed before matter, and therefore before time, as matter existed before time. 

The formation of matter diminishes the quantity of space; for matter fills the area in space where there was nothing before, that is, nothing but space. 

Reputable scientists are very careful not to adopt a theory as a law or principle unless and until it is proved to the full satisfaction of reason. As long as a theory is only a theory it is no more than that in science, and remains susceptible to proof and acceptance or disproof and rejection. Science is not and cannot be arbitrary in dictum concerning a conjectural subject or idea. 

Science is inquisitive and progressive, and in its research, observation and study relies on confirmatory evidence which is scientifically sound.

We too often are unjust to science by our own carelessness. A deduction which is offered to us as a theory we mistake as being offered as a finding. A theory advanced today may be thrown out by a new theory found tomorrow. Theories seriously considered in the past year or two as to the origin of the cosmic rays afford illustration of the trend in science. Page 76

CHAPTER XIII. 

Philosophy for the "Bookmakers"

You caught the implication above, we believe, as we discussed the second, that what would take place after you read that thought, as well as after you read this, would not happen, but would occur. We intended to convey to you the import of the law that everything in nature is ordered and nothing occurs by chance. 

Whatever happening concerning you or another individual which you attribute to chance is not ascribable to chance in the cosmic scheme. The unexpected which occurs to you, either for good or bad, is a reflex of cause in the operation of the general order, in which the law of occurrence is fixed and unalterable. 

The law of chance, as it is called (perhaps because it would not be interesting or understood by another word), is one of the most important in physics. It is probably more generally misunderstood than the principle of the survival of the fittest, which Darwin enunciated. However, this is not amazing, for the simple reason that persons do not think seriously on subjects other than those which center on bodily sustenance, and, in this age, we might add, pleasure. You should understand the law of chance, which bears on everything in nature, in order to understand clearly the correlated harmony which is the universal cosmos.

That we may grasp the law of chance more intelligently, let us readjust our views regarding the survival of the fittest. Comparative illustrations will help us to get both principles clearer in our minds. 

We shall suppose that a great painter and a great

Page 77

sculptor go into the jungles to depict and fashion in art wild objects in the primeval environment. Both are sturdy and robust, and are able to cope with hardships to which they are unaccustomed. With the paraphernalia of their professions, they carry along supplies and arms and ammunition. They take the precaution to prepare for whatever contingency may arise. 

A few attendants accompany them. They are equipped adequately for a stay in the wilds of several months. 

Two Artists Against a Pair of Tigers 

But after they are settled in the rukh, as Kipling calls it, they are surprised by a pair of tigers mad with hunger, and are killed. Their attendants flee in panic. 

Here is an instance of the survival of the fittest. 

The tigers were not, of course, as fit in all natural assets as the painter and the sculptor. Between the wild animals and the two men there could not possibly be any plausible basis of comparison. The attendants who escaped with their lives were not as fit as their masters, who were killed. But in the exact fitness which was required to meet conditions in the prevailing circumstance, the advantage was with the tigers. 

The special experience of the attendants gave them advantages over the artists, though the latter, in allaround fitness, were superior to them. 

The principle of the survival of the fittest does not mean that the fittest in all respects survive, but that the fittest to meet the specific situation do. Another example, which Chesterton gives, is that, in a storm at sea, a man is drowned, but the whales and fishes survive. 

Among a number of men some would be fitter than Page 78

others in an epidemic. Some could resist grippe, whereas others would succumb to it; some could resist a fever, whereas others could not. In each case the fittest in the specific actual condition would survive. 

But the principle does not mean that the fittest in all points prevail over the others in any arising situation. 

The fittest to contend against particular circumstances survive in those circumstances. This is the principle according to the scientific demonstration. 

Science Differs from Shakespeare

So it is with the individual and his fortune or misfortune. He ascribes much to chance or luck, and from his own viewpoint he may be right. But in the large arrangement of the cosmos and the functioning of its divisions the causes which produce certain effects and conditions leave nothing to mere chance. Nothing happens; everything takes place by sequence of correlation in the cosmos.

An oft-quoted gem on chance in human destiny is taken from Shakespeare. It is in a speech by Brutus in the fourth act, third scene, of Julius Caesar.

There is a tide in the affairs of men, Which, taken at the flood, leads on to fortune;

Omitted, all the voyage of their life 

Is bound in shallows and in miseries. 

On such a full sea are we now afloat;

And we must take the current when it serves, Or lose our ventures.

A popular classic along the same line is the famous ode of the late United States Senator John J. Ingalls,

Page 79

of Kansas, on "Opportunity". Were it so, as he contends, that opportunity comes to each of us but once, the hope which should incite us to effort would be destroyed.

OPPORTUNITY 

Master of human destinies am I. 

Fame, love and fortune on my footsteps wait; 

Cities and fields I walk; I penetrate

Deserts and seas remote, and, passing by 

Hovel, and mart, and palace, soon or late 

I knock unbidden, once, at every gate! 

If sleeping, wake-if feasting, rise before 

I turn away. It is the hour of fate, And they who follow me reach every state

Mortals desire, and conquer every foe 

Save death; but those who doubt or hesitate, Condemned to failure, penury and woe, Seek me in vain and uselessly implore- 

I answer not, and I return no more.

The true demonstration of the survival of the fittest and the law of chance is set forth in the tense novel "The Typhoon" by Joseph Conrad. The noted writer shows us a determined captain of a ship opposing the elements with his will. The story is a good illustration of the play of the external relations of man and the consequences of antagonism to the arbitrary in nature. 

CHAPTER XIV. 

Fortune Is as Inexplicable as Fickle 

You have had many occasions to believe that luck was with you or against you; sometimes you "got the

Page 80

breaks" and other times the breaks were contrary to you. You think of persons who have luck with them all through their lives and of others who have naught but misfortune. You say that with some it is luck; with others, chance, and with others, their own incompetency or foolhardiness.

In the first place, what do you mean by luck or fortune? Do you mean wealth? Or pleasure? Or fame?

Or success (that is, with reward in money or fame, or both)? And by ill luck or misfortune do you mean poverty? Or adversity? Or lack of pleasure? Or disrepute? Or failure? Or disappointment? 

How do you know (or don't you) that opulence, or pleasure, or renown or success is a boon which you may classify to a certainty as beneficial to the possessor? On the other hand, are you sure that the lack of any or all of these is the reverse? Before we decide what is luck we must find a law by which we may be positive, in individual application. It is not unlikely that there is as much torment in luck as there is anguish in misfortune. Could we pry into family and personal secrets we should doubtless be shocked at the dissimulation which we should unmask and reveal. Every life can only be judged as a whole, and until after it is terminated a reckoning cannot be made as to the imperative averages. 

Chance? Fortune? Have you ever thought what you would do if you lost health and money, and had to exist as a social parasite? Or what you would do if you became a millionaire? You could become either a beggar or a millionaire in a minute. 

The average man is crushed in adversity. In pros- Page 81

perity he is puffed up with self-esteem. Misfortune is due to ill luck. Success is the result of personal capacity and enterprise. 

Mr. Rosenwald Measuregraphed Them 

The late Julius Rosenwald, the Chicago capitalist, who gave most of his wealth to charity, had philosophies, which, on account of his shining virtue and achievement, command the highest respect. The few following aphorisms selected from his philosophy of life are of interest in this connection: "The fellow who gets to the top usually gets too much credit. Often he gets credit for ideas that come from brains of his fellow workers. "Rich men are not smart because they are rich. "Don't ever confuse wealth with brains. "Some very rich men who have made their own fortunes have been among the stupidest men I have ever met in my life. "No one was more surprised by my landing in the midst of American multimillionaires than I was myself. 

Having made money, my problem now is how to use it so as to give and to get happiness out of it. "I believe that success is 95 per cent luck and 5 per cent ability. "I was in luck, not a genius. "The generation that has contributed to the making of a millionaire should be the one to profit by his generosity.' 

Whether in what we call fortune or ill luck one real- Page 82

izes health and happiness is the final test.

A person may be poor and have happiness and health, or rich and not have them. Jean Henri Fabre, studying and writing on bugs and insects, was happier than if he had money and fame, and Shakespeare, though he had wealth and fame, was unhappy in his latter years on account of shattered health. 

The Satisfaction Almost Extinct

The hedonic satisfaction can be the sole acquisition definable as lucky or fortunate. This would not be the satisfaction derived from pleasure, for there is no satisfaction in pleasure, but the satisfaction which is happiness in good, sound health. This satisfaction is not the result of luck, but emanates from conformance to the cosmic laws.

As diversion, if not for study, we may take a few examples from individual experience of luck.

The most romantic instances are true stories of love. 

A rich youth sights the melting eyes of a beautiful maid, who is poor, and marries her. A rich damsel falls in love with a prepossessing youth, and they elope, and are wed. You might get the evidence that you wish in cases such as these in the divorce court dockets. 

John Cecil Clay, the noted painter, had years of illness, adversity and trouble. At length he was compelled to sell some property which he had inherited, and before arranging for the sale looked through the buildings on the land. An heirloom was discovered which he sold for a large amount, an amount sufficient to guarantee him a comfortable livelihood during the rest of his life.

Page 83

After the American Civil War two men ran a hardware store in partnership in the downtown section of a large city. The business did not prosper and they could not get along; so they agreed to terminate the partnership. The assets consisted of $60 in the till and a scrap of paper in the safe. Each desired the $60 in cash, about which there could be no question as to value. The scrap of paper was a certificate of title to worthless property in Southwest Missouri. The lucky partner got the $60, while the scrap of paper which the unfortunate partner reluctantly accepted turned out in a few years to be worth several million dollars, as the land contained one of the richest lead deposits in the world. 

The "Ponies" as a Chance Element

In the news of this day is a brief item of "a small liquor dealer” in Chicago, who was shot in his neck in his own establishment while telephoning. The shade at the window was down and his assailant outside saw his shadow through it. As the gunman aimed, the victim, who had been standing, sat in a chair; had the dealer remained standing he would have been hit in the stomach and killed.

A stranger dropped off in a big city with the idea of renting a store, and he visited a well-known bank. 

A young clerk accosted him. The clerk told of a store which had just been closed by a merchant who had lost his money "on the ponies. "on the ponies." The visitor leased it and made money of it, and afterward employed the young man to select and lease places for a big chain system. 

You would learn of even more dramatic instances of Page 84

luck among your neighbors, friends and acquaintances than these, and also of strange cases of adversity. We are well aware that inexplicable happenings confer fortune on some persons and misfortune on others. Does chance or luck play any part in them? There must be a law or principle which brings them about, but what it is no one so far knows; it is, apparently, one of those hidden laws, a mystery in the human kind, for the proof of which we are unable to procure evidence. 

The most interesting case is that of Herod Agrippa, the last Jewish king of the Hasmonean dynasty. The pretentious monarchy which Herod the Great had formed crumbled after his death. Herod Agrippa, a grandson, was educated in Rome, where he became familiar with the youth of the royal and patrician families, dissipating with them. Two of the friends which he made afterward ascended the imperial throne, and one made him king, reviving and restoring the dynasty, and the other extended his dominion. We lack the space to tell the whole tale here. You may read it in 

Josephus' History of the Jews. And you would enjoy it. Lady Fortune bestowed favors on him frequently and often enabled him to capitalize his debts when he was discredited. She transformed his misfortune into fortune. 

Nothing Fortuitous in Nature

But it cannot be that these occurrences in human experience are fortuitous. To those concerned and to baffled observers they do look so. No explanation of them can be found, and, unable to solve the puzzle, we pass them off with the belief that they are incidents or accidents in the chance which Darwin thought that Page 85

he discovered in connection with change and evolution in inanimate substance.

We are rather of the opinion that the explanation of them lies in the law of compensation. This principle, like every other, operates in the present in correlation with the past and through many individuals, things, conditions and circumstances. The individual's externals have relationship, as he has, with causes which were begun long ago. It is conceivable that the law of compensation could work out through many human generations. However, whether it is the law of compensation, or another, we may take it for granted that there is no chance in these personal and family events. 

There is continuity in every circumstance of nature as there is continuity in time, and continuity itself should logically imply to us that mere chance would be impossible. One law co-ordinates with another, even opposite forces co-operate (and because they are opposite), and a universal harmony is perceptible in all forms of nature. The anomalies which we find with amazement, appearing to be indications of disorder and confusion, are, we finally decide, methodical developments in the natural homogeneity. 

Darwin took the accidents in living nature, which are comparable specifically with those happening in human experience, being similar in their occurrence, and deduced a theory that they were constructively and progressively active in the process of evolution.

He speculated scientifically that chance was one of the principal factors in the exemplification of the thesis of evolution.

Page 86

CHAPTER XV. 

The "Adaptive" Principle in Nature

The wind blows a seed across the English Channel. 

The plover carries a plant across the sea. A herd of animals roams from Europe to Asia, or Asia to Europe.

A cataclysm alters local natural conditions, and living nature in the place is compelled to adjust itself to the change. These and other accidents contributed to evolutionary progression. 

Dr. Henry Fairfield Osborn, president of the American Museum of Natural History, an international authority in paleontology, controverted Darwin's theory of chance in an address which he delivered at a meeting of the National Academy of Science in New 

Haven, Conn., on November 16, 1931, producing new evidence from research and experimentation that nature does not do anything "by fortuity, by accident, or by chance". "It has taken sixty-two years of observation”, Dr.

Osborn said, "to demonstrate that organic, like inorganic, nature does nothing by accident or by chance, but only by means of universally prevailing adaptive principles which are only revealed in long, secular periods of time. The very words adaptive action, reaction and interaction imply a chasm between biomechanism and inorganic mechanism across which there is no bridge at present. "Darwin worked altogether on the old variational concept. He lived too soon to see the fruits of the paleontology which he created. Not a single phylum or actual line of descent, not an ascending or descend- Page 87

ing mutation, not a single specific origin, not a single character origin, was known to Darwin.”

Tenpins With Scientific Speculations 

The new principle which eliminates chance in living nature, as in inanimate substance, was presented by Dr. Osborn in connection with a new hypothesis relative to evolution, a hypothesis, based on recent progress in several branches of science, which disposes of most of the previous theories, if not all, in the opinion of some modern scientists. Yet Dr. Osborn made it plain that he was a complete agnostic as to the causes of evolution, and not only should we expect difference of opinion among the learned, but also new discoveries. 

We must always look at science as being on the way forward, advancing a step or a few inches at a time. "We are compelled to return to a creative concept of evolution", Dr. Osborn explained. "Mark that the word creative is used without any of its old theological or philosophical implications; it is clearly distinct from the word 'created'. For this concept the term 'aristogenesis' is provisionally suggested until the classicists can furnish us with a single term signifying the creative origin of the adaptive."

Six important points in which the new concept differs from the old, accepted hypotheses were cited by Dr. Osborn. The new view tends to concentrate our thoughts on uniformity and regularity in the universal order of the cosmos, and, while it attaches due importance to externals and their influence on living nature, it stresses the importance of living nature itself, and, therefore, of the internal conditions of all kinds. Page 88

New Concept With "Teeth in It" "The new concept", Dr. Osborn stated, "is based on thousands, if not hundreds of thousands, of observations on fossils gathered from all museums of the world, and all the countries of the world, except Australia. 

These for the first time disclose the complete life history of a great hoofed family, the titanotheres, during 12,000,000 years, and of a great hoofed order, the proboscideans, during 30,000,000 years". The titanotheres was a family of animals which developed horned snouts, and the great hoofed order, the proboscideans, is the elephant. "Recently", continued Dr. Osborn, "the grinding teeth of the proboscideans enabled us for the first time to establish the rate of evolution in an extremely important adaptive organ, very slow even in the course of 10,000 years. This incredibly slow rate exposes the futility of modern experimental research, which would produce a new character in a single year or a few seconds. "The anatomical contrasts between these two groups of animals, namely, the arrest of tooth evolution in one and the marvelous progress of tooth evolution in the other, serve to establish new principles of biochemical evolution, which were not only unknown to Darwin, but are directly antagonistic to one of his fundamental theses, that nature does anything by fortuity, by accident, or by chance".

The creative concept of evolution, to which Dr. 

Osborn said we must return, is not appalling to us in antagonism to any of our beliefs. "Moreover", he says, "we are compelled to return to a creative concept of Page 89

evolution. Mark that the word creative is used without any reference to its old theological or philosophical implications". It is his theory that the creative process, which he names aristogenesis, is wound in the process of adaptation. Wherever, in circumstances which previously were described as accidental, living nature has the will of adaptation, it finds the way. The contrast of the arrest of tooth development in one group of animals and the progress of tooth evolution in the other are examples of adaptability, the one in the halting of evolution of one sort in one group and the other in the progress of evolution in the other group. Dr. Osborn maintains that a creative force is still at work. 

Looking Backward 30,000,000 Years

Radical points as to the past reach of time and the equivalency of time in evolutionary progress were stated by Dr. Osborn in the same address. He placed the existence of animal life on the earth as far back as 

12,000,000 years for the titanotheres and 30,000,000 years for the proboscideans, and from the grinding teeth of the proboscideans he fixed the rate of evolution in "an extremely important adaptive organ, very slow even in the course of 10,000 years".

Both relativity between time and the progress of evolution and between time and living nature and inanimate substance were brought out by Dr. Osborn. The relativity is continuous and general. The equivalents are mathematically computable, as easily computable as the equivalents of time and distance in a running race, or of age and growth in persons and lower-order animals, or of seasonal transition and fruition and rest. Page 90

Clearly, there could not be such a circumstance as that of chance in nature without disturbing the coordination of action, motion and change in the cosmos. 

If chance occurred at all in any circumstances, it could and probably would occur in connection with the greater fundamentals, and so, were there a law of chance, it would threaten the law of regularity or order. 

We must accommodate (or, if you will, adapt) our mental attitude to a complacency of vision and idealization. Looking into the wonderful, we should expect one wonder to be more wonderful than another. Nature is a complexity and diversity of wonders. We should think, for instance, that 30,000,000 years or 3,000-million years are a lapse of time which is not nearly so wonderful as the heart beat of a mouse, or the volitional illumination of the network-like frame of the phosphorous worm, or the instinct of the homing pigeon. 

Marvelousness of the Simple

We expect nothing else but the wonderful in nature. 

On the other hand, we should consider that the wonderful is simple. We do not suggest a complacency which would take everything for granted, and not be inquisitive, or a conviction that the simplicity in nature should be treated as ordinary. We mean just the reverse. We mean that we should be complacent in the expectation of finding new wonders and understanding old wonders and that the simplicity in nature is one of the greatest wonders, as well as one of the greatest beauties.

Stages of time were necessary and consistent with stages of evolution. With a very slow evolution in Page 91

10,years of an important adaptive organ, as mentioned by Dr. Osborn, 3,000-million years would not be long in inanimate evolution, nor 30,000,000 years in living nature. The processes are progressive and inter-active, and time is one with the elements and with life.

We may suppose in reason that the establishment of initial order in the apparent universal chaos took a vast quantity of time or the equivalent of time. The first stage may have taken the equivalent of many millions of years; it probably did. We may suppose that the initial evolution in living nature consumed much time in the first stage. We may suppose that in many ways evolution proceeded faster and in many ways slower in the formative stage than in any subsequent stage. 

To universal nature either 30,000,000 years or 3,000million years is a trifle, and in exactly the same sense that an ant is a trifle. There may have been a billion years of darkness before there was light. Inanimative substance went through many millions of years before there came vegetable and animal life. And it is quite likely that the advancing theories of science will conform to other beliefs that you may have. 

Most Beautiful and Wonderful in Nature

The most wonderful thing or state in nature is within yourself. It is an intricate thing and a strange thing; the anomaly of a labyrinth of cells and a mushy mass; an ugly thing to look at, and a slimy thing to the touch.

Nevertheless, it is the most wonderful and most beautiful among all the wonders and all the beauties in the whole universe; in fact, it contains within its small Page 92

compass all the other sensible wonders and beauties, with the extraordinary, the near-divine, wonder that it is in itself. This is the brain, the skull-encased seat of the mind.

The argosy of knowledge and culture and of the vaulting aspirations which ramify to and from the mysteries of the infinite, it is a small hunk of plastic substance which seems to be isolated, and locked up in a rounded, oblong box of hard bone. Nothing in us bears so close a resemblance to common clay. And this is what it is common clay. The gray matter, the mazelike convolution of cells and the chemical liquids are a collection of inanimate substances; yet in their combination and co-ordination as the brain, they are quick with the principle of life, without which they would be a loathsome chunk of substance similar to a sponge soaked with lemon juice.

This is the brain! The mind of man resides here! 

Herein lives the highest faculty in all space and matter, in all forms of life and inanimate substance! The eye sees here; the ear hears; all the senses and all the functioning parts of the body have their master-contacts here, and here is the only intelligence which is capable of understanding the purpose, causes and effects in the grand connotation of the cosmic system. 

Whole Cosmos and All Time in One Idea 

The ages are hidden in this turret of the human body. Perchance, in one of these cells, as a material impression, is an idea that has come down through thousands or millions of years, perchance the only intact and unchanged vestige of aeons devoured by time. 

You may conceive such a heritage to be reasonable- Page 93

a thought stored in memory in a prehistoric era and transmitted latently through many parents, to be a living force whenever, now or centuries hence, it can perform the service for which it is naturally adapted. 

A sudden thought that bobs up in your mind may be a recollection of one which originated at the dawn of civilization in a quaint place and a more or less primitive person. Science could not tell you, dictatorially, that this could not be; there would be no method or means by which science could determine this to be impossible. The generic principles of heredity are fairly well known, but the specific may never be; surely, the details of the specific principles cannot be studied; and it is possible that you often revive ideas, or ideas are somehow revived in you, which have long been at rest in seriatim transmission. "Spencer", said Dr. Osborn in his New Haven address, "believed that 'mind is built up through experience'. But observed facts proved otherwise. We have found that much larger intelligences exist among primitive people than there is any actual need for, intelligence capable of grasping mathematical concepts among Eskimos who had no need even to count on their fingers".

From this we may deduce, and no one can gainsay our conclusion, that the dimensions of the mind have had for an indeterminate time the capacity of evolution equal to progressive change and the uses and needs of varying environments and conditions. 

Your brain is you. It is the organ that gives you your superiority in nature. It is your identity and also the control of your identity. You would not know

Page 94

yourself without it; you would be an unreasoning animal, one incapable of reasoning except on the instinctive level. (There are, as you know, some humans who are unreasoning and might as well be incapable of reasoning, and some who are irrational, and some who are unreasonable. But we are not making such distinctions; we are taking it for granted that whoever reads this philosophy and whoever hears it discussed is a thinker.) Your brain distinguishes you from the lower orders of living substance by its higher intelligence. As to you individually, with your varied idiosyncracies, it is you.

Joy and Grief Marks of Superiority The higher intelligence in the human mind is the sole state in the universe having understanding and having understanding associated with will. It is by his intelligence that man stands out as "the lord of the universe". The physical abode of this power, for such it is, is this slimy mass that looks not unlike a small "blimp”. 

Scientists say that we could not feel pain or suffer from grief and worry had we not this higher intelligence. Study of animals, including household pets, has proved that their sense of pain is different from the human. Dogs, for instance, act as if they have no idea as to death; one that is dying may show a realization that something unusual is taking place; one that is threatened with a deadly weapon may express fear and surprise in his face; but to a dead dog other dogs behave the same as if he were sleeping.

At the same time this higher intelligence enables us to appreciate the finer themes in the whole scope of vision and perception and impels us to search and strive Page 95

for knowledge and to cultivate our appreciation and knowledge toward a finished quality of mind which we prize as culture. This intelligence enables us to stand above our circumstances in the poise of authority and to be masters of ourselves in every situation. 

Having this higher intelligence, with understanding and will, man dominates nature. He sees and hears with understanding. He looks from cause to effect, from effect to cause. Curiosity or the keen desire for more knowledge goads him to inquire to the root or source of everything, and his findings jog him on to adapt his knowledge and the things in nature to his own use and profit. 

Pearls in a Hunk of Clay

This clammy ball of chemicalized yellow clay, the brain, is the center of human advancement. Civilization has been produced by it, and without it man would have continued to live in the trees and in caves, and to use flint and tree-branches as weapons and tools. His only ambition would be to pick up enough food to sustain his body. His home would be wherever the day's roaming left him in the blackening evening and he could find a refuge from animals more brutish than he; and it is doubtful whether any would be more cunning or cruel, for he would be an irresponsible fiend in the hard struggle for existence. His only music would be the elementary response within himself to the warbling of the birds, and the purling and splashing of the waters, and the hum of the happy wind. His objectives would be the same as those of the crawling, walking, climbing and flying species of living substance surrounding him.

Page 96

Now, the fundamental fact about the human mind is that it is the exclusive state in nature of an intelligence major to everything else and potential to overlord the living beings and things and the inanimate matter on the earth and even to reach out with it with similar power to the objects and forces beyond the firmament. It is so preternatural, as it were, as to be in contact with the infinite.

You, with this higher intelligence, are in a different position in regard to nature than any other animal. 

You have the faculty of appreciation because you have that of insight and understanding. Your sense of pain and suffering is greater, but so also is your sense of understanding and enjoyment. You see and feel with your higher intelligence; you see and feel with the mind. You think; you decide. You reason, and act from reason. As an entity in nature, the human entity, you rank superior in it because your mind gives you special power.

CHAPTER XVI. 

And Man Made the World

Man has made a world of his own upon the earth, and entirely from his higher intelligence. The works of man are the artifice of his mind. They are dreams come true, and had there been no dreams the works could never have been produced. The conquest of nature was not accomplished by the arm, but by vision. 

Nature was not transformed, but put to use, and not only put to use, but to new and steadily increasing and diversifying use. Each step in progress foreordained another, and at length, when man was fully acquainted Page 97

with nature and had an estimate of his great capacity, progress was faster.

Civilization has gone through an evolution through the ingenuity of man. We could imagine this from the invention which marks our own era. But archaeologists have dug up evidence in the past few years in Asia and Africa, as well as in Europe, which enables us to see exactly how people lived and dressed and worked five to six thousand years ago. Whole cities have been unearthed, one beneath another, revealing strata of ancient civilizations. Houses have been rediscovered, intact, and palaces, temples, stores and public buildings, and furniture and household ornaments, and clothing and jewels. Remnants of human bodies have been found in the posture in which they were when death arrived. Tools, vehicles and art treasures have been obtained. We actually visit cities which flourished centuries ago and visualize how men, women and children lived and dressed, and how cities were governed and how business was conducted. From the works, customs, hieroglyphics, stone tables and parchments we see what they thought and how they thought. 

Wisdom Not Improved With Age

We have similar evidences of different stages of civilization in its ascent to the present level. The evolution of civilization is traceable by the tangible proofs. 

We compare the writing in wet earth with a sharp instrument in the primitive stages with the typing and printing machines of this era, and the ancient abacus with the computation machines of today, and the wax taper with the electric bulb, and carrying the pitcher Page 98

from the well with the faucets which release cold or hot water at finger-touch.

The span of life has not increased in four or five thousand years, and perhaps the profound products of the mind have not improved. The wise men of the ancient epochs were thinkers. Of this, too, we have evidence. There were artists then whose genius and skill have not been excelled since. There were great rulers and great philosophers. And we should remember that they brought about their ends without the experience and implements which we have. Men lived longer when civilization was simple than they do now, and we are sure that they could not be less happy than we are.

That the human mind had the capacity many thousands of years in the past to imagine, think out and construct civilization to the prevailing standard, as is portrayed to us by the philosophy and art of the ancients, coincides with the theory stated by Dr. Osborn in refuting the Spenser hypothesis. The evolution has been within the original capacity of the human intellect, but is not yet up to it. 

Mechanical has gone ahead of other improvements in the march of civilization, and to a considerable extent we are misusing many of our benefits to our own detriment, especially in health and happiness. While we have learned how to work more effectually, we have retrograded in the humanitarian relationship to actual employment, and while we are living more comfortably and more luxuriously than men formerly did, we are not living either as healthily or as happily.

The triumphs in science, in invention, in business and Page 99

other pursuits are results of our domination over nature, and they represent the subjugation by the human intellect of the complexities of nature in and off the earth. They exemplify the large role that man enacts in the universal scheme, and thereby they show to what great extent he depends on nature as a whole for both existence and advancement. 

Man Has Always Helped Himself

Evolution in the universal cosmos has not ceased, but goes on in compliance with the controlling principles of uniformity and regularity. It is scarcely noticeable except by scholars and investigators who are watching and studying it; we are so busy with more immediate problems that we might be unobservant or ignorant of its progression; but scientists now and then offer us facts and deductions, and we take their theses for it that development in nature is continuous. 

During centuries man had been oblivious to the stupendous and the trivial, orderly transitions, both extreme, in his immediate and remote surroundings, and his attention missed the universal trend. Meanwhile he took what he wanted from these surroundings and proceeded onward. It is possible that change in nature has not been so radical in the latter ages as it was in the earlier, particularly the first several, as these embraced the long formative period, and in a sense were more basic. Man, anyhow, overlooked important significances in the colossal harmony, while he went through a civilizing evolution himself.

Among all the inventions, facilities and improvements which we use, there is none that is outside of Page 100

nature. On the contrary, all are adaptations from nature. For the moment we may be surprised at this. 

They, being our works, products of our genius, we have casually thought of solely as completed ideas; we have connected the results with the ideas, but have failed to connect the ideas with nature. 

At the radio receiving set we picture the transmission station. We may conjure up a mental figure of the power system at the station. But we do not take into consideration the principles of nature employed in the service from beginning to end. We readily concede that the materials used in the talking and motion pictures are natural products, yet we are predisposed to insist that the processes are inventive, without considering that every principle in them is derived from nature, to which it conforms. We get into the habit of automatically thinking that we are not only superior to nature, but also apart from it. We take credit for our dreams, our ingenuity, our skill and our works, but the important truth escapes us that our progress is with natural things and founded on natural principles, even in the functioning of the brain. 

The Analogy of a Paradox The paramount conviction in reason is that the human intellect is the most wonderful organism in universal nature. All inventions emanate from it. It is the source of the lofty buildings and the long bridges, the machines of industry, the money and articles of commerce, the apparatuses and instruments of science, and the tools, implements and utensils of art. It learns the principles of nature and with ingenuity applies them to everyday service.

Page 101

Higher intelligence is intangible, but it abides in the tangible brain, which is the intelligence and control of nature's human quantity. As it is the only condition of the kind-higher intelligence-in nature, it is the sole state of higher will; therefore, it is the only intelligence in nature with higher volition. Reason and will is man, the intangibles in the pulp which is called the brain.

We note in this what appears to be a natural paradox. The human species appears to contain a contradiction, and consequently be a contradiction to the pervading natural laws in possessing option through the will in every decision. Limited volition is exercised by animals of the lower order, but in vegetable life what is said to be the will is the plain adaptation described by Dr. Osborn and is like conformance to the exigencies of environment.

Reason and will in man are different and exceptional, yet in principle they evidently correspond to the restricted functioning in lower-order animals. Clearly, they are in accordance with natural principle, for, though the condition is bounded in the lower-order animals, nevertheless it exists. But reflection suggests to us that the natural scheme would necessarily entail the presence of an active fabric having understanding and appreciation with correlative supreme volition.

Your mind itself thus reasons along to the sound conclusion that the seeming paradox is a logical analogy in nature.

We recognize that the synthesis between us and the rest of nature is superlatively intimate. On our part it is the affiliation exalted by the dominance of under- Page 102
