standing. We are bound so closely with the universe as to be competent to reason to interpretation of its variegated components. We have the faculty of divination and the power to use what we ascertain by it from nature.

A Grand Vision Compressed in an Idea 

The intimacy of man with nature and the advance of civilization you may picture to yourself swiftly and completely in your imagination. Is it not amazing how in one idea you can compress centuries and millions of humans and the changes and complexities of different grades of civilization? You see it all in one feat of mental imagery. Or would the illustration be clearer if we turned it about, and said that all time and all change in the world are conjurable in one picture in the imagination? This shows you better the magnitude of the bundle of cells, which, with the gray matter, constitute the active principle of the brain. 

With one vision your mind brings up a vast review of civilization from its beginning to the present moment. At your wish each stage passes before you, or at your wish all stages may appear in a composite panorama. In one idea you see the advancement of civilization from the uncouth savage to the modern baron of commerce, or there is a procession from the wooden ox-plow and chariot to the motor-driven plow and pleasure automobile; you see the ancient astronomer on the housetop studying the stars with his naked eyes and the modern observer peering into remote space through the huge telescope on Mount McKinley. 

The talking motion picture is a marvelous application of but two initial ideas-representation of human Page 103

life and repetition of the human voice. The action and dialogue may be preserved as long as the materials of which they are made resist decay. You get unique and almost weird effects at beholding them. For example, while we were in a theater a few days ago, football pictures which were projected on the screen were explained by Knute Rockne, the famous coach, who had been killed in an airplane accident a year previously. 

It seemed that he still lived; for there he was, addressing the audience. 

Perceptiveness of the Intelligence

These wonders are products of the brain. We must remember this. They were evolved by the mind from two ideas. The ideas were developed and converted into machines, and the machines imitate the mind and the voice. Although they are now commonplaces, they show us visibly and audibly what the human mind can do, and you feel that there is practically no limit to the achievements of the inventive capability. It is a long advance, you perceive, from the making of a blunt tool of flint to the manufacture of machines and materials which cause pictures to act and speak, although we must remember also that the fashioning of the hammer or knife of stone was the same spark as that which resulted in the designing of the radio, the electric bulb, the locomotive and aircraft. 

Have you ever considered that the human mind speaks and hears, as well as sees, that it employs all the senses? A voice that you once heard you hear again and again; a face that captivated you, you see again and again. You feel sensations over and over againsensations of grief or happiness. A sweet scent in the

Page 104

garden pleases you over and over again. You see again, as but yesterday, an important event in your life; it is re-enacted in your mind in almost every detail. What are the picture on the screen and the voice in the cabinet in comparison to the multifarious performances of the mind in its many phases?

You properly regard the human mind as the most wonderful entity in nature. Its properties are almost divine. It dreams, and thinks, and produces. It comprises all that is past and queries the future, and a scintillation of fancy it transmutes into a science or art, an intricate machine or a gigantic structure. It studies the elements and principles of nature and inquires into the mysteries in space, and investigates the theories of life and even attempts to analyze itself. There is a genius in it whose enterprise in thinking and dreaming discovers the composition and laws of the beings, things, conditions and principles in nature and turns them to use.

Self-Interest Man's Ruling Motive 

In constant contact with the units and aspects of nature, the mind is alert to the opportunities suggested by intelligence for adaptations for benefit in service and pleasure, or, in the first and ordinary way, for opportunities for the increase of knowledge. The mind is able to find profit in anything, and does. From within and without the mind is always busy with ideas, and it is the directing force among beings and things, utilizing them to mankind's advantage.

Page 105

CHAPTER XVII. 

As you will later realize, the artificial complement of our civilization is not conducive to health and happiness. Dr. Crile, whom we quoted in another chapter, alluded to this in an address which he delivered in 

Rochester, N. Y., June 4, 1930, under the auspices of the Committee on Periodic Health Examinations of the Medical Society of the State of New York, the Medical Society of the County of Monroe and the 

Rochester Chamber of Commerce. "It is in the field of nervous and mental diseases", Dr. Crile said, "that we shall be in 'red ink'. The complicated mechanism of the web of life in which civilized man is enmeshed will lay a heavy toll on the brain and nervous system and on the ductless glands and in consequence on the blood vessels and kidneys. Therefore, as time goes on there will be an increasing number of wrecks. The reproduction of the unfit will be reduced, and as a consequence there will emerge the beginnings of a superior race. Within certain limits, therefore, the physician of the future will be able to control the growth of the body and the development of the mind. 

He will acquire such a knowledge of biochemical and biophysical processes that he will be able to guide his fellowmen as an engineer of living processes".

Let Us Not Blame Civilization 

The strain of the circumstances of modern living has blighting effects on the brain and nervous system, and consequently on life, and Dr. Crile submits the warning that the strain will wear out the individuals and families which cannot bear it. But I shall try to show you in Page 106

the following pages that it is possible, with the advice of a trustworthy and competent physician, and your own individual interest and help, to minimize this strain, if not to eliminate it altogether. 

Incidentally, I may remind you that the strain on the mind and nervous system is not due mainly to the facilities and circumstances of advanced civilization, but rather to the misuse and disuse of these facilities, excesses and negligence in the mode of living, and neglect of health. While the mind has been concentrating on material progress, it has ignored the strain and the necessity of adjusting the mode of living under new conditions so as to preserve health. I drew this to your attention in the first pages of this volume and soon will revert to it with constructive suggestions. 

You may adopt these suggestions or wave them aside, as you will, but if you desire to obviate the killing strain you will at least consider them carefully.

The old adage, "a sound mind in a sound body", used to be often quoted. Nowadays, the health of the mind is less thought of than the health of the body, and the health of the body is not much more as a matter of human interest than a war-cry for athletics; we are more concerned about promoting a certain branch of sports than in building up health, but health is the lure or excuse, and we talk of better health while we are simply pastiming in a game. 

Man's a Man for All That

The point is that the mind, which you agree is the most wonderful state in nature, is part of the body. 

The brain is the control of the body, but the brain Page 107

derives its nourishment from the organs in the body proper. A wise head and a warm heart, we say; and it is literally true that each produces certain good and pernicious effects in the other. The wonder of the universe depends for health and vigor totally on the animal part of man.

Dr. Crile, as I have just indicated by a quotation, observed that the strain of civilization is impairing the mind and disturbing the nervous system. He predicted that the mental and nervous disorders would eventually exterminate genealogical lines which would be unable to hold up in the tension. The other side is that the physical strain normally tends, when its consequences are unchecked, to debilitate the body and destroy health, resulting in nervous breakdown and mental disorder. The injury works both ways owing to the interdependence of brain and body.

The body can get along in a more or less healthy condition under nervous and mental strain, but only for a limited time. The destructive consequences are postponed, but not prevented.

The mind, through the brain, which is its material casement, is an integral part of nature, just as much and in precisely the same way as the body proper, and it is amenable to nature's laws for health and efficiency, notwithstanding that it is the dominant wonder in the universal economy. The human characteristic which interprets and commands the things and forces of the earth does so because man's real relationship with them gives it sympathetic understanding. Page 108

CHAPTER XVIII. 

They Beat Us to It

The earth was here before our forefathers were, but while they lived they never were anywhere else. We have been nowhere else. The world was their habitation, and it is ours.

From the moment when man stood first on the solid earth, and saw the blazing sun overhead and the flowing waters at his feet, his race has gone through the successions of change. The earth undoubtedly was old when life entered him; the formative evolutionary process antedated his existence. The grass was green; the fruits were ripe; the seasons were in their regular course. Order reigned in the universe. He had only to reach or stoop for food, and there was plenty for his sustenance, no matter how many years he might live.

He must have felt at home in this wonderland. It could not have been strange to him. Was he not made of the same stuff as the birds, the sheep, the fishes, the stone, the trees and the plants? One of his first thoughts must have been that the earth was his, and another as to how he could control and use it. Then started the process of his civilization.

When Man Was Unsophisticated 

He was primitive and the earth primeval. His brain no doubt had the ultimate capacity of yours. The earth had potentialities at that time which are utilized in this. But he had limitations of knowledge, as so far he had of experience. He thought doubtless of uses for objects that he beheld, but it was impossible, with his Page 109

small knowledge and experience, for him to outline the progress of civilization. He could not plan ahead for sixty or two hundred centuries.

But he was of the earth and himself earthen, and progress or existence would be a matter between the earth and himself. All that he saw was at his command. The moon and the stars were no more strange to him than the sun or the soil and water. With them he was part of universal nature, a living integrity with a higher intelligence.

He was in the cosmos, and the cosmos in him. There was peace. There was harmony. The melody of the stars and the rhythm of the orbs were in the same happy measure as the tune in his heart and the lilt in his mind. 

He sensed this, you are sure, with rapture. You sense it today in this civilization which withers man. 

We can visualize the primeval earth and the primitive man as if we were at his side. It is possible for us to feel the tranquillity and peace which he felt in the fresh and beautiful world. We can picture the world divested of civilization. Like him we are in the cosmos, and the cosmos is in us. 

Nevertheless, Some Can't Imagine It

It is delightful to contemplate primitive man in the primeval earth. A quiescent ecstacy suffused the universe, and if peace and contentment ever soothed mankind with feelings of joy and security, they did in that ideal state before civilization wrought cares, responsibilities and abnormal tastes and desires into the works and culture of human intelligence. 

You have been exalted in spirit frequently by that Page 110

same transport. You have been as close to nature sometimes as the first man and have sensed the composing sweetness of the grand unison. You have had this sublime, yet natural, emotion in rural isolation, and now and then in the din of the city. As if you were floating in the air, you have felt this kinship with this environment, extending farther even than your sight, extending into the haze of distance unimaginable. 

You are standing on a busy corner. The yearning to liberate yourself from the contention and worry and slip away from the roar of trade and the wearying struggle affects you suddenly. For an instant, though it seems longer, you feel relieved. You do not hear the rattle of traffic, the scramble of humans, the rumble of business; you do not see the haste, madness and confusion. All of these fade out while you feel this longing for calm and happiness, and you see and feel in fancy a universe void of the complexities of civilization. You are for the moment the primitive man in the primeval earth.

The Pyramiding of Progressive Ideas 

The primitive man in the primeval earth gazed down, out and up at a universe in which civilization had not as yet begun. The things which your emotions sweep away as your imagination reconstructs the vista of simplicity in the world were not so much as ideas; they could not have been, for civilization has been progress from one idea and accomplishment to the next thought and work, and it would have been impossible, in the circumstances, to think of inventions which could only be suggested by consecutive marks in progress. We know from experience that one discovery follows from an- Page 111

other, and in this way civilization has been evolutionary. The first man could not have dreamed of the enormous buildings, railroad trains, the motorcars, aircraft, electricity, steam, and so on; there was nothing in his needs or wishes which indicated the demand for any of them.

We look back at him at the beginning of civilization, when the earth had not been converted to his use, in order to perceive more clearly the fact that man is an integrity in nature and that the balance in evolution applies to him and other beings and things in a common relativity.

CHAPTER XIX. 

Can you believe that man was ever fatigued in the early centuries of his existence? Can you believe that he was ever ill? Or ever troubled? Or hungry? Or cold? Or unclothed? Or without shelter? Or dissatisfied? Or despondent?

Did he not take rest when he commenced to feel tired? And eat when he felt hungry? In his common sense did he not regulate his living naturally for the preservation of his health and equanimity? He was always a rational animal (and lived like one until civilization took on frills and thrills), and in the general prevalence of simplicity health and happiness must have been his principal and first objectives.

Paradoxes of Luxury and Efficiency 

Can you believe that he wore himself out in his ramblings and studies or investigations, or in his play or diversion? When he felt tired, he probably recuperated Page 112

himself immediately with brief repose, and stayed fatigue. When his brain felt jaded from one concentrating on one subject, no doubt he revived it with a little rest. He conformed his employments to the implied requirements of nature.

You may be comparing to yourself the complexities of the present time with the simplicity of that, thinking that it was easy then to live according to the plain laws of nature, whereas now it would be difficult, probably impossible, or, if it were done now, we should be unable to be efficient in our occupations. However, the difference is not only in conditions, but in the mental viewpoint. Our remote forefathers did not have the conception of efficiency that we have, but their compliance with the laws of nature was subconscious obedience with the purpose of maintaining physical and intellectual efficiency.

They did not think of efficiency as we do; it was normal fitness to them; and had they reasoned on the subject they would have concluded that infraction of the natural laws would have made them unfit. If you would give thought to the subject, you, too, would realize that imposition on the strength and resources of the body does not effectivate efficiency, but, on the contrary, diminishes it temporarily and ultimately destroys it. You have the authoritative word of Dr. Crile of 

Cleveland, the Doctors Mayo of Rochester, Minn., and other noted physicians for this. Have they not declared that we are breaking down the nervous system and the mind, and shriveling away and dying early, from diseases and afflictions caused by emotional strain? Page 113

Men Formerly Were Not Savage

When civilization was almost as simple as nature, men lived naturally. We cannot question the inferences that we draw from our glance back to the dawn of civilization, with the first man obtaining from his outlook an understanding of his status in the great scheme. 

Nothing could be more natural to his belief than that he should use the earth and its contents properly; any other idea would have been alien to his constitution; it would have been savage, and we are certain that he was not savage. This may not tally with suppositions which we have drawn from later conditions in tribal and racial degeneration; nevertheless, it is obviously consistent with sound reasoning.

Man could not have been savage. To be so would have been contrary to the serenity and order in nature. 

The sole being with a higher intelligence could not have been savage, which would have been aberrant, when every other form and factor in nature was regular. 

The right thought for him would have focused on observing the principles which were implanted in his mind and body, and which were clear and distinct to him. 

He probably was crude and uncouth, but not graceless or savage. What we are sure that was in his mind was that he was to live on the earth and use it for his health and happiness. 

Law That Cannot Be Amended

The sermon on the natural law was visualized to him in the spacious prospect stretching out to his gaze, and in the response and promptings within himself. All that he envisioned and more was his. Every living and Page 114

every inanimate thing was kin to him. Instinctively, he knew this.

Perhaps, there was less that was mystifying to him than to us. He had, surely, a balanced conviction regarding nature and the simple principles. He got his knowledge from the source, and, unlike us, had no mistaken theories engendered by blunders and mistakes in the growth of civilization. He was as natural as nature.

The relationship between himself and all other things and beings and all principles and conditions was plain to him. He knew that he was an integrity in nature. 

He knew that there was order in the heavens, order in the spheres, order between day and night, order in the seasons, order in the earth and waters, order in vegetation, order in the animals. He realized the common. uniformity and regularity. He knew that there was order in his heartbeat and in the organs and functions in his body. A set of universal laws applied to all, he knew. All, he knew, comprised the cosmos, and also he was in and of the cosmos, and the cosmos in himself. 

By Reason We Should Know Better 

Would you say that in the primitive era men sensed the cosmos more distinctively than we? Perhaps, the all-inclusive harmony is clearer to us by intellectual appreciation, but it was clearer to them in the simple natural manner. 

"Self preservation is the first law of nature.' As well as at present, this was true in the Year One of mankind. It was true then in positive sense, whereas now we generally think of it in the negative, or, rather, Page 115

passive. This stresses to us the extent of the revulsion of conditions affecting living beings, and the radical change in their habits of thought and conduct. Selfpreservation means today, with most of us, the law of offense and defense. We think of rivalry and combat, and of vigorous and rapacious aggression. We think that in the wilderness the larger forms of life are subsisting on the smaller; that everywhere in nature this is so, and living for the dumb animals is a predatory pursuit from first to last. The stronger, we see in all circumstances, are ravaging the weaker. The latter are killed and consumed by the former. A great carnage goes on all the time from the garden behind your house out to the tangles of the jungle. 

We say that under this first law every living thing has the natural right to obtain by any method indicated by the precariousness of circumstances as much food as it needs to sustain life. Every living being has a natural appraisement of life; life is the being's all. It may not by instinct have a conception of death, or of oblivion, or of nothingness, but it must have a vague idea of extinction. However, it certainly has a direct appreciation of life. It has life, and realizes how precious existence is, and resorts to any means to preserve it. Every being is not only prepared to resist attack, sensing jeopardy in every suggestion of a trap or ambush, but is ferocious in hunger and ever ready, when the need arises, to attack, slay and devour a victim.

Life Is a Multiplication of Uncertainties 

As to ourselves, we consider existence in this complex Page 116

civilization as a struggle, and we hold that this first law is ours, too, and suspends all other laws in dire extremity. 

A great and relentless war goes on continuously among the beings and things of nature. It is waged in inanimate matter and among the forces of nature; and beyond the stratosphere, which is the mantle of the earth, enormous spheres are clashing and flinging substance, gases, vapors, fire and what-not in all directions in space.

We are not exempt from the strife which we think is normal to the duration of existence. As we are the highest order of life, having intelligence and volition, the conditions which we confront are different, and, we believe, harder. There was a time when we had to fight the voracious brutes with our strong hands and with clubs and stones, and our forefathers were equal to their predicaments. But in this age the struggle is keener and more complicated. We oppose one another. 

We manage to get food not only by working, but also by ingenuity and trickery. There is less hard physical labor, except for the common workmen, than there used to be. But there is harder mental and nervous strain, for we have, as a result of our intelligence, developed a state of civilization so complex that it has become a cyclopean menace to us. We are really killing ourselves by the fruits of our intelligence. 

The earth, as we consider it, is a war zone. We are born to a struggle. We call the struggle life. Life is made up of one battle after another. We scarcely have received it before we have a fight to save it. Thereafter we have one contest after another with one condition after another, to try to strengthen and retain it; and Page 117

finally we have a series of battles in the hope of prolonging it.

As we look into the long ago, it is impossible for us to believe that this contentiousness prevailed. It seems largely to be a consequence of the development of our civilization, at last as far as we are concerned. 

He Didn't Have the Radio or Motor Car As

The first man and his immediate descendants in all probability lived amidst universal peace and concord. 

At first there must have been a kindred friendliness among all animals, and between them and man. it were, there must have been a universal brotherhood. 

The huge beasts were comrades of man, and the small and weak had no fear of the large and strong. We can reasonably conjecture an era when this pacific state was general.

Man understands nature when he is close to it. He was very close to it then. He could not have been closer to it. The world, as a civilized growth on the earth, was unimaginable. Civilization would have been incongruous to that age, and man, could it have been explained to him, or if he could have contemplated it, would have abhorred and denounced the very idea. 

The human dream was very simple, the human wants were simple, and the human desires and hopes simple and primitive. All that men could wish for they had. 

They had plenty and health and happiness. They could not possibly have ambitions for greater boons. Whatever they would get, or try to get, in excess of plenty, health and happiness would detract from the blessings which they enjoyed.

Page 118

Man has been endeavoring for sixty centuries to procure more than this, yet every effort has produced less. 

A time came when he was not satisfied with plenty, health and happiness and he determined to take more than enough and improve on happiness. He made existence the war that it is, shortened his life span and subverted his happiness.

CHAPTER XX. 

The Family Responsible for Civilization 

The primary step in the evolution of civilization was the family. The next was the multiplication of families. The initial problems of civilization then arose. 

Even so, they were simple. Each family founded its own homestead, with ample ground for tillage and hundreds or thousands of heads of live stock. The questions that came up were easily settled. Rude places of shelter were built. More work had to be done. 

Amusements were invented. Rules and regulations had to be developed from experience. 

The patriarchal system of government grew out of the situation. It was a centripetal form. The head of each family was the lord. He declared the laws and enforced the discipline. Each family was a government to itself, and the head of it was the absolute ruler. If a member of the household was disgruntled or incorrigible, he was banished or left of his own will, and settled somewhere else and founded a family himself. 

The mode of living was as simple as it could be. The worst worries were disagreements among members of families, and, when they were grave, they were elim- Page 119

inated by voluntary or compulsory expatriation of the offender or aggrieved. Each family was a social unit. 

Ample ground was available for the sprouts of the families to settle on, and sustenance could be had, and was, with the minimum of labor. 

When, afterwards, the simple civilization waned before the complex, selfishness became ascendant with mankind as motive and objective, and every impulse of self-gratification polluted every idea and advance. 

During the patriarchal epoch it is doubtful whether the inevitability of death was contemplated often. 

Death was not the appalling and ever-lurking specter that it is today. Men did not trouble with the fear that fifty-eight years was the average span of life, which would have implied that some would die early and some late, that the years would be many to some, but few to others. Death was remote. Methuselah and Noah each lived to nearly a thousand years, and doubtless many had the individual confidence in an expectancy of 500 to 1,000.

Chefs Then Had No Complexes 

In so

Illness in all likelihood was occasional indisposition, which must have been suffered now and then. simple and natural a mode of living sickness would have been inapt. Men did not work too hard or too long. They ate and rested. They had no perplexities to cause them worry. They lived in harmony with nature, and their health must have been sound. This is all reasonable, as it is reasonable to suppose that man was never so nearly happy as he was then. 

You think that they had health and happiness be Page 120

cause their mode of living was simple and natural. Of course, this is true. You think that they could not have health, or perhaps not happiness, either, in the complex state of civilization that we have. I hope to convince you to the contrary. Nature's laws have not been suspended in present civilization; their force is as effective as it was in the Year One. Some of us, proceeding individually, act as if these ordinances have been allowed to become obsolete, because, we presume, we have decreed better; but we learn, generally too late, that they are more mandatory than ever in conditions in which extraordinary strain on every organ and part of the body is to be expected. 

It cannot be that the conditions themselves are the cause of our physical decline in the prime of existence; however, if they are, we are able to alter them, for we made and can unmake them. But it is more plausible that the fault is within us, in that we have presumed to set aside the arbitrary laws of health and happiness. 

The human species expanded from the patriarchal into the racial stage of social increase, and out of the races came tribes and nations. The population was larger and society divided and subdivided, and man had problems in economics and politics. The homestead on the big farm or plantation grew into villages, towns and cities, with the domiciles of hundreds to thousands of inhabitants. The lords of the population centers became kings, and as kings they had dreams of aggrandizement. 

Savagery Grew Out of Selfishness

The tribes spread out over the earth. Marching on land and crossing waters in rude craft they roamed Page 121

beyond the fringes of civilization. Principalities and monarchies were established wherever there was promise of easy sustenance and opportunities for the chiefs to wield supreme power and extend it. 

Incentives and objectives had changed fundamentally from those which were uppermost in the simpler periods. Men forgot about health and happiness in the lust for wealth and power. Now they conspired and fought for riches and territory; they warred and plundered, and enslaved the weak. Pleasure and debauchery superseded happiness.

Savagery signalized this long interval in the evolution of civilization. The throes of depravity and false ambition threatened the annihilation of society. War, greed, pestilence, oppression, tyranny and all other phases of malevolence supplanted peace, plenty, health, freedom and happiness.

Man was building civilization from his intelligence and experience, but in exaggeration of selfish impulse and desire. He temporarily lost his great ideals of brotherhood and kinship, amity and contentment. To gratify the bestial hankerings he raised a riot in his world against the predetermined order of nature. 

The fact was that man stultified his intelligence by giving way to his animal proclivities. He misused and disused his advantages to his own detriment. Nevertheless, he progressed. A constructive undercurrent ran through his deteriorative madness. He learned in suffering and damage the law of compensation. The debasement, misfortune and misery resulting as reactions to contumacy toward nature and toward the regularity and insistence of fixed law taught him that he could Page 122

not exercise his will in a carnally selfish plan without paying penalties. The injurious reaction, he found out, was equal to the seriousness of his antagonism to nature. 

Compensation, he perceived, perceived, was in kind and proportion, surely in proportion.

Man Forced to Become Inventive As the family was a settlement in the simple mode of living, in the evolution of civilization mankind. showed an invariable propensity for community existence. Division of labor had something to do with this as the population increased, and protection of life from anarchy was a factor, but the social considerations were among the most important. 

Existence became more difficult and complicated. 

Food had to be transported from the farms to the cities, some of which were critically located with regard to the means of subsistence. Man had become an artisan, and the products of his handiwork were hauled out into the rural sections. Serious economic problems arose, especially in connection with livelihood. Needs increased with the growth of large communities. 

Division of labor created new wants, and new wants extended the division of labor. 

The trials and problems compelled mankind to fall back on the higher intelligence, and minds took to inventive thinking. According to the old adage, "Necessity is the mother of invention”, and men applied their minds toward the solution of economic problems. Yet invention lagged behind political evolution and was checked by military embroilments for spoils and conquest, and it was not until man, after some thirty cen- Page 123

turies of conflict, arrived back at the original conceptions of peace and freedom in the more complex civilization.

CHAPTER XXI. 

The economic reaction to man's insolence to nature reached the peak in the eighteenth century, when conditions similar to those of the recent worldwide business depression (1931) prevailed in England. Men, women and children were starving. Although men were anxious to work, employment was not obtainable. We were just at the portal of the great era of invention in all activities. The outlook seemed hopeless for the moment. 

Nothing to Eat But Food 

An English clergyman, the Rev. Mr. Malthus, after studying the situation, wrote a book in which he declared as a principle of nature the thesis that nature would exterminate man by the inadequacy of fertility and productivity. He declared that the food supply increases by addition and the population by multiplication, and, therefore, a time would come when there would not be enough to eat. His was called the Malthusian theory. Most economists indorse it in the main; at least, they agree that food production increases by addition and population by multiplication. 

But the probability or possibility that the food supply will ever be inadequate for the sustenance of the total population of the earth is a speculative question. Naturally, we may look forward to a compensatory evolution from which will ensue a balance between wants and supply.

Page 124

That there is an ultimate limit to the aggregate of population which could find living space on the earth is a technical assumption within reason, and it is a logical assumption that there is an ultimate limit to the area and productivity of the soil. Yet, whether either of these limits will ever be reached is also a matter of speculation. Time is an important factor in the working out of problems of this kind, and if either or both of the two limits mature, the crisis could not take place for thousands or millions of years. Nature, however, has methods of striking balances over long periods, and we may expect that the food supply will always be adequate without waste.

We have witnessed during the recent depression (1931) enormous quantities of food in storage in the United States, Canada and Argentina, with millions in want in Europe and Asia. The explanation is that economic chaos prevails. We in these times do business with money, and those peoples in need of food have been unable to buy it, lacking the medium of exchange. 

Man "Adaptive" in Seeking Wealth

Of course, there has been a close and active interoperation between nature and human intelligence from the beginning of human existence. The men of the first few generations could not look far into the future concerning civilization. They were ruralites. A prophecy of the machinery and implements used on modern farms would have been a joke to them, if for no other reason than they could see no need of any but the primitive things which they had. They naturally foresaw that complications would ensue from increase of population and its dispersion, but nothing resembling Page 125

the complexities and diversities of our civilization. would have seemed plausible to them. 

Even then there were indications of the ingenuity which man would exercise in controlling nature for his particular purpose and benefit. The most conspicuous, and one which would be of credit in modern science and agriculture, was that of Jacob in predesignating the markings of the vestiture of the animals on the estate of his father-in-law. His portion of the animals was to consist of those which would be striped and mottled; and he arranged sticks and stones in the water at the drinking places so that pictures of stripes and spots would be impressed on maternal instincts and be communicated to the offspring. The device succeeded to Jacob's considerable enrichment. In an incidental way this was a real demonstration of the adaptation theory which Dr. Osborn has just now propounded; it illustrated the ability and inclination for adaptation, though it did not actually prove the theory. Jacob's idea was decidedly modern in every aspect. 

This was illustrative of what man expected of nature and of the mood of nature toward man. Nature was at his service for any wish or whim which was not opposed to natural principles, and on his part he had the intelligence to cipher out nature's workings and laws. His intelligence would assist in the evolution of nature, and nature would be at his command in the development of civilization. 

As there was a precise balance in the inter-relationship between him and nature, so throughout the double evolution a balance would persist continuously. The progress would be dual, uninterrupted and interactive, Page 126

and consequently of mutual effect. Jacob's ingenious trick was an example.

Medicines Are Concentrated Food 

We are restricted to the things and principles of nature for livelihood and health. We are all aware of this. However, it does not occur to us that even the medicines which the physician prescribes and the apothecary prepares for us are the same as food. Having dieted improperly, or neglected to diet, or indulged in excess, or otherwise deviated from the mandatory charges of nature, we become ill. To correct the abnormal chemical reactions we are obliged to take concentrated food in medicinal form. The medicines are derived from minerals, plants, animals, and consist, in individual instances, of the kinds and quantities of food-ingredients necessary for proper readjustment. 

The medicines are made of liquids, minerals, plants or animal matter, and in principle correspond to food. 

The body needs certain quantities of certain chemicals for curative, constructive or counteractive purposes.

The special quantities or chemicals necessary are not contained in the regular food, and therefore must be supplied in other kinds of food, which are medicines. 

The need of medicinal food is due considerably to our failure to comply with the laws of nature and partake of the bounty of nature. Sunlight, fresh air, water and fresh eatables are at our convenience almost free of cost and almost free of effort, yet we slight either or all of them. They also are foods and also medicines. 

We clash with nature in one manner or another, and nature avenges our foolishness.

Page 127

The Era of Individual Hygiene 

If I could convey no other conviction to you than the imperative necessity of yourself embarking in preventive medicine for the conservation of health, I am sure that this alone would be of remarkable benefit to you.

Doctors Crile and Mayo are among many eminent physicians and surgeons who point out with emphasis that the physicians must depend more than ever on the efforts of the individual in his own behalf. The physicians, with the assistance of chemists and research scientists, have achieved amazing success in diseaseprevention, and how much farther they can progress in this direction is uncertain; therefore, they advise the individual to use conservational and preventive measures himself.

The age span, as we shall note subsequently, would be much shorter without the preventive results brought about by science. In fact, the showing would be appallingly disappointing minus these results. You and your neighbors and friends have placed the whole burden on the doctors, and not only have disregarded the natural facilities for maintaining good sound health, but have also affronted nature with major and minor excesses. 

You are, you should remember, as close to nature as our ancient progenitors, notwithstanding that you may live in a city. You are, in some respects, closer to nature; that is, you have the conclusions of science for using the benefits of nature more intelligently. Science is able to tell you what you especially need for the pres- Page 128

ervation of your health and vigor and which foods contain the required ingredients, and so on. 

Were we guided exclusively by conditions as they exist, we should be tempted to assert as a principle that the human life-span has abbreviated in proportion to the advance of civilization. In thirty or forty centuries but one factor growing out of civilization has checked the death rate, this being the scientific prevention of disease in the past two or three decades.

Pestilence and epidemics, which, during the thirty or forty centuries, wiped out millions of human beings, are under the subjection of preventive medicine. By these measures the death-rate has been decreased and the life-line prolonged. But with this exception the advance of civilization has tended to break down health. 

CHAPTER XXII. 

Man Sees the Earth, But Not Nature

It is not nature's fault that most of us are not as close to nature as our forefathers, and it is not because we live in large centers of population. No matter where we live, we live in the sphere of nature. Nature does not stop at the corporate limits of any city or hamlet, or at the front door or windows of an apartment house. 

Nature penetrates the round canopy of pall and gas which envelopes the populous center and at least tries to enter the domiciles.

Thousands of residents of cities never lift their eyes to the sun; or, if they do, their glance is but cursory and no impression is made on the inner sense; they look at the sun, yet do not see it mentally; their eyes glance

Page 129

at a huge, bright disc, but the mind is on a piece of gold, or a scheme to get rich quick, or the prospect of pleasure after the day's imprisonment in office, store or factory. Dawn they may have witnessed some time; now, if they behold it, the glory is intervals of blinks of sleepy eyes. The sun that they see at mid-day is a glare on a glazed wall or glass-window or the light outside the shadows along the sidewalk and street. In the evening the sun that they see is a stream of light on the street car, motorbus or automobile in which they ride home. When they go out riding on holidays they are careful to ward off the bright, hot rays, which are as offensive as the flying dust or the strong wind. The sun is the star which illuminates the earth for the day's work, and were there no day it might not be necessary to go to work. The sun, at any rate, has no significance to them except as the natural orb of the day. 

They think seriously that they could get along without the sun; or that they would let it shine on as dispenser of power and energy, and use for illumination light that they can regulate mechanically. The sun brightens their pilgrimage some and drives away melancholy. It has some uses, they are willing to concede, and perhaps they should lift their eyes upward now and then.

De-Naturing Process in the Cities 

Thousands would not know that the ground is carpeted with a velvety green, soft to the eye, were they not in the habit of looking down. They look at the grass-decked earth oftener than they do at the sky, yet they are aware that the firmament is azure, having carried this recollection from the lighter years of play. Page 130

Concrete, they think, is cleaner and healthier, and there is no dirt or grime on it to stick to the shoes or fingers. 

Thousands in the cities live as independent of nature as possible, and by their manner show that they despise it. They prefer nature denatured, feeling, apparently, as if nature is crude and uncultured and should be run through a refining process.

Nature belongs to the cows and sheep, the birds and vermin, and the free animals, they believe. But the city residents can get along better without it. The substitutes for nature are much more agreeable. The natural is tolerable only when it is improved by man's ingenuity. 

But we may be induced by neurotic disorders, organic diseases and mental tension to entertain a kindlier feeling for nature, remembering that we cannot, after all, have health, efficiency or happiness without being what we really are-natural. In certain ways we may improve on the things of nature which we use, or, better, adapt them to our sensitive refinements, yet we cannot benefit from them if they are denatured.

The same affinity which existed in the Year One between man and his environment exists in our complex civilization. If we are denatured to some extent by the conveniences and customs of civilization, it is time to think with some concern that we cannot delay at going back to nature.

We are made soft by the comforts of civilization. 

But we overlook the fact that we are also being made hard by them and are shriveling from head to foot, and, perchance, within the head. From the complexities of our advanced civilization we are nurturing many "complexes". Nature resents our contemptuousness.

Page 131

PON BOOK TWO. 

CHAPTER I. 

grave

ONDER gravely the visible fact that you began to age as soon as you were born, and each succeeding minute from that moment until this has ticked, like a muffled or subdued alarm, the vanishing of your time. Time does not return. Time is irrecoverable. Nor can you fully retrieve the equivalents of time. The profligacy of your earlier years was wasteful and irresponsible extravagance, for which a compensatory penalty will be exacted of you, if you are not already expiating it.

You need to go into consultation with yourself on the state of your being. Do not deceive yourself by condoning your wantonness; if you do, you will be guilty of more contumely toward nature, and the atonement which you will be compelled to make will be harder. You are warned right now of the physical decadence that you have allowed to go on, and no one but yourself can check it. You have the option of shriveling in body and brain by further indifference, or recuperating your health and expanding your life expectancy. 

You cannot disavow your folly with health and time.

During the first seven or eight years of your existence time was like a bauble. You frolicked it away. You were incompetent to weigh the value of health, as you were childishly lightsome and were earnest only in play. Page 133

Treasure Instead of a Boon

During the next stage you were prodigal with health and time. You gave both over to the rashness of youth. 

As far as your health was concerned, you were a libertine. You lived for the pleasures of each day. You exulted in the reflection that tomorrow would take care of itself, and bragged that your health could stand the abuse, and, anyhow, you boastfully said, if you burned out your health, you would take that consequence, for the zest of your self-indulgence would more than requite you. Your incentives were in leading a more or less gay life. You lived but once, and were young but once, and later on you could give attention to your health. If you broke down, why, you would do so merrily. You could build up your health in the future, but these opportunities for pleasure were lost if not seized in youth. You had to take your delights in youth or not at all, for you thought they were seasonal. 

You were too busy in the next stage to think of health. You concluded that time was not to be reckoned in years or minutes, but in making money and being successful in your profession or business. You mixed up in this period excess of pleasure and excess in work. You could not get time enough for all that you wanted or had to do, and your wishes and duties were equally important. As your time was inadequate, you filched it from your health by reducing your hours of rest. You excused yourself with the proud belief that you were energetic and ambitious. You had to hash your recreations and activities in two-thirds or three-fourths of every day for a few years, you argued to yourself and friends, as you would not have the Page 134

spirit and vigor a few years hence. These things, too, were seasonal. You did not realize what you thought and said concerning the timeliness of your multifarious interests and the weakening results of mental and physical strain. Why did you say that you would not have the spirit and vigor in a few years? Did it not occur to you that this was an admission that you were destroying your health?

New Parts for the Human Machine 

How old are you? I do not ask you how young, but how old? I know that you, whatever the number of your years may be, are older than you should be. I am sure of this, because you have carried a mental reservation that you could look confidently to the physician to fix you up again, and have let yourself run down. 

You would overwork, overplay, live high and underrest, and the doctor would give you back your strength, your vitality, your rest, and your energy and ambition, and you would be sounder than ever in body and fresher of mind.

Frankly, have you not presumed too much on the physician's skill? Can even he restore youth to you, or middle age? Has he not said or intimated that you have done him an injustice by relying solely on him? 

Has he not said, in a friendly, yet cautionary, manner that you cannot shift the responsibility entirely, but must help yourself? Has he not explained candidly that, although he can and will benefit you, you must co-operate with him? 

A new gland, and you will be young again? A blood transfusion? A new set of muscles, a new bunch of Page 135

nerves, or, perchance, new organs?—all from men who are floundering and have ceased to care what happens, or how soon, or where. Why not a new brain, a new nervous system, a new heart, new lungs, a new liver, new kidneys and a new stomach? You have trusted in the physician. He can make you a new man. minor surgical operation and two or three bottles of medicine, and you will be able to read detective stories and get-rich-quick fiction again. Reductio ad absurdum!!! 

Ah! the physician did not tell you that he had the panacea for the ills besetting Eternal Youth in the tip of his fountain pen or the point of his knife. He did not assure you that he could indemnify you against your squandering of time and health. On the contrary, he volunteered the statement that he was not a conjuror or a quack, and reminded you that the insane asylums, hospitals, prisons and cemeteries were filling up with cracked brains, creaking hearts, wheezy lungs, petrified arteries, appendix stubs and other horrors of human foolery.

Individual Hygiene the Only Security 

What did the honest physician admonish you? To develop preventive medicine for yourself, as he had; to change your living habits, and, in general, to help yourself. He and allied chemists had carried preventive medicine about as far as they could, and the time had come for you to practice it in your daily life. 

How old are you? Old enough to care whether you have good health and long life? Old enough to understand that the physician knows what he is talking Page 136

about? Old enough to feel that you are slipping and to do your part toward renewing your health?

It is not necessary for you to have lived many years in this complex civilization to be actually old, or, I should rather say, to be ageing. If you are anywhere between 28 and 49, you have nervous, corporeal and mental premonitions of the blighting touch. You have frequent scares about the insane asylum, permanent retirement and the cemetery. You smell ether, you sense a withering of the whole body, you scent tube roses.

You feel that you have entered "the valley of the shadow" and the valley will become steadily deeper and the shadow darker. 

You should not be seized with these gloomy fears either at 28 or 49. At 30 your term of greatest efficiency and happiness should begin. You should then have the fortunate combination of useful experience and perfect health. Your ambition and energy should not be staled, and your mind jaded, and your body numbing or trembling; on the other hand, you should have poised vision, rational energy, a clear intellect and great reserve of health. 

Practicing Magic on Yourself 

The magician, Thurston, provokes laughter with a catch phrase, "I wouldn't deceive you for the world". 

You have been saying that to yourself and believing it. 

But anywhere between 28 and 49 you would do well to give up relying on legerdemain. You, assuming that you are average in condition of health and regularity of abnormal habit, feel the creeps of age. Every tingling of the flesh frightens you; and it should. Your

Page 137

thoughts, turned in to self, are the gravest which you ever have had. You are ageing when the prime of life should begin.

CHAPTER II. 

The average woman muses, some time in the 21-year interval between 28 and 49, that de-wrinkling is but a counterfeit of de-ageing, and casts about furtive glances to see who else may inherit her jewelry, furniture and silverware as her successor. The average man considers what other man may spend the money which cost him his health and life. They shudder at the realization that they soon will be only numbers in the files of the probate court. Their identities will be strong manila envelopes in fireproof boxes, while they will be under or behind birth and death dates, and, perchance, legends, which are pretty reading, wrought in stone or bronze.

The situation turns into a predicament when the idea persists in recurring to them that they may all too soon be known to relatives and friends by dusty and musty documents among thousands of other numbered dusty and musty documents. They imagine the pillar of stone or bronze with the names and dates on it. 

The legal records in the probate court and the brief memorials out in the woods will be the remnants of that nature defiance. 

Whoa! to the Ageing Process

But this gloomy outlook can be averted, and the 

21-year space between 28 and 49 made, what it should be, the principal section of the prime of life. The physician has correctly advised how to bring about this Page 138

happy result. He says that we must help ourselves. 

We can to some extent rely on him; but, as he says, we must utilize every reasonable precaution at our command. We must live in conformity with the laws of nature.

The ageing process can be stopped at any instant. 

To convert progressive decadence into a natural reconstructive functioning does not necessitate radical change in our habits. Taking care of health is like saving money for the rainy day; we do not have to be penurious and deny ourselves everything, or dispense altogether with luxury and entertainment; we go on using part of our earnings for comfort and recreation, but we set aside a part that can be spared for future contingencies and to assure ourselves of plenty and ease indefinitely. As we save money and accumulate a reserve of capital, so we should conserve our health. 

If we have been lavish with our money, and gone into debt, we simply counsel with ourselves and decide to change our policy. We do not give up in despair; on the contrary, we set about systematically to retrench, live within our means and straighten out our affairs. It is never too late to curb dissipation of health and regenerate it. We just arbitrarily cease the practices which are wrecking the constitution, and study out a plan adapted to our living and working conditions, and faithfully follow it. We adopt and adhere to a conservation policy.

Truly, it is more trying to disregard the ordinary rules for good health than to comply with them. No sacrifice is entailed on anybody in being reasonable in taking care of his health. On the other hand, breaking Page 139

these rules causes much trouble, discomfort, ill health and loss of time and efficiency and fitness. You do not feel right half the time when you are a spendthrift with your health, whereas you are always in tip-top shape when you are just ordinarily careful. You know this positively from your own experience.

Conservation of Health at Prime 

You commence to age as soon as you are born. Your time begins with your birth, and technically age increases with the increase of years. Technically, we measure life in time. But the state of your health has an unbroken and continuous connection with your life-span, and your age at any moment depends quite as much on your physical condition as on the array of years. Your life expectancy varies throughout your life; it may be more encouraging today than last week, and more encouraging next week than this. Your health is of vital importance, and if it is sound your life expectancy may be brighter at 28 or 49 than it was at 20. If it is not as good at 28 or 49 as it was at 

18 or 25, it is within your capability to restore it and lengthen your life-span. 

Although ageing commences with birth, we go through a building-up course during the first two decades. We are, in a sense, growing younger as we grow older. As the years increase we collect strength and vigor. We gradually ascertain our physical and intellectual resources and gradually improve them. We are able to find out between one and 20 what our capacities and deficiencies are.

These are constructive decades. Through them most Page 140

of us live normally. We play and go to school and university and equip ourselves for the work for which we are fit and which we like. All in all, we are very considerate of our health during these intervals. We live pretty much by routine. Our habits are regular and we are disciplined to avoid excesses. 

While these years pass we really add years to the lifespan by watching and improving our health. As we grow older, but not old, we grow more vigorous. It is true that we may be younger when we go to work than when we began to walk or started working. Provided we have a satisfactory genealogical inheritance, it is likely that we do launch into business with a fair outlook for a long and successful life. 

Love, Solicitude and Sacrifice

It is the general rule that the development of health receives close and constant attention during childhood and early youth. Parents are vigilant. They feel that sound health in their offspring is a better legacy than wealth, and they are scrupulously conscientious in their watchfulness, advice and dictates. Girls bloom into young ladies and boys mature into men with the best physical assets that love, care and discipline can produce. 

Ageing in the sense of growing old and wearing down begins as soon as we discontinue this rational and essential attention to the fundamentals of health. It dates generally from the day when we become our own masters. The discipline of the previous years is not consistent with the enlargement of our environment. We drop it, but most of us fail to study out a new system.

The only discipline that we have after we are grown Page 141

is that which is enforced on us in our occupations. We are obliged to be at our duties at a fixed time every morning and remain at them until a fixed time every evening. We cannot, under any circumstances, deviate from them. Earning a livelihood being our most important concern, we hinge our other interests on it, and as a consequence our scheme of living is lopsided, for we neglect to prescribe rules for ourselves during the hours when we are away from our work, whatever it may be.

By rules I do not mean a written schedule which must be adhered to literally. I mean that we should have a practicable system whereby we would have the order or regularity conducive to the further development of good health.

We should not abandon interest in health as soon as we are left to ourselves. The constructive period does not end when we reach our majority, but goes on during the next decade, in which we are developing up to our prime. The first three decades are constructive as to health. They do not line a downward curve in the span of life, but an upward. They mark, or should mark, a steady improvement in health up to the prime, when we should be at our physical and mental best. 

Fast, Furiously and Luxuriously 

With the same care of health during the third decade as during the first two, the curve of the life-span would not tend to incline downward in the prime of life, but to run on horizontally over a long and protractable space. The life expectancy could, and with many would, be better at 49 than at 20. This would signify

Page 142

that those who live sanely from 20 to 30 and from 30 to 40 are younger at 30 than at 20 and younger at 40 than at 30. This may not be a precise conclusion from a strict actuarial viewpoint, yet it would be exact from the actual. We do not count age in terms of years alone, but in the relationship between time and health. 

It is in the short stretch of years from 18 or 20, approximately, to about 28 or 30 that we impair our health. We weaken or ruin it by neglecting and straining it. When we arrive at 28 or 30 we suddenly sense that we are ageing. The few years elapsed could not have brought on this feeling; five or ten years at this stage of life should cause no perceptible wear, for we are vigorous as a result of the care taken during the preceding two decades, and we are in condition to offset the debilitating conditions of time alone. Nevertheless, at 28 or 30 we are sure that age is telling on us. 

We notice little portents of decline, or maybe there are omens of destructive retrogression.

CHAPTER III. 

The causes of our premature ageing lie in the complexities of our advanced civilization. We have departed almost wholly from the natural. We have discarded most of the absolute laws of nature and exist on artificial contrivances by which we expect to live fast and luxuriously. We try to cram our efforts into a few years, and achieve easy and immediate success in our activities and enjoy ourselves virtually without restraint. 

Slaves to the Economic Machine

I do not contend that our civilization is blamable Page 143

of and within itself, but rather that we are misusing and disusing our facilities and opportunities, and are delaying the adjustment of our methods of living to the change and progress in business. At the same time, I do not admit that everything in our civilization is right. Besides adjusting ourselves to new conditions, we probably shall have to scrap some of our socalled improvements.

Useful and efficient as the machine is for most purposes,

I would not concede that we should enslave ourselves to it. Necessarily, there are limits to the usefulness and efficiency of the machine; there are points at which the machine becomes a social, moral and political, as well as an economic, detriment. While I feel confident that we obtain economy with efficiency by standardization and routine in business, I am equally sure that there is a boundary at which standardization and routine react disadvantageously to business management and human character and health.

It is not a good thing for society to be a machine or part of systems of routine and standardization. This leads us to live on the quantity basis, instead of the quality. We get into the habit of estimating everything by volume or action, and we try to dispense with time, and distance, and labor. From using machines in our employment and diversions we become machinehabited, and are, in fact, no more than automatons. 

Having standardized our offices, stores and factories, we standardize our homes; and we automatically accommodate ourselves to a routine which covers everything that we do.

It is a necessity, we think, to have all the living fa- Page 144

cilities, without exception. We say that we save time and money and labor. 

Not Efficiency Nor Economy, But Wrack

But we are not saving labor or time, or producing efficiency or economy. We are simply converting the labor and time into physical strain and mental anguish. 

We are committing suicide by degrees. In effect, we reduce our mechanics, standardization and routine to a human replica which works out as standardized selfdestruction. 

You are as cognizant of these conditions as I. You may fill in the details, yourself. You may trace the physical devolution of any of your friends. Not one, in all likelihood, has a clean bill of health. Each feels the peril of his status. Neither are you exemptable. 

You have misgivings of nervous or physical collapse from the high tension of our advanced civilization. 

This is a high-tension era, and normal to it are high blood-pressure and other physical and mental stringencies resembling "frozen assets" and dropsical credit in commerce.

Our occupational and general experience between 28 and 49 should qualify us to look forward to extraordinary satisfaction in achievement and happiness from 

50 onward. If our money, positions, machines, standardization and routine are as economical and efficient as we believe them to be, together they should be our guarantee of health, comfort and greater productivity after 49. We should be at our best from 50 onward.

But our apprehensiveness before we arrive at the fortieth milestone makes the forty-ninth fearful, and we Page 145

dread to think of the interval from 49 to 70. Even at 

40 we observe the physical stigmata of reactionary civilization, and 49 to 70 is jammed with the specters and terrors concerning which the physicians have warned us. 

Skeleton in the Period of Leisure

Yea! If we are scarred at 40 by the maladies of the epoch, what horrors may we reasonably anticipate between 49 and 70? Logically, it should be the term of retribution. Nature would visit upon us every form of chastisement for our desecration of her benefits.

The outlook would be of living death-of skeletons in taut, shriveled skin with bleary eyes, fagged brains and rickety hearts and lungs.

Is it not a fact that we are tossing human beings out on the dump-heap when they should be most efficient physically and intellectually? We explain that machine-production and standardization are displacing them, but whisper that in these strenuous times the human machine is incapable of meeting the demands of application and speed after 30 or 40, as the pace is too fast and the stress too burdensome. We cite this as a benefit of machine-production and standardization, in that it gives us more time for leisure. 

Leisure evidently is proscribed between 20 and 30 or 20 and 40, and in this decade or two we are supposed to concentrate most of the exertion of a lifetime. Our entertainment, which is more indispensable to health in the monotony of dull routine than in one of more leeway, must consume the vitality which standardized concentration does not attenuate, and we work and play to the understood purpose of taking leisure and Page 146

repose in the latter years, when, in our prime, we are ejected from business for unfitness, recking not that we probably will be as unfit for the long vacation as for regular employment.

CHAPTER IV. 

The economic phases are outside the pale of our inquiry, except to the extent that worry is damaging to general health. Inasmuch as the machine and standardization and routine expel us from the domain of activity when we reach 40 or 45, they should compensate us for service sufficiently to enable us to enjoy our belated leisure; we should accumulate between 20 and 

40 a capital reserve adequate for easeful subsistence, free from anxiety, during the rest of life after 35 or 40. 

Here's Your Hat! Best Wishes!

But the economic problem seems not to enter into the subject. If we are unfit for business after 40, we are probably unfit for leisure. Our days are numbered, and we do not need a competence for old age. Whether the period of leisure is long or short, it will be taken up with attempts to regain our health. Business smiles us out through the front door at 40 to 50, with the last pay-check in hand, and graciously alludes to the prospects of long leisure which we have, and hopes that we shall enjoy the rest, and we are away from the machine and out of the standardization and routine at last. Synchronized with the system, we go forth feeling that the human machine, still instinctive to the co-ordination, has run down. The business machine has been kept up by "maintenance, depreciation and repair”, but Page 147

the human machine is ramshackle and feeble, as the counteraction to depreciation has been deferred to the period of leisure.

We have not slighted pleasure or the emoluments of standardized and routined business. We have been as earnest in the pursuit of pleasure and in schemes to get rich quick as in our attachment to our work. Business has not prostrated us physically and mentally more cruelly than our indulgence in our own desires. We have agreed with business that it is all right to put off attention to health until we retire from business; we shall retire while we are young and we shall have enough resistance in the body to ward off organic disease and will be able to recuperate quickly. Meanwhile we shall have a good time and make a heap of money easily and speedily. We shall have enough money to buy good health back.

Business is only concerned in our fitness for ten, fifteen or twenty years, from 20 to 30 or 40, and does not care, if we do not, whether we shall be fit then and thereafter. The condition of your health, after business is through with you, is your own matter. Business does not bother about your mode of living away from the office, store or factory, provided you are straight in your accounts and efficient in your work; and surely business is not responsible for your impairing your health by failing to cover "maintenance, depreciation and repair" in your conduct. Business, in the strictest sense, is interested in you only between 20 and 40 or 50, and your productivity during this period; it looks to your common sense and self-interest to preserve your health.

Page 148

Standardized Business Donates a Problem 

As you are living at present, what is your healthexpectation between 28 and 49 and between 49 and 

70? Are you fit for your work? Will you be able to work after standardized business is through with you? 

Will you be fit for your leisure? Is the state of your health now such as to indicate that your responsibilities will lessen in the later years? Or will deteriorating health pile up responsibilities in the term set aside by standardization for your leisure? 

If you are to be efficient between 28 and 49, it is necessary for you to obviate deterioration, as business does with the iron machine. If you are to have health between 49 and 70, you must conserve it during the preceding years. The human machine is much stronger than the iron and has greater endurance, but it is not so easily reconstructed. You cannot buy new parts for the human machine, or order a new machine. You have to maintain the parts and the whole as time passes. 

Business repairs an old machine or installs a new; you cannot do either.

Let us examine our physical and mental liabilities at 

28 and at 49. We started to work some time between 15 and 20. Most of us had excellent health; a few had important hereditary deficiency. Some had better natural health than others, as some had greater ability and talent than others. The individual differences and idiosyncracies are implied. Inequalities among human beings are natural; they are products of nature in the course of development or evolution. No two human beings are alike; it would not be normal for them to Page 149

be so. Differences are essential in the natural plan. At any rate, we start to work in good health. 

Once at work we are cogs in the business machine. 

We are in standardized business. We are bound by the regulations of routine. In order to do our work properly, we adjust ourselves to the exigencies of business; it would be impossible to adjust business to our conditions or circumstances. We are urged, first, by the desire to earn a livelihood, and, next, by the desire to advance and succeed in our business or profession, and both tend toward concentrating every consideration in our existence on our employment.

Human Unit Is Easily Replaced 

Business is more complex than ever, due chiefly to the great division of labor and the volume of output and extent of trade. Business is large-scale and widespread, and it is standardized to the minutest details with the object of simplifying operations for efficiency and economy. Business is conducted at high speed and under high pressure. The individual is, therefore, a mere unit in the big machine, and a unit which can be easily replaced and which may at any moment be displaced by a new invention.

The struggle for a livelihood and for success and preferment is a physical and mental tax. The human machine not only functions at high speed and under high pressure, and according to routine and standards, but also under severe mental strain, which is felt most injuriously in worry. The human is a thinking machine, with nerves and emotions, and hopes, ambitions and ideals.

Page 150

CHAPTER V. 

Routine is one of the most important characteristics of modern business, if not the most important. It figures directly in every operation of whatever kind. 

Routine is the fundamental of standardization. Routine even governs the motions of workers as it does the movements of the iron machines. Routine is even connected with time. It is in effect in every incident of business. To standardization in the machine and system in business is co-ordinated routine on the part of the individual. Routine is comprehensive and continuous in modern business.

Routine is to you what unvarying iteration is to the iron machine. The latter is designed and made to go through predetermined movements or revolutions over and over again, and it can do nothing else. On the other hand, you were not made for routine, but for diversified and irregular activity of mind and body, and the recurrent or incessant gesture, motion or performance, with the recurrent or incessant noise, in an unchanging posture and in a place which always looks the same, humdrums all the senses with a depressing monotony, until you do function exactly as the machine. 

Routine for Swivel-Chair Wiseman

A few of the officers in an establishment have some variety in their work, yet in the main their work, too, is routine. Similar questions come up again and again; if a machine could hear or read them it could decide them, each is so much like the other. But taking any office, store or factory, most of the work done by all the officers and employes is routine from first to last. Page 151

Every hour, every day, every week, every year, the same thing is repeated. The same process is followed. 

All the human units motion, move, think like co-ordinating parts in a big machine, which runs standardized business by rigid system.

The routine of modern business is different in its effects from the routine connected with common labor. 

The latter is wholly mechanical and almost wholly physical, and, while it strengthens certain muscles, and it does not irk the mind, it has the effect of partially paralyzing it and benumbing betterment-ambition. 

The routine to which I refer is distinctively monotonous, in that it grooves the intellect as the senses function in ruts fixed by the system. Thus the entire human machine works constantly in monotony. 

The physical consequences of the endless monotony are dispiriting. Imperceptibly the dulling status affects the mind and body somewhat like worry. The effects on the human economy are stagnating, quenching the fires of energy and ambition. As a result you are urged by your blunted sensibilities to neutralize the deadening effects by compensatory diversions outside your work, and these are carried to the other extreme of inflaming your senses and emotions. 

Believe It or Not, Work Is Beneficial 

Of itself work is beneficial to the body and intellect. 

Work is constructive. The body becomes stronger by work, the mind more alert and competent. Work, like experience, is improving. We should rust away without it. Idleness is far worse than monotony or excessive labor; it is nothing more than a method of rotting Page 152

to death. It is the form of self-destruction which is just the opposite of over-living and over-working. 

You could not utilize your capacity or achieve efficiency without work and experience. 

To work to excess is next to impossible, provided we diversify our activities and interests. Diversification is an easily available method of obtaining rest and reviving our strength and energy. We must resort to it at frequent intervals, in fact, whenever we feel the least fatigue; otherwise, we wear down and diminish efficiency. In a given time or under given conditions, you could work too long or too hard and bring on fatigue; yet, as a general rule, you could not do enough work, and the more work that you do the more healthy you grow.

As to routine, which is synonymous with monotony, you finally attach it also to efforts at diversion. The means of entertainment and relaxation which you contrive for the time when you are not at work are at first as diverting as you intended, but gradually you allow them to become habitual, and, therefore, routine, and they become as monotonous as your work, whereby you compel yourself to draw more on your energy for excitement. Instead of getting resting and refreshing change, you seek stimulation, which, again, is enervating. 

Standardization of the Manner of Living 

You may reflect that this last contention is not well founded. But you will ratify it if you will take the trouble to analyze your behavior in seeking diversion. 

You will see that your thoughts veer from one idea to another and you drop one thing immediately after you

Page 153

have begun it to take up something else. You are after stimulation, not after the change which is a restorative and soother.

Habit is routine, and habit rules you from breakfast to bedtime. Between routine at work and habit when not at work, you lead a standardized existence. You try to overcome the monotony of routine with the stimulation which habit offers, and your last resort is to more stimulation by multiplying your pleasures.

You have developed an interplay of monotony and stimulation, the one depressing and weakening you and the other exhilarating and burning you up, and both devastating your health.

A well-known professional man presents to us the theory that the mind itself never tires from concentration or activity, but becomes languid from physical exertion. Alternating to another subject, that is, diverting the interest of the mind, has a vivifying and compensatory effect, he maintains, even on the body, and after such a rest the intellectual work may be resumed without the least indication of fatigue. The theory is mentioned in order to show the physical value of alternating work with rest, by which a physical balance may be kept up at all times. Fatigue may be overcome with immediate rest or diversion, and its injurious effects counteracted.
