When we consider physical tension and over-exertion, we are obliged to take into account the constructive efficacy of the mind. It is not only a fact that tiring of the body causes lassitude in the brain. Far more important is the influence of the mind over the body. The most staid physicians agree with the less Page 154

conservative and more daring that psychiatry is an active and powerful factor for preserving or improving health. We cannot here minimize its importance. 

Although psychiatry is used under many names and systems which disguise its true identity, and schools of doctrine have adopted it as the rudiment of cultural platforms, and although its power and capacity are exaggerated, it is, as far as the majority is concerned, not appreciated sufficiently. 

CHAPTER VI. · 

You Have Heard of It-the Brain

As I illustrated to you in a previous chapter, the brain is an integrity in the human body, composed of natural substance, and has intimate relationship with each organ and sense. That the sublime intangible puissance of man resides in a labyrinth of cells inclosed in a slimy bag is a wonder that no one understands, and, probably, never will. It is seldom that we ever understand a first natural cause or condition; there is something mysterious about everything, as there is to the brain. However, from experience and experimentation we acquire much practicable knowledge of the faculties of the mind and their influence and power. 

That the brain exercises domination over the body is not amazing. It is the seat of the intelligence and will in which man's superiority in nature lies. It is consistently natural that the mind should have great sway and scope over the functioning of the human organs, ample sway, in many circumstances, to settle the health of the body.

Page 155

Dr. Crile, you recall, said that modern diseases, which result from emotional excitation and depression are not intellectual. Emotion atrophies, shrivels or consumes the nerves and vital organs. On the other hand, we have our intelligence as a ready means to curb or prevent emotional ruination, through our sympathetic nervous system.

I interpolate this extended allusion to the power of the mind in order to accentuate by special illustration the advantages with which it vests us. Each of us has a resourcefulness, within reasonable latitude, to control the state of his health. This resourcefulness is in the will, as well as in the judgment. 

Products Rule the Intelligence 

You saw many examples of the sovereignty and ineptitude of the intellect during the world-wide economic depression. Men and women heroically rose above the sombrous difficulties which presaged chaos gloomier and more inextricable, whereas other men and women relinquished hope, and, crushed in spirit, to themselves acknowledged disaster to be inevitable. The latter defeated themselves. Adversity exterminated their stamina and they lugubriously moped through the calamity, pitying themselves and dreading every moment more shocking persecution by malevolent fate. 

The former realized that they surely would triumph over misfortune if their only weapons were confidence, fortitude and perseverance. The cowards and weaklings incapacitated themselves for the struggle by first losing spirit and next impairing their physical health. 

Decisive will, had they used it, would have guided

Page 156

them through the long-drawn-out trouble in comparative equanimity. The heroes who viewed catastrophe in self-confidence and courage, retained their health intact and strenghtened their mettle. 

One or another explanation may be offered of this pronounced difference in human beings. But the fact still stands out that the intellect is the most potential faculty that we have. Nobody can be certain of success or failure, but every one has the means of passing through either with honor and credit. This same asset which enables us to stand superior to our circumstances and to modify environment, is subject to our wish for the good of our health.

Scientists allege that we have allowed the products of the intellect to rule us. "The last few years," says 

Mr. E. E. Slosson, a celebrated interpreter of scientific ideas, "have made it manifest that in our civilization the mechanical forces have got ahead of the moral (and intellectural) forces. Man is mounted upon a bigger horse than he can ride". He adds that "the physical sciences evidently have developed so far beyond the political (and social) forces as to constitute a menace to our civilization". 

Chance to Be Everyday Heroes 

Mr. Harold de Wolf Fuller, professor of journalism in New York University, suggested in an article in the 

New York Herald-Tribune of November 29, 1931: 

"Suppose that human nature lived up to the blessings of this machine age instead of living down to its mechanical perfection." In other words, as I have previously illustrated, suppose that we use our advantages, instead of misusing and disusing them. Like the men and Page 157

women who relied on their intelligence during the depression, we might be everyday heroes in our manner of living. "A tremendous step forward will be made," said Mr. 

Fuller, "when the truth of some great principle in the sphere of the social sciences can be demonstratedsomething comparable, for example, with the principle of magnetic induction, from which, in conjunction with certain related principles, so many of our extraordinary facilities of communication have been derived." 

We know how to invent, he implies, and how to make machinery, but we have not learned to control our facilities or how to live sanely under the new conditions developed by our complex civilization. 

Mankind is waiting for an idea from some one as to how to live! We can go up in the air, down in the sea, through the earth; with the voice we can circle the earth; we see living pictures and hear the vibrant words of the dead; we look into space and see light or a body traveling toward us which represents a cosmic force of the past; we begin a work whose results will not be operative until some time in the future. But we do not know how to live, and we watch for a message from somebody with an acceptable idea. 

Picture of the Synthetic Human Being We lived aright during the first two decades of our existence. We lived aright as long as we lived by command and advice of seniors whom we trusted. We lived aright by our faith in them. We lived aright up to the day when we went to work. However, after that neither we nor any one about us had a suggestion to Page 158

offer on how to live; so, after 20 we lived carelessly, and, of course, according to Mr. Slosson, we let "the mechanical forces get ahead of the moral and intellectual forces". Like the slinkers during the economic depression, we succumb to what we imagine is the inevitable, and we, too, become machines. 

About 30 to 40 approximately many of us are ageing so that half of our teeth are new and perchance machine-made. We read the advertisements in the eager hope of running across something to save or restore our hair. We have different eyeglasses for different activities. 

We have taken every serum developed by science. We are so mechanical-minded and so mechanical-habited as to look forward in firm confidence that our physician, when we at length go to him with our sad story, will be able to extract our nervous system and insert a new one, perhaps and preferably a synthetic one; a new heart and lungs, perhaps and preferably chemical composition turned out by large-scale production, and a new brain, perhaps and preferably made from wild flowers like the new synthetic rubber.

At 49 the physician disappoints us with the information that chemistry has not yet succeeded in producing new arteries. Ours are drying up. Further, he doubts whether surgery could install new arteries and veins in you. You also need, he says, a new pump. 

Your kidney and liver are bad. And your glands! "Well, you'd better revolutionize your mode of living and give nature a chance for a few months or a few years". You go out to a health resort for a long visit, and, if nature is vindictive toward you for your desecration of her gifts, the surgeon may (as a dernier re- Page 159

sort, as he calls it) utilize you for a series of drastic experiments by trying out in you new parts taken from lower-order animals. 

Physician Writes With Red Ink

Your prime commences just before or after 50. The physician is obliged to use red ink in drawing up the balance sheet of your health. You may be twenty-five to fifty per cent normal, but because of organic disease there is a minus sign after the figure. He closes his record with a debit of fifty to seventy-five per cent. 

Expectancy of health and happiness at 49 to 70 is described by the physician's findings, just reported, plus your recuperative power and patience. You will allot your leisure toward compensating to nature for your neglect of ordinary health precautions while you were striving for success and money, and living and working as a machine to routine and habit. 

CHAPTER VII. 

"What fools these mortals be!" says Puck in Shakespeare's "A Midsummer Night's Dream". The little wag stressed the word "mortal". Yet he could and should perceive wisdom in our folly, as well as folly in our wisdom. Our fault is that we do not blend them so that our folly always is good for us. 

Indeed, we mortals are fools. Nothwithstanding that we sense, ere we are able to think, our substantial affiliation with all beings and things, it is when we begin to reason that we become irrational. Nature whispers to us sweetly of the vast harmony, and it would suffice for our health and happiness to heed the call.

Page 160

We are scarcely able to day-dream and weave bright hopes in our fancy before we envision a realm of fairies and enchantment of which we are part or with which we have a sympathetic contact. The fairies are as real to us as the white clouds floating across the azure sky and forming all sorts of shifting and changing things, and the magic as enthralling as the silvery moonbeams trickling through the foliage into the bedroom and diffusing a filmy luster in which we discern tiny objects dancing. 

Fairies Frolic on the Porch

It does not matter how sumptuous or squalid the room is, the pale light enters it so that the sprightly minims may see to frolic there. There may be a soft sward in the yard and the sweet aroma of nodding flowers, or a coarse top of cinders and a background of rough, slanting and broken fence, it is fairyland nevertheless, and you might see much gamboling and hear much song and laughter. In truth, you do.

This was when you first sensed the intangible in the tangible, and the tangible in the intangible, and you pictured, as it were, in an emotion, the merry piper 

Pan. His music in the quivering trees by day and the humming breeze by night reverberated soothingly and blissfully within you, and went up to the stars and reechoed back to the earth. 

Ah! sweet, precious life! Felicitous diapason of the universal harmony! Joy and bloom of outstretching nature! Youth-Eternal Youth! All thrilled us in that indescribably happy moment when our dreams soared out to the enchanted realm and the blithe fairies winged and skipped into our temple of aspirations. Page 161

Pan in his small person typifies the concord in the cosmos-in nature of the earth and nature of the outer space and revolving globes, compared to which our planet is a mite. A mere child is he; a carefree, gay, and, we might say, an almost irresponsible, lad. But what other figure could be imagined which would so aptly connote the cosmos? His heart is glad, his countenance serene, his bearing free, and in time and vitality he is young, very young. 

Peter Pan Whistles a Tune

The reed lute which he poises to his lips signifies the dulcet melody which permeates the universe. He pipes his happy lay in the tune of nature to the evenness of the continuous stream of cosmic rays pouring into the earth from enormous stars out in illimitable space, to the dazzling parade of the sun and the bewitching splendor of the languorous moon, to the sparkle of the snow on mountain peaks, to the bright waving green of the earth, to the roar and purr of the waters, to the rumbling and steaming at the earth's core, to the whistling and screeching of the winds, and to the warbling of the birds and the strange, varied notes of animals of a thousand different casts. He plays, too, the cheery strain in the heart of man. 

Peter Pan! Who is he? All the earth, all nature.

This is what his name means. To the ancients he was a boyish god. He was not a man or woman, an adult matured by time and changed by experience, not a person who had lived up a special philosophy from individual growth. He was as fresh and natural as nature, a human waif thrilled with appreciation of all the beauty, order and rhythm surrounding him. Page 162

The modern Peter Pan, portrayed by the delightful British writer, J. M. Barrie, and represented by the fascinating American actress, Maude Adams, is a rollicking little adventurer. An offshoot of the soil, his garb consists of plaitings of leaves, grass and flowers. The dawn glitters in his hair and the night sparkles in his eyes. His speech is wistful and his manner jaunty. 

He is nature. He lives; he exults. He is the unison of nature, kindred to every being and thing. Like him, nature lives. There is a great unanimity, as it were, in and around and about him. Nature is a song. 

In Harmony With the Cosmos

We understand how Pan feels. The gentle zephyr skims across our cheeks. How soothing is nature! We look up at the sable clouds, and playful little raindrops tap us in the eyes and on the lips. How cooling! The sun rays warm us. How invigorating! The earth is calm and motionless in the dead of night. How tranquil and peaceful! We are responsive like Pan to the moods of nature.

Each of us is Pan. This is it. We discover the realm of enchantment and see the fairies in it. We discover the wizardry inherent in our natural environment. Our high intelligence enhances the charm of nature by sympathetic understanding. Our dreams and tempers are internal expressions of the human sensitiveness to the wonders of nature, wonders which are most marvelous in their very ordinariness.

The modern Peter Pan symbolizes Eternal Youth. We joy in living and yearn to live long, and as long as we live we would like to be young, carefree and happy. We Page 163

wish that we should never grow old. In tune with nature, and young in body and spirit, we wish to live as long as nature exists. The law of the earth's gravity is in us.

We love this nature of which we are a part and we love life even more, for to us the two are one. 

The living earth loves the inanimate earth.

We cannot grow old with day dreams of fairies and enchantment. We may keep the fairies with us for years and years and exist in enchantment for as long a time as we can have bright visions and fair hopes. By cherishing the spirit of Eternal Youth our intelligence cannot be tinged with the moroseness or acerbity which comes upon those who let themselves age.

But Self-Gratification Is Alluring 

But too many of us after a while forget about Pan and the fairies, and our imaginings are of selfish and material gains. We cease to appreciate the benefits and wonders of nature, and sordid thoughts and ambitions bury the idealistic, and rivalry in strife takes the place temporarily of the desire for Eternal Youth.

And subsequently we are shocked by the radical changes in our mind and body, and we realize that we lost Eternal Youth when we ceased day-dreaming in the mood of Pan. Gravely now we ponder whether there is not a nectar or a spring of which we may drink the spirit which we slighted. We could have had something like Eternal Youth had we remained young.

Rejecting the advantage which we had, and could have saved, our hope for Eternal Youth was extinguished with the gradual disappearance of Peter Pan and the fairies from our lives, and we cogitate whether Page 164

there may be a substitute. It is useless to exclaim "Turn backward, turn backward, O Time, in your flight, and make me a boy again, just for tonight". The dreams and wishes which used to come true fail to materialize nowadays, and we cannot wish time away or recall Eternal Youth. After we renounced the spirit of youth, ageing was resumed, and the spirit cannot be revived. 

But a yearning like that of the enchantment age still lingers in us. We would, like Ponce de Leon, travel far and undergo much hardship and suffering to discover the Fountain of Youth. There were magical springs on the American continent, to which the Indians swarmed to renew their health. We call them medicinal springs at the present time. Inasmuch as the 

Indians had found these healing waters, their legend of the Fountain of Youth was credible. 

Ponce de Leon Had the Wrong Hunch

Ponce de Leon doubtless felt like our decrepits of 30, 40 and 50, and he had the hankering to live on for many years, as they have. He noted from symptoms that he would be feeble before long. He would have fought his way with his sword for a thousand miles and more for an option on a few more years. 

The ageing process has one of two effects on each of us. Many of us, as the years multiply, become gradually resigned to the shortening of life. On the other hand, many are alarmed and become more eager to live; they resist even the idea of senility and eventual death. Those who have had heavy responsibilities, or much trouble, or shattered health are resigned to destiny; however, those who still have incentives to live shiver at the thought of decay. Page 165

The spirit of Eternal Youth was real. We had it. It was as real as day-dreams, wishes and Peter Pan, and they were as real as nature. But the Fountain of Perpetual Youth never existed anywhere. Ponce de Leon searched for it in vain, and every one else who has looked for it has been disappointed. It is one of the tangibles which cannot be located. Youth is a condition or state which is in trust with nature, and this we are able to obtain if we are willing to pay the price which nature asks.

CHAPTER VIII. 

Pardon me for a few seconds, if you please, while I soliloquize on the pricelessness of the intangibles. Unfortunately, we do not realize how much we forego when we give up our early dreams and ideals. We cannot be surprised that, as Mr. Slosson stated, “in our civilization the mechanical forces have got ahead of the moral (and intellectual) forces" so far as to "constitute a menace to our civilization". We traded our birthright for a mess of pottage. 

We Desire Heaven Here and There

The immortal in us inspires our lofty ideals. But they have the natural phase in the attachment to earth and life which every one feels. We wish to be comfortable, happy and healthy in the journey through our time, and nature to be beautiful and congenial. We have all these desirable boons while we exist in enchantment with fairies and imaginings. The moral and intellectual forces are then stronger than the mechanical. 

The situation alters after we focus our purpose and efforts on money, work, pleasure, success and fame. Page 166

We abandon the natural and idealistic for the material. 

We divert our intellectual forces from the advantages which we have to the mechanical. We dismiss the natural and altruistic altogether and struggle in frenzy for the artificialities of our complex civilization. 

You may prove to yourself from your own life the truth of this contrast. The retrospect saddens you over benefits which you threw away. You had a fine conception of happiness and your intellectual and ethical standards were exalted. You had aesthetic principles and objectives. Some of your ambitions and wishes were, of course, fancies of inexperience; but in the main, as you see now, they were full of practical promise.

Everybody else looks back with regret. He is sorry that he ceased to appreciate the spirit of Eternal Youth and the reality, in effect, of enchantment and fairies. 

He has enough common sense to know that he would be as big a fool as Ponce de Leon if he set out on an expedition in quest of the Fountain of Perpetual Youth. 

Nevertheless, he still believes that his physician will be prepared to tell him which science has discovered a pabulum. 

We Must Go Back to the Pipe

We must return, however, to the initial intellectual viewpoint. We must go back to Pan and his pipes, the fairies and nature. Youth is where we left it. 

Nature did not abandon us. We gave up our dreams and ideals for pursuits strictly and absolutely dissolute and mercenary. We abandoned nature. 

Dr. A. G. Pohlman of the faculty and in charge of research for the St. Louis University School of Page 167

Medicine alluded to points which we are now considering in a lecture which he delivered for the purpose of showing that the time has come when the individual must himself take up preventive medicine in frequent consultation with a trusted physician. "Before the time of the great dissemination of knowledge," he said, "and before people at large were possessed of educational facilities, men were more concerned in the mysteries of life and developed a wonderful faith in the supernatural -the metaphysical. The human being of today still possesses this wonderful faith of his forefathers." "But he chooses," he continued, "to allow the faith to be diverted or perverted to material quantities. If this were not so, he would not so eagerly gobble the seductions of the advertising writers. Indeed, his attention has been so attracted to the marvels of the material and the manufactured world that there is little room left in his imagination for the contemplation of apparently abstract problems, even though some of these problems represent the priceless heritages of his people". 

Physician Considers Human Animals 

He drew in this instructive address approximately the same picture of the distinction between the rational and the instinctive. "Nature," he stated, "has produced in animals two different kinds of individuals, necessarily different in structure, in function and psychology, that these two shall survive and beget at least two viable offsprings to replace them when they shall have gone". 

You will remember that I accentuated to you the contention that a new proposal should not be rejected on the fallacious assumption that it may not be worth

Page 168

investigating or trying because it had not been used before. I went further and argued that failure with an idea in several previous experiments did not necessarily indicate demerit. I would emphasize these suggestions to you again.

Laymen who advance new theories which border on the scientific are said to be incompetent on account of their lack of technical knowledge or of this knowledge conjoined with experience. Laymen are looked upon as unskilled theorists. It has been said of laymen who have presumed to encroach on the domain of medicine (in which I include, generically speaking, surgery and dentistry) that their ignorance of anatomy is a serious deficiency. I agree that any one who undertakes to discuss the human body and its health should inform himself on anatomy. As to the implications regarding inexperience, let me suggest that laymen (having, of course, the respect which they should have for reputable physicians and other scientists), who submit new ideas in good faith, have one great advantage, which is that they are not constrained by the intellectual contraptions of grooved procedure. I take particular pleasure, therefore, in offering the opinions of leading medical authorities in support of the cardinal aspects of my theories. "The animal," Dr. Pohlman continued, "has no choice, but is absolutely governed by instinct and instinctive reactions to the forces with which it comes in contact. Only the human being possesses a capacity for reason, which sits in judgment over the instinctive reactions. This advent of reason, therefore, brings with it an absolutely un-animal type of reaction. Page 169

Instincts in the human being may be stimulated or inhibited at the will of the possessor, and, because of this, environmental conditions which might eliminate any animal are made over to conform with man's desires and requirements. 

Scientist Says He Discovers Culture "The animal exhibits a limited adaptation dependent on the basis of selection, the training and the contact. 

One of the first results of man's ability to reason is found in the development of a culture; in the modification of environment. But presently, because of the consciousness of self, and because of the selfishness which such consciousness brings with it, man tends to overcomplicate the conditions under which he lives to the point that self-survival and self-interest are the paramount issues. "History teaches us of the rise of nations, of their inevitable over-development of environment through worship at the false shrines which do not include a consideration of the past or a reference to the future; of the dominance of doctrines of self-survival and selfglorification, and finally of the workings of an inexorable law which declares such peoples unfit. Races have come, and races have gone, but man as such goes on to the end of time." 

Without flattering myself, I believe that I might have written the foregoing sound wisdom in a lay style, although not so well or thoughtfully as the renowned Dr. Pohlman. Similarly, I might have written the following: "The next component of 'I' is age. How old is 'I'?

Page 170

Is 'I' in the active, constructive, growing period of life?

Is 'I' approaching a balance between destructive and constructive processes? Or is 'I' frankly in the age of decline?"

Please allow me to indulge my vanity in another statement of his which might be mine, which, in fact, like the preceding, expresses my thought in more scientific dignity. "The human being is not a passive machine, and his activity is dependent largely on his training; on his mental, moral and physical education. We do not refer to education the word, but education the fact, and we may regard it not as a universal panacea, but as a series of keys to as many different unpickable locks. Education is a result of training; the result of applied intelligence, perhaps even the result of razorstrop arguments why faces must be washed and teeth brushed. It includes the element of regularity in habits of eating, sleeping, exercise and the like. In connection with the topic under discussion, it implies the care of the machinery of the body. ""How well does "I" keep house in the little mansion that heredity built?' 

Ugh! Dinner Gets on the Nerves 

I should like to quote Dr. Pohlman just once more on a subject which we have been discussing in these pages. "There is no question of the influence of the mind”, said he, "over the body, and there is nothing in suggestive therapeutics which might be construed as an insult. Suggestive therapeutics is merely the name for a reaction which is very imperfectly understood. "But let us remember that the prescription works both ways! You worry, and do not digest your din Page 171

ner; or, you do not digest your dinner, and therefore worry! Many a case of love-at-first-sight has been confused with a biliousness dependent on a change of diet or perhaps a sluffing of the winter underwear. Your general reaction may depend on whether you are apprehensive or unconcerned; because fear, like fatigue, lowers resistance, even local resistance. The expression 'cold feet' is found in many languages." (You cannot fail to notice how accurately this coincides with Dr. 

Crile's assertion concerning the consequences of emotional strain.) "Like heredity", still quoting Dr. Pohlman, “environment is a charming word that makes for conversation. The statement that 'I' is the product of his heredity and his environment may seem to make sense, but does it really mean anything to us? Environment is an arbitrary space, and some regard it as definitely related to the four points of the compass with a prevailing wind as in mah jongg. A moment's reflection, however, will convince almost anyone that environment is like an open orange crate. It has four leaky sides and an equally leaky bottom, and the sky is the limit. Each unit of environment differs from the orange crate adjacent to it, just as each individual is unlike every other individual."

CHAPTER IX. 

Overdose of Suggestive Therapeutics 

Now, I desire to make it clear that I place the same boundaries to suggestive therapeutics or psychiatry as the conservative members of the scientific fraternities. 

I do not approve of it with the trimmings of a fad or Page 172

the talismanic sedateness of a dogmatism of a school of doctrinaires. It is my purpose to be reasonable, and, therefore, consistent with the logical theories and conclusive demonstrations of science. When we consider the power of the mind we too often ignore the interposition of conditions of environment. And as we shall later show in the words of another celebrated scientist, the last environs of environment are beyond the sky, while the nearest circumstances and phases to you are in the place where you now are.

In dealing with the human economy we are dealing with the tangible and intangible in conjunctive existence and co-ordinated functioning. The physical is substantial or material and the casket of the intellectual and spiritual is real, although the spiritual and intellectual forces are incorporeal. We have a jointure of the material and insubstantial, each affecting the other to benefit or detriment. Therefore, the fanciful idea of the harmony and vitality of nature personified by a merryhearted boy with reed pipes and the innate desire for eternal youth is not a fantastic vaporing of the imagination. 

You often observe a dog dreaming in his sleep. Every brute with a well-developed instinct doubtless dreams at times. You are aware of the subject of the dog's somnular reverie. His low, jerky, staccato barks and his muscular reflexes give you, with your higher intelligence, a definite interpretation of the picture which is flashing through the animal's brain. Obviously, therefore, certain motion pictures in the brain are within the range of strictly instinctive and bodily interest. Page 173

Dog, Too, Likes a Square Meal 

The dog's idea of Pan is restricted to his instinctive sensations. He simply conforms to nature's provisions and regulations as faithfully as possible, and his happiness is commensurate with the sating of his animal craving. Not looking as far ahead as death, the idea of eternal youth never occurs to him. He is not concerned about the span of his life beyond the passing minute, but concentrates such thinking as he does on his comfort. You may smile, reflecting that a number of humans live by instinct rather than by intelligence; I hope that you do, for this is one conclusion to which I am driving. I promise you a broader smile after a while.

Man's depiction of nature as a carefree boy with a rustic flute and his sanguine hope to prolong life in the spirit and health of youth are emanations from the affinity of the material and intellectual. They are dream-hopes of the higher intelligence, and scientists are engaged in research in efforts to verify them, if possible.

CHAPTER X. 

Commenting on extraordinary assertions by Mr. 

Henry Rhodes to the British Association of Chemists at the annual convention in Toronto, Canada, a Paris edition of the New York Herald broadly alluded to the permanency of the human body. "Mr. Rhodes", said the editorial, "declared that if experiments made at the University of Toronto yield the eventual results which are expected, man may 'perpetually rejuvenate his body and arrest its decay', in other words, live forever. At 

Toronto a professor has promised that science will solve Page 174

the mystery of the artificial production of protoplasm, the primal substance of life. This would mean physical immortality”.

Scientifically, it would be impossible to prove the physical immortality of man, and consequently I am unwilling to declare that we could live forever. As there is no termination to forever, science could not maintain that the living body of man is immortal, as the proof could not be given until the end of forever, a state to which there is no end. Reproduction of life is more an incentive than an objective; could we artificiate human life, which is unlikely, we should almost certainly deplore the arrival of a vitalized, laboratorymade automaton which could (and would) be manufactured synthetically on a plan of mass production. 

How the New Man Is Developed 

The principal and conspicuous point is not whether physical man can live forever, but that he can "perpetually rejuvenate his body and arrest decay". Undoubtedly, man can live much longer than he does on the average, and in better health, having always the spirit of youth. The object which I have in mind is for each of us to follow the advice of the best physicians and try out preventive medicine on our own account and adhere to the obvious requirements of nature, by means of which each of us will have a much greater life expectancy, an expectancy ultimately far beyond the present normal span - an expectancy he cannot see how far protracted, and necessarily, therefore, living much better. "It has been abundantly demonstrated", said Prof. 

F. A. Crew of Edinburgh University at a meeting of

Page 175

the Social Hygiene School in Cambridge, England, in August, 1929, "that life can, and does, continue without ceasing. It is possible to take a fat worm and by repeated processes of starvation keep it alive twenty times longer than it would have lived in the ordinary way. "Man knows the causes of ageing, the cause of the loss of the proteins, and, when he knows the methods by which that loss may be replaced, he may prolong his life indefinitely". He added that, "as knowledge increases, as increase it must, so also will man's power over his physical environment and over the mechanism that is himself". Further, he stated that "science, as well as religion, affirms that in the future mankind may, if it be so desired, not only remain permanently useful, but also may live forever".

I would remind you again at this juncture of Dr. 

Fisk's definition, which I have previously quoted, that "life is an internal response to external conditions", and his statement that there is "a continuous adjustment of internal relations to external conditions". That is, life is man, time and his environment; and life and time are within man, and time and environment are outside of him. There is the nature inside of man and the nature without. You should keep these principles in your thoughts when giving attention to the subject of prolonging life by the natural means of preservation, deageing and rejuvenation. 

Theory That Matter Is Renewed

Along the line of these conjectural investigations into the possible perpetuity of life and man, Dr. Richard Page 176

C. Tolman of the California Institute of Technology in Pasadena, exhibited mathematical models at the meeting of the National Academy of Sciences in Yale University, on Nov. 8, 1931, to suggest a new theory that the universe contracts and expands in cycles and can or may endure indefinitely. His concept is radical, in that it contraverts the theory of Jeans and Eddington that the universe would expire eventually in "heat death" by the exhaustion of energy.

The Tolman theory of the processive reconstruction of matter from energy is the latest in speculative inquiry. The Jeans and Eddington prediction that the universe would finally go to pieces by entropy was based on the second law in classical physics, which is that energy can flow only from a higher or more intense state of availability to a lower or less intense state, and finally all energy would be dispersed. The Tolman concept disregards the theory of the conservation of energy, if it does not really refute and displace it. Instead of conservation of energy, the Tolman concept is of a periodic replacement of energy and matter by reciprocal activity, with a perpetual balance or interbalance by which equilibrium is or will be maintained perpetually in the universe. Instead of conservation of energy there is reconstruction of energy and matter. 

Dr. Tolman supposes that the universe may carry on a deageing and rejuvenating process whereby energy is renewed and matter restored, and, instead of a conservation of energy leading on to decay after the lapse of millions of years, the universe goes on alternating matter to energy and energy to matter and holding a normal equilibrium of stability. This theory, relating Page 177

to inanimate matter, is similar to that which I propounded some time ago with regard to human substance and the prolongation of human life. 

Span of Life Can Be Extended 

To what possible ultimate extent in time the life of men might be elongated, nobody could foretell. But 

I am sure that you agree with me that it is plainly reasonable that the life-span can be pushed onward many years simply by our co-operating with nature. This anyone can do without discommoding himself or revolutionizing his living mode.

The dreams and hopes within us which, from the dawn of reasoning, are associated with the great natural affinity, do not come true up to our expectations. Yet they are more than vanishing rings of fancy. It is partly because we let them drift away that we do not realize more from our longings and ideals. If, with the passing of youth, we expel the spirit of youth, we accelerate the process of ageing, for we loosen the determination of will without which it is impossible for us to achieve objectives in health and culture. 

You do not particularly care whether the universe will last forever. You only care that it will last longer than you. When you had youth you desired perpetual youth. But what you wish for now is the health of youth with developed intelligence. You would like to live as long as you would be healthy, useful and happy, and you would like to be healthy, useful and happy as long as you live. You would like to resuscitate in its pristine vigor the spirit which inspired you to revel in delightful day-dreams.

Page 178

No Pabulum to Neutralize Penalty 

Much of your past is gone irrevocably. The same circumstances could not recur. You have changed, and much of your past would be strange and alien to you were it repeated. Experience has weakened or strengthened your character. But the sentiments and ambitions from which your day-dreams emanated are only dormant, and you may have the spirit of youth again if you will only waken it. After all, it is this spirit which is perpetual youth, for it contains the better incentives for long and happy life.

The Fountain of Youth does not exist. There is no panacea, the physicians tell you, for the ills of the flesh. 

But Peter Pan blows his lute to the cosmic melody, and nature is ready to renew your youth and prolong your life if you will only pay the trivial price, which is only conformance to the laws and regulations of nature. 

Better than the Fountain of Youth and better than Perpetual Youth are subject to your wish.

Page 179

A BOOK THREE. 

CHAPTER I. 

S man is an entity in the great solar system, he is subject to the same general laws and principles as all the other beings in this division of the universe. We suppose from what science, especially astronomy and physics, demonstrates to us that the same general laws prevail throughout the whole universe, which, of course, comprises an incalculable number of stellar and planetary groups. But as far as human life is concerned, it is subject to laws and principles of this solar system in particular. We need no graphic description to touch up our reason to the conviction that our residence on the earth, and our close and unbroken contact with the forces and conditions of our own special environment, subject us directly, intimately and vitally to those laws which are distinctively characteristic of our solar system. Naturally, there are specific conditions and principles to the solar system to which we belong which are supplementarily inherent in it, and it follows that they constitute the cosmic phase by which we exist. 

No matter what you surmise as to the origin of the earth and of man, and as to the evolution of the earth and of man, the existence of the solar system is indisputable. Neither can there be a reasonable argument over the existence of man in this division of the universe as an identity in it. Never has a question been Page 181

raised by any philosopher, scientist or theologian as to the inception of man from the slime. We are not wandering in speculation in placing man in the solar system as a unit, but are relying on obvious and incontestible fact.

Jaunt Into Domains of Science 

I have, as you will perceive, sound reason for stating this proposition so definitely, just as I have sound reason for taking you on a ramble through theories of science. You have always connected yourself with the earth, and the earth with the sun and the moon, and you have always thought of yourself as a small living and reasoning identity in the all-comprehensive universe, yet I doubt whether you have fixed yourself as, as it were, a child of the sun.

Man as a being has his being in the solar system. This system is the immediate reality of his environment, and his being, which, though a state, is a reality of the greater reality. If you query, What is Reality? you have the answer in the foregoing; or, What is Being? the preceding brief explanation makes it clear. When we consider the state of health, we must first understand the state in which being exists and the state of being itself, for nature, being and health are rudimentally the same. 

It would not only be illogical and presumptuous for us to try to set up regulations for our mode of living which would be contrary to the laws of nature, but it would also be foolhardy. In our being we are more than in nature; we are substantially of nature. There is no universal force or principle to which the grass and the mule are liable which does not apply to us. Page 182

Our being is wrapped up in the reality of nature and its variform operation. We have the higher intelligence in addition to self-consciousness and instinct, but it dwells in an organ of natural substance. Thus, our intelligence, too, is natural. We have, like the lowerorder animals, the instinctive natural sense, by which in a simple way we realize our individuality in the natural order, and by which we are impelled indeliberately and spontaneously to fulfill the requirements of nature. 

Yet with this higher intelligence we are able, better than any other species of being, to look understandingly into the principles of nature and the concord and rhythm of the universal cosmos.

Short Term Figures Also in Destiny 

We see in our ordinary family, business and personal affairs two sets or series of variation and progression, all tending to some destiny or purpose. Running through our experience are consecutive, although unconnected, matters which we settle or which are settled for us speedily, and others which are indefinitely suspended. The former are the short-term, which may or may not have important bearings on our whole experience; whereas the latter are the large issues which sweep over a long-term, some, in fact, over the whole of our existence.

Similar movements we observe in nature. Some are in the minute or the unit, and some in years or millions of years. Some are concluded quickly, while others extend on indefinitely. Both correspond to the natural order. Singly and jointly they work out a law of average, a law of compensation and harmonious adjustment. Page 183

To the individual, as I have previously said, there may be an element of chance or apparent chance or accident in segregated instances in experience. Nevertheless, in the whole scheme there is no chance, and everything, as well as every being, is submitted to a unison of law and order which is fundamentally cosmic. 

Nature is in us and we are in nature, and not even our higher intelligence is independent of natural influence. We have the sublime gift of will, which is absolutely free and superior in determining sincerity in character, but this alone is exempt from the natural absolutism. At the same time, though the will is per se right of option, nature sometimes sways it in all individuals, and often influences it in most persons. Otherwise, the relation of the will to nature is an interplay of environment on the will and the will on environment. 

The human will is the greatest power in nature, as the human intelligence is the greatest light. 

Society Theoretically Is Aesthetic Beauty and grace abound in all varieties of animal and vegetable life, and as well in material objects. But man is the sole being having culture, or, in other words, intellectual beauty and grace. We have the sense of understanding linked with the sense of the aesthetic, as a result of which we evolved our advanced state of civilization. Society in the mass is the composite of individuals, and civilization is the social complement of individual ideas and endeavors, which stands for our presently best achievement, both in satisfying our natural demands and promoting our cultural ideals. 

Therefore, when we think of the reality of our being we must include in it the intangibles and their effec- Page 184

tiveness. Again, I remind you that our relations are twofold, internal and external. But now I split each of the two in particularizing. The internal are intellectual and physical, and the external, physical, in conjunction with the tangible and intangible products of intelligence, or, in brief, physical and cultural. 

Now, we should have a clear and complete idea of human existence or state of being. We have described and located ourselves in the universal cosmos. Besides knowing who we singly are, we know what and where we are. We have our "place in the sun".

Every condition and force in and about us is real. 

Dreams and ideas are converted into realities, and realities into new dreams and ideas, which latterly evolve into new realities. We use nature for our health and comfort; and our health and comfort for our culture; and our culture for our health and comfort. 

Compliment for Your Intelligence

By self-introspection you prove these facts and deductions. Every thought that I have written here coincides with the knowledge and wisdom which you possess. You do not have to inquire outside yourself and your own environment and experience to exemplify my concept of reality and the state of being; the illustration is plain to you through your own method of reasoning. Your mind sees into itself and perceives all nature roundabout.

Why am I so explicit in discussing reality and the state of being with you? You say, perhaps, that you have always had knowledge satisfactory to you for all practical purposes of each. But have you? I venture

Page 185

to say that you did not delve deeply into these subjects, and I have conveyed to you novel suggestions; it is not that I am more profound than you, but that you have not bothered about reality and being. You are; and the ground is solid and the building safe; and this is as far as you have cared to go. Yet, you see that reality consists of much more than you supposed, and being is much more than existing. If I have failed to impart knowledge to you, I have at least done you good in stirring you to thinking along new lines.

However, I am explicit in treating of these two subjects because I believe that we will be induced to benefit ourselves by understanding them. It is not altogether right, I believe, for us to take reality and being as the most commonplace of all ordinary conditions and remain indifferent to their meaning and importance. 

They are quite commonplace, I admit-as commonplace as life, food and time. It seems to me that they are extraordinarily commonplace; and this is one reason for my attaching so much importance to them. 

Most of our important problems are commonplace, and we make the mistake of deeming them to be commonplace only, instead of important also and in particular. 

We have come to the conclusion that reality embraces the working and products of our intelligence, in addition to living and inanimate objects, and, in the finality, that even the intangibles are realistic in their effectiveness. There is correlative progress between the real and immaterial, and we may say, in a sense, that nothing is unreal except dreams momentarily. Page 186

Indications as to Human Possibilities Concerning being, there is the state, which is body, life and intelligence, with, besides, the conditions and forces in environment. Thus, each of us is a conjunctive identity in the cosmos, an intelligent identity, with specific place and relationship in our solar system. 

I have striven to impress upon you by exemplification almost purely personal and natural the concept of the exact status of man in the scope of the cosmos. 

Whether we care or not about the gradations in our progress, we should know precisely what we are in nature and the counter-influence between us and the cosmic forces. With this thorough knowledge, we should be equipped to use our advantages and opportunities to better result. It is not enough to see with our eyes. 

It is insight that carries us onward. 

CHAPTER II. 

You are in the involuntary habit of regarding yourself as the same individual which you were two, five, ten, twenty years ago. A friend whom you have not seen for a long time remarks, "Why, you haven't changed a whit! You're the same fellow that you were twenty years ago. I'd recognize you anywhere. I suppose that you're the same fellow in disposition that you used to be. Why, I don't believe that you've altered in the least". You reply that you are exactly as you used to be. You say that you not only look the same, but you also are the same in character and temperament. 

Such a greeting and retort are everyday occurrences. 

Those who make these statements believe them. But Page 187

they are decidedly in contrast to fact. I may illustrate this to you clearly by a little incident from real life. 

Countenance Altered by Experience 

A fairly successful man made the acquaintance of a psychologist who had developed a system for applying theory to conditions and persons, and persons and conditions to his theory. The man had been more successful in his profession than with his own affairs; in fact, his talents were exceptional, and those who knew him intimately often were puzzled that he had not risen higher. He had everything in his favor, except, perhaps, opportunity, as far as his friends could see, yet his ability brought opportunity now and then. 

The psychologist thought that this new friend had extraordinary ability. He was so positive of this that he sought the man's opinion and advice. The psychologist was pleased in these conferences to ascertain that his judgment was confirmed, and he concluded to make a study of the subject's career.

The professional man gave the psychologist a sketch of his life, outlining the work that he had done and citing the responsible positions which he had held, and so on, and a printed sketch, written some ten years previously, which contained a halftone cut. "I should like", said the psychologist a few days later, "to try to make our friendship of practical benefit to you and incidentally to me. I would appreciate your giving me, in the strictest confidence, an insight into the secrets of your life. Now, I have compared your photograph in this leaflet with your countenance; while you were concentrated on your work I have sat oppo- Page 188

site to you and made an analysis of your countenance today with your features in your picture, which, you say, was made ten years ago. "Now, I find, and my chief assistant agrees with me, that you have undergone several radical changes in this time. The photograph, that is, the cut, shows you to have been imaginative and emotional, and consequently to a degree sentimental, whereas our study of your features at the present time indicates a positive subordination of the emotions to the will. We observe virtually a complete change in character and temperament. You are an entirely different person. Our observation of your deportment bears out this conclusion". 

Growth in Size, If Nothing Else "You are correct", the professional man replied. "Well", continued the psychologist, "I am greatly interested in such a transition. I am interested scientifically; and I believe that I could, if you took me into your confidence, offer suggestions which might be of much value to you. For such a radical change to have taken place there must have been extraordinary causes”. 

To the average person, unacccustomed to looking into details, the professional man looked the same as ten years previously. He was, in all respects, apparently the same person that he always had been. He seemed the same to his relatives and friends. But the inquisitive expert perceived to certainty that in the decade there had been great change both in the body and the intellect.

We believe that we simply grow in size and in years. 

We go to school and acquire knowledge, and go to Page 189

work and acquire skill, and we get experience and certain convictions. But we believe that we are the same. 

Oh, yes! there are a few minor markings in our features and occupational traits in our bearing, and perchance a few gray hairs. Nevertheless, we are unaltered in appearance and in the main are the same in body, intellect, temperament and character. 

But we are erroneous in this belief. On reflection we cannot help concluding that change in every individual is as natural as the flight of time and the movement through experience. It would be impossible for any one to remain the same. Necessarily, there would be changes in the body and in the mind. 

You are Surprised at Yourself 

To you the changes which have taken place in you may be imperceptible. Most of them probably are.

They are imperceptible because they were gradual. If you had had a great crisis, you might have noticed particular items in change. However, in the ordinary course of events the changes in you would go on unnoticed by you and also by your near relatives and associates. 

Those near to you, with approximately the same environment as you, would not be more likely to notice changes in you than you would in them.

Looking back into the past, it would seem different from the present. Of course, you say, the environment then was different, and you were younger. 

But this was a change in things, in conditions and circumstances. 

You may note small changes in your likes and views, yet you believe that you are the same now as then. If you would or could analyze yourself as the psychologist Page 190 1

did his friend, you would have no doubt whatever that you changed in important ways.

To yourself you are an identity which always has been the same identity and always will be. To relatives and friends who have known you for a long time you continue to be the same identity. It is because your identity has not changed, and will not, that you unconsciously deduce that you have not changed. You are still you, but you are a much different you than you were twenty or ten years ago, or last year, or last week.

Every individual goes through an evolutionary process of his own from the cradle to the grave. This evolution is his life in its productive form, or, if you will, its efficient form; it is his life in growth and in performance. This evolution is his making. It is effective in his intelligence and on his body and in his relationship to his external environment.

CHAPTER III. 

Assets Which Could Be Used 

You were born with specific intellectual and physical assets and in an appearance which was the aspect of your identity. You had innate endowments, which, relatively to other individuals, were advantages or deficiencies. You were born with physical and intellectual capacities characteristic of your individuality. 

The range of your intellectual and physical possibilities in after-years was determined largely by your hereditary advantages and deficiencies taken together. I mean that there is, or works out, a balance in your advantages and deficiencies; that is, the deficiencies mitigate against your advantages, potentially, at least, and Page 191

the advantages minimize the deficiencies. Anomalously, it not infrequently happens in the course of an individual's growth that the deficiencies prove helpful toward success. The principle is, however, that individual capacity is shown from birth in the inborn characteristics. 

Your own parents could not decipher your gifts. The physician could tell them the principal facts concerning the physical organs, and in a comparatively short time could report reliably as to the state of your body. This would represent the limit of the knowledge which they could have concerning you. All were aware, nevertheless, that you had advantages and deficiencies which would be disclosed in time.

Your assets were strengthened during the years when you were helpless. And within a short time after your birth you began to feel the influence of environment. 

The primary forces which were to be the most important factors, with your will and spirit, throughout your existence, were assertive in you, and you were at once affected by short and long term impelments of both. 

We fail to consider adequately the importance of environment in the formative years. As the babe is incapable of reasoning, we do not give much thought to questions of environment for him. We distinguish between one raised in a mansion and another raised in a tenement, between one having the advantages of money and refinement and the other having nothing, perhaps not even nourishing food, or between the offspring of a gangster and that of a respected citizen; otherwise, we do not think of environment in connection with the several years of infancy.

Page 192

Yes! You Have Whimsicalities

You acquired many habits and idiosyncracies from environment. You took many mannerisms from your parents. You got used to certain foods. Certain attentions from your parents had something to do toward developing your temperament. Your parents, realizing some of their own assets and deficiencies, took care to improve the former and fill in the latter. 

You received the intellectual effects of environment first of all in the home and afterwards in school and among your playmates. This second period was the formative for your intellectual faculties. Your environment expanded and its effects were larger and greater. Moreover, you were acting on your own volition, making decisions for yourself. 

You were in the state of being. You were no longer just an animal, merely existing and growing; you were a human being with identity and consciousness. You had come into your own. You were cognizant of responsibility, as well as of life. You would have counsel and guidance some time longer, but you were thinking and determining for yourself. You were using judgment and will. You were modifying your environment in one or another instance. You were a being in the larger sense that there was in your state of being the higher human intelligence in continual action. 

I do not propose to discuss the comparative influence of heredity and environment. We are apt to overestimate or underestimate one or the other. I am alluding Page 193

to them only to a sufficient extent to describe our growth with them. Heredity represents the internal relations in you, environment the external. 

You Manage to Keep One Resource

It is quite obvious to you that you must undergo physical, intellectual and spiritual change in the course of your time and experience. You keep your identity, but both you and your environment go on changing, especially you. Even if your environment did not change, you would. With you the old adage holds "not to go forward is to go backward". You change for better or for worse. 

Going through the years you pass through an evolution, an evolution not only of growth in time, body and intellect, but an evolution of change. Notwithstanding that you preserve your identity (which, of course, is in accordance with plain reason), you change in a gradual progression. One may not improve in character, intelligence or health, but he will go forward in all three and change in each. This evolution is normal to every individual.

Your advantages may become deficiencies, your deficiencies advantages, or both may grow equally and maintain their corresponding balance or difference to the last. To an individual his advantages may prove to be detrimental in the environment in which he is placed, or in the change of environment his deficiencies may be beneficial to him. Experience alone, with his response and reaction to conditions and problems, can Page 194

determine an individual's development or evolution, but only in the finality. A life is incomplete until it is finished, and individual evolution continues all through life.

One who leads a simple life, and whose environment is simple, has a simple experience, and his evolution is along a simple course. Change is a trivial matter with him; there is little or none, either in himself or his environment. But he grows in character, intelligence and time in a quantity of practically unvarying experience. His development is along simple lines, yet it goes on. He is a different person at 50 than he was at 

30, 20 or 15.

There are some who lead a simple life in a simple environment and who undergo hard trials. For example, take the common laborer. Fluctuations in employment and income, a difficult struggle, and family sickness and death, are among numerous troubles which tend to bring out either the worst or the best in him. 

Environment Always a Plausible Excuse

Many have agreeable environment. Many are in partial harmony with their environment. Environment jars on many. Many are in conflict with environment. 

The principal psychological divisions are the two which include those in harmony with environment and those out of harmony with it.

The individual evolution is an advancement or digression, a betterment or deterioration, but nevertheless a Page 195

growth, in which the elements are time and the interplay between internal and external relations. Some persons weaken in force of character and stability of intellect, while others strengthen in both, in the evolutionary process. Some invigorate their health, while others debilitate it. The state of being lies in going through this human evolution either properly or improperly; anyhow, in going through it. 

I have in mind two striking instances on the borderland of my own environment which illustrate individual evolution under tranquil and agitated conditions. 

One life is like a smiling day. The other is a storm center. The former is that of a professional woman and the latter that of a professional man.

The domestic environment of the woman was refined and serene. She grew from this environment into a profession which blended perfectly into it. She bloomed in her environment. The family troubles and sorrows were normal to the environment. The whole situation was ideal. She was as sunny as her environment. Every circumstance was harmonious. She achieved remarkable success and a fair reputation.

Washington and Lincoln Take Up Arms 

The man had had no domestic environment from his childhood, and in a general way it may be said that he had never had a home. He entered a profession for which he was adapted. This environment was electrical; it was charged with great tension; it was one in which very few persons could work with intellectual poise, unless they made up their minds to "take things as they come and make the most of them". But the ablest

Page 196

and most ambitious would not be so easy on themselves or their surroundings. Both he and his environment were always in discord, and, although he was consistently successful the environment did not redound to his personal profit. However, measured in terms of great achievement, and in the evolutionary development of character, intellect and ability to the utmost, the oppositional struggle was harmonious as to purpose. 

Among the great figures in history who are generally mentioned as examples of this type are Washington and Lincoln. Neither was often in accord with his environment, yet their work and problems called for the special qualities which they displayed. 

Circumstances often, perchance generally, appear to go through almost methodical repetition day after day.

This is so because there is more or less order in our rule of living. We arise at the same time day after day, go to work at the same time, do the same work, go home at the same time, eat at the same time, and pass the evening much the same. Except for events out of the ordinary, there is no noticeable change with us. 

Change Goes On With Time 

But change does go on. Even in circumstances which seem to be invariable there is some change. 

Though they may be the same over and over again, there is change in us and in our internal and external conditions. Were there no other change than that of time, it would be change. Time alone would and does cause us to change. I might go farther and say that we should change even if circumstances were always the same, for the very recurrence of the same thing and the very repe Page 197

tition of similar thoughts and actions would cause change in us. This last view may appear to be paradoxical; yet it is not. As I have indicated previously, monotony and routine have pronounced effects on the human system.

You have, I am sure, had the reality of change impressed on you forcibly by contrasts in your own experience. You have, for instance, mused now and then of a friendship of long ago, or certain work which was interesting, or a pastime or diversion which was especially pleasing. You remember each in a delightful glamour. If you have tried to revive the friendship, you have found a repulsive coldness. In the work or diversion, if you took either up again, you would feel that the joy was solely in the glamour retained in memory, for neither would be gratifying in actuality. You had changed.

You are convinced by events in your own life that you have changed not only by the multiplication of years, but also by the conglomeration of ordinary and exceptional happenings. You can see in yourself much change between the present and five, ten or twenty years in the past. However, the change in yourself from one week or day to the next is not so obvious; therefore, you may have an idea that change is periodical, instead of continuous. One experience, occurring in an instant, or even a thought, a word, a gesture, may cause a radical change in one; or radical change may unfold from common experience by a gradual process. 

Evolution for the Average Man

Unquestionably, you undergo an evolution which affects your whole being. Your knowledge, your cul Page 198

ture, your morals, your whole character are shaped by time and experience, in which you are conforming external relations to your judgment or will, or in which you are "following the line of least resistance", or in which you mix decision and compromise. Whatever your character is, strong or weak, and whatever your principles and ideals are, noble or contemptible, they are products of the evolution in which your innate advantages and deficiencies have coordinated and clashed alternately with the forces and conditions coming up in time and experience, in the mutations and problems of your environment. Every physical and intellectual part of you has been affected by this evolution. 

Look into the quality of your character at present, and compare it with the quality of a date in the past. 

You notice strength in this respect and weakness in that. Make a similar comparison of your ideals and principles. You have gone forward with some, backward with others. How about your knowledge and your culture? And how have you come through the evolution thus far as to health of body and mind? 

Were you to make a very careful investigation of your life from important stage to important stage, you would perceive the actual process of this evolution in yourself. You would see how and why you improved in several respects and how you had retrograded in other directions. You would witness your personal evolution in a connected line.

In like manner you could chart in your mind your physical progress or retrogression. You would see how indifference to your health, and possibly dissipation and excesses, had caused wear and tear which could have Page 199

been prevented. You would see with understanding how your body went through the evolution up to this hour.

CHAPTER IV. 

Age or Youth Is Individual Question 

The ageing process and its results would be apparent to you in this evolutionary inquisition. It would be clear to you whether you grew old in body and mind with the current of time, or you conserved your vigor of body and your intellectual sharpness and optimism. 

You would realize whether you were in a moribund state as a consequence of reckless living and working, or now, in the prime of life, you are fit to be most efficient in your business or profession, as you should be, and to reap the full rewards of knowledge and culture. 

Ageing or deageing runs along with changes in body and mind and character in the evolution through which each of us is obliged to pass. We grow old or stay young, in accordance with our wishes and efforts, with years and experience. As we form character in the personal evolution, so we improve or decline physically. 

Our general state as to age and health depends naturally on the judgment which we have employed in managing our internal relations. It is the personal evolution in each of us that is of greater importance than the changes which took place sequentially outside of us. 

Evolution does not necessarily imply a drifting into decay of body or mind. Inasmuch as it should naturally broaden and better the intellect, and generally does, there are many reasons why it should strengthen and rejuvenate the body. The mind functions in a Page 200

mass of substance, and, therefore, the brain is as material as the body; and, inasmuch as the intellect improves with age, the body should become more vigorous by use.

While it is a fact that ageing begins at birth, it is also a fact that ageing is counteracted during the early years of life by the scrupulous care which parents take of children's health. During this stage in life, the effects of time are practically overcome, and, except that in childhood a person is a few years older than in infancy, he is physically younger. The evolution so far has been altogether constructive, as, wherever natural process has been involved, every effort has been exerted toward assisting it. At no moment, in a wellguided family, is the natural law of health in the child disregarded; on the contrary, the parental solicitude shows in performance full appreciation of the necessity of doing for the child what nature requires. 

Two Stages in Human Existence 

In

At the conclusion of infancy and childhood the evolution denotes results equivalent to deageing. Time, which ages us as we count the years, has been used most conscientiously for building up health of body. youth there is no thought whatever of rejuvenation. 

We do not say that a being has grown older in ten or fifteen years; on the contrary, he has become strong and is in condition to live happily and study effectively. 

With the increase of years health has improved, and one starts out in active life, to do the first work, which is to go to school and study, with age at zero, or, I might say, with physical condition at plus in comparison with uncertainty after birth. There has been no ageing up

Page 201

to this time, but a normal and continuous neutralization of it.

Circumstances of living are altered in the next stage. 

Ageing actually begins then, not because time is going by, but because in work and play and in all other activities less attention is given to the matter of health. 

The natural obligations are ignored in favor of absorbing pursuits in work and play. Ageing is not thought of either as a current process or a consequence which will be felt in the future. Throughout this step in the personal evolution some degree of ageing does take place, although the body has resistance as a result of the conservation of energy in the previous stage. The ageing process is not so rigorous as to outbalance the effectiveness of the constructive efforts in the years immediately preceding.

I shall not dwell on the destructive method of living during the years of maturity, when, due to nonconformance with the ordinary demands of nature, most of us ruin our health. Many of us in these days flagrantly antagonize the laws of nature, and we should expect nothing else than the dire and almost overwhelming deterring conditions as to health which all authoritative physicians, notedly Dr. Crile, have described.

I content myself with a passing allusion to the picture which I drew in a previous chapter of the mechanical existence which we are living, in which we are concentrating our interests and efforts exclusively on pleasure and instantaneous success and riches. Because we are leading an artificial life, contemning the favors offered by nature, and misusing and disusing the advantages of Page 202

our complex civilization, we are wrecking body and mind. I have not asked you to take my word alone for this; in fact, I have been very careful to bolster my opinion with the solemn warnings of physicians and scientists of the highest repute.

Furrowed Faces in Life's Summer 

It is in the stages between 28 and 49 that our health breaks down. During these stages ageing proceeds at a terrific rate. Many persons are decrepit at 35, 40 or 

50. The outlook for some is desperate, but there is still a chance for the majority to rebuild themselves physically, on condition, however, that they show respect for the simple laws of nature. 

The progressive physical collapse during these periods is not a consequence of the flight of time. The passing of time represents ageing to us, chiefly because we are accustomed to register age in the number of years lived. As a matter of fact, time is the kindest of the ageing factors, and there is much reason for insisting that we should, taking into account time exclusively, feel that we were only beginning to live and enjoy serenity and happiness at say 50, or even 60.

We pay too much attention in some ways to the piling up of the years and in other ways too little. As far as the years in themselves are an issue, they are of minor importance to our manner of living. Time is very friendly toward us. However, the years are and should be alarming to us if decline of health is coincident with their increase. We must arrive at the viewpoint that we are aged not so much by the flight of time as we are by a devolutionary process which we Page 203

ourselves put into effect. Those among us who will not live consistently to the evolutionary order imposed by nature can only expect physical reverses. 

You are obliged to live through a personal evolution.

Up to the day when you entered college or started to work, you lived in a manner obliging to nature. But you failed in your obligations to nature and yourself from that day to this. The evolution of the average person—and the exceptions are very few-is at odds with the regular harmony in nature. This is why ageing goes on, and not because time hurries by. 

CHAPTER V. 

Responsibility Becomes Personal Charge 

My concept of the proper manner of living in the years when the responsibility of health has been transferred from parents or guardians to ourselves is that there must be a constant balance between the evolution and regularity in nature with the personal evolution. I call this balanced evolution. The laws of nature are not amenable to change, and I would remind you that they are active inside, as well as outside, of each of us. In order to accentuate this undeniable fact to you I went into some detail in showing that we are earthly beings, composed of the same materials as inorganic substance. "Thus far, however," says Dr. Lorande Loss Woodruff, professor of biology in Yale College, in his treatise on "The Origin of Life", "the study of protoplasm has given no reason for abandoning the productive working hypothesis that life phenomena are an expression of a complex interaction of physicochemical laws which Page 204

do not differ fundamentally from the socalled laws operating in the inorganic world". In other words, there is no reason for saying that human life is independent of the natural laws.

I have for years held concerning living matter precisely the same theory which Dr. Tolman recently advanced relative to the longevity of the earth. Speculatively, he eliminates time altogether from the future, as he suggests that the earth may last forever; while time is measured out by the solar system, and the earth is ageing, it is possible, as he supposes, that our planet may last forever. His theory, which I have submitted to you, is that there is an alternation perpetually in progress between energy and matter; and, instead of conservation of energy, a classic principle in physics, he suggests reconstruction of energy. 

Balanced evolution, whereby the human being conforms in his manner of living to the common requirements of nature, is the reproduction of energy in the human body, just as in all nature at large. The accumulation of decay over rehabilitation is stopped by a process of contra-ageing.

Interplay of Equals and Opposites 

The contemplations for promoting balanced evolution, or the maintained equilibrium of prime, are directed toward the equals and opposites of compensations; thus, the constant functioning in unison at equal freedom and obligation of normal exercise, which amplifies normal rest and normal completion of wastes, as well as thoroughly eliminating these putrefactions and obstructions at occurrence; and, on the other hand, of Page 205

normal rest, which amplifies normal exercise and normal completion of balanced supply as wastes occur, which constantly compensates in deriving potential supply from environment as developed wastes are disposed of thereunto.

The principle underlying my concept is that in balanced evolution or the maintenance of equilibrium of prime it is necessary to equalize exercise and rest and thus produce and preserve a mutually constructive interplay between energy and matter. The natural action in a state of balanced evolution results in a constant equilibrium of prime in the human economy, preventing physical deterioration and obviating disease. 

As an exemplification of the reasonableness of this theory you may consider the success with which, in a large majority of individuals, the ageing process is counteracted, and physical health and vigor are gradually built up, during the earlier years of one's life. I brought this well-known fact to your special attention in a preceding chapter in the hope of making the idea of balanced evolution clear to you.
