A business concern figures depreciation on its property at so much per cent a year. It spends a certain sum periodically for repair and rebuilding; otherwise, it has to go out of business in a few years. In effect, I simply recommend to you a plan for taking care of physical depreciation of the human body.

Depreciation and Maintenance in Organism 

Our bodies must charge off depreciation. We think that we are rebuilding and renovating them with food and rest, taken in the way that suits our convenience Page 206

or follows our traditions; but along in the 60's or 70's we find that what we were doing was only temporary maintenance of a makeshift sort. Why this is so we may find from a consideration of the cell-structure of the human body.

The unit of life of which the human cell is composed is the protoplasm. "Chemical analysis," says Dr. Woodruff in "The Origin of Life", "shows that protoplasm is a colloidal complex comprising chiefly the elements carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulphur, phosphorus, potassium, calcium, sodium, chlorine, iron and magnesium—all of which are commonly found in the inorganic world. Indeed, there is no chemical element present which is peculiar to living matter". He continues that "the structural units of all organisms are microscopic masses of protoplasm, called cells". The chemical combinations which are distinctly characteristic are "the proteins, carbohydrates and fats, and of them the proteins are the most significant because they are universally present as a part of all living matter and form quantitatively its chief organic constituent”. 

CHAPTER VI. 

When Schwann and Schleiden established the fact of cell-structure, a century ago, they did away with the old idea of the body as a mere collection of assorted organs. They showed that the body is composed of an almost inconceivable number of living organisms, or cells, and we have learned of the manner in which these cells are built up and are depleted. 

In his authoritative work, "The Cell", Dr. Oscar 

Hertwig describes the formative activity of the cell- Page 207

structure, and its relation to metabolism, or the process of transforming dead food into living matter. 

Marvel of Vitality—the Cell "Each living cell," he says "exhibits the phenomena of metabolism; it absorbs nutrient material, which it elaborates, retaining certain portions of it, whilst it rejects others; it resembles a small chemical laboratory, for the most varying chemical processes are almost continually taking place in it, by means of which substances of complex molecular structure are on the one hand being formed, and on the other are being broken down again. The more intense the vitality of the cell, the more considerable are these processes of destruction and reconstruction, the latter keeping pace with the former * * *” "In the reconstruction of living substances, or progressive metabolism," he continues, "new material is taken up from outside to replace that which has been used up; these substances become incorporated and transformed into new chemical combinations * * *. 

Each cell respires; that is, it is essential to it to take up oxygen from its environment, by means of which it oxidizes the carbohydrates and albuminous substances of its own body, and produces carbon dioxide and water ***. The gases, fluids and solid substances introduced as food, and through respiration, compose the varying raw materials which are elaborated in the chemical workshop of the cell, and which are converted into a large number of substances, the most important being carbohydrates, fats, proteids and their numerous compounds". Page 208

"The vehicle of life manifestations," quoting Dr. 

Woodruff again, "is thus clearly a physicochemical complex and one of its most conspicuous and fundamental phenomena is what has been described as 'energy traffic' or the function of trading in energy. This consists in appropriating energy from the environment, storing it in a state of higher potential and later expending it in the kinetic form. The liability of many of the molecular aggregates of protoplasm, that is, their tendency to change in composition toward greater or less complexity, coinciding with deoxidative and oxidative processes, is an important characteristic and one which endows the protoplasmic system as a whole with its perennial plasticity-its peculiar proneness to change its composition under the stimulus of slight changes in the energy-equilibrium between itself and its surroundings". 

No Encouragement for Sloth and Gluttony

Generally speaking, rest and nourishment build up cell-structure, and exertion eliminates waste cell-structure. But those who rest much and eat much do not attain longer and better life than others; nor do those who work hardest and longest, thus most certainly getting rid of waste, live longer than others.

This is because prolonged exertion, not rightly timed in its relation to rest, causes over-depletion of the bodily cells, with the final result of disintegrating the embedded cell-structure by eruption. It is equally true that rest, too much prolonged, will cause an insufficient amount of structural waste to be thrown off through the circulatory and respiratory systems.

Page 209

The waste of cell-structure not normally removed is partially sealed in, resulting in the disintegration of embedded cell-structure through collapse. This sealedin waste, through a gradual process of semidecay, becomes poisonous, though not virulently so, to the organs and tissues of which they are part. The overrester may sometimes live longer than the over-worker, but few physicians would care to argue that he lives better.

It is interesting to read, in recent correspondence from Moscow, that the Russian biologist, Tushnoff, has reached practically this same conclusion in his efforts to increase the span of human life, for which he has fixed a definite objective of 150 years. He has concluded, from 20 years of experience, that to stop the advance of age the deterioration of cells must be stopped, and the poison thus infused into the system must be removed. His proposed method of changing and regulating the cell-structures is by chemical products, taken internally or by injection. 

Sorry to Have to Mention Work 

I am proposing, on the other hand, that we find and practice in our exercise, eating and rest the way that will put nature to work on the physics and chemistry of our bodily rebuilding. I am proposing that we alternate activity and rest more frequently, with recurrent brief exercise periods to remove the naturally created wastes of embedded cell-structures, and with frequent nourishment and short rest periods, to give just enough rebuilding, and not too much, after every period of activity.

Page 210

"The properties of matter and the course of cosmic evolution," Dr. Woodruff states in his excellent treatise, "are intimately related to the structure of the living being and to its activities. Indeed, 'the whole evolutionary process, both cosmic and organic, is one, and the biologist may now rightly regard the universe in its very essence as biocentric'." As to the cells, he says "the bodies of all higher animals and plants are congeries of these protoplasmic units, while many of the simpler forms of life consist of but one". He deduces that "a fundamental characteristic of organisms as we know them today is not only that the material basis of their activities which we call life is the somewhat protean, though no less real, protoplasm, but also that this physical basis is individualized into structural moieties, the cells".

You are invited, therefore, to consider yourself as being a conglomeration of single, yet coherent cells, or, as Dr. Woodruff says, you are made up of "congeries of these protoplasmic units". Your structure in organs and tissues is cellular, with the protoplasm as the evidence of vitality. 

The mechanical frame and structure of man are such that the cellular structuration appears in a great complexity and diversity of organic forms. Compared to the simplest specimens of life with but one protoplasmic unit, the human body is, without doubt, a marvelous entity consisting of an uncountable number of cells. Here is not only a body built up of cells, each a constructive laboratory according to Dr. Hertwig's description, but of different bodily organs built of cells, the organs being not only different in shape, in texture, Page 211

in consistency and size, but different also in their functioning. 

Love Ditties are Protoplasmic Harmony

The heart, which pumps the blood through the system, and the lungs, kidney, liver, stomach and brain, each performing specific biochemical functions, are formations from cells. As it were, the whole body is equivalent to a large cell. Exactly how the protoplasmic action brings about various cellular results with practically the same materials, or with the limited number of materials, which we have named, science cannot explain, but research indicates that the variations in products are the results of slight chemical variations in the processive activities of the life-units. 

Just as you are asked to consider your body to be an accretion of cells, you are requested to regard your heart as an organ composed of cells, and your lungs, liver, kidney, stomach, brain, and so on. 

The organs are parts of the human machine which performs certain works and produce predetermined results. But each is composed of cells. Would you not, therefore, look upon these organs and the several components of the body as objective cellular achievements? They are working parts of the body, but they and the body as a whole consist of cells. Rather than think of the pulse and respiration as the breach between life and death we must think of the cells thus.

The cause of life lies in the cells, with the protoplasm at the base. The organs and tissues are results of cellular structuration; therefore, they are products, notwithstanding that they function as parts of the human Page 212

machine. When we think of life or death, we must think of cell construction and expansion or cell contraction and decay. We grow or keep in sound physical condition by cellular activity, or we rot away by destruction of the cells.

CHAPTER VII. 

The materials for cellular action, which is, of course, biochemical, are taken in as food and air. Stated scientifically, they are derived from environment. They are introduced into the stomach through the mouth, and into the lungs through the nose, and into the whole body through the pores. Action and reaction in the cells, and among them, bring about construction and repulsion not only from food and air, but also from the general habits of the individual; for instance, there is action from rest, but reaction from excess of it, and action from exercise, but reaction from too much exercise; thus, there is action from normal rest and normal exercise, but reaction from fatigue. 

Cells as Opportunistic as Politicians

The cells, which Dr. Hertwig pictures as chemical workshops, instinctively or naturally accept or reject the materials which are received. They instinctively accept those which are necessary to produce specific results, and reject those which are not adapted to the particular purpose. They also reject materials which come to them which would have destructive results. 

While they have vital instinct, accepting and rejecting what they want and do not want, their selection is restricted to the materials or foods which are presented to them. Their instinct of discrimination is Page 213

opportunistic only. First and last they rely chiefly on the higher intelligence of the human individual to supply the materials which are requisite to constructive action and reaction and interplay, and they rely on the individual to regulate his habits and behavior to the extent which these contribute to constructive cellular purpose.

I am impressing on you the fact that the cells cannot select the materials needed, but are compelled to do the best they can in current conditions with the kinds of materials which you purvey, and to do the best they can, according to their instinct, with the current actions or reactions to your manner of living. You are aware by your natural instinct that you are bound to adhere to ordinary rules of nature, and by your higher intelligence you know what materials the cells need in order to carry on their operations properly and efficiently.

When the cells obtain the desired nourishment their action is constructive in result, while their rejection of what they do not particularly need for the action of the moment is a normal and not a destructive reaction. 

On the other hand, when the nourishment is not consistent with the momentary requirements, the reaction which takes place in the manner of repulsion has destructive effects on the cells themselves. It is the same with our rest and exercise; by following a natural course with each, the results are constructive, whereas excess of either is destructive to the cells. 

Heart Disease Is Something Else 

I do not find it imperative to say that some lesions are strictly organic. For example, a person may have Page 214

heart disease, apart from cellular impairment. Otherwise, you should not think of your heart, brain, lungs, and so on, but of the cells. Your health and youth are the outward manifestation of the stability of the cell structure and its normal functioning in the constructive process.

In these preceding pages we have cited the materials of which the protoplasm consists and explained the constructive process or evolution in cell formation and expansion. We see that the body is a conglomeration of single, yet coherent cells, and that each of the organs and tissues is made of them. Biochemical variations, as we have noted, are characteristic of the diversities in the different organs, shapes and functions. We have observed that the proper nourishment for the cells is the life-building nutriment, whereas the improper in quality or quantity is the destructive. Therefore, it is quite plain that we are able to preserve our health of mind and body and counteract the ageing tendency by very simple measures. 

You would not care to live the hundred years promised to you by Mahatma Gandhi if you had to live them according to his program, although it is apparently suitable to him and his circumstances. His longevity system probably would unbalance your mind. It is too hard a program for any but an eccentric. However, you could elaborate a plan which would fit your idiosyncracies and living circumstances. 

Gandhi's Wheel and the Slot Machine

The Indian leader's day consists of five hours of sleep, one hour of prayer, one hour of eating, two hours of Page 215

meditation, one hour of spinning, and fourteen hours of work. This schedule he has ascertained is suited to his manner of living. To you, however, it is ridiculous. 

You might not believe it to be absurd on your part to spin a wheel of fortune or turn the crank of a chance slot-machine, or something like this for an hour in a day, but you would feel insulted were you asked to ply the spinning wheel. Your social environment is different from Gandhi's. He in his habits goes to one extreme, while you, in yours, go to another. Such is life!

The principal merit in the Gandhi program is that of restfulness. The mahatma plans his day in work and relaxation so that there is somewhat of a balance between exercise and rest, with periodical alternation of the two. You would not have to live on goat's milk and almond paste, or walk about the streets in a loincloth, in order to divide your day so as to accomplish this end.

The state of your health is a result of the condition of your general cellular structure. Obviously, the preservation of the cell-structure at prime condition means the opportunity of ridding the body of waste and obtaining a compensating supply.

It is, I believe, clear to you from consideration of the cell-structure as the active secret of human life that the process is one of continuous construction of selfreproducing cells and reconstruction of embedded cells. 

As a self-reproducing cell builds up by vitality from a number of protoplasmic units it naturally operates into the next stage, which is that of self-dividing into two complete cells; and this building and self-division go Page 216

on naturally and constantly in general and in particular with instinctive objectives. Not only does this building-up process persist unceasingly, but it is also accompanied by a reconstructive process of the organ and tissue cells, which is the attempt of the active principle to resist and overcome depletion and collapse of cell structure.

Vital Principle Is Constructive 

We see the principle of life active in expanding the units of life, that is, making new structures containing protoplasmic units, and at the same time striving to prevent decay in the organ and tissue cells already existing and functioning. In these cells and the operation of the life principle we have the source and mainstay of health. While the functioning is normal and efficient, and the results naturally adequate, there must, of course, be a constant prime in the individual's state of health.

The cells in their constructive and protective functioning necessarily require certain materials which contain the chemicals essential for the projected results. 

These materials are required in a naturally fixed quantity and quality, and they are introduced, as I have said, through the mouth, nose and pores.

The digestive apparatus, which receives the food materials, is the primary and principal dependence of the cells for their operations. The materials undergo in the digestive apparatus a preparatory treatment prior to their transmission to the small chemical workshops, which Dr. Hertwig calls the cells.

Physiology distinguishes the upper and lower diges- Page 217

tive tracts—the upper tract, the stomach and passages leading to it; the lower tract, the intestinal system, leading from the somach onward. The two tracts assimilate different substances-the upper tract principally food liquids, protein, water-soluble vitamins; the lower tract, principally food fats or concentrated carbohydrates, simple carbohydrates and fat soluble vitamins.

If the upper and lower digestive tracts can be kept busy alternately, and can rest alternately, we have obtained a balance of internal activity. To get the upper and lower tracts to function in this manner, we shall have to choose, as far as we can, the best adapted food substances, and we shall have to observe the periodicity of the digestive operations.

The Expected-Spasms of the Stomach The information we need on this latter point has been available only a short time. Dr. Anton Julius

Carlson, professor of physiology in the University of Chicago, an authority on the digestive functions and hunger control, has made a study of the rhythmic action of the stomach in human beings and animals. He has found that the human stomach has a rhythmic action of its own, with alternate periods of spasm or contraction, lasting from a half hour to an hour and a half, and periods of rest.

The phenomenon is shown in all animals; in humans it is observed even from the prematurely born to the aged. The motor nerves are not involved; therefore, it is not nerve action. As might be expected, the action of the sensitory nerves is noted prior to indication of hunger. The whole digestive tract is a machine, Page 218

a machine within the big human machine, and it would have to function in its several parts according to some kind of rule or regularity. Dr. Carlson found a rhythm in the stomach, corresponding, in its special way, to rhythm in pulsation and rhythm in breathing. 

The heart, liver and other internal organs co-ordinate with the lower digestive tract, being most active when it is at exercise, and being most nearly at rest when the lower tract is resting. The functioning of the stomach plays probably the most important part, directly, in the interaction or co-ordination with the life principle in the cells.

The natural alternation which we must establish, therefore, is that, while the person eats and relaxes, the upper digestive tract shall be occupied in exercise and the balance of the body shall be at rest. Before eating and resting the lower digestive tract must have been emptied of completed wastes through natural evacuation. 

Putting the Food Away

During the period of bodily exercise, the lower digestive tract works, in assimilating the necessary energyyielding substances, and the upper tract is at rest until the next meal begins. The enforced rest of one group of organs gives added and amplified energy to the group which is at exercise. All this aids in developing orderly functionings of the bodily organs, and in the creation of an instinct which shall be a correct guide to, and shall have effective control of, the system.

The digestive organs play an important part in the elimination of waste. Even more important is the Page 219

elimination accomplished through respiration. Elimination of waste by respiration makes possible the assimilation of a supply of oxygen compounds; and the more thoroughly the blood is purified, the greater the quantity of oxygen compounds supplied will be.

Since the blood takes up the waste of the organism, it must be purified by vigorous functioning of the respiratory processes; otherwise, poisonous matter accumulates and re-circulates and auto-intoxication results. The frequency of exercise and the increase of exercise serve subsequently to bring the respiratory organs to top efficiency in their waste-ridding task-the purification of the blood stream.

As the correct balance of bodily processes is established, the normalizing of under-developed organs, and the reducing of those over-developed, will begin. Overrigidity of some internal parts, and over-flaccidity of others, will tend to disappear as processes and activities are set to the time of the stomach's action. Results of misuse and disuse will subside.

The embedded cells depend on the circulation of the blood to remove their waste and to supply material for repair, and to build new self-reproducing cells. Blood should be circulated freely and functioning energy transmitted freely. Embedded cells of organs and tissues are derived from self-reproducing cells in the blood. 

If an organ or tissue receives a deposit of self-reproducing cells which exceeds the removal of disintegrated embedded cells, the organ or tissue grows; if the removal exceeds the accretions, its size diminishes. Selfreproducing cells require a supply of nutrition for growth, and it follows that an organism which has Page 220

reached a balance between accretion and decretion, and requires little or no supply of self-reproducing cells, would need only a minimum of nutrition for the repair and growth of such cells.

Reciprocation Between Exercise and Rest 

The embedded cells of organs and tissues are structurally depleted at exercise and repleted at rest. By prompt and frequent removal of the structural waste, each structure is prepared for a wholesome repair supply at rest. When exercise is carried on too long without rest, embedded cell structures are disintegrated; and when rest continues too long, putrefaction sets in. 

Exercise and rest should be so divided and regulated as to establish a healthful recurrence of natural waste and natural rebuilding, fitting in at the same time with the rhythmic recurrence of the stomach's activities.

In order to remove abnormalities of the internal organs and to fit them for exact and healthful functioning in unison, we must reduce overdeveloped and rigid tissues and organs, through removal of disintegrated embedded cell structures, and replace with a smaller deposit of self-reproducing cells. We must do also what is more difficult, to bring up the under-developed and flaccid tissues to normal, by enabling them to get a greater deposit of self-reproducing cells than the amount of disintegrated embedded cell tissue. 

The human organism must function constantly to generate functioning power and body heat. By keeping one group of organs, the upper digestive tract, at exercise, while the other group, including the lower digestive tract, is at rest, and then putting the lower Page 221

tract to work while the upper tract is at rest, we obtain an equilibrium between exercise and rest by alternation and amplification, and the natural balance is established.

Proteins, the organic base of cell structure, are chiefly assimilated by the upper digestive tract. The alternation of activity and rest in that tract favors preservation of the proteins, and insuring a supply of energy-yielding substances. The upper tract works when the body is at rest, and rests when the body is being exercised.

Food fats, chiefly assimilated by the lower digestive tract, unite with the oxygen produced by respiration to generate a maximum of life energy functioning power required at exercise. The oxygen, combining with the carbon of food fats, of carbohydrates, and of carbohydrates converted into body fats, thus frees the hydates, creating body liquid. 

Natural Life Consciousness

The aggregates of energy regenerations and degenerations in equality is the net constant of functioning uniformity, which must preserve "a natural life consciousness" of occurrences of energy efficiency; that is, for every part of the conjunctive whole which exercises in functioning-power degeneration, a compensating part rests; so that the relative efficiency of all parts sustains the omniscience of the conjunctive whole. 

It also follows that occurrence of energy parts which evolve in balance are necessarily at one with the conjunctive whole in efficiency; because such parts equally and oppositely receive latent energy from and return Page 222

developed energy into environment. Barring accidents, so they continue for the duration of an environment favorable to that part's functioning efficiency.

CHAPTER VIII. 

The similarity of the human organism to other forms in nature I have explained with some particularity. Not only is the body composed of earthly elements, but in its operation it follows the universal principles. Man is an entity in the cosmos. 

The entity of being, reality or existence always was and will be, as it is now, the ever-present oneness. It is composed of inseparable protons of physics or form and electrons of energy or character in the rudiments of atoms. This inseparability provides for a constant relationship of physics to energy, as neither can be converted into the other, and likewise, as physics and energy are inseparable in the atom, it follows that any readjustment of physics must be co-ordinated with reeducation or training of energy.

There is a constant interfluxing between atoms, molecules and particles, as expressed in the gaseous liquid and solid elements. These elements are termed "phenomena of physics", which at integration or recomposition are recohered by the confinement of energy, and which at disintegration or decomposition are detached by the liberation of energy. Such liberation or actions and confinements, or reactions of "phenomena of physics", are termed "occurrences of energy".

General Co-ordination in Functioning 

As all recompositions and decompositions are equal and opposite, so are co-ordinated all actions and reac- Page 223

tions. The aggregates of adding to and subtracting from equally is the balanced evolution, which must preserve a natural arrangement of phenomena of physics competency; that is, for every part of the conjunctive whole which disintegrates, a compensatory part integrates, so that the relative competency of all parts sustains the omnipotence of the conjunctive whole. It also follows that phenomena-of-physics parts which evolve in balance are necessarily at one with the conjunctive whole in potency, because such parts equally and oppositely receive from and dispose of unto environment, and so continue under a favorable reciprocity in the environment. 

Have you ever compared this human machine of flesh, blood and bone with the hard machine of industry? The latter is strong, complex and rigid; it is built to precision and functions to precision. The operator cleans, oils and greases it, and the machinist sees that each of its parts is always in perfect condition. 

Every attention is given to it, as it is an investment of considerable capitalistic and productive value. 

The human machine is a machine of machines. It is much stronger than any steel machine and far more complex. It is an inconceivably greater investment than the man-made machine; it represents the greatest possible capital investment. As to its productive capacity and value, they cannot be measured in mathematical terms. Yet the upkeep of this wonderful machine and the mechanical organs in it is a trivial matter with the most of us.

The United States Department of Agriculture in a Page 224

bulletin (No. 142) on "Nutritive Value of Food" calls special attention to this important fact. 

The Most Marvelous Machine 

"In a sense," says the author of this bulletin, Dr. 

W. O. Atwater, "the body is a superior machine. Like other machines, it requires material to build up its several parts, to repair them as they are worn out, and to serve as fuel. In some ways it uses this material like a machine; in others it does not. "One important difference between the human machine and the steam engine is that the former is selfbuilding, self-repairing and self-regulating. The steam engine and the body are alike in that both convert fuel into mechanical power and heat. They differ in that the body uses the same material for fuel as for building, and consumes its own material for fuel. In the use of its source of power, the body is much more economical than any engine". 

You are the owner, manager, machinist and operator of this machine. This machine is you. If you are to have good health and happiness, and are to be fit and efficient, you must appreciate the value of this machine and maintain it in prime condition. You must realize that its value is so priceless, and the life which it contains so precious, as to enjoin you by the most ponderable natural and moral obligations to respect it as an irreplaceable treasure.

Of all the beings and things, of all the diamonds and gold, of all the art and wealth, in the huge solar system, the human machine is incomparably the most beautiful, the most useful and the most valuable. And what could have so much worth to you as this body Page 225

which is your physical identity and the repository of your life and personality? 

What a piece of work is man! how noble in reason! how infinite in faculty! in form and moving how express and admirable! in action how like an angel! in apprehension how like a god! the beauty of the world! the paragon of animals! 

-Shakespeare; Hamlet, Act II, Scene 2. 

Better Even Than You Pretend 

You admire the artifice in the industrial machine, yet are not awed by the inexplicable wizardry of your own body and its works. The fixed regularity of action of the iron machine, going through the same operation over and over again, engrosses your interest. The human machine, on the other hand, being a commonplace subject to you, does not charm you with amazement, although it is tuned in all its multifarious functionings to the all-enveloping harmony of the universal cosmos. 

Every beat of your heart, every respiration in your lungs, every rhythm in your stomach, every illumination in your mind, is a cadence of your evolution with the widespread evolution in the rest of nature. 

The universe is in you. It is a conjunctive whole of parts, known as "phenomena of physics," which function, as "occurrences of energy", separately, but in mutual dependence. The human system is a conjunctive whole of organ and tissue parts which function separately, but in mutual dependence. Thus, there is basically a distinct similarity.

The universe automatically preserves a balanced evolution of "physics phenomena" and "energy occur Page 226

rences", because of compensating equals and opposites, and likewise mankind's organism must automatically preserve a balanced evolution in order to sustain a constant prime of vigorous health and contentful happiness.

Your fundamental objective in living, as is that of every individual, is to enjoy happiness in good health.

More than this could not be realized. Health is the prime of "competency" in physics, and happiness the prime of "efficiency" in character. The person who succeeds in producing both in himself must radiate this sense or state of internally generated orderliness to all other units in nature, and he thus contributes directly to the common social welfare.

Balanced Evolution in Human Nature 

Self-sustained prime of organism is the balanced evolution of structures in condition and operation. The interplay of equal and opposite, bringing about recomposition of supply and decomposition of waste, selfpreserves a natural arrangement of organ and tissue sizes; while the natural rechemicalization of secretions self-preserves their natural resiliency. Herein is the orderly alternation of normal functioning, which, by equal and opposite actions of exercise, and reactions of rest, self-preserves a natural poise of ease, peace of mind, contentment, or supreme joy of living!

CHAPTER IX. 

You are in a condition of constant prime or balanced evolution while your manner of living conforms to the requirements for health instinctively laid down by nature. In the evolution in nature as it pervades the Page 227

One is earth science marks out two primary causes. change in the earth itself. The other is climate, or, in general, conditions arising in the solar system. These causes are still operative, affecting both general nature and man, and in the apparent sameness of ourselves and of our environment change is always taking place. "There are times of quickening, the expression points of evolution, and these are found to be coincident with geologic change", says Dr. Richard Swann Lull, professor of vertebrate paleontology in Yale University. "These coincidences are so frequent and so exact that the laws of chance may not be invoked to account for them. They stand to each other in the relation of cause and effect." "This does not imply," he continues, "the acceptance of any one philosophical factor of evolution, for whether the creature is directly modified by environmental change, or indirectly through induced habit, or whether nature merely sets a standard to which the organism must attain if it would survive, matters not; the fact remains that changing environmental conditions stimulate the sluggish evolutionary stream to quickened movement.

We May Fall Back on Climate 

"Whenever it has been possible to connect cause and effect, the immediate influence is found to be generally one of climate, back of which lies, as the main cause, earth shrinkage and a consequent warping of the crust, with the elevation and spread of the lands and the formation of the mountain ranges. In addition to this mundane cause, there are the complex rhythms in solar Page 228

energy and the consequent variation in the amount of solar-derived heat."

Although Dr. Lull writes principally of evolution through the ages, and although he depicts chiefly the course of evolution in the past in connection with all of nature, the same causes are in action at the present time. Of course, the outstanding forms of life are well established at this date, and very few species are forced to evolution in order merely to survive; nevertheless, the causes continue to bring about changes which affect the life and health of man. The universe is still the universe; nature is still nature, and change and evolution go on as continuously as in the past. Conditions are altered, but the cosmic principles are the same as ever.

The advanced state of complex civilization in which we exist has not excepted any of us from the empirical force of these universal principles of nature. It has, however, changed our mode of existence fundamentally from that of our ancestors. Up to about twenty or thirty years ago citizens of the United States lived quite simply, as nationals of many lands do at this time, and they were, therefore, in a more direct relationship with nature. Our civilization has interposed barriers between us and nature, and our mode of living is more artificial, as well as more complex. Whereas our progenitors lived in small towns and settlements for safety, we huddle in large cities for convenience. We close out nature with a convex pall of smoke and gases and rows of concrete walls, and exist, as I have said, on synthetic light, air and food, and to the routine of system and the conventionality of habit.

Page 229

Sun Not In It With Artificial Light 

We act as if we are no longer entities in the solar system, but in a universe of our own invention. We do not need the sun, moon, stars and cosmic rays, or water, air and wheat and corn. We produce our light and heat from water, our air from ballyhoo, and our foodstuffs from such synthetics as are not used up in making jewelry, clothing and beautifiers. We could get along without the earth itself except as an anchorage for our high buildings and pylons. We have improved on nature to such an extent that we resort to it only as a luxury. With machines, system, motor cars, radios, apartments and tin cans, we are not obliged to rely on nature. We can afford to scrap nature, now that nature is out of date, for natural evolution has not kept pace with inventive evolution. When we wish to live close to nature, we rent a penthouse on a high ledge of a set-back building and install perfumed artificial flowers and plants on the parapet, or place a spreading fern on a tabarette in the living room. 

For the one sufficient reason, that we have expatriated ourselves from the earth and solar system because we have carried inventive evolution beyond natural evolution, am I constrained to remind you that this relationship between you and the rest of nature has not been sundered? I shall not here summon the doctors of medicine and science to repeat and emphasize the principles and requirements of nature. I am taking it for granted that you are waking up to truth by alarm over some compensatory punishment with which nature is afflicting you. 

You have sound reason to realize, I believe, that the

Page 230

synthetic manner of living cannot be a substitute for the natural. You cannot spurn the gifts of nature and retain health of body and mind. You cannot remove yourself from the solar system or exile yourself from the earth. You cannot separate your individual evolution from the evolution of nature at large. 

The men who by profession study nature describe an evolution which will progress as long as nature lasts. 

Man goes through an evolution which will progress as long as he exists, and the individual through one contemporaneous with the duration of his life. 

Of Great Interest to Legislators

Naturally, there should and would be a concordance between the evolution in nature at large and that in man, and between the general evolution and that in the individual. This supposition is reasonable from the universal order and harmony. An adjustation proceeds as a natural sequence, for the unison between the units and parts cannot ever be interrupted. The conformance of beings and things to cosmic principles is mathematically accurate; there is an absolute precision between cause and effect, a precision absolute in compliance in trend and finality with the active principles. 

So, within the individual, nature requires unison in the functioning and co-ordination among the organs in the preservation of a normal equilibrium. I call this poise balanced evolution. It is the complete harmony within the individual entity which corresponds with the smoothness and order in the universal cosmos. My concept is that the harmony which naturally should exist in the human being is natural co-operation with Page 231

the universal harmony, and that as long as it prevails it is the regular, normal exemplification of perfect balance.

Man can show antagonism to the principles of nature, as with his intelligence he combines option of will. 

He has, in fact, developed a great antagonism in the misuse and disuse of his advantages. This antagonism exists largely in the complexities of advanced civilization, and it is intensified by individual indifference and obstinacy. Loss of poise in the human being, which is proof of departure from natural law, is the antithesis to balanced evolution, and it leads inevitably to depreciation of health and disturbance of mind commensurate with the wantonness of the breach. 

Nature Likewise Is Smooth

Balanced evolution appears in both cause and effect. 

In cause it is indicated in the smoothness of the conformity with the natural order, and in effect by perfection in the health of the body. Any infringement of natural principle disturbs the equilibrium, and disorder is notable at the source, that is, in the cause; subsequently, it is seen in the digressive consequences to individual health.

As long as one adheres to the requirements of nature he succeeds in maintaining a salubrious equilibrium. 

Then his cellular structures are vital in the process of construction and repair. Then his evolution is balanced. 

I have tried to be plain in explaining how this balanced evolution is accomplished by comparing the efficaciousness of health measures in the early part of life with the recessive effects of carelessness and abuse Page 232

in the period of adolescence. I had in mind picturing to you more than conservational results. In the early part of life more is achieved with the majority of persons than preservation of good health. Voluntary, even serious, observance of the mandates of nature in the smallest detail results in the formative years, as I have indicated, in building up health to such a degree as to mass a reserve in vigor and energy which counteracts the devolutionary tendency in the following stage or two.

A state of balanced evolution exists during this early or formative period. A person is in a condition of prime health consistent with his age-period and his progress through adolescence. There is continual accord between the human body and the external environment. The chemical laboratories-the cellsfunction regularly in the constructive and replacement process; integration supplants disintegration in the embedded structures, while the self-reproducing cells proceed at their vitalizing work. New self-reproducing cells are drawn from the blood-stream as needed, in response to the natural instinct, and from the same source the embedded cells obtain new material to compensate for the waste removed. The physical condition is normal. The prime of health is constant. 

Exposing Ponce de Leon's Mistake 

Now, I have also endeavored to make clear to you that one of the most important results of this constant prime or balanced evolution is contra-ageing; that is, that the ageing of the body is suspended by both the vigor of health and the accumulation of a large reserve of vitality in all the organs and tissues. The speed of Page 233

time is diminished, and, I might say, is almost reduced to a standstill. 

Contra-ageing obviously is the first step in a system of preventive medicine by the individual. It is the effect of a precautionary cause. If it is possible (and we see that it is) to overcome the subversive influence of time on the human body by adherence to the ordinary dictates of nature, thereby protecting health and conserving vigor and energy, then there can be no doubt that ageing is checked. This is an actual demonstration, from the everyday experience of everybody, of the virtue of what I describe as the constant prime or balanced evolution.

Constant prime or balanced evolution is simply performance of every natural health obligation and active concert with the general external evolution. The state of full unison between the body and the cosmic principles is an equilibrium, which, of itself, realizes stability of health. It is not necessary in order to achieve constant prime or balanced evolution to adopt extraordinary and bothersome methods; it is enough to live up to the common requirements of nature. 

Women Can Estimate Their Age average, of compara-

While we of life, length of 

The state or con Again, please remember that time is but one of the elements of ageing, and is, on the tively minor derogatory effect. longevity by the number of years time is, in fact, a standard indicator. dition of being in entirety is the sole real determinant of age. Each of us is not as old or young as he is in years, but what he is in bodily and mental health; each of us is as old as he is as to normality of health, or, I Page 234

might add, supernormality or subnormality. Though we measure age by years, we compute present length and future expectation of life by the evidences of nature. You are, therefore, as old as you are in regard to your physical condition.

I have illustrated this idea to you by the contraageing in the first stage or two of life. You have seen that health in this term improved with the lapse of years. The body is built up during ten, fifteen or twenty years; and, notwithstanding that this extent of time has been consumed, the body is stronger. The body in its prime fitness has gathered vitality and vigor for the harder experiences during ensuing years, and this fitness contains reserve strength and energy to preserve a balanced evolution, or approximately a balanced evolution, for some time against reactionary causes.

We may consider in this positive conformity with the law of nature in the manner of living the acquisition of two potential advantages, these being conservation and reserve. In this distinction, it is assumed that conservation is simply a normal result, whereas reserve is something more, being a kind of supernormal benefit. However, this distinction is not necessary; whether we take conservation or reserve, or conservation and reserve, the fact is clearly obvious that there are two vital effects-the preservation of constant prime during the continuance of proper, natural living, balancing the individual human evolution with the cosmic, and the collection of a store of life-prolonging reserve. 

Good News for "Fair, Fat and Forty”!

It is extremely important, in my opinion, that you should thoroughly understand these few points which Page 235

I am presenting at this juncture. They are fundamental. I do not rest them on theories or conjecture, but on realities of life which are readily perceptible to anyone. I am not asking you to take my word for them. 

I am offering you proof from your own experience.

First, I assert that time is but one element in the ageing process, and with most persons and in most cases it is of minor vital importance. You do not live on or of time, but through time. I do not hold that time is without effect; on the contrary, as you will remember, I stressed at some length the significance of time in all universal affairs. I say that we cannot estimate age by time alone, but rather in connection with time. 

Second, I assert that the ageing progress is mainly physical and mental. Ageing is due to the breakingdown of the cellular structures, in which the principle of life resides in the active and subsistive states. The principle is in the active state in the formation of the self-reproducing cells, and in the subsistence state in the embedded cells. Dependent on the cause, the ageing progress may begin in either group of cells, or in both; at any rate, physical decline is a consequence of deterioration of the cell structure. Therefore, it is a logical conclusion that youth or age, to some extent regardless of time or age, rests on the state of the cell structure. 

Third, I assert that the ageing process can be, and is, arrested and counteracted by a constant prime in the cellular conditions and functioning. Moreover, I assert that the ageing process is counteracted by this balanced evolution, which is practically regular coincidence in the natural order. The proof of this is that improve- Page 236

ment in health during the first few stages of existence progresses faster than the flight of time. 

Better Than Dewrinkling and Facelifting 

Fourth, I maintain that the ageing process can be counteracted indefinitely by permanent maintenance of the constant prime in the human organism. Time becomes a more important factor as it is spent, but its depressing power is only a few shades deeper in the latter years than in the earlier; it might be contended that the relative power of time in the ageing process in the latter years is determined by the constancy with which sound health has been preserved.

Fifth, I assert that inasmuch as the ageing progress is checked in the earlier part of life, it can be abated in the latter. You notice that I declare as a reality the success of the contra-ageing in the first several stages. 

I assert, in addition, that the same means are available in the latter years which were effective in the earlier. 

Sixth, I assert as a practical deduction from the foregoing statements that it is within the reach of each of us to modify the ravages of time at almost any period in life by resorting to health measures which will restore the condition of bodily prime. In other words, de-ageing or rejuvenation, by means of a special course of self-treatment in order to arrive at and establish the natural manner of living, brings the body back to a relatively prime condition, after which the relatively constant prime is maintained to the result of prolongation of the life span. 

A Straight Tip from Ma Nature

In brief, my concept regarding constant prime and balanced evolution is that: Contra-ageing goes on in Page 237

the first several stages of life; contra-ageing can be carried on in subsequent stages; ageing can be arrested after health has begun to break down; health can be rejuvenated at any period of life; that constant prime can be re-established after rejuvenation, and that the life span can be prolonged by maintaining constant prime or balanced evolution. 

By contra-ageing, you understand, ageing is overcome or counteracted. As long as the constant prime is upheld by following the common-sense requirements of nature, the ageing process is held under almost total restraint.

The ageing process is arrested and reversed by resumption of the common-sense practices for good health. After the resumption of the proper health practices, balanced evolution is again attained, and thereafter the constant prime is retained. 

Whenever health has slipped by violation of the natural law, it can be restored by rejuvenation, which is simply restoration of balanced evolution, from which ensues prime and then constant prime.

The rejuvenation does not call for drastic measures with most persons, but it does necessitate a brief vacation and temporary relaxation and change in order to rebuild health and reacquire the attitude of commonsense respect for the ordinary principles of nature. 

Owing to the health-abuses and disdain for nature which have grown up in our complex civilization, it is a deplorable fact that the health of the great majority is waning or nearly ruined; and as a consequence most individuals should figure on a brief rest in order to re-institute the naturally normal manner of living. Page 238

CHAPTER X. 

Mental Astigmatism Must Be Corrected 

Any reputable physician will tell you that it is imperative for one whose health is undermined by strenuous and destructive habits in employment and the pursuit of pleasure to take time out for bodily recuperation. Before one can re-adopt the common-sense manner of living, he must first find again the correct mental viewpoint; he must realize that his living habits are injurious to health, and he must be willing to go to a little trouble in order to get once more into prime condition. It would be useless and foolish to try to regain robust health without having the will and determination to make the trivial sacrifices for rejuvenation. 

Nature is beneficent, not tyrannical. But she is a despot to the extent that she exacts chivalrous deference to the absolute laws of the cosmic harmony. We say that we must pay the price for good health. We say that we do not get any good without remuneration for it. Nature is not mercenary or empirical. She expects nothing more of us than self-discipline. She expects us only to be temperate in all things, and therefore only to be reasonable. She favors or punishes us in accordance with our deserts. If we comply with the common-sense requirements, she rewards us with prime health and concomitant happiness; but if we show contempt for her favors, she manifests resentment by allowing our health to deteriorate.

After all, therefore, the price that you pay for good health is not a price. It is simply a mark of respect and appreciation. We really have less trouble or bother in Page 239

living naturally than in not doing so. The reward for doing only what we should do is health and happiness. 

The penalty is imposed on us when we fail to display proper courtesy for free benefits. Nature gives us our choice of good or bad health exclusively on the terms of being appreciative of her generosity. 

Why do I contend that you may have as good health after you have engaged in the activities of life in a profession, business or trade as when you were at school and in college? You may feel inclined to argue that the strain of occupation and responsibility would have effects of wear and tear which would be ageing in body and mind, and, therefore, no efforts which you might make could stop or retard ageing. If, you may argue, that it is reasonable to expect to have continued health by complying with the natural law, then it is equally reasonable to expect weakening of health from work and burden.

A Little Work Would Be Profitable 

I reply that work is not a destructive cause of itself, but, on the contrary, is a constructive cause. Work is as natural as repose. The human body is not worn out or torn down by work. Nor should it naturally be subject to decline as a consequence of the responsibilities or burdens of life. Like employment, responsibility is or should be a constructive force. 

Does the body of the prize-fighter or wrestler weaken from the great physical exertion of training or contest? 

It becomes stronger by the exercise. It is directly because of the hard training and exercise that the muscles are developed, and that the body grows strong and is Page 240

capable of great resistance and endurance. The baseball player also goes through many years of development, not only to acquire dexterity and skill, but to build up all parts of the body so that he may be fit. 

If you doubted this statement, you could prove it by a little experiment. You might go down to the corner tomorrow and get into a game with a band of youngsters; or you might play tennis with some girls. The girls, being used to exercise, would be adroit, swift and vigorous, whereas you would have sore arms and muscles in ten or fifteen minutes, if you did not have to stop sooner on account of being winded. The lads would, in baseball, handball, football, or whatever game they played, weary you in a very brief while. 

But it seems to me that you do not need evidence to convince you that exercise within reason, moderate exercise, builds up the body. I am alluding to exercise in order to convey to you the fact that work is practically the same as exercise, and it, too, develops the physique. Virtually the only difference between exercise and work is that the latter is necessary and fixed and is more or less regular or routine. 

Can you conjure up in your mind a clear idea of a life without work, without difficulties, without problems, without some trouble? Would you wish to have such a life? Can you form an idea of the specimen of humanity a person without these trials would be like?

He would be a parasite in society. He would have no thought except for self-gratification and would give nothing to humanity. Neither would he be of benefit to himself. The good which he would take from other beings he would waste in giving himself petty pleasures. Page 241

He would have ingrown, self-centered desires, and it would be impossible for him to radiate orderliness or benefits.

In these preceding pages we have considered the necessity of work for physical and mental well-being. 

Work is synonymous with exercise; so that in our subsequent discursions whenever I refer to exercise I imply work, and when I refer to work I imply exercise. Each is physical activity, exercise is superactivity and rest is subactivity.

No Wear and Tear from Work 

The body would decay and the mind dull without employment. We develop our organs and tissues by use. It is a fact that the cells disintegrate from use, but this is a phase of normal functioning. It is also a fact that they disintegrate from misuse or stagnation; this sort of disintegration is, however, destructive, as the cause is contrary to the normal. This distinction is made plainer to you by comparing the body to a machine. The machine which is operated steadily or intermittently in industrial production depreciates somewhat right along from wear and tear; this is normal depreciation, and, as the usefulness and value of the machine are appreciated, it is maintained in working condition, being always as good as new. The idle machine rusts away; in a short time it becomes useless and has to be scrapped. Work, as far as the human body is concerned, is, of itself, a quick factor in maintenance, as it participates in cell-depreciation toward constructive purpose. Work is fully as necessary to sound health as its opposite, rest.

The body could and would wear down from excess Page 242

of work or exercise, and in this case work would have exhausting physical effects. Nevertheless, it would not run down, as it would from habitual lethargy. But in moderate activity, that is, controlled and normal activity, which is consistent naturally with individual strength and endurance, work is necessary and beneficial. 

You agree, I feel certain, with my opinions as to the necessity of work for building up organs and tissues and preserving prime health. The proof of them presses itself to your attention.

Two most important features of exercise should be understood as clearly. The first is that exercise must be restrained within the limit of physical exhaustion, that is, it must be regulated to the normal maximum, but not beyond this. The second is that exercise and work should be planned and carried on so that they will have effect in all parts of the body. I suggested this feature to you in an illusion to occupational activities, indicating that the exercise obtained from regular employment is confined to certain parts of the body only, and not to all.

The human body requires work in order to be fit. 

It would waste away without activity. Muscles and tissues would soften, and the organs would function without vigor and vim. In a few years of idleness the body would lose all power of initiative. No physical momentum would be left. The mere effort necessary for ordinary motion and movement would be a comparative strain. 

Don't Leave Every Task to One Hand

It is a matter of scientific knowledge that parts of the body which are not used are virtually abandoned Page 243

by the cell functioning and depreciate as a consequence of natural neglect. Say that a person does not use one arm. Before long he senses that it is not as strong as formerly; there is about it a feeling of weakness and stiffness. This is so because nature, through the maintenance of cell structure, by the process which I have described, concentrates on those parts of the body which are used and ignores any part which is not used. 

Now, this leads us to a second important point regarding work or exercise. The first is that which we just considered, that work is constructive. The second is that work or exercise should be so planned and conducted as to contemplate complete physical fitness, that is, with the object of bringing about constant prime which would embrace the whole body. 

I shall illustrate this second suggestion by a reference to occupational employment. For the first example, take a business executive. He sits all day at a desk; he may divide his day between employment at his desk in his office and in conference at desks or tables in other offices; he is at a desk practically all day. He rides downtown and back to his residence in an automobile. 

We might say that his eyes and brain are exercised to the exclusion of the rest of his body. The occupation of the stenographer is sedentary. As the second example, take a bank teller or retail salesman. He stands virtually all day, and, besides exercise which he gets from standing (and as a rule he does not stand up straight), the only exercise is with his hands and fingers. In the trades, one person works with the hands, another with the feet, another with the eyes, one sits, another stands, another takes a few steps, and so on. Page 244

Looking into the variety of occupations, you find that the regular work gives exercise only to certain parts of the body, but not to all. There is vigor in the parts used, but weakness and a tendency to depreciation in those not used. 

Something in the Load

But you are not ready to agree that the responsibilities and burdens of life, which are inevitable expectations, are of constructive effect physically. You regard them as depressing to the mind and debilitating to the body. You think that they shrivel the vital materials and disintegrate the cell structures, and weaken the body and quench energy.

Responsibility doubtless is more likely to be detrimental than physical work, especially when worry is associated with it. Nevertheless, it is a natural requirement to a normal extent, and as physical work or exercise conditions the body, so the intellectual and moral exercise connected with responsibility, and even with the cares and troubles of life, actually does improve the brain, and, which is of like importance, trains the will. 

Weigh this thought. Your character is formed from the responsibilities, perplexities, burdens and troubles which befall you. Were every circumstance in your affairs simple and easy, and had you no difficulties or cares, your character would be frail. Had you no problems or troubles, you would have no moral capacity or force. You would be a moral weakling. 

I may go still farther. I may say, and in truth, that life would want zest, interest and incentive were it devoid of responsibility and the divergencies occasioned Page 245

by baffling difficulties. Life would be dull without responsibility. Would you be pleased with yourself if you demanded unvarying serenity in your business and personal affairs, if you could not be at ease unless your problems solved themselves, and if you could not be even-tempered unless you got everything you wanted as and when you wanted it? Your life would not be interesting or profitable to you or anyone else unless it were, at least within limited bounds, comparatively hard. 

You could not be happy without character. You could not be intellectually or physically competent without it. You would be deficient without exercise for the higher intelligence similar to that, in a general way, which is necessary to physical development and fitness.

CHAPTER XI. 

Bambino and Others Are Psycho-Analyzed 

Somebody might say that the irresponsibility displayed by several prominent athletes refutes this contention. Somebody else might declare that there are conspicuous examples among professional and business. men of health and happiness in environments of extraordinary luxury and tranquillity. Somebody would raise the objection that there are notorious cases of fame and success as apparently the immediate result of irresponsibility and turpitude. 

Take, somebody may say, the popular George Herman Ruth, better known as the Babe or Bambino. Babe is described as a big boy. For many years he splintered all the rules of discipline, and consequently all the exigencies of nature for sound health and physical fitness; Page 246

he estimates that he received $1,000,000 as income, but squandered it riotously; his most serious perplexity was in devising conceits for spending every increase in salary. 

Babe Ruth is an excellent example for elucidating moral responsibility. Let us consider his case in full.

He was a wayward lad on the streets of Baltimore, and his environment was so debasing that he already was in peril of drifting into a dissolute career; he was already so capricious from boyish licentiousness that he rebelled against the mere suggestion of restraint, and, without injustice to him, it might be said that he was not far from becoming incorrigible as a fixed habit issuing from his surroundings. He at first resisted the invitation to go into a Christian Brothers orphanage, where he would learn a trade and get moral training, solely because he resisted the indication of discipline. 

In this school he learned the trade of shirt-making and the game of baseball, and, in addition, acquired moral training by being subjected to discipline. It cannot be denied that all this training developed both his body and his intelligence, and that, when his friend in the institution at length decided to place him on the 

Baltimore team as a professional baseball player, he was in the prime of health and in the habit of submitting to discipline. I say that up to this time there was fulfilled in him the conservation of health which I have explained as being usual to the formative years of life. 

Ruth Realizes Might of Big Stick 

His dissipations in the next stage of his life may be construed as misunderstanding of circumstances in his changed environment. The pleasures of his new world

Page 247

carried him away. He had been unaccustomed to large income. He had the money with which to have a jolly time.

Ruth was twice taken to task for his lapses. The first disciplining forced him to realize that he had to live according to the league standards in order to stay in a major organization. The second, which occurred at the height of his fame, caused him to see that he had to settle down in careful habits and comply with the rules of discipline, or he would have no employment, and would be in disrepute with the public. Mayor 

James Walker of New York City, a friend and admirer, appealed to his idealism, saying that Ruth, the idol of the youth of America, was honor-bound to set an edifying example in both behavior and play. Persuasion and disciplining induced the Bambino to adopt and follow a manner of living which would be creditable to himself and the national game and assure him health, employment and fame indefinitely.

Obviously, Ruth's health and standing, which were in jeopardy when he disregarded training rules, are due to the development of individual morale. He would have retrograded as a player had he not reformed his habits. He safeguarded his health and stabilized his popularity by the change. When money came easily and was tossed away freely he was retrograding; but as soon as he recognized his responsibilities to himself, his employers and the public, he became steady in his work and made his popularity secure. 

Ruth in his career so far is not an example of success and health in irresponsibility, but of success and health through the growth of character. He arrested the age- Page 248

ing process in time to obviate physical disaster, and by so doing unquestionably prolonged his usefulness and won respect from the people as a crown for his fame. 

So, as I see his example, it furnishes convincing proof of all the arguments which I submit in interpreting my concept.

Soft Living Ends on Scrapheap 

The foregoing explanation also answers the questions as to the apparent happiness and success of business and professional men in soft environments or as the seeming result of indifference to natural health requirements. The generally acknowledged law of equalization operates in instances such as these. You need only figure-and this you would do to a certainty — that abuse absolutely ensues to depreciation. These persons, instead of procuring the maximum obtainable benefit or gain, procure something under that. Their possible net benefit or gain is reduced or wiped out. 

Call up in memory the careers of friends and acquaintances, and you will have plenty of evidence from your own environment of this law of equalization. 

You will remember this man who was "going to pieces", but checked his excesses and established himself firmly and permanently in health and business and realized all or nearly all the promise in his physical and intellectual capacity. You will remember the fatalistic termination of the brilliant prospects of a friend whose experiences were chequered. You will think of another friend who had to retire in disappointment in his prime because of broken health. You will think of another whose prestige petered out because his patrons found him to be unreliable on account of health enfeebled by Page 249

abuse or character impairment by habitual insouciance. 

If you peer closely into the records of those who appeared to achieve success or position or power, notwithstanding indifference to the laws of health or character, you will see clearly that the rule applicable to them is that of equalization, and their net accruments were diminished by their idiosyncracies of extravagance. 

You well know that one cannot give what one does not have, or that a source cannot issue what it does not contain. These are recognized principles. However, it cannot be determined what hidden, but inherent, quality or capacity an identity has until the occasion of need or advantage calls it forth. Major and minor episodes in which external conditions smile or frown on individuals are inexplicable as to cause. Good luck or misfortune is often puzzling to our inquiry. But in instances such as I have just pictured the effects of external relations offer no excuse, for the law of compensation works out with the law of equalization to an adjustment of reward against penalty or penalty against reward.

No Storage Tank for Energy 

You well know that the human organism does not possess an organ for the storage of generated life-energy functioning power. Such power is radiated externally almost instantaneously as soon as generated. Although the principle of momentum, as applied to the force of conservation, prevails to give the appearance of continued prime of health, the reactionary impulse begins at once in depletion which, while not visible, is under way.

Page 250

You well know that a rash person, who is committing slow suicide through cumulative derangements of form toward dissolution, and with cumulative diseases of character toward decease, is a natural agency which must externally radiate the disorderliness which it internally generates by such flagrant desecration of nature. Per contra, the self-reserving person, who stays at a constant prime or balanced evolution of health and happiness, is an agency which must radiate externally the orderliness in form and character which it generates internally by its faithful consecration to the conventional ordinances of nature. 

The constitutional provisions of nature do not excuse ignorance any more than the principles of human law. 

With nature carelessness is more reprehensible than ignorance, for it is a deliberate affront. A new order in health retention and betterment is possible by adapting the manner of living in our complex civilization to the old order of the cosmic unison. Babe Ruth gives us an outstanding example of the retardation and reversal of the ageing process and of the prompt and effectual substitution of the contra-ageing.

CHAPTER XII. 

The lives of different persons are as different as individuals are. Some are sunshiny from beginning to end. 

Some, from first to last, are like a long, gray, misty, drizzly day. Some are always cloudy and threatening. 

Some are bright and gay, like a perennial festival. Some are made up of faint lights and as faint shadows, ever like the late gloaming. Some are effulgent of splendor like the summer, and others are dismal and cold, like Page 251

the bleak, despondent winter. But each is intermingled with both joy and grief.

The Whiner Is Wrong Inside 

Whenever I hear a person complain of the bitterness of his life, I think that he has not as yet brought his intellect to the moral poise which is akin to the balanced physical evolution. His character is still in the course of development. Were I a physician or psychiatrist, orthodoxically scientific, and not submerged in some sort of a pseudo-scientific or pseudo-spiritual hobby, I should look into his case for a physical or mental ailment and tell him of the natural method for recovering prime of health. Most of the mental and physical complexes are within the natural sphere and can be rectified by normal means. It is foolish to trust in a fetich, as thus you would only deceive yourself with a subterfuge, with which, by indirection and sorcery, you would hope for a metamorphosis, whereas you could by normal faith and self-confidence and selfservice and by natural means readjust your system from the longest hair on your head, or the smallest, to the sole of your foot. 

And whenever I hear it said that neither the individual nor humanity derives fleeting or permanent benefit from the difficulties of life, the difficulties which mold character and strengthen mind and body, I think of David and the immortal songs which he composed.

These poetical lamentations would not and could not have been written had not the Shepherd-King, moved by great ideals and stirred by turbulent passions, suffered from remorse and misery. Millions of persons in the thousands of years which have elapsed since he Page 252

sang of sorrow and hope have been re-inspired by the heart-music in his verse. We see that our passing reverses and tribulations are of constructive value to society.

I am committing to you that sacred principle, which is verified in everyone's experience, that the best of life, whatever it may be, is a condition which you can create. 

To a considerable extent, our life, in its entirety, is, like our health and our ideals, what we make it; and conversely I may say that our health of mind and body, like the purpose in our life, is what we make it. 

You may obtain advice and encouragement from your external environment, but good counsel is worthless unless you adopt it. You are the sole master of your own destiny. No person can function for another in sensing his organism requirements. The capable physician, who successfully prescribes remedies for a patient, can only remove interferences with what may have been the accustomed methods of functioning. It is an utter impossibility for another than yourself to sense and adapt ways and means for accomplishing your balanced evolution after the physician's treatment has been successful. 

Every Man His Own Philosopher

There is a sublime concept in philosophy, accepted as a metaphysical classic, that the individual who faithfully observes the natural rules which are obviously normal lives in a state of natural perfection. The concept applies to the higher intelligence, even to the moral verities, as well as to the body. It assumes that the affiliation between the human being and the cosmos in the universe is so vital and intimate that the universal Page 253

laws of nature are a conjunctive force by which man can conduct himself in a state of practically total balanced evolution.

It is this philosophical concept which I am expanding to you as a natural proposition for building up sound health and the character which is essential to it. 

There is a natural quality of character in the mind as there is a natural standard of health for the body, and I have shown you from your own experience and that of others that the establishment of balanced evolution is not a worrisome and hard task, but a simple and easy procedure which entails nothing more than compliance with the ordinary requirements of nature. 

But I have admonished you that you cannot acquire and preserve health solely by a physician's prescription and that it would be vain to trust in telepathy, exorcism or magic. The physician gives you medicine or nutriment which will help you, and advice which is founded on natural requirements, but he leaves it to you to cure yourself and then adopt the natural rules which insure prime condition to you and thereafter make it your standard.

CHAPTER XIII. 

Sherlock Holmesing on the Lopsided

Persons are so neglectful of equalizing exercise in connection with occupational work as to show deformities due to habit in their personal appearance. You may designate the occupations of many persons from their bearing or manner of walking. One is somewhat stooped in one shoulder; another in the other shoulder. Page 254

One bends forward at the neck, another with his back.

One has an arm which looks as if it is paralyzed. One has a certain way of posing his head, as a result of focusing his eyes in his employment. Another walks as if one leg is longer than the other. These and other oddities are symptoms of habitual deformities resulting from occupational work. 

Each of these persons requires some exercise, in addition to the regular work, in order to balance the use and appearance of different parts of his body. A little more exercise might feel fatiguing for a while, but it would soon prove to be helpful. Correct posture and balanced body-functioning always prove to be easier, after practice, than incorrect and unbalanced. At first, for instance, it appears to be an exertion to stand or sit erect, yet after either becomes a habit it is found to be restful and invigorating.

Another point is that many persons who take exercise as a diversion do so improperly. I notice many golf players in a public park walking over the greens with stooped shoulders or bent bodies. Ostensibly out for exercise and pastime, they are omitting one of the principal means of physical benefit. These postures denote either fatigue, or physical weakness in some part of the body, or occupational habit. They detract from the benefit of the exercise. The correct posture is more comfortable than the incorrect, after one accustoms himself to it. 

Please Do Not Work-Too Much

I will sum up now the contentions which I have presented to you concerning work. Work is necessary to Page 255

physical fitness. It builds up organs and tissues and causes the cellular functioning to operate normally in accordance with natural requirements. Besides regular occupational employment, some special exercising should be followed in order to bring all parts of the body into natural action, and to balance all parts in use and appearance.

Work and exercise should be restricted to limits which prevent tiring. Both should be carried on in correct posture and by correct methods, so that the best benefits to health may be produced, and so that deportment and functioning will be natural, and, therefore, easy and comfortable.
