Work and exercise should cover also the employment of the brain. 

I feel sure that I have made it plain to you that mental exercise is necessary for facilitating intellectual effort and likewise for developing character. Our will is our supreme power. Unless we have it under absolute control, and, I should add, with the balance of common sense, we are not progressing as we should in character development. Were it not a fact that common sense is adequate, regardless of knowledge, wisdom or judgment, the opportunity of the average individual for character and health would be futile. It seems to me that common sense, rather than genius, is the mark of human superiority over the brute. As far as existing is concerned, living in common sense is apparently about the best that can be done, for common sense is normal sense, and the normal is natural. 

Briefly, I say that natural exercise, which includes occupational work, is imperatively requisite for normal health. As I have indicated, even the difficulties, prob- Page 256

lems and troubles which man confronts are conducive to the formation of sturdy character. We all desire, in the finality, natural health of body and mind and masterly character developed in the conditions in which we exist.

Rest Is a Phase of Exercise 

Rest is really a phase of exercise. Strictly speaking, there is no such a state as that of rest. That which we describe as rest, chiefly because differentiating it from exercise tends toward clarity in everyday experience, is an interval of activity; it is, as it were, an insertion of time into exercise. We see that in the large functioning of nature there are chemical and other conditions which resemble rest, but which, on scrutiny and analysis, are but fluctuations of activity in which the factor of time is interposed. The interplay of exercise and rest in the human organism is, in fact, an alternation in activity exclusively, with the time-factor indicating the spacing. Rest, therefore, we shall consider as a modified form of activity, a temporary change in the degree of activity whose object is constructive. 

CHAPTER XIV. 

Oh! To Rust Rather Than Wear

Inter-related with exercise as a requisite for complete and balanced natural health is its opposite-which is rest. Rest in its various forms is as indispensable as exercise. And rest, like exercise, must be balanced.

There should normally be a natural reciprocity between exercise and rest, each redounding in the interaction to the improvement of general health to the normal prime and the preservation of this state.

Page 257

As an example, let us take an industrial machine.

Even this solid structure requires rest. While the rest is not the same kind which the human body needs, it is conformably like the latter. The parts in the factory machine loosen after it is operated for a long time; there is wear at the joints; in particular, the friction caused by the movement and motion softens and wears the metal. The machine has to be stopped at intervals in order to adjust and tighten the parts, and temporarily eliminate the friction and its destructive consequences. Every once in a while the machine must be overhauled completely and put into proper condition. 

Were the machine operated continuously, without ceasing, it would wear out, break down or fall to pieces.

My concept is that rest and exercise should be so alternated that the one reciprocates with the other in a normal equilibrium. Bear in mind in this connection that 

I maintain that exercise is a normal necessity and functioning, and in effect is of constructive benefit to the whole body.

By rest I do not imply sleep alone. Repose of the whole body, or of any part of it, is rest, and in some ways this rest is frequently as invigorating as sleep. 

Change in action is a kind of rest. For instance, if you shift from sitting to walking or standing, or from standing or walking to sitting, you are resting. You may rest your eyes, for example, by veering their focus from one object to another. The mind is rested by changing from a profound subject to a light one, or vice versa. Almost any diversion is rest. Page 258

Edison Believed in the Open Eyes

The apparently minor and inconsequential intervals of rest which it is possible to obtain during the day are of much physical value. To some extent and in some ways they are more effectual than sleep. This is so because they give the repose at the moment when it is most needed, and thus they stave off fatigue and maintain the physical balance without the least reactionary effect. It is even held by many, among them being the late Thomas A. Edison, that we could do without sleep if we took rest aright. "Nothing", Mr. Edison said, "is more dangerous than too much sleep, except, perhaps, too much stimulation. 

The man who sleeps too much suffers from it in many ways and gains nothing from it. The average man who sleeps seven, eight or nine hours daily is continually oppressed by lassitude. There is really no reason why men should go to bed at all. A million years from now we won't. Sleep is an absurdity, a bad habit. We can't throw it off suddenly, but we will eventually". 

Mr. Edison himself reduced the "bad habit" to a sleeping period of four hours in the twenty-four. He thus gave to the service of his fellow men, in his inventive toil, the equivalent of many hundred added working days. Had Mr. Edison gone into the matter with some other purpose than that of saving time for business, he might have concluded that the danger is not too much sleep in the aggregate so much as it is too much sleep at one time.

We could avoid this danger by getting the restoration that moderate sleep periods or intervals give to the Page 259

system.

Our short and frequent sleep periods could be part of a system for obtaining rest as needed. 

I propose that we alternate activity or exercise and rest more frequently, with recurrent brief exercise periods to remove the naturally created wastes of embedded cell-structures, and with frequent nourishment and short-sleep periods, to give just enough rebuilding, and not too much, after each period of activity. 

Man Can Sleep Like the Horse 

The argument might be submitted in reason that if active diversion affords rest in periods of exercise, similar effects would be realized from intermittent brief intervals of sleep. This is the same as saying that we would vary rest as we vary activity. The logic of this is apparent when you consider that excess of rest is as detrimental as excess of exercise.

The idea is not so absurd as it may seem to be. On the contrary, it is quite consistent with nature. Have you not seen horses asleep in harness on business streets while their drivers were off the box? Thus they take intervals of sleep. Dogs and cats, as you know, are alert day and night; many, in fact, seem to be awake all the time; many sleep soundly only for brief periods, while they are, at other intervals, only dozing lightly. 

They mix wakefulness and sleep by alternation through day and night. At least, we have proof from nature that adequate sleep is obtainable in snatches.

Allow me to remind you of an explanation which I previously made, which is that rest is really a temporary mutation of activity, that is, that rest is a diminished form of activity in the transition of time and Page 260

constructive natural functioning. There is no cessation to activity, but simply a deviation of it. The heart-beat gives us a plain exemplification of this concept. The apparent interruption to the beats, indicating regularity, is tantamount to a timing of two degrees or variations of activity. The intervals which divide the beat are moments of rest, but also fluctuations in activity.

I would emphasize to you the possibilities of procuring sufficient rest by means of brief intervals of sleep and diversification of activities. The competency of this kind of rest should be greater, in comparison with that of long and unbroken sleep, because the relaxation would be taken at the moment of need of restorative vigor or energy. We are accustomed to taking stimulation at moments of threatened tiring, whereas recuperation by brief rest would be more in accordance with nature.

Everybody has noted the resuscitating effects which he gets from a sleep of five, ten or fifteen minutes' duration when feeling worn out. He has noted how the variations of activity by intellectual or physical diversion have revived his vigor and spirit. After such rest, or variation of activity, or diversion, he has felt freshened and able to resume his occupation more efficiently. 

Don't Sleep at Business Session 

I would scarcely advise you to take a visible sleep or rest, howsoever brief, while attending to an important business matter. If you were present at a business conference, it would not be advisable, and probably would not be safe, to remark "Excuse me, gentlemen, I need

Page 261

a snooze", and lie on the table and go to sleep for a few minutes, or doze off while seated in your chair.

The others would take care of the business while you were in slumber, and the rest might not be remunerative to you. However, it doubtless would benefit you to rest for a little while prior to the conference. 

Another benefit of the brief intervals of sleep throughout the twenty-four hours, in comparison with the long sleep at night, is that a sufficient number of short sleeps is virtually effortless and consequently fully efficacious. Two beverage manufacturers accentuate the idea of rest and refreshment by pause and reinvigoration in their advertising. One alludes to "The Pause that Refreshes" and another advises refreshments at "10, 2 and 4”.

For the long sleep an extra quantity of nutrition is required to be stored in advance, in order to sustain general functioning in the body, and upon awakening, the body, affected by the excess of rest, the internal effect of which is toxic, is prompted to counteract the condition otherwise by excessive physical labor or effort. 

Sleep, I might explain, continuing the comparison, is the abnormal rest of an abnormal organism, superinduced by the abnormal wastes of abnormal exercise and abnormal rest at abnormal alternations during wakefulness for the purpose of rebuilding the cell-structures of organs and tissues. Sleep, which is subrelaxation, not only increases the cell-structure waste of abnormal rest at wakefulness, but also disturbs the needed quiet for the cell-structure repair of organs and tissues at abnormal exercise during wakefulness, and energy generated during sleep is not otherwise diverted. Page 262

Sleep, or subrelaxation, slows down the circulation of the blood, so that the cell-structure supplies are sent to the countless cells abnormally slowly for competent repair; and such slower movement, on the other hand, causes much retention of venous blood wastes or poisons (auto-intoxication).

Why Some Persons Are Dull 

Sleep may be termed a species of coma or paralysis, the co-partner of death, as it stupefies activities which are so necessary for the recuperation of organism power and wisdom. Sleep negates the consciousness activity of guidance or sensing natural opportunity, and of control in opportunity adaptation; therefore, the ageing process continues.

Sleep is a state which promotes incompetency of structure and inefficiency of functioning, leading to self-destruction disorderliness; hence, the imparting externally of war-conflicts or social debilitation of form and diseases of character, as between man and man. 

The superpassivity of sleep desecrates nature, as it destroys the organisms' balanced evolution through lack of compensatory superactivity during wakefulness. Sleep overworks the organism during wakefulness to provide, as I have said, for conservation of supply during sleep, which is a reconversion of a conversion, or 50 per cent waste of life-energy functioning power in such instances.

We see that while excess of work is injurious by the wear-and-tear process of disintegration in the cell structures, excess of rest, especially of sleep, is destructive by Page 263

a noxious process. Excessive rest poisons the cellstructures and practically rots them away. 

Obviously, it should be possible to equalize rest of itself, without immediate relation to exercise, by brief intervals of sleep and diversion during the twenty-four hours. At first, of course, we could not dispense with long sleep; but each might cut it down gradually through adjustments suited to his own manner of living. Edison thought that mankind might get along without sleep in a million years from now; but at present we might reduce the long sleep as much as an hour or two by forming new habits of rest by the interval and diversion plan, and thereby obtain more effectual rest.

The principle which refers to exercise and rest is that the two are compensating opposites in creating and maintaining balanced evolution in organs and in functioning. The cell structure is purified by removal of waste at normal exercise and is rebuilt at normal rest. 

As abnormal exercise cumulatively depletes the cellstructure until it disintegrates by eruption, and as abnormal rest, after cell-structure repletion, prevents the thorough removal of cell-wastes at completion, causing disintegration by collapse from poison retention, it follows that normal exercise and normal rest should normally alternate in balanced evolution. 

CHAPTER XV. 

The Constant Prime from Efficiency 

A prime organism is a conjunctive whole of all cell structures at competency and of all cell-consciousnesses at efficiency. Consciousness or functioning memory of Page 264

all cells is the true guidance or sensitory receptivity. 

This faculty provides a natural opportunity, composed of countless supplementary opportunities, and is the true control or motor delivery; it urges a natural discipline, composed of countless supplementary disciplines, for beneficially adapting natural opportunity to the organism for sustaining balanced evolution. 

Natural alternation of exercise and rest re-educates the natural or instinctive consciousness of the cellular functioning, so that normal depletion and repletion are achieved spontaneously. This cellular consciousness is a phase of the cosmic rhythm. The proper alternation of exercise and rest trains the consciousness of all cell functioning memories to that orderliness of instinct or automatic habit which guides the organism by sensing natural opportunity, and naturally controls the organism by sensing natural discipline in adapting natural opportunity.

Thus, a free transmission of life-energy functioningpower and a free circulation of secretions provide the organism with natural power facilities for functioning in unison, while natural waste and natural supply meet organism requirements. The physical situation is that of one group of organs and tissues at a natural freedom of enforced rest for natural repair, and another group at a natural obligation of enforced exercise for natural cleansing and purification of wastes. 

Natural guidance, of course, alternates the natural discipline to prevent undue depletion of cell structure from excessive or improper exercise, which causes the cell structure to disintegrate by eruption, and also prevents the retention of poisons, after the normal reple- Page 265

tion of cell-structure at excessive or improper rest, which progressive corruption causes the cell-structure to disintegrate by collapse.

Precision of Opportune Functioning 

Efficiency is composed of two factors. The first is the sensory receptivity, which provides the organism with natural opportunity; the second is the motor delivery, by which the organism avails itself of the natural opportunity provided, without derangement of form or disease of functioning. Thus there is natural discipline. 

Natural opportunity is that which enables the organism to create a natural waste, evacuating it immediately at completion, and then to adapt a balanced supply as the waste is evacuated. This is the natural opportunity of true guidance.

The natural opportunity of true control is that which simultaneously amplifies a natural exercise for organism parts which are at exercise, so as to remove wastes, while it amplifies natural rest for parts which are at rest, so as to rebuild cell-structure. Then, to alternate from natural exercise to natural rest, before the cell-structure is depleted to a thinness at which it would erupt, and to alternate from natural rest to natural exercise, before the cell-structure is repleted to a thickness at which it would not exacuate all poisonous wastes, at which stage there would be disintegration by collapse.

The human organism must function steadily and continuously in the generation of life-energy functioning power, of body heat, of body liquids, and so on; therefore, when most organs and tissues are at enforced Page 266

rest in reduced functioning, in order to rebuild their structures, a smaller group should be at enforced exercise, in advanced functioning. At the major condition of exercise, when most organs and tissues are at enforced activity in the advanced functioning for creating and eliminating structural wastes, simultaneously a smaller group is at enforced rest in the state of reduced functioning for rebuilding its structures. 

The interplay between activity and rest is a natural compensatory co-ordination of opposites toward bringing about a balanced situation in organs and tissues. It is a reciprocal interaction between compensating opposites, as a result of which the whole body is at normal and constant prime. The opposites necessarily must be equal in their effectiveness toward each other, as thereby the reciprocal interplay is balanced. 

Balance Between Exercise and Rest 

The twenty-four-hour day balance between the two is the objective of our prevailing habits. This is doubtless so because in our complex civilization we have regulated the rest wholly to the exercise in conformity with our convenience. Why should we not aim at a balance by the twelve-hour period, or the three-hour or the one-hour?

Mankind's program at present provides, ostensibly, for eight hours of rest. It is an outcome of the old division of the twenty-four-hour day into three equal parts, allowing eight hours for work, eight for play, and eight for rest. However, it is questionable whether many persons, with the possible exception of those who are engaged in salaried, routine employment, obtain as Page 267

many as eight hours of genuine rest. The custom is to try to get ten, twelve or even more hours of sleep in one day of every week, on the assumption that the rest obtained thus equalizes with the tax of the week's work and pleasure.

You do not need, even now, in your circumstances of habit, more than six hours of sleep per twenty-four hours, provided you are in sound health, and provided also you have learned to take rest by diversion of activities.

Both the time of activity and the time of rest, in general practice, are prolonged unnaturally, and this induces ageing and decay. It is the program of selfdestroying man. Abandoning it to seek the program of self-preserving man, we should modify it to some extent.

You are able to tell by natural instinct how much rest you need, and when you need it. Nature warns you when you are tiring, as she warns you when you are slipping physically. It is much easier to sense when you are tiring than when you are beginning to break down in health, for fatigue is a state of the moment, as well as, if not counteracted, a prolonged state. 

Another Surprise Impends at Home 

I leave it to you to decide how much rest you need, yet I caution you to take it when you need it. Do not procrastinate. Do not wait until tonight for it. Tonight you will have a social engagement; you may not be aware of this appointment at this second, but you will be notified of it as soon as you arrive in your home. 

You may take it for granted that you will not have the Page 268

opportunity to rest tonight. You will postpone it until later in the week, and, as you imagine, make it up in one night and part of a day. 

Oh, no! Do not deceive yourself! You will not make it up in one long slumber. As I have explained to you, that rest would be untimely and too extended, and its effects on your system would be destructive.

Train yourself, is my advice, to get rest at the moment when you feel fatigue coming, or when, as you know from self-knowledge, you are about to feel tired. 

Experiment for a while at taking brief, sporadic intervals of rest. If you cannot rest on a lounge, doze in a chair for a few moments. Sit, or even stand, in repose now and then in the day. Rest an arm, or a hand, or your feet, or your eyes. Straighten up your body again and again. Stop your work for a few seconds now and then. If it is possible for you to vary your work, do this off and on. Change your posture frequently. 

After you reach your home in the evening, resort to a little moderate exercise with the purpose of overcoming deformities or defects acquired in your occupation. And diversify your conversation, reading and recreation. Endeavor, in small ways, to rest and exercise all your organs and faculties. "There are no two human beings exactly alike," says 

Dr. Pohlman, whom I have previously quoted, "and the reactions of these dissimilar individuals to the same conditions of environment, inside or outside, are correspondingly dissimilar". The conditions of environment of two individuals are never exactly alike. You have physical and intellectual idiosyncracies, and so has every individual. As between two men who work in the Page 269

same office and reside in the same house, there is difference in environment. 

Passing It On to You 

I cannot address you with specific recommendations for improving or modifying your manner of living in order to better your health. I give you my concept and general precepts, and it is for you, yourself, to find a system of adaptation suited to your idiosyncracies and whimsicalities and the arbitrary circumstances of your environment.

I submit to you a concept, with natural principles and suggestions, coincident with natural requirements, and, leaving the health-restoring obligation to you, also leave to you the pleasure of elaborating a plan of your own to follow. I have no wish to standardize the human race by the clock and an obligatory schedule. 

Toward the end of this book you will find a schedule, which, as a standard, indicates how exercise and rest should be alternated, and with it are some very valuable food suggestions. I do not expect you to adopt this schedule, but to use it as a standard from which to prepare a plan suited to your circumstances. 

There is no other means of preserving health in the conditions of modern civilization than by individual hygiene and no other method than following nature. 

It is not that civilization is wrong, but that we are misusing and disusing its great advantages. The sooner that you take up the natural manner of living the sooner will you secure your health against the suicidal practices which are characteristic of the time. 

One of our worst mistakes in our advanced civiliza Page 270

tion is that of attempting to standardize the individual in the social and economic systems and machines. There is no particular harm done in standardizing fashion; it simply results in many women looking alike, walking alike, posing alike, gesticulating alike, talking alike, and so on, and all men looking and being alike. It disguises individuality and narrows intellectual interest.

Inasmuch, however, as the majority is pleased, who cares? Whether changing the color of the hair in obedience to the fickle whims of style, or having the face lifted, and making other alterations, renovations, innovations and decorations to nature (to use the terms of house decorators) are all right, we leave to physicians and individuals for a verdict. 

All Alike, Yet Different

In general, however, individuals cannot be standardized successfully or beneficially. They are naturally different as before explained. Their environments differ. At the most they are only capable of conforming to standardization of general social convention and general system and routine in business. It is the abnormal standardization, contrary to nature, in social and business affairs, which is introducing so many thousands to insane asylums, sanitariums, the last hope among the physicians and the scouts of the undertakers. 

You can, I am sure, preserve your health at constant prime by accepting my concept and following it as closely and faithfully as may be consistent with your circumstances, and add years to your life. It is not necessary for you to change your mode of living radically. 

It is enough for you to show respect to nature in your manner of living.

Page 271

CHAPTER XVI. 

Our complex civilization furnishes us extraordinary advantages of all kinds, and it is not the fault of our civilization that you and others pervert them. The advantages for better health and also for happiness are superior to those which former generations had, and they are more multifarious; they are relatively as advanced as our mode of living and our progress in business, science and invention. The truth is that we have been dazzled by our advancement and our conveniences and luxuries, and are living in a frenzy of more or less base and carnal selfishness. We are neither working prudently, nor living reasonably, and are more unfair to our fellow-men. 

Going Fast, But to Nowhere

You are but one among millions who are lured by false objectives. You strive for riches and pleasure, instead of plenty, health and happiness. Your ideals, like those of virtually all others, are mercenary and selfish. You like to be moving, whether you are going somewhere or not; and doing, whether you are achieving something worth while or not. You wish to "keep up with the Joneses", who wish to keep up with the 

Astorbilts. You are willing to live your life out in five or ten years; that is, you say that you are willing to do this. However, when you realize suddenly that you rushed through a few years and have done nothing but break down your health, your ideas are no longer highfalutin. 

You cannot realize too soon or too seriously that you, as well as most others, are making the worst of our advanced civilization, instead of the best. Remorse

Page 272

stalks a few feet ahead of you, and death a few feet behind him. You are defying nature in your mad manner of living, and the penalty is at hand. 

I have stressed to you the alternation of exercise and rest, as it naturally provides the physical poise for depletion of cell waste and the repletion of cell structure. The interaction is rhythmic in principle, and would be rhythmic in functioning, were you in the habit of assisting nature with common sense conformance with ordinary requirements. As closely as possible, the purpose is fulfilled when the alternation is opportune, that is, when rest is taken prior to indications of fatigue, and exercise before the lassitude of excess of rest is perceived. The alternation at intervals throughout the day is preferable to the prevailing method, and it is more efficacious.

You facilitate the natural process of removing the cellular waste and rebuilding the cell-structure. The exercise clears out the waste, and the rest repletes the cell-structure; it is natural decomposition and recomposition. The regular, normal alternation of exercise and rest of the whole organism produces constant prime, and all organs and tissues are sustained in balanced evolution, achieving equality in effectiveness as a result of compensating interplay between opposites. 

We Still Live a la Belshazzar 

Today man's span of life approximates that of 2000 years ago, which was three score and ten. A similar ageing process or method of progressive self-destruction or slow suicide prevails today as in the long ago. Page 273

This is not a favorable commentary on our muchvaunted claims of civilization enlightenment, in view of such futility of endeavor to benefit from the experience of intervening generations. 

Longer and better living not having been acquired in 2000 years, clearly demonstrates that for it to be acquired necessitates a revolution in the preconceived notions and prejudices of organism guidance and control. 

Such whipping of prejudices certainly proves that what all men in their sublime ignorance thought was right was wrong.

Self-destruction, in the present application, is the effect of misguidance of consciousness or functioning memory, in providing unnatural opportunities and uncontrol in adapting them by unnatural discipline; therefore, it follows that the madness of cherished false convictions, or the disorderly functioning of experience impressions, must be unlearned to develop self-preservation. To unlearn in this case is to learn what is not so, as thought-re-education toward the reality of fact negates the unrealities of fancies. 

Amateur Scientist Is at Least Unbiased

The unlearning of the irregularities of consciousness functioning, which are the prejudices of experience, should be more readily initiated and developed by the intelligent amateur than by the tradition-bound experts or learned specialists, whose training or learning has ingrained a more deepseated prejudice. That is, a more careful observance, clearer thought and shallower prejudice enable such an amateur to see fundamental facts just at hand in the primitive science of crude horse Page 274

sense, which cannot be visible to an expert who obscures a premise by the deeper-seated prejudice for abstruse theories, with formulas, complicated chemistry, laboratory paraphernalia, and so on, directed by a brain which already pretends to know what his eyes should see. A well-known man of affairs says that "The expert who knows why a thing cannot be done is civilization's heaviest liability”.

The human organism has a strange power of adaptation for partially remaking itself. Now and again degenerative diseases have been known to get better quite suddenly and disappear spontaneously. Such is its accommodation under favorable circumstances. Surely, as more favorable circumstances of sane and sensible living are afforded, the human organism, thus assisted, should more readily respond to this power. By the same ways and means of the manifest reduction of disease to latent disease of slower self-destructive effect, so should latent disease disappear spontaneously as sane living at prime is substituted. With a true and easy functioning in unison, an organism must automatically resist latent disease, and, with no latent disease from which to generate, the cause of manifest disease is removed.

CHAPTER XVII. 

When the Physician Should Ask Cash 

You may look upon me as an amateur in medicine or any other science. When sick I have gone to a physician; and when the physician gave me a prescription and advice, I took his medicine and followed his instructions. I don't go to him when I have a pain in the neck or feel a trifle woozy; not because I hate to part

Page 275

with the amount of his fee, but because, in my opinion, these minor symptoms are too insignificant to mention to a scientist. Why squander his valuable time?

I consult my physician in either of two conditionseither when I am really sick, or, in advance, to get his directions for warding off disease. 

I regard the physician as a learned and conscientious gentleman who deserves my highest respect as a man and a scientist. He is an ornament to society, as well as its servant. Men, women and children of all colors, creeds, conditions and caste-strata are human beings from his professional viewpoint. He fixes no metes and bounds to his charity. He is the guardian of the most valuable treasure in the universe, human life. He is prepared to sacrifice his health or comfort at any moment, and for a large stipend or none, as the case may be, to perform his duty. He puts no restraints on duty, which is as broad as the extent of service which all classes and sorts of humankind ask of him. I feel that I cannot praise him too highly, have too warm an affection for him, or respect him too much. 

I think that we do not award deserved credit to the modern physician. We compare him more or less disparagingly with the old type of family physician, who was a grand figure in the past. The latter we praise, and justly, as a martyr to humanity. No work was too hard for him, no sacrifice too great, no day long enough. 

He was a noble character.

The modern physician is a doctor of a changed society and a changed time. The family physician of today has as wide a range of practice as his predecessor, but his work is different. He concentrates his efforts Page 276

primarily on preventive medicine. He acquaints himself as nearly as possible with the idiosyncracies of families and of the individual members of families, and by sage advice and good treatment accustoms his patients to measures for preserving health and staving off disease. He is able to give the most efficient service to his patients within the scope of general medical practice. If we wish to valuate his service in monetary terms, we must conclude that he is underpaid as much as the old-type family physician.

The Surgery-made Man 

The largest, immediate benefits which the public has received from the medical profession are free. They consist of the discoveries for preventing disease. Approximately the whole gain toward prolonging the span of human life is due to advance in preventive medicine. 

We should credit this advancement and the offices accruing from it to the public service of the medical profession.

The modern family physician reaches the limit of his ability when, after training his patients in healthpreservation and disease-prevention, he successfully treats diseases which require active medical attention. 

Extraordinary cases he feels obliged to turn over to specialists, for a wider division of labor has come about in medicine and its scientific branches as a result of the expansion of science. It would be impossible for any man, howsoever brilliant, to be a Jack of all specialties. 

And the specialists can do no more than cure their patients. They go amazingly far in patching up, repairing, and remodeling human machines.

Page 277

Imagine a patient reconstructed after breaking down. 

One specialist gives him a new eye; another mends his heart; another removes his appendix; another reconditions his liver and kidneys; another installs new parts in his ears; an exodontist jerks out all his teeth and a dentist puts a new set in his mouth; another cuts out his goiter and another gives him several new glands; his legs and arms are repaired; another fixes up his stomach; another treats him so as to give him practically a new brain and nervous system, and several fill him up with serums. He is a new man. 

A new man! Had he followed the physician's counsel he probably would not have had to be repaired, remodeled or renovated. He would still have been himself, with no alien parts in him, and would have been healthy and young, that is, as young as he was healthy. 

The physicians had done their best for him with instruction and advice, but to no purpose; and it became necessary to graft reconstructive parts on him. 

Now that he is new, physician, surgeon and dentist have done their utmost for him, except to hope for the best. 

Might as Well Tell a Dictaphone

I have pictured this extreme case in order to bring home to you the fact that the good which the physician and surgeon can do for any one is restricted. The physician cannot act as guardian of each of his patients. 

He cannot give up his valuable time to the hearing of the thousand petty symptoms which his clients feel. 

He cannot afford to conduct office auditions for hypochondriacs and health-suicides. He should be an authority to whom we go when we have real physical sickness. Page 278

You see, when you visit a physician in his office, that many men and women look to him for exceptional sympathy. They yearn to tell somebody of their pains, ailments and symptoms, and they impose on the good nature of the physician, because, as he has regular office hours, they know that they will find him in, and, because he is long-suffering and kindly, he will hear the tale of woe with professional complaisance. I have often thought that the custom of a doctor of walking to the door with a departing patient was a product of evolution, and that his opening the outer door, or signaling an assistant to do so, was nearly a throw-back to primitive man. Many persons do not consider that the capacity of endurance of a physician is human. 

Objectively, the physician practices his profession for the welfare of mankind. First, he purposes by advice and instruction, as well as remedies, to promote health and prevent disease; second, he tries to effectivate cure of disease and restore health. He is the first and last authority on matters concerning health. 

Physician Cannot Be Your Chauffeur

But he cannot possibly substitute for his patients. He cannot run about with them and oblige them to follow his directions. He cannot take the medicines for them. 

He cannot endow them with common sense or think for them. He does his part, and relies on the patients to do their part. If they fail in their responsibility, his counsel and treatment amount to naught.

I have quoted several distinguished physicians and surgeons to the effect that the time is already here when the individual must embark in preventive medicine on Page 279

his own account. The physician does not want to be classed as a prestidigitateur, but as a scientist. He may recondition you if you are worn out, that is, put you in more or less fit shape again, or he may not; he may by his preventive offsets spare you from contagion or disease, or, on the other hand, he may not. No matter what he says or does, your health is your personal affair.

The physician judges by external indications or what you describe to him as your symptoms. He cannot possibly be familiar with your physical and mental idiosyncracies. You should know yourself; if you do not, take the blame on yourself.

Even as to the ordinary care of health, by which you keep in fit condition, in a state of constant prime, you are the one who must do the actual work. You must take the exercise and the rest, you must select and eat the food, you must plan and regulate your manner of living, you must do the things to safeguard health and prevent disease. As to rejuvenation, too, you must rely on yourself. You must undergo the treatment, and you must develop the correct habits for good health. 

Common Sense Instead of Science 

I do not suggest that you study materia medica. 

That is where the physician comes in, if you need him. 

But I tell you that the time is here, now, when you have to use common sense. As it seems to me, you have the natural instinct, and in addition reason and will, and you are supposed to use common sense and will in protecting your health.

The best physicians state that the worst diseases of Page 280

this time are more easily preventable than curable. 

They state that these diseases are brought on by desecration of nature, and can be prevented simply by following common sense. "Know thyself" is an old and honorable maxim, and so is "The Lord helps those who help themselves". Both apply to you in regard to the preservation of health and the prevention of the disease. According to the implication of renowned physicians, nothing else can prevent the terrible diseases of heart, nerves, kidneys and mind which result from the self-destructive habits which have grown up in our complex civilization. This is stating the case strongly and directly.

It is truth and fact, however. It is absolutely imperative to you to be your own doctor in forming habits to safeguard health. No other treatment will be effectual. The individual must take up preventive medicine by living along the natural lines. 

You Receive Your Sheepskin pre- 

Inasmuch as you are to be your own doctor of ventive medicine, practicing individual hygiene (John 

Everyman, D. P. M., doctor of preventive medicine), you should have some good scientific information and not be a quack to yourself. You must know where you belong in the universal scheme and what you are in it, and what you need in order to preserve your health.

Besides the alternation of exercise and rest, it is necessary to regulate your living habits to natural requirements, and to know what to eat and what not. You might, at present, smile were your physician to say "Eat sunlight", or sneer at the idea of proteins, vita Page 281

mins, and so on. Nevertheless, it is imperative for you to obtain basic knowledge as to the needs and functions of the body, food, air and sunlight. 

CHAPTER XVIII. 

The sun-worship superstition of tribes and races which had wandered from early civilization, scattered into various parts of the earth, and were lost in isolation, had some justification in untutored reasoning. This assertion may be shocking to the staid opinion that superstition is a product of ignorance; but it becomes plausible after we look into the motives of the sequestered families and bands whose sole guide was natural instinct.

Even in this day the great blazing sun is the god of the universe to savage and unlettered human stocks which survive in the crudest imaginable environment. 

The simple folk stand on mountain-tops, or on towers which they built besides lakes and rivers, and, with uplifted hands and hearts, flamed with hope, hail the resplendent dawn. Mounds of earth, which took years and the hardest kind of labor to construct, and other rude structures, all within the scope of their tools, skill and strength, rise above the surroundings as lookouts from which they glimpse the first faint glow in the eastern sky, and in rapture watch the luminous shafts increase and spread out over the convex roof of the world. 

Superstitions of the Gold-Digger

Thousands of persons who are vain of their refinement, intelligence and wealth, and glory in ostentation, are not as yet as far advanced in true culture and ra- Page 282

tional logic as the wilderness dwellers who salaam the sun. We brag of enlightenment, yet millions among us are existing in darkness, and there is not only more superstition than ever, but it is worse superstition, in that it is a subversion of common sense grounded in opinionated phariseeism.

Sun-worship prevailed in all parts of the world at one time, and it is still followed in many climes and lands. 

It is a singular fact that the tribes and races which idolized Sol were relatively the least ferocious and most intelligent, and this was so because the worship was a reasonable conclusion of a thinking mind. There was a minimum of superstition and a maximum of bold common sense in perceiving beneficence in the light and heat of the huge, gleaming orb, which in movement and radiation seemed to be animated with life, than in the fearful cringing to dumb, immovable things, man-made objects and fanciful ideas. I maintain that sun-worship was at least reasonable and natural, and typified exalting aspiration, expressing the highest sensations of natural human intelligence.

The Incas and Mayas, the Greeks and Romans, the Brahmins and Buddhists of India, and innumerable peoples looked up to the sun as a spirit of life. The emperors of China and Japan were descendants of the sun god. The Indians of North and South America were sun-worshippers. Old cities, which have been unearthed in Mexico in the past few years, reveal that the Aztecs, a highly civilized race, believed that the sun was a deity. Sculptures on buildings and tablets in different sections of Mexico depict the sun as a figure of life.

Page 283

What were the motives of the belief that the sun was the source of vitality? Worship of the sun was, primarily, the manifestation of the noblest idea and finest feeling in man; it indicated that the natural instinct in the mind sensed the existence of the author of the earth and soared reverently in an impulse to solve the great mystery and give homage. The same motive is, of course, notable in every spiritual effort among primitive people. 

And Some Now Have Lipstick Idols

Some races worshipped the moon. Some, the snake. 

Some this animal, some that animal. Some, the ocean, and some, lakes and rivers. Some, the winds. Some, a mountain. Others made unique figures consisting of united parts of different animals, and adored these images. Barbarous nations which developed materialistic civilization made their own gods and worshipped them, as many citizens of modern nations do. The exalting motive was the same with all. 

Men who lived close to nature observed that the earth was in gloom when the firmament was cloudy at night, and that the moon was pale, lusterless and inanimate in comparison with the sun. The sun was so bright and dazzling that it blinded them when they tried to look at it. Splendid as the moon was, it lacked warmth and brilliancy, and looked like a specter or shadow of the sun. They observed that the seasons changed with the moods of the sun. They saw that growth and bloom on the earth appeared and vanished in conformance with some force emanating from the sun. Although their deductions were not formed scientifically, they figured out in reason the chemical action Page 284

of the sun on the earth and the effects of this chemical action on beings and things. They theorized that the sun dominated the earth and the moon and all visible nature, and they felt that it somehow controlled the life in themselves. They reasoned out with their common sense what science teaches us today, that the earth, the moon, they and all nature were constituents of the solar system. 

Of all forms of idolatry, sun-worship was the most reasonable and uplifting. It elevated men's eyes up to the sky and to the light in the east, and vision blended with their reason. The sun, they perceived, was the power in their visible environment. The sun shone and its fiery darts quivered with vitality, and they saw this life which appeared to flash from the moving sun and felt it. The natural instinct in them to pay homage to God expressed itself in honor to the sun.

The idolatry was not naturally unreasonable. It was a natural response to a spiritual prompting. Groups among the sun-worshippers were fanatical. But in the main it was as inspiring as was naturally possible, and the general idea led uneducated men to understand the chief principles of the natural order and their operation. 

Sun Worshippers Would Be Shocked 

Excepting those who think that living basely is living naturally, and who, therefore, exemplify a degradation of civilization which would horrify the sun-worshippers, we of this era regard the sun as the center of that section of the universe in which we have our being. 

We see that the sun-worshippers, although mispurposed in idolizing the radiant sphere, were correct in deduc- Page 285

tions which would be thoroughly scientific at this time, which are that our lives, and our health, our comfort, and, to a great extent, our happiness, are intimately associated with the sun.

The scientific theory which is most generally approved by leading savants is that the earth is a castoff of the sun. It is supposed that a large star eons ago passed by the sun and in doing so caused a physical disturbance in the sun, and consequently fragments of the sun flew into space, where, in a balance between the forces of the sun and of the passing star, and perhaps also the forces of other heavenly bodies, these fragments became the earth. This new planet gradually developed an orbit and rotation of its own. 

At any rate, the earth, with the moon and other planets, is part of the system of which the sun is the dominant orb. The sun itself is a star, like the other stars which twinkle out in space. It is larger than a few, but smaller than most. The earth, notwithstanding certain natural laws which might be described as earthly idiosyncracies, which are specifically natural as far as the earth is concerned, is subject to the sun. The sun is the control sphere in our solar system. 

Greater Than Hollywood's or Broadway's

Man, therefore, is a unit of the solar system, having his residence on the earth. His star is the sun. Like the earth he is subject to the sun, while, at the same time, being under the immediate influence of the earth's idiosyncracies, he is subject directly to his environment. 

The whole environment is solar. "The sun," says Dr. Joseph Barrell, professor of

Page 286

structural geology in Yale University, "is a star, and is but one among the millions of stars, and, though apparently so great as seen from the earth, is mediocre among them in size and brightness. The origin of the earth is evidently bound up with the origin of the other planets and all in the history of the sun.”

Besides the earth, our solar system comprises the minor planets, Mercury, Venus, the Moon and Mars, and the major planets, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune. 

All the planets mentioned, including the earth, form a common system, which is, to us, our solar system. 

They are all accessory to the sun, from which they obtain light and by which they shine. The sun itself is a member of the great stellar system, comprised of countless millions of stars, many of which are so far away as to be invisible by telescope and never can be seen by human eye. 

Rain of Fire on the Earth

Besides the influence of the sun and the whole solar system in general, we are, as I have shown, subject to the influences showered on us from the entire stellar system. Dr. Millikan holds that the cosmic rays, according to conclusions drawn from his investigations, emanate in the outer universal space, beyond our solar system, and stream on the earth on all sides incessantly and regularly.

I mention the foregoing facts to emphasize again to you that man is an entity in the universal cosmos. He is a member of our solar system in the same general way as Mars, Jupiter and the moon, and the great stellar system in the same general way as the sun. Small as he Page 287

is in size, with his average height of 5 feet, 7 inches, in the relation to the sun, 866,400 miles in diameter, he is the most important entity in the solar system on account of his higher intelligence. 

CHAPTER XIX. 

You might muse with much profit to your higher intelligence itself on the grandeur and stupendousness of the vast universe. You may obtain a vague, a very vague idea of the infinite by contemplating the mysteries in the heavens. Space itself appears to be endless. Space is something, although it is seemingly nothing, and this nothing is something whose interminable reaches are so extensive as to paralyze our efforts to imagine distances which cannot be imagined by the finite mind. We think of nothing, which is something that goes undecipherable distances into nowhere. In that enormous nothingness are sparkling lights and dark bodies, visible and invisible, so innumerable as to be uncountable; and each giant orb is so colossal that we cannot possibly fancy its proportions in an understandable idea.

Man is the superior being in the great universe, small, comparatively, as he is. It is well for him to ponder of this as he beholds the wonders of nature by day and night. On the other hand, it is well for him to think of himself in a comparative relationship with any of the huge entities in immeasurable space, and consider his tininess and unimportance physically. 

Lilliputian Shakes His Fist at Cosmos

If, he may reflect, each of those whirling spheres must obey the cosmic principles, observing order and rhythm in the scheme of nature, how dare he oppose Page 288

the unswerving purpose which his higher intelligence interprets to him? He has free will in order to live naturally and nobly with keen appreciation of the beauty and harmony which pervades the universe. 

Nature does not heed his decision as to his own pleasure or depravity, but follows the cosmic principles, and, if he lives in contempt of laws which the stars and planets and the earth must follow, penalties will be exacted of him commensurate with his higher intelligence.

From the cosmic viewpoint exclusively, without considering the spiritual, it is almost unthinkable that human beings should desecrate nature by living improperly. It is almost inconceivable that one of the smallest entities in nature, less than a speck in comparison with the sun, has the audacity to defy nature. 

The sun-worshippers were ignorant and superstitious, but they used their intellectual gifts rationally. They were both wiser and better than the modern goldworshippers, and less superstitious. The gold-worshippers have not the excuse of ignorance, as they exist in a state of civilization in which enlightenment is practically forced on them. 

I would not ask any one to worship the sun or self.

Self is a mean idol, worse even than business or wealth, because self-worship is an ingrown capitulation to insatiable desires and vanities which antagonize nature and cause pollution inwardly and outwardly. But I would ask all to regard the sun as the star of salubrity. 

Reference: Any Man's Wife 

The average man does have a sort of self-idolatry and he invents a score of personal idols, among his crav- Page 289

ings. He spawns a lot of bugs of superstition from his self-centered desires. And there are thousands among us who have less common sense, to say nothing of spiritual aspiration, than the sun-worshippers. Outside the foolishness of their idolatry (which, at that, is better than many modern forms of idolatry), the sun-worshippers have opinions concerning the sun which are ratified today by advanced science.

It should be convincing enough for me to repeat that the sun is the head of our solar system. The solar system, as I have outlined, is a small universe unto itself. 

It is a stellar system in the cosmic stellar system. The earth and the planets and planetoids which I have mentioned are bound up in the history of the solar system, and altogether they are our little universe, with the sun as the center.

The light and heat which are necessary to life come from the sun, and it is not too much to say that none of us could, without the several benefits derived from the sun, have life or health. The properties of the sun are the vital principles, or collectively the primary vital cause, in the existence of animals and vegetation. In a general sense, the sun is the active cause of life, inasmuch, at least, that there could be no life on earth without the energies and forces radiated and transmitted by the sun. 

Square Deal for Father Sol 

I request you, therefore, to think of the sun as life and health. I ask you to think, by contrast, as the lack of the sun's properties signifying decay, dissolution and death. Think of the earth itself always in an active Page 290

relationship to the sun. The sun takes part in every action and change in every being and thing on the earth. 

It is likely that the earth and other planets in the solar system would crash in space, if the sun, their stabilizing control and vital force, were annihilated; but we may imagine, for illustration, the sun becoming as dead. as a planet. Then there would be absence of light and heat, energy and probably also force, on the earth, and life would disappear completely. So, you must consider the sun as typifying the active principle of life. of Let us imagine that the sun is black in death. The light is extinguished. The fire is cold. Such fundamental change in so great and important a body would cause changes equally great in the cosmic forces operating among the stars and planets in a wide extent of space, and cataclysm would occur in or among many the large orbs. The sun and the earth, the moon and the several planets of the solar system would be hurled to destruction. But, although the cataclysm would begin to happen immediately, there would be some lapse of time prior to the crisis on the earth; either before the planets would blow up and disintegrate, or before they would collide with other celestial bodies, they would be fugitives in space for a long or short time. 

Picture to yourself conditions on the earth then. 

Gloom more dense than that of night would shroud the world to every nook and corner. As the source of light had been darkened, it would be impossible to generate electricity or strike a light, in a short while, that is, as soon as the conserved energy in matter had become exhausted. As the fire had been extinguished, there would no longer be heat in the earth after that con- Page 291

served had been consumed. The earth would have neither light nor heat. 

Too Little Equals Too Much

Vegetation which shrivels from too much heat would shrivel from too little. Animals would starve from lack of heat and light, as well as from want of food. 

Man would die from cold, hunger and madness. Life would become extinct in a short time, and while it was dragged out in the miserable conditions just described the human state would be worse than death. 

The illustration gives us a concept in reality of the scientific statement that the earth and the large and small planets of the solar system are attendant on the sun and their history is wrapped up in that of the sun. 

It shows that all living and inorganic matter on the earth owes nature and character to the action or influence of the sun. Life is a product of the action of the sun, and its continuance depends on the vital supply provided by the sun's action. The sun is indispensable to human life.

We perceive the actualities of the sun in regulating life and change on the earth in our regular experiences. 

We understand instinctively that the heat and light of the sun are necessary to the sustenance of life in the animal and vegetable kingdoms. We know instinctively that the influence of the sun on inanimate matter is similar. We behold the regulated succession of life and death and of material transition in the seasonal changes, and from waking spring to dormant winter we note in the seasons themselves the testimony of the active power of the sun. We count our years, as Page 292

we count our days, by the light of the sun, and we think as to ourselves that we are wrapped up in the history of the sun as closely as huge Jupiter or the dewsprinkled bud on which the sun's rays are now glittering. 

More Light on the Heated Subject

The heat and energy in all matter, quick and dead, are received from the sun. The heat is transmitted to the earth by the infrared ray and the energy by the ultraviolet, these being the two principal rays of Sol. 

The ancients, while they had not reasoned out the scientific theories, had observed the chemical actions produced by the rays of the sun, and it was from this idea that they came to call the earth Mother Earth and to think of the sun as Father Sun. Many of the myths which sprang up were poetical efforts to idealize the natural beneficence of our star.

Inasmuch as life is a product of the sun, so must health be. The rays which by their activities grow the foods which sustain our bodies, must themselves be foods for us as they are for fruits and vegetables. 

It is necessary for the preservation of our health that we should obtain our light and heat as food directly from the sun. "The ultra-violet ray, termed the actinic ray," says Dr. Frank McCoy, "is very important to the maintenance of all animal life. Idea Perhaps Explains Caste?

Dr. Assar Hadding, a noted Swedish geologist, speculating on the origin of life on the earth, treats interestingly on the vitalizing effects of the sun's rays. His theory was submitted to the Smithsonian Institution in Washington, which published it in December (1931). 

Taking the old assumption that life first appeared in salt water, Dr. Hadding suggests that it probably appeared in salt pools which had formed in the North and South Polar regions. These pools of salt water were in that remote time warm and muddy, and he conjectures that the conditions in them were right for the formation of protoplasm by chemical action. Of course, the changes between heat and cold and the chemical action which produced life were results of the sun's action. "We depend on the sun for life, warmth and all mechanical and electrical powers," says Charles C. Abbott, secretary of the Smithsonian Institution. Coat of Rouge or Bleach of Tan

CHAPTER XX. 

How does sunlight enter the human system? What is the means of ingress? You have the answer ready. 

The skin.

I enabled you to reason this feature out simply and quickly because I believed that the conclusion would thus be accentuated to you. Like most of us, the skin, to you, has seemed to be no more than a thin, hard surface or permanent coating to the flesh. The skin, we think, is the outer finish to the body, which gives the body a presentable appearance.

Page 307

Trifle Causes Heart Disease 

Would you doubt my wisdom if I told you that neglect of the skin causes heart disease? I concede that a statement to this effect would strike one as being preposterous. You are aware that one may become infected through the skin or in the skin, but it is not so clear how heart disease could result from an unkempt condition of the skin. 

Professor S. Metalnikoff, the renowned Russian specialist, who is connected with the Institut Pasteur in Paris, is the authority who states that heart disease is often due to neglect of the skin. 

In preceding chapters I made two important points which I wish to recall to you. I said that our organs are more competent as a result of use, but wear out or become inert from disuse. "It has been known since the time of the great French biologist, Lamarck", says Dr. Metalnikoff, "that use causes every organ to become stronger and more vigorous, while absence of exercise and activity renders it inefficient and brings about atrophy. "By living in warm dwellings and covering ourselves with clothing we condemn our skin to constant inactivity. In the course of time, especially in elderly people, the skin gradually falls into complete decay and ceases to react with power and energy characteristic of the skin of a young organism. "Not only the skin itself, but the entire organism, and especially our vascular system, suffer from this. 

As is known, the skin contains an enormous network of large and small blood-vessels which bring nutrition Page 308

to the skin. Under the influence of inactivity the walls of the vessels lose their elasticity and power to react against external stimuli. This condition leads to premature sclerosis of the vessels and causes the diseases of the heart which are so prevalent among members of the professional classes."

Immunizing Through the Skin The part which the skin plays in health is shown by the fact that smallpox, anthrax and many other diseases are contracted through it, and, on the other hand, that the skin, when in healthy condition, is an armor against disease. Metchnikoff advanced the theory that the chief protective agents of the skin are the phagocytes, which are conveyed to the skin with the blood. Bezredka demonstrated the theory that the skin itself takes part in defensive resistance to reactions. 

To prove his conclusions Bezredka experimented with guinea pigs. It was impossible to immunize them with attenuated Pasteur cultures. The attempt to immunize them against anthrax, to which they are unusually susceptible, by rubbing vaccine into the skin, was successful. He found that immunization of the skin provided permanent defense against specific attacks. 

The sensory nerve-endings which permeate the skin. conduct various sensations, as, for instance, of heat, cold, pain, touch. The skin protects the body against parasites. "Apart from this", says Metalnikoff, "there are other important functions of the skin. One of these is respiratory: The skin absorbs oxygen and discharges carbon dioxide. It also serves to regulate the temperature of the body. This is effected by the activ- Page 309

ity of the sweat glands. At high temperature the perspiration becomes more vigorous and causes increased evaporation, which, in its turn, leads to a lowering of the temperature of the body. 

Active Part Played by the Skin "Lastly, there is the excretory function, many harmful products of metabolism being eliminated from the organism in the sweat. The skin shares this function with the kidneys and thus relieves the latter of some of their work. The magnitude of this work is illustrated by the following figures: An adult loses about 

1-67 of his weight, or one kilogram in fluid, through the skin per day. "The skin thus serves not only as a protective tegument, but also as an organ of definite physiological processes of vast importance in the life of the organism. "Its role is of special importance in the defense of the organism against various parasites and microbes, i. e., in immunity, both natural and acquired. If man is able to protect himself against the invasion of the numerous microbes, this is due entirely to the defensive action of the skin. "The skin contains an enormous army of phagocytes, some of which are motile and others stationary, which rapidly engulf and digest the microbes that have penetrated into the skin."

Dr. Metalnikoff describes the resistance of the skin as a battle. "In cases", he pictures, "when the microbes prove to be very resistant, and the local phagocytes are unable to overcome them, numerous minute phagocytic cells or microphages come to their assistance. These Page 310

cells are always present in the blood, and, when the necessity arises, they penetrate through the walls of the vessels and attack the affected site. "A real battle then ensues, which frequently ends in the destruction of the microbes. Sometimes, however, the microphages prove to be unequal to the struggle and perish themselves. Then large numbers of macrophages, or large phagocytes, appear on the scene. 

These cells, which are capable of stronger resistance, join the issue and often ingest and destroy the weakened microphages together with the microbes". 

Skin Regulates Body Temperature 

"It is thus seen", Dr. Metalnikoff continues, "that the skin is an organ of extraordinary complexity. It respires and excretes the noxious products of metabolism, i. e., it relieves the lungs and kidneys of part of their work. Then the skin regulates the temperature of the body. It is furthermore supplied with sensory nerve-endings, which evoke in it the sensation of cold, heat and pain, and thus regulate the activity of the entire organism through the central nervous system. "The skin is one of the most necessary and active organs of protection against all kinds of parasites and microbes, all of which are constantly trying to penetrate into the organism". "When one understands the excretory functions of the skin", says Dr. Frank McCoy, "it is necessary to see how the skin must be kept healthy to insure general bodily health. It is also apparent that if the skin is stimulated to an unusual degree, its functions can be extraordinarily increased, so that there will be which Page 311

might be called even unnatural but helpful throwing out of toxins".

The skin is more than a thin sheet, stronger than steel, though pliable and sensitive, defending the body against disease, and more than a sensory shield for the body, receiving and transmitting signals and indicating symptoms. It also exercises constructive properties of several kinds, and displays resistance and immunization to disease of a positive nature. 

How to Eat Sunlight

That the skin must have sunlight and air is obvious; as it is obvious that the sunlight and air are most effective on the skin. The skin should be kept clean, so that free air and energizing sunlight may penetrate the pores. And everyone should take frequent air-baths and sunnings.

Therefore, in order to eat sunlight, which is absorbed principally through the skin, the skin must be clean. 

Any coating on the skin is an armor against the energizing darts of the effulgent distributor of health beams. 

Sunlight is a food indispensable to prime health. It must be eaten or used wisely, just as we have to be reasonably particular in the choice of meats, vegetables and other foodstuffs.

Food should not be distasteful. It is a mistake to believe that wholesome food is more wholesome if it is unpalatable. As I shall explain later in another chapter, proper food can and should be agreeable. We cannot subsist without eating, and I should be the last person to wish to make a penance of a necessity. Page 312

CHAPTER XXI. 

Do most of us eat to live or live to eat? I should say that most, while we eat to live, have a penchant for the foods and delicacies which appeal to the palate. We eat to live, but also live to eat, and we are so firmly set in this attitude that we measure off the day by three meals and perhaps a few luncheons, and devour much time daily in dreaming about what probably will be served to us at the table at home and what we shall select at noon in the restaurant, or the high-seat counter, or sweets-salon (or drug store).

Taste and the Domestic Exchequer 

What to eat is the most momentous problem of every family and individual from day to day. From the viewpoint of self-gratification it is more serious than that of budget. Were the mathematics of the budget the hardest phase, the solution would be easy, as we should merely set aside so much money and buy so much food. But the question which makes the matter extremely difficult is to regulate the cost of food to the combination of taste and appetite.

We are a nation of good eaters and big eaters. While we are busy we are exhilarated by happy fancies of food to be eaten, or unforgettable memories of food that we liked; and when we are not working and simultaneously meditating in delight of delicious preparations and desserts, we are actively eating. Eating is, in fact, the principal function in our existence. 

We eat before the play or concert and afterwards, and between breakfast and luncheon, and between luncheon and dinner; we eat going to and from the Page 313

GREATER CHEER--GREATER EFFICIENCY 

street car or garage; we munch while listening to the radio and reading the newspaper, magazine or book. 

While, in these circumstances, it cannot be denied that we eat to live, it is clear that we like to eat. 

It is my observation that practically all of us eat with our eyes. It occurs to but few of us to eat with our brains. The majority doubtless thinks that the brain is to be used for other purposes, and it is unnecessary to use intelligence in eating; this may be so; at any rate, the brain is seldom used in eating, whereas the eyes are always functioning alertly in sanguine desire to cater pleasingly to the appetite. 

Eyesight Is Artistic and Fastidious

We are epicures with our eyes. We eat colors and designs and visual ensembles of patterns and decorations. If a blending of flashy hues on display attracts the greedy eyes, it is irresistible. A fabrication of four or five different pasty and fluffy substances of lavender, gold, silver and several more shades into the semblance of an airplane and a biped is called something a la Lindbergh; and it is an appetizing eyeful at any price. Or a purple stuffing in a browned duck may feaze the optics. Our eyes tell us how agreeable it is, and it seems as if the stomach has a sort of vision in the stimulation of the appetite. Dr. Carlson, having made a thorough study of the stomach in hunger, might give profound scientific attention to the eye and the appetite, and furnish the nation with a more or less patriotic treatise on the most popular American pastime, that of gorging an unfillable bag with optical harmonies in food incompatibles. He would find eccentric variations of rhythm in the stomachic region in response to distension and Page 314

brightening of the eyes at color schemes, fretworks, molds and plasms in food concoctions. 

We do practically all of our eating with our eyes. 

Almost any food is nutritious which captivates them; and the capacity of the stomach is elastic to the cajolery of the taste. Thus, we see, appetite is a visual ceremonial.

The culture which has superseded intelligence in eating is one of the most conspicuous traits of the advancement of civilization in the United States. Eating is, if possible, more complex than civilization. Art and invention have gone farther in the preparation of foodstuffs than in any other industry and profession, and the average person probably would eat a square of canvas on an atelier or a slice of paper from a polychrome advertisement from a billboard or magazine if the colors or the scrollery charmed the palate through the eye. 

White House Made Breakfast a Feast 

I would not venture to say whether the stomach is a slave to the eye, or the eye to the stomach. It would take a very searching investigation to settle this doubt. 

Certainly, in regard to eating the brain is a passive organ; and the will does the bidding of the optic nerves. 

It may be argued that in our efforts to save labor and increase our time for leisure, the human body needs more food and needs it more frequently, and our progress in culture instinctivates in us the artistic motive in food selection. In the practical way it appears to be a factitious deduction that we require more food in adverse proportion to the saving of labor and that there is no other means of employing our valuable leisure to Page 315

intellectual and physical benefit than in eating oftener and in doing so satisfying our artistic hankerings. 

We found that we could not give deep thought to business subjects of importance unless we assembled for conference at a luncheon. Business men got so at last that they would not dare to venture with a great enterprise without first discussing it at a luncheon or banquet. Finally, it became obvious that the opportunity for heavy thinking was not sufficient at luncheons and banquets, and the White House itself added breakfasts. 

Human Mind's Activities Between Meals 

The day's routine of the intelligent American is now made up about as follows: While dressing and shaving, wondering what there will be for breakfast; breakfast, considering or discussing the work to be done in the morning; 8 or 9 till 10, doing the work outlined at breakfast, also giving fleeting thoughts to colors and ideas in connection with hunger pangs coming to a peak at 10; 10, luncheonette, planning work for the next two hours; 10:15 to 12, performing the work planned at breakfast and luncheonette, and giving fugitive thoughts to lunch; noon, lunch, even greater ceremony than breakfast, outlining the work for the afternoon; 1:30 or 2 till 4, doing the work planned at luncheon, and now and then feeling hunger pangs; 

4, another luncheonette, devising work for the rest of the afternoon; 4 to 5 or 5:30, musing of golf, tennis or bridge and of dinner, and meanwhile doing some work; 6 or 6:30, dinner, recuperating from the day's work and trying to forget about it, and deciding whether to have a snack at home later in the evening or in a restaurant.

Page 316

Heavy stomach, heavy head! In other words, a head so heavy that it is too light for heavy thinking, instead of a stomach so light that the brain is capable of functioning. However, in the prevailing economic complex of machine production, business routine and standardized human employment, the fueling of the body may not have a cerebral aspect. If brains are not required for choosing food, we may afford to be as short on them as we are long on leisure and hunger. "The chemical substances of which the body is composed," says Dr. W. O. Atwater, special agent in charge of nutrition investigations, office of experiment stations, United States Department of Agriculture, “are very similar to those of the foods which nourish it." 

Here is a very strong statement by an authority. The implication is scientifically that the food supplied to the body should be the nutriment which the substances require. "They are," he continues, "made up of the same chemical elements, and hence the two may be discussed together." 

Implication Concerning Taxicab Drivers 

I notice many taxicab drivers entering and leaving "hamburger stands". Taxicab stations generally are near hamburger or hot dog stands, or vice versa (whichever way it may be). I seldom see a taxicab driver off duty without a hamburger or hot dog sandwich in his hand. I do not wish to hint as to what a biologist or chemist would conjecture from this notorious fact. If, I should say, a hamburger or hot dog, taken as luncheon, dinner and between-meals keeps a taxicab driver fit, he is no worse off than the more fortunate men and women who follow art in their eating and live by their eyes. Page 317

Stenographers, everybody must have observed, stay in mental and physical efficiency for their occupations by loading up on parfaits and various beautiful and fluffy pastry chiffons at noon. The men go to hurryup restaurants at noon and toss a ham sandwich, a portion of pie or ice cream and a crockery vessel of coffee into the digestive tract. 

It is useless for me to go to further length in order to remind you how most of us live. For further information I refer you to the menu cards of drug stores, cafeterias and hotels and to the show windows of bakery shops. The rest of the information you may get by collecting a few banquet and luncheon fare bills, and, if you are married, reading over the bills from the grocer, butcher and confectioner. 

I would suggest that you do read these cards and bills and show some curiosity in the food exhibits in front of different persons in hotels and restaurants. By noticing what persons live on, you may be able to form reliable conclusions as to their state of health, and their temperaments and dispositions, and also as to what not to eat in order to be healthy and happy. 

Do you think that it is far-fetched to infer that a person is pretty much what and how he eats? The reply must be that he cannot eat non-nutritious or incompatible food and still preserve good health.

What Man Is Or Should Be 

The eminent Dr. Pohlman, whom I have quoted several times in this book, states that man used to be guided greatly by his nose in eating. Man exercised his vetopower by means of his sense of smell. But the nose Page 318

would not be more trustworthy than the eyes at the present time. Unless a person eats with his brain in this synthetic-complex age, he eats to gratify his palate and his sense of vision, but not to live in good health. 

Speaking of the substances of which the human body is composed, Dr. Atwater says that "from fifteen to twenty elements are found, among the most abundant of which are oxygen, hydrogen, carbon, nitrogen, calcium, phosphorus and sulphur. The elements are so combined as to form a great variety of compounds in both body and food. The most important kinds of compounds in the body and in foods are protein, fats, carbohydrates, mineral matter and water". He explains that the functions of these compounds are "to build and repair the various tissues of the body and to supply it with heat and muscular energy.'
