That eating should be a deliberate act in the science of dietetics, instead of an amateurish encroachment on the fine arts, may be realized fully by taking two points into consideration. The first is that, as the body is composed of a number of elements in chemical combination and action, it follows logically that sound health cannot be maintained unless the foods eaten are such, in quantity and variety, as to sustain these elements in their natural proportion and normal condition. 

The second is that, as the digestive tract is the most important system in the body for the cell-structures, scientific selection of food as to quantity and variety is essential for preserving physical and mental fitness and efficiency. "Blood and muscle, bone and tendon, brain and nerve-all the organs and tissues of the body—are built Page 319

from the nutritive ingredients of food," states Dr. Atwater. "With every motion of the body and with the exercise of feelings and thoughts as well, material is consumed and must be resupplied by food. In a sense, the body is a superior machine. Like other machines, it requires material to build up its several parts, to repair them as they are worn out, and to serve as fuel. In some ways it uses this material like a machine; in others it does not.

Intake Stuff for the Human Engine 

"The steam engine gets its power from fuel; the body does the same. In the one case coal or wood, in the other, food, is the fuel. In both cases the energy which is latent in the fuel-the potential energy, as it is called in scientific language, is transformed into power and heat. "From the time foods are taken into the body until they are digested, absorbed, utilized, and finally converted into the carbon oxide and water vapor of the breath and the nitrogenous and other excretory products of the urine and feces, they undergo great chemical changes, very many of which liberate heat as a result of oxidation or some closely related process. "It is through these complex chemical processes that the body derives the energy for internal and external muscular work. Heat is evolved by such chemical changes and also results from the muscular work of the body; and there is reason to believe that within wide limits the heat thus produced is sufficient for maintaining body temperature. The amount of heat produced in the body must, of course, vary with the amount of food eaten, the work done, and other cir- Page 320

cumstances. However, the body is such a perfect piece of mechanism that the loss of heat by radiation, etc., is so adjusted to heat production that body temperature remains fairly constant."

Dr. Atwater reminds us of these facts in everyday language, which layman and scientist may understand. "The principal tissue former," he says, "are the protein compounds, especially the albuminoids. These make the framework of the body. They build up and repair the nitrogenous materials, as the muscles and tendons, and supply the albuminoids of the blood, milk and other fluids.

What Is Converted Into What 

"The albuminoids of food are transformed into the albuminoids and gelatinoids of the body. Muscle, tendon, and cartilage, bone and skin, the corpuscles of the blood and the caseins of milk are made of the albuminoids of food. The albuminoids are sometimes called 'flesh formers' or 'muscle formers', because the lean flesh, the muscle, is made from them, though the term is inadequate, as it leaves out of account the energyfurnishing function of protein. "The gelatinoids of food, such as the finer particles of tendon and the gelatine, which are dissolved out of bone and meat in soup, though somewhat similar to the albuminoids in composition, are not believed to be tissue-formers; but they are valuable in protecting the albuminoids from consumption. That is, when the food contains gelatinoids in abundance less of albuminoids is used. "The proteids can be so changed in the body as to Page 321

yield fats and carbohydrates and such changes actually occur to some extent. In this and other ways they supply the body with fuel. "The protein compounds are not only used for building and repairing tissue, but are also burned directly in the body like the carbohydrates, and thus render important service as fuel. A dog can live on lean meat.

He can convert its material into muscle and its energy into heat and muscular power. Man can do the same; but such a one-sided diet would not be best for the dog and would be still worse for man. The natural food for carniverous animals, like the dog, supplies fats and some carbohydrates, and that for omnivorous animals, like man, furnishes fats and carbohydrates in liberal amounts along with protein. Herbivorous animals, like horses, cattle and sheep, naturally require large proportions of carbohydrates. 

Fortunately, Substitutes are Accepted "Fats and carbohydrates are the chief fuel ingredients of food. Sugar and the starch of bread and potatoes are burned in the body to yield heat and power. 

The fats, such as the fat of meat and butter, serve the same purpose, only they are a more concentrated fuel than the carbohydrates. "The body can also transform carbohydrates of food into fat. This fat, and with it that stored from the food, is kept in the body as reserve fuel in the most concentrated form. "The different nutrients can to a greater or less extent do one another's work. If the body has not enough of one kind of fuel, it can use another. But, while protein can be burned in the place of fats and carbohy- Page 322

drates, neither of the latter can take the place of the albuminoids in building and repairing the tissues. At the same time the gelatinoids, fats and carbohydrates, by being consumed themselves, protect the albuminoids from consumption. "Heat and muscular power are forms of energy or force. The energy latent in the food is developed as the food is consumed in the body. The process is more or less akin to that which takes place when coal is burned in the furnace of the locomotive. For the burning of food in the body or the coal in the furnace, air is used to supply oxygen. When the fuel is oxidized, be it meat or wood, bread or coal, the latent energy becomes active, or, in technical language, the technical energy becomes kinetic; it is transformed into power and heat. As various kinds of coal differ in the amount of heat given off per ton, so various kinds of food and food ingredients give off different amounts of energy; that is, have different values as fuel in the body." 

The body needs certain kinds and quantities of food in order to sustain itself in prime health. It needs the particular foods which re-supply the substances of which it is composed. The chief substances I have enumerated twice. The body, too, must function normally in absorbing the necessary foods. The synchrony of the chemical action with the elements and the principle of life is in the cell-structures, being the protoplasmic units of which the self-reproducing and embedded cells are formed. 

Alternation of Exercise and Rest 

The variety and quantity of food, to which Dr. 

Atwater alludes in popular terms, yet with scientific Page 323

fidelity, are requisite not only to sustain health as material supply, but also to facilitate proper functioning of the different parts of the body. Naturally, food conducive to health-building would be compatible also to functioning.

The human organism must function constantly in the generation of life-energy functioning power, body heat, body liquids, etc.; therefore at organism major rest, when most organs and tissues are at the enforced rest of reduced functioning for rebuilding their structures, then a small group is at enforced exercise of advanced functioning; and at organism major exercise, when most organs and tissues are at enforced exercise of advanced functioning for creating and eliminating structural wastes, then a smaller group is at enforced rest of reduced functioning for rebuilding its structures.

These two groups may be based on the upper digestion tract (mouth to stomach inclusive) as the smaller group, and the balance of the organism as the larger group.

The upper tract digests principally water-soluble vitamins, albuminous proteins and non-oxidizing mineral matters for cell-structure repair; also, oxidizing mineral matters for neutralizing any arterial blood impurities not eliminated at respiration when the tract was at reduced functioning; also, liquids to supply secretions at deficient bodily precipitation during reduced respiration-functioning of lowered volume in oxygen intake.

The lower tract digests principally fat-soluble vitamins, food fats or condensed carbohydrates and simple carbohydrates. The food fats unite with excess oxygen intake at respiration at the tract's advanced function- Page 324

ing to generate a maximum functioning energy for clearing up poisons in all cell structures at exercise; for generating a sufficient supply of body heat, as it is so rapidly radiated at exercise; for thorough clearation of arterial blood (including any neutralized substances remaining from reduced respiration functioning); for supplying vitamins for the upper-digestion tract cellstructure rebuilding (now at rest), and simple carbohydrates, almost immediately converted into body fats for secretion storage, and to be a source of energy, heat and liquid supply while the upper tract is at exercise. 

Digestive Tracts Kept in Condition 

In this world of ours, the opposite vital processes of the animal and vegetable kingdoms by the conversion of each other's waste into wholesome supply forever sustain a balanced evolution of organic life. Thus the waste of the living animals' evacuations, as well as the inert structure of deceased, are natural fertilizers for the vegetable kingdom, and the vegetable product for succession as self-detached from the parent stem is the reconversion of wastes into the wholesome natural supply for the animal kingdom. This is basically sound reasoning, even if various animals have through rigorous selection evolved into genera which can subsist upon other supply, either wholly or in part. 

Again I say the digestive tracts, being the machine parts which receive and process the foods for the sustenance and functioning of the body, necessarily must be kept in the best condition. For these tracts, as well as the whole body, the naturally requisite kinds and quantities of foods must be taken. Unless, I warn you, these tracts function properly, the rest of the body 

I will not be well and fit.

Page 325

FOR BOOK FOUR CHAPTER I. 

OR your further enlightenment I should like to present to you a few of the opinions which I have formed concerning the special concept which underlies this scientific potpourri. Though you may surmise that I have made something of a hobby of the fountain of youth, I shall be satisfied if I do no more than give you a finer appreciation of the wonders and beauties of nature and inspire you to follow nature more faithfully in your manner of living for your benefit and that of your family. 

Please do not imagine me as a doctrinaire. I have no desire to impose a bunch of rules on you, in a dictatorial attempt to persuade you to find the constant prime. The fountain of youth is in the universal cosmos, and therefore in yourself, and it is not a piece of psychic magic with fantastic trimmings, but a plain, common sense pursuance of the ordinary regulations of nature.

My concept is called a philosophy. But this is not because it is an abstruse system. It is a philosophy because it is a set of quite logical conclusions extracted from the perceptible operation of natural principles. 

I would rather have it called a philosophy than a system, for I wish you to feel that I am not trying to hand you a packet of precepts framed by me, which I would command you to obey implicitly even as to the Page 327

minutest details, but that I am submitting to you reasonable inferences from nature herself, and am expecting you to adapt your manner of living to them in accordance with your own conviction and convenience.

You Develop Your Own Plan 

You may think of this philosophy as the foundation of a school for proper, natural living, and not, however, as of an arbitrary system. I fully realize that you are under individual obligations to the conventions of this complex civilization and that you cannot live without working any more than you can without eating. So, I am giving you my thoughts, with a few practical suggestions, and it is for you to decide whether to accept them, or to what extent, and to apply them in a way suited to your inflexible circumstances of living. 

It may be that this special feature of natural philosophy will lead to the establishment of a few institutions or sanitariums, under medical supervision, where those desirous of recuperating their health may go. 

This is an objective, which, I trust, will be realized. 

I hope also that recreation havens will be founded, to which well persons may resort occasionally for brief vacations, enjoying themselves while recuperating their health. Here they would live agreeably for several weeks at a time, free from care, and not taking medical treatment, but pastiming in delightful environment.

Each of us should take such a vacation now and then, and there should be a number of convenient, wellappointed and responsible places in different parts of Page 328

the country.

An occasional recreation of this kind freshens the mind and brightens the spirit, besides improving bodily health. However, in this work I aim especially to present my views to you in a straightforward, clear and convincing manner and prove to you that you can preserve your health only by your own efforts.

I have cited great scientific authorities to show that the doctors of medicine and surgery have gone about as far as they can go in serving humanity and they rely on you and all of us to go the rest of the way. The era of individual hygiene has begun, according to all scientific authorities, and my concept or philosophy is a tablet of ideas which should be of extraordinary practical value.

CHAPTER II. 

You Hold the Key of Health

There is no mystic psychiatry by which you may regain or preserve health. Nor is there a magic of science which can provide your body with new organs or new parts. Nor is there somewhere a sparkling fountain of eternal youth. There is no faith-cure or sciencecure by which the laws of nature may be controverted in order to rectify abuse of health and insolence toward the cosmos and its ordinary functioning. Nor is there a gold-cure for health. Instead of telling you that you may remain in sound health or recover it by a system of mental or physical magic, in which the abnormal would predominate, I remind you that you can only regain or preserve your health by living in compliance with the principles of nature. Page 329

The philosophical concept which I propose is as simple as the profundity of nature, which, though mysterious, is as clear as day. In the beauty, grace and charm of the bright flower abloom in the blazing sunlight and of the cold, white snow, irridescent in the silvery splendor of the ghostlike moon, nature pictures the oft-recurring miracles of life in death and death in life. It is an infatuating rhapsody. Like nature, I would lead you into the enthrallment which lies within sensible understanding of the wonders of the universal symphony, and there pause and leave you in the glamour of the inscrutable. 

To me, every form of life, every inanimate object, every active principle, in the stupendous cosmic scheme is marvelous. Of them all the most wonderful are man with his higher intelligence and free will and the protoplasm in which life is realized by co-ordination between the elements and the principle of vitality. Life and the human intelligence are common miracles. 

Common miracles are the preservation of health, which is the state and form of life, and the succession of life in different creatures, like the succession of the seasons from that of annual birth to winter. 

Common Sense Instead of Elixir

Far from my mind is any idea of a pantheistic or psychic elixir, by which, in a sort of superstitious internal or external bewitchment, we may preserve health. 

The lessons of my school of philosophy are only those which I discern in nature, and which, I am confident, you will see with your mind after I show them to you.

Our school is that of universal nature, and there will be no frills to the beauties of nature and no utopian Page 330

conceits to the principles of nature as we consider the natural order in connection with health of mind and body. 

You must agree with me as to the oneness of the stupendous cosmic order. I have offered to you the most trustworthy scientific opinion to elucidate this point. Your own observation and your own instinct would suffice to convince you as to the actual relationship which prevails between the beat of your heart and the rhythm among the huge orbs in farthest space. But the views of natural scientists which I have included in this work should, I believe, clarify the principles which affect all beings and things in universal nature. 

The sun, as science indicates, is a star like other stars in space, and the earth and moon are planets like other planets in space. Life may exist on other planets. The earth, the moon and other planets which I named are attendant upon our star, the sun, and constitute our solar system, and our solar system is similar to other stellar systems out in space. Herein we recognize the oneness which exists in matter and space. 

Health Is Result of Chemical Action 

Science shows us that change is going on continuously in the universe, just as motion and action persist throughout nature. We do not know whether space increases or diminishes in the net quantity, or whether matter increases or diminishes so, but we do know that when matter is formed space is diminished. Science definitely demonstrates to us that there is matter and space, and they are in motion and action consistently to cosmic principles, and that there is an inter-relationship, or oneness, in the whole universe.

Page 331

Science shows us that life is a natural result of chemical action between the elements and the principle essential to vitality. It shows us that the unitary condition of life is the same in all beings. The general principle and action by which life is produced in the animal and vegetable kingdoms is the same, and there is natural kindredness between the intelligent and instinctive forms of life.

Life is a natural result due to the action of the sun on the earth. So that science informs us that life is an active condition in the universal oneness.

In this oneness of the cosmic (for universality is oneness) there is an interdependence among beings and things; nevertheless, no two beings of the same genera are alike or no two things. Protoplasms are supposed to be exactly alike, but doubtless small differences would be noted in close research. Individuality results from difference between beings or things, and each entity has certain natural idiosyncracies. 

The cosmos, with its oneness, is a conjuctive whole of phenomena parts, which function separately, but in mutual dependence. Such, too, is man-a conjunctive whole, consisting of organs and tissues, which function separately, but in mutual dependence. Although the organs and tissues are different in form, texture and other features, they are radically the same, being made of protoplasmic cell-structures. 

Man Within Himself Is a Cosmos

Man is a living body of flesh and blood composed of substances taken from the earth. He lives, grows and continues to live by replenishment of used earthly Page 332

substances in his body. He lives by food, air and the sunlight. He exists on and of the earth under the direct influence of the sun.

Man is the human cosmos. Though a child of the earth and sun, he is a godlike creature. He is the master of nature with his higher intelligence. Yet he is subject to the active principles of nature for continued existence.

CHAPTER III. 

Throughout nature we observe adherence to a general active condition of positive and negative, or action and reaction, or, in the finality, reciprocation. The opposing forces of the immense spheres preserve in space the equilibrium by which the stars and planets hold their fixed places and the apparently fugitive nebulae dissipate or become fixed. One scientist, whom I quoted, supposes that a gradual repletion of energy goes on continually in nature. We may conjecture from this a continual transition of space to matter and matter to space, or a continuous decomposition and recomposition of energy and matter. Anyhow, change goes on, constituting or causing stability and recomposition. 

Decay Is a Transition to New Form 

The monstrous beasts which inhabited the earth in prehistoric times have become extinct, no doubt because of cataclysm or lack of an element or food-ingredient requisite to life. The decomposed matter of which they had consisted contributed in turn to the preservation of life, animal or vegetable, in subsequent years. 

Man, who is "like the grass", dies and his body decom- Page 333

poses, returning to dust, and provides fertility and sustenance for new, succeeding life. 

Cosmic security seems to be bound up in the interplay of equals and opposites of action and reaction, and so on, among forces and material bodies. Between the sun and the earth there is evidently an electrochemical activity in the concordance of the positive and negative. The mystery of life, from the natural viewpoint, as a natural process, may be wrapped up in the achievement of this principle in the invisible protoplasm. In decomposition and recomposition, elaborating as a constructive procedure, a natural law of compensation operates.

Technically, we may speculate that nature is never or nowhere static. It is logical to deduce that the static, when or where it occurs in nature, is a prolonged trend, term or condition in a great and extended interplay between activity and rest; that is, the seeming inertia is probably an incident of the time-element in connection with change or transition. The principle of compensation and reciprocation is probably in a longterm functioning with large causes, and the seeming inertia is transitional motion in the semblance of static. 

Permanent Constancy in Nature

Whether in counteraction or in static, there is, in the general cosmos, a permanent natural constancy. Compensatory adjustment is always progressing. Apparent derangement in nature is not derangement at all, but, like decomposition, is a transitional and constructive process in which a comprehensive balance is maintained. 

Equal and opposite, and action and reaction, and so Page 334

on, naturally maintain equilibrium. There is always and everywhere poise in nature, for poise or balance is the evidence of natural propriety. 

Chaos does not occur in nature and probably never has occurred. We refer to the earliest stages of evolution or development as chaotic; but we mean by this that nature was in the primordial turmoil of action and reaction from which the orderliness which we observe on the earth at present transpired. That was a vast circumstance in which time was an important, longswing factor. 

You may believe what you will as to evolution or development or the origin of the earth, man or life. Scientifically, those subjects are still speculative. In explaining my theory, I intend to avoid the conjectural. Common sense alone, with the certainties disclosed by the several branches of science, should expound plainly to anyone the ideas which I set forth in everyday terms. 

In the action, motion and change in nature there is a fixed constancy effectivated and maintained by the cosmic principles. Nature is ever going through in the action and reaction among principles, forces and objects a reciprocation between inertia or static and activity. Nature rests or recuperates, but does not sleep. 

The principle of positive and negative continues to operate in immediate or long-term eventuality. 

Different Activities, Yet All in Concert 

You may compare the suspended operations of the cosmic principles to plans which you are working out in your own affairs. The work which you do in your business or professional operation is partial to a gen- Page 335

eral operation or result in which others besides yourself are concerned; it is only accessory to the whole, and, therefore, appears to be incomplete. You perform certain amenities and duties at home; they, too, are only partial. The wife or mother performs her amenities and duties. There is a division of labor which appears to be a division of interests, yet all the plans and services tend to convenience, accord and happiness for all in the home.

CHAPTER IV. 

Of course, the cosmic harmony in nature is perfect. 

Nothing occurs by chance or accident in the realm of nature. The principles function inexorably from cause to effect. Man, with variations according to individual environment as to climate and locale, is subject to these undeviating laws. These principles are in the nature which is man.

As far as the natural externals are concerned, there can be no factor of chance in relation to man's health or life. Natural occurrences take place as sequences of natural principles in operation, and man is obliged to heed these principles in his manner of living, for he cannot change them. 

The Greatest Assets in Nature

Look upon man as an entity in the universal cosmos. 

He is in his being superior to all else in all nature, possessing the two greatest marvels, life and higher intelligence. Life is more wonderful than the sun, notwithstanding that the vitalizing principle emanates from the sun. The intellect of man, with its understanding and Page 336

free will, although in one respect less marvelous than life, in other respects is far more wonderful. 

In the immense orbs and in nature generally we note action and motion and a natural instinct which emanates from the cosmic principles. In the vegetable kingdom we see a form of life and a natural instinct similar to that which controls inanimate matter. However, we observe among many animals an instinct which is higher and different than that in the lower orders of nature and a reasoning faculty which is higher than instinct, yet is distinctly limited practically by this instinct. 

Thus, we arrive at the paradox of the superior being, the wonder of wonders in nature, taking life and sustenance from the inferior and even the inanimate. 

The crux of the preservation of health, as of the solution of most human problems, lies in the intellectual superiority of man. Here and now, I would accentuate by repetition the point which I have previously stressed, that the weaknesses of the average man come from his higher intelligence, as well as his exhibitions of strength and force. The intellect of man, with his free gift of free will, represents his superiority in the whole universal domain; yet the subversion of it to animal propensities and irrational, bestial and vicious debauchery and licentiousness degrades him to an abyss beneath the lowest level in the natural order.

Radiation of Human Benevolence 

Man has intellect in order to appreciate the beauties and wonders of nature, and to revere them, and to use them as boons for individual benefit and the common welfare. In his intellect he has the afflatus of ethics and Page 337

culture, the capacity and predisposition to be almost divine in both his thoughts and his deeds. Nature is never unethical or iniquitous, and whenever in the human identity there is desecration of the nobler principles, the fault must be in depravity of the precious gem which endows man with the properties of superiority.

Man should be as a sun, naturally, in his earthly environment. The favors which he receives from the cosmic homogeny he should burnish with the finer qualities of his intelligence, enhancing their value and efficacy by gracious understanding. If there is nothing better than the feeling and expression of gratitude in the human heart, so, on the contrary, there is nothing more contemptible than the lack of this normal sentiment.

Man should be godlike. In the universal cosmos he should radiate a gracious beneficence. Taking the gifts of nature, he should better them by appreciative understanding and use and distribute them with the purpose of serving his fellows and helping the animate beings inferior to himself. He has the power to draw from all nature to foster his own advantages, which, by doing, he may even improve on nature. 

I have told you that character is indispensable to health and happiness. Character is in the verdict of the will. It is the cumulative consummation of ethical motive and habit. The nucleus of it is inborn with the intellect as a hereditary legacy; but it does not grow; it must be built up in the continuous clash with the external relations in life. The conflict between the pos- Page 338

itive of ethics and the negative of the wrong and decadent gradually forms character. 

Lack of Character Is Contemptible 

The necessity of discussing lack of character in man is repugnant to the sensible regard of all that is splendid and inspiring in nature and all that is noble and aspiring in him. Want of character is a loathsome corruption of the greatest gift bestowed on man. 

Most persons, I believe, hold erroneous ideas as to character. Force is too often mistaken for it. A person who is frantic, loud and demonstrative; or one who is domineering and demands his own way; or one who is opinionated, and so on, is said to have character. In fact, any of these persons, like any too docile, is imperfect in character. There is moral poise, with submergence of self-interest, to true character. 

Man by his will can control some of the conditions in his external relationship, but not all, and he cannot sway the great forces which move in accordance with fixed principles or laws. He may lay plans for riches or success, yet he can have no preassurance of achieving either, for it is impossible for him to regulate the various external forces to which he and his ambitions are amenable. The maximum best procurable through the exercise of his intellect and will is rectitude of motive and objective, and it is by the application of his higher faculties in compliance with established principles, unchangeable and undeviating principles, that he produces character.

Mind and Matter Influence Each Other 

I have gone into some particularization concerning character, as I deem it to be a primary factor in the Page 339

effort to preserve health and realize happiness. Naturally, the intellect and will dominate every decision and action; and in turn every decision or action has some beneficial or detrimental effect on the mind. Where in the one result the law of satisfaction operates, in the other operates the law of compensation. 

CHAPTER V. 

No doubt, the mind, with or without character, has power over matter. On the other hand, matter has power over the mind, and external relations affect mind and matter favorably or adversely. So, we must be careful not to attach too much importance to mental influence. Psychiatrists continue to experiment in mental therapeutics and allied sciences, and they already know pretty well the limitations of mental force. We can use the mind for health preservation, and must do so, yet it would be absurd to make a hobby of a psychiatric design.

I am trying to bring you to an equalized conviction regarding the relationship of the intellect to health and happiness. We are expected to use our intelligence intelligently. Common sense apprises us as to the reasonable employment of our higher faculties, and it is enough to think and act normally. This is, in brief, the theory which I propound to you. 

Common Sense a Trustworthy Standard

The will of man cannot alter or guide the forces in his external relationship. The option rests in the freedom of will of conciliating or opposing these forces; however, this liberty cannot imply that these unchangeable forces can or would be governed by human com- Page 340

mand. A will obstinate to forces which are arbitrary in their functioning to basic principles of the cosmos invites retaliation from the compensatory reaction of nature.

We are considering the superiority of man in the natural sphere and his character, both of which reside in the intellect. The standard of his superiority and character rests simply on common sense. Common sense is, or should be, the average, and the average is the standard. One may be reasonable, healthy, happy and good, even illustriously magnanimous, noble and refined, with no more than common sense; in fact, I doubt whether without common sense he could be either.

We are too prone to form impressions from appearances. Thus, we make mistakes about culture and integrity similar to those which we make regarding character. We associate culture with wealth or education and honor with conventional behavior. Integrity is more rugged than this, and culture finer. The spirit of each is true charity.

Inferior Essential to the Superior This superior creature depends absolutely on the inferior beings and things and forces in his total environment for life and health. He cannot exist without them. Physically, he is in a status similar to that of wheat and cotton, the bluejay and the minnow. His

Man takes part in the cosmic arrangement. part is greatest because it is taken with understanding. 

With each of the other entities, instinct is the rule of correspondence, whereas, with man, reason enlightens him.

Page 341

Pervading the whole natural arrangement there is general and stable constancy. Decomposition and recomposition, action and reaction, equals and opposites, positive and negative, seeming separately and altogether to be in a chaos of processes and conditions, are really producing the constancy which is distinctive of the cosmic order and rhythm. The oneness of universal nature is perceptible in a confusion which is really concord.

Man Is Subject to Cosmic Principles 

Man within himself resembles the arrangement by which the universal constancy is maintained. The different organs and parts respond to the principles which govern equals and opposites, action and reaction, in the rest of nature. He is a figure in the oneness of the universe, and there is a like oneness in his physical and mental economy. As in the large universal arrangement there is unshifting constancy in action, motion and change, so in the complex human body, containing the most wonderful organs and machinery in nature, there is an unremitting constancy in physical competency or health, functioning to efficiency or happiness.

From life to death and from death to life and through various processes of transition, in which action and reaction are involved, this ever-prevailing constancy is one of the most remarkable phases in the cosmic harmony. It is another of the several conditions of poise in action and motion in the universal complement which attest to the balance at which nature consistently remains.

The melody of the cosmic co-ordination is the Page 342

rhythm of accuracy and perfection. Time and process function together through change and development with never a variation in the principles or their working. The song of the stars is the tune in the human heart. 

Happiest or Unhappiest, Matter of Choice 

Everything is relatively conjunctive toward making a universal knowledge oneness of physics form and a universal omniscient oneness of energy character. There is in all substance and forces a universality of principle, which is the manifestation of nature in life, action, motion and change.

CHAPTER VI. 

Man occupies the happiest position in the natural cosmogony. Or he occupies the unhappiest. The choice between the two is with him. His position is the happiest or the unhappiest, as the case may be, in that he is capable of knowing and feeling the effects of the cosmic principles. Other animals feel these effects, just as they respond to the principles, but their sensations are instinctive. The ability of man to understand what he sees and feels, and to recognize forces, laws and principles, realizes keenly to him in reason the difference between joy and pain. You remember the saying that a dog has not sense enough to worry.

Life, therefore, is of greater value and significance to man than to any other being. So is life's equivalent, time. So is health. And the position of man is the happiest if he manages by his intelligence to fill his life and time with health and happiness. Nature takes care of the instinctive animals. But the creature en- Page 343

dowed with free will in his higher intelligence must take care of himself. This is one of the responsibilities of his superiority.

Limits of Psychiatry Are Definite 

Constancy, like that prevailing in universal nature, should be a trait in individual man, and it is and will be to him who chooses for himself the happiest position in nature. In physical nature it is the balance or poise brought about by the interaction between equals and opposites, the positive and the negative, resulting in instinctive fulfillment of principles. In man it is the poise or balance which is the contentful product of constant prime of health and mind and body. 

Once more, let me make it clear to you that the healthy or unhealthy state of the body affects the brain at least as often as that of the mind affects the body. The old adage, “a sound mind in a sound body”, is still true. There is no psychiatric nostrum which is more than presumptive sophistry. The influence of mind over matter and over the functioning of matter is definite, and science takes decided cognizance of the fact that the condition of the body is reflected in the brain. The reasonableness of this conclusion is obvious from what I have previously explained at length, showing that the brain is not only in sympathetic touch with the body, but is made of the same substances. 

I charge you to use your intellect with reason in your endeavors to preserve your health, or to regain it. 

Treat your body with remedies prescribed by nature; above all, comply with the ordinary requirements which nature imposes on you. Keep a rational, mental poise.

Page 344

Look upon the mind strictly as the organ of judgment and decision and as the governor, within reason and within nature, of the body. And use it to follow nature in taking care of your health.

Mysteries Which Never Will Be Solved 

The mystifying secrets of the human mind itself will never be revealed to man. The visions, ideas, thoughts and inspirations which flame up in it are emanations of the kaleidoscopic experiences of hundreds of progenitors during thousands of eventful years in different climes. The most astounding action in all nature is that of the human mind inquiring eagerly into itself with the hope of solving these riddles.

The universal cosmos, with its precise harmony in the interplay of action and reaction between compensatory equals and opposites, contains mystifying secrets which man never will unravel. Causes are not easily distinguishable. But there is no mystery in the functioning of principles and forces, or in their effects, or in sequences. The mathematical orderliness of nature is a motion and action picture which the human mind understands.

The operation of the principles and forces by which the constancy and equilibrium are maintained in the universal harmony is somehow discernible, and, therefore, in a practical way, understandable. The balance is the result of reciprocation between equals and opposites in action and reaction. The principle involved in positive and negative reigns throughout nature. 

I would call your special attention to the correlation between use and decomposition on the one hand and Page 345

use and recomposition on the other in the general functioning of nature, and to a spacing of rest in the process of use-destruction and use-reconstruction. The process of use and replacement, or use and equivalent transition, continuously goes on with inserted intervals of rest. 

Time is an element in the spacing of rest, as it is in the action and reaction toward reconstruction or transition. 

There is an opportune alternation, with rest interposed. We observe these important details in the cosmic operations.

Decomposition and Recomposition We find in man the same principles motivating similar functioning. In him goes on decomposition and recomposition in a process of destruction by use and reconstruction by use. In him is the similar interplay of equals and opposites. In him is like action and reaction. There is the reciprocal functioning of the different organs and parts of the body, and the alternation between work, use or exercise, and rest, with invigoration in the exercise or use and recuperation in the rest. 

Two phases of the time-element also participate in the state and functioning of the human body, as in the state and functioning of the universe; one is the immediate or momentary, and the other, the long-run, which extends through months or years. 

When the human body is in the condition of sustained equilibrium, with all the organs and tissues prime, it is in the poise or constancy resembling that of the natural arrangement. The discipline of exercise and rest, with the opportunities for decomposition and recomposition in action and reaction, is capable of maintaining the constant prime of health. Functioning Page 346

and effects are practically the same in man as in physical nature.

There is one difference between man and physical nature. This is that there is no decay in nature, whereas there is in man, although there should not be. The appearance of decay in nature is the aspect of a trend toward recomposition. But decay in man is not a simulation; it is real. It is a direct consequence of violation of the laws of nature. 

CHAPTER VII. 

The human organism is a living body of conjoined different parts, with functions which are different, yet interdependent. Each part or unit performs its specific function, but at the same time co-ordinates with the other parts. There is synchrony of functioning not only in each organ or unit, but there is also synchrony in the correlative functioning among all the parts. The human body is a self-sustaining, automatic machine, with equilibrium of operation. 

Black Carbon and Glittering Diamond

Though the parts are different in forms and texture, the material constituency of all is the same. The actuality of life is the basic unit of the substance of which they consist, and it is the primary impetus of the action and correlation. Thus, the parts are made up of lifeunits and their performances may be said to be lifeaction, for existence and functioning are both due to the continued operation of the life-principle. 

First and last the human body is protoplasmic. The protoplasm is life. We see in this particle, which is the mainspring of life, the source of vitality and the initial Page 347

display of interaction of the positive and negative. The elements essential to life are in the substance, and the principle of life arrives in some solar potency, some ray or property of the sun, causing chemical action of which the product is life. The protoplasm has the faculty of multiplication, and thus a number of protoplasms issues into a cell, which, in turn, is selfreproducing.

Human Life Is But an Active Cell 

I mention the protoplasm because it is the life unit, and it illustrates the reality and actuality of life. Life is nothing more, physically, than this vitalized and active midget of matter. From the practical viewpoint, our interest is in the cell-structure, which is an accumulation of protoplasms. You may get an idea of the formation of the cell-structure from the massing of tumbling snow crystals. You have seen the falling flakes fit together and build up a snow-structure. The microscope discloses a structure of crystals which looks like a net. Or, you may compare the upbuilding of cell-structure to the formation of the honeycomb, which is a structure of comparatively large cells. 

What are cells but vitalized crystals? Otherwise they are practically the same. What difference is there between certain other forms in nature? What difference is there between a nugget of gold and a block of stone, or between a diamond and a piece of carbon? 

There is no other difference but of arrangement of elemental combination. It is generally known that black, grainy, deathlike carbon is the same as the polished diamond, which flashes with all the hues of the spectrum and sparkles as if imbued with life. All things in na- Page 348

ture trace down to the same unit and back to the same source of origin, and the difference between life and death is a chemical or dynamic trifle.

So, from the more practical standpoint, we assume that the self-reproducing cell in the human organism is, first and last, human life. The body of man consists of innumerable cells, doubtless trillions, and his existence and his life is the composite of these cellstructures. Therefore, in the absolute sense we do not say that existence, life and health are the countless cells in the body, but the one cell, the original parental unit from which all others are derived. Organism prime depends on the common prime of all cells, yet, strictly speaking, on the prime of each cell, self-reproducing and embedded.

Again in the cell is notable the interaction of elements, forces and principles which is observed in a larger way in universal nature. It proceeds from the cell to all the organs and tissues, even to the cavities and liquids in the brain.

All Elements of Phenomena Are Common 

If the principle of existence is the intrinsic nucleus of all form and character, or the common entity of physics and energy, it may be held that all atoms of cells and crystals are common, differing only in the arrangement of minute, constituent particles. Therefore, it may be said that all elements of phenomena are common, differing only in the arrangement of constituent atoms, and that all occurrences of energy are common, differing only in the respective constituents of atom and phenomena arrangement. That is, there is a Page 349

positive factor emanating from the sun and a negative inherent in matter, as a result of which in each comparable condition, thing or being the same principle applies.

Inasmuch as human life originates and lives in the cells, it is these and their structures in the normal body which necessarily require proper and ample sustenance and care. My saying normal body implies that the organs are sound. An organic lesion is taken to be the exception. Existence and life would be in the cells of the body having an impaired or diseased organ, as, say, the heart; but the unsound or abnormal condition of the organ would be a special hazard to life and health, distinct from the cellular efficiency.

The organism's parts are embedded cells of organic alkali-albumen and protein rudimentary cells of living vitamins, with inorganic nitrogenous or non-oxidating mineral compounds peculiar to each part. The selfreproducing cells, which are formed in the common blood-circulation, and increase by multiplication (each growing and dividing into two cells), repair or replace, as the case may be, the embedded cells as the latter are decomposed by use. Therefore, it is impossible to evade the conclusion that the constant prime of the body depends on maintenance of the cellular structure at prime.

High-Speed Living Destroys Cells 

The cell-structures being formations of protoplasms, they are composites of innumerable units of vitality. 

Health depends for its soundness on the prime condition of each cell-structure.

Life itself is an active condition which cannot be Page 350

retained without cellular competency and efficiency.

This is why Dr. Appel warned the public that highspeed living and inattention to health bring on disease, shorten life and hasten death.

CHAPTER VIII. 

The human body as a whole undergoes processes like those which are observed in all nature. There is the alternation between action, which includes motion, and rest in the whole body, by which decomposition and recomposition are regulated, and in each part and organ there is a like alternation, with, in certain activities, the rest interposed in pauses or lapses of time. Combined with this alternation is the compensating interaction of equals and opposites, or the positive and negative. The alternation and the adjusting interaction are the processive requisites for receiving, treating, distributing and otherwise using the necessary quality, quantity and variety of nutrition for the sustenance of life and the balanced maintenance of good health, and the proper drainage of body wastes.

The opportuneness of the alternation between exercise and rest naturally takes into account the timely and proper excretion of waste divested in use or functioning. As a consequence poison from decaying matter is prevented. It is hardly necessary for me to add, or to emphasize, provision must be made in the alternation and rest of the whole organism for the proper drainage of all body wastes.

Alternation, Nutrition and Drainage 

So, we sum up in balanced evolution and the constant prime the general condition in man's body which Page 351

prevails in nature: Alternation of exercise and rest, action and reaction between equals and opposites in constructive equalization of substance and forces, and adequate and proper nutrition, and the proper drainage.

It cannot be said that adequate and proper nutrition is more indispensable than the alternation of exercise and rest or the compensatory interaction of the positive and negaticve. We are obliged to conclude that food and functioning are about equally indispensable, equally necessary, equally balancing and constructive. 

You are familiar with the law which is popularly described by the old saying that "a chain is no stronger than its weakest link". The human body, we may say, is no stronger than its weakest cell. And the contention is by no means farfetched. 

A germ in the blood-stream, as you are well aware, in many instances does result in rapid destruction of one cell after another, ending in complete cellular decomposition and death of the body in a few weeks, a few days, or even a few hours. Impairment of one cell, as a result of unsuitable or toxic supply or of noxious intrusion of some kind, is followed by the breakdown and decay of cell after cell, terminating in complete physical collapse. 

Reconstruction or Decay

The body with its cell-structure is like a barrel of apples. If a parasitic speck contaminates one apple, before long the whole apple is rotten, and like infection the corruption spreads from one apple to another and rots all. Thus it is with the cell structure in the Page 352

human body, the physical condition of which is no better or stronger or sounder than the weakest cell.

When waste occurs in the human organism, as it does, the condition which exists is that of decay without reconstruction. The trend is deteriorative, though it be restricted to but one cell. In man progressive destruction is inevitable in deviation from the constant prime of health, for decay indicates failure in the recompositional phase of interaction. The constructive process is interrupted.

The disposal of waste, that is the phase of excreting the matter decomposed in natural use, takes place as opportunely in man as in the functioning of nature in general. If in the discipline between exercise and rest, or interaction, or as a consequence of malnutrition or an emotional cause, the recomposition is not opportune as to time and circumstance to the decomposition, the consequence is retrogressive and destructive. 

The Blending of Opposites 

As the functioning power or life-energy of the organism is the cellular adaptation or temporary persistence of electricity, generated by the rechemicalization between inorganic oxidating or non-nitrogenous mineral compounds, of secretions with secretions and of secretions with environment, it is imperatively necessary that the resultant precipitates, which do not yield further life-energy, should be excreted. Unless they are normally excreted, cellular decay begins. Constant and equalized prime in living organisms is the compensating conversion of waste into wholesome supply, and the balanced conversion of detrition into nutrition is the natural preparation of waste for assimilation ease Page 353

and thoroughness, or the equal and opposite rearrangement of the phenomena which preserves natural arrangement.

The primal, original or germ-reproducing cell of the individual organism is the blend of two opposite, positive-negative sex-reproducing cells into one. The unit grows and subdivides into two complete reproducing cells, two into four, and so on, so that in only 100 progressions about a decillion would be developed, each of which, when attached to embedded cells, immediately functions like its associates. Then, does not each reproducing cell have the composite form and character of all embedded cells, instantly modifying as embedded cells modify with variations of experience? 

Small Immensities in the Body If it may be assumed that physics waste and energy exhaustion cannot be approximated in the absolute sense, then the supply of physics and generation of energy in the natural relation of balanced requirements cannot be estimated. This simple inference is staggering when we consider that the human organism is composed of approximately 30 trillion (30,000,000,- 000,000) embedded cells, congestion waste and relaxation repair. A basic self-reproducing cell as deposited upon embedded cells is composed approximately of 758 carbon atoms, 1203 hydrogen atoms, 195 nitrogen atoms, 218 oxygen atoms, 3 iron and sulphur atoms, totaling 2380 inorganic atoms, with an unknown number of vitamin organic atoms. To calculate the physical waste of congested embedded cells, which are rechemicalized with other inorganic atoms, to effect a Page 354

compound, peculiar to the requirements of each tissue, seems to be hopeless.

Nevertheless, the question of the absolute does not enter into nutrition supply, which is estimable as to quantity, quality and variety. Proof of this idea is obvious in nature. The ninety-one known and one unknown chemical elements evolve in countless combinations of air and food supply, and the supply continues to evolve in countless combinations with the various secretions of the varying functioning processes, ever varying with the various circumstances and conditions of performance. It is evidently impossible to estimate and provide a properly proportioned sustenance of chemical combinations and vitamins to balance evolved wastes.

CHAPTER IX. 

Basis for Regulating Nutrition

We know the elemental constituency of the body. 

Therefore, we have reliable information by which to proportion and regulate the supply of nourishment. 

The natural environment produces a sufficiency of the needs which are principally assimilated as tissuebuilders at the tissue-building opportunity of physical rest and as energy-yielders at the energy-yielding opportunity for physical exercise. Then it may be assumed that these respective supplies relations depend on the relation of protein nitrogenous compounds (composing about 18 per cent of the body weight as the organic basis of tissue), to mineral-matter nonoxidating compounds (composing about 6 per cent of the body weight as the inorganic basis of tissue), and Page 355

on the relation of simple carbohydrates (composing about 1 per cent of the body weight), which are sugars and starches transformed into body fat composing about 15 per cent of the body weight, in masses, and scattered in minute particles throughout tissues as secretion substances for yielding energy, to food fats, which are two and one-fourth concentrates of simple carbohydrates, as arterial blood supply for combining with respiration oxygen for yielding energy and for precipitating internally generated body fluid. 

Knowing what the body is composed of, we know what nourishment it needs in proportion and variety. 

We are able to sense the quantity. We know that a natural balancing of exercise and rest is constructive. 

We know that the substantive principle of life and sound health is the cell-structure. Therefore, we are aware that the state of constant prime or balanced evolution is the perfect, rhythmic functioning of the body in accordance with the cosmic principles. 

Flesh and Bone Stronger Than Steel

Constant prime exists when the human organism is supplied with exactly sufficient material of uniformly proper quantity, variety and quality, consistent with natural exercise and rest, which sufficiency of each element also continues a proper supply quantity during intervals between participations, and is of that quality which prevents interferences between elements to divert them from their respectively indispensable special work of supply.

The human body is the most intricate, complex and tender machine, one of flesh, blood and bone. No other

Page 356

machine has the capacity, vigor, or endurance of the human, and no other is capable of performing such a great diversity of labor. No other is so effective, and, of course, it is incomparable in initiative and resourcefulness. It requires far less care and attention, maintenance, repair and upkeep than any other, and gets much less than it needs. It is as strong as steel, as powerful as the storm, as energetic as the sun, as supple as the wind, as solid as the earth, and as endurable as a conglomeration of troubles, efforts, labors, difficulties, obstructions, vicissitudes, achievements, failures, ambitions, disappointments and years. Intelligent, it is greater and mightier than the solar system, and in its wisdom so foolish it can use its advantages and potencies to destroy itself. Lord of the earth, it is the slave of self. Within itself are the probabilities for realizing the only treasures worth having-happiness, harmony and health, and these are the rewards which it seldom obtains. 

Greater Cheer for Well Persons

The concept of greater cheer and greater efficiency for well persons which I submit to you, confident that you will accept it, is the philosophy of nature in the perfect beauty and rhythm of the cosmos. To those who have health and happiness I show the means of selfpreservation, while to those who are without these ordinary, and at the same time extraordinary, blessings, I offer, I believe, much more than hope.

As there is a condition of proportions in universal nature, so is there in man. He has a sense of proportions both within himself and in relation to all beings and things. This sense is, as it were, a sense of the Page 357

symmetry and synchrony which is observable everywhere. 

I am almost ashamed to speak of reward for living up to the principles of nature, or to allude to duty. 

Every one feels this natural obligation to follow the cosmic principles and is goaded on agreeably to be reasonable and progressive. It is one of the few duties which are pleasant in themselves, and to fulfill them is not irksome; in fact, there is gratification, which is adequate reward, in conforming to the harmony to which the great, shining stars and the blind crawling things in the depths of the earth are attuned. "What Do I Get Out of It?"

There is a taint of imperfection in human character in desiring profit. But the strain of selfishness seems to be characteristically human, and, therefore, cannot be ignored. The average man does not ask what his duty is, but what he will gain by doing it. Slight as this blemish is, it is the principal cause of the worst misfortunes which have befallen mankind. Greed courses through every distortion and reaction in civilization. It might be declared almost as an axiom that in most adversity to the individual and the masses the prints of the grasping monster are distinguishable. 

Greed is selfishness carried to the most barbaric extremity.

First, I simply call your attention to the agreeable obligation of harmonizing your mind and body in all natural things to the cosmic symphony. Performing this obligation you will find to be a constant delight. Second, I say that conformity is ample reward of itself; but this is only a part of the greater reward, which is Page 358

sound health and the happiness associated with health and tranquillity. On the other hand, I warn you, with the deliberate purpose of exciting fear in you, that you will, if you antagonize the principles of nature, wreck your health and destroy your happiness. 

Fear Is Natural Human Trait

We boast that we have advanced beyond the stage of fear. We say that fear is a kind of superstition, and most of our mental and physical ills, most of our unhappiness, misery and misfortune, are consequences of fear. I believe that this generation is about as superstitious as any in the past. Almost any theory or fad is adopted by thousands if it only lures them with the chance of getting health or something desirable easily. 

We not only want to get rich quick, but to get anything and everything at a minimum of effort and at no personal sacrifice. We do not believe in effort or sacrifice any more than we do in fear, and, to be honest with ourselves, we do not believe in duty any more than we do in fear. We believe solely in self-interest. 

Fear is as characteristic of mankind as self-love and the yearning for pleasure and profit, and I maintain that, to some extent, it is necessary for us to be actuated by fear. How would we restrain our selfish impulses, which are largely pernicious, without a wholesome fear to counteract them? It appears to me that many of our troubles, reverses and complaints are due not so much to fear as to lack of it. 

CHAPTER X. 

Are we not irrational and superstitious in taking up mental and physical cure-all flimflams to get health, Page 359

wealth and happiness by tomfoolery? Apparently, we wish to be deceived and to deceive ourselves. Is there not superstition in the pursuit of the unique and the novel? Does not chasing wills-o'-the-wisp invented by charlatans betray either lack or deficiency of intellect, or sloth or inability in using it? Any scheme which plausibly grotesques the unchanging and unchangeable principles, which are plain and simple, of the natural order, leads thousands of us away into a maze of modern superstition in quest of health and happiness. We pervert our enlightenment into a superstition of introspection and self-love.

Take Your Head to a Psychiatrist 

Most of us should have our minds investigated by a sharp, conservative psychiatrist. He would ridicule our professions that we aim to be right and do right for right's sake, and that we are not moved by fear, superstition or the desire for reward. He would prove to us that these motives figure most in all that we do, and we are guided by mental quirks of self-deception. 

With our natural aptitude, which corresponds with instinct in the rest of nature, we combine intelligence. 

In the natural order nothing more is required of us than to be swayed in our manner of living by the natural aptitude. However, having higher intelligence, man should be disgraced if he lived by less than common sense-if he is, under these circumstances, capable of feeling humiliated to himself. Unfortunately, those who flatter themselves that they have advanced beyond fear and respect, are puffed up with the opinion that they have improved on cosmic principles and Page 360

the eternal verities in general, and they are more apt to contemn these than themselves. 

I do not propose to attempt to institute a new religious denomination, with a new interpretation of the Bible or the laws of nature, or a new branch of medicine, or a new system of psychiatry or metaphysics, with pompous platitudes deformed and mystified by equivocation of truth and controversion of fact. 

The immutable fundamentals seem to me to be more sublime and impressive than any speculation which I could invent and try to dignify with a structure of conceit. 

Eternal Verities Are Still Eternal

I take the fundamentals for granted. They are perceptible, normal and reasonable. I assume-with reason, I trust that the unison and rhythm of the grand cosmos are revealed by the natural aptitude in man and confirmed by the understanding and appreciation vested in his higher intelligence. I take it for granted that, although it may take less common sense or some eccentricity of intelligence to misconceive or falsify principles, it takes no more than common sense to be healthy, normal and happy, and, for that matter, progressive, too. 

Permeating the universal cosmos is an inspiring altruism which per se is cheering and elevating. It is a state or principle of natural virtue. It is a transfusion of the connoted rhythm of all nature into every entity. 

The first cardinal law of the cosmos is not deviation or retrogression, but continuous mathematical order and progression, and my theory (which I regard as a demonstrated law) is to "go forward with nature".

Page 361

The theory as to the cosmic harmony and natural virtue, even relative to the behavior of man, is not an innovation of mine. It is a philosophical classic. You must concede, therefore, that the idea of the altruism existing in nature is sane. This cannot be said of aesthetic persuasions which appeal to superstitions, passions, vanity and self-love in misguiding the dull and bewildering the super-intelligent.

Chiefly, I address myself to those sound of body and sane of mind, giving them assurance of greater cheer and greater efficiency. At the same time, I hold out more than hope to the thousands whose health has declined as a consequence of unnatural living and whose ideas are misdirected by contranatural casuistry. The latter should consult their physicians immediately and act.

CHAPTER XI. 

"Individual Hygiene!" Say Physicians

I have quoted to you the vigorous assertions of several of the most distinguished scientists in the world to the effect that physicians, surgeons and their colleagues have gone as far as they possibly could toward preventing sickness and disease, and that the principal health-destroying and death-dealing mental, emotional and organic afflictions cannot be cured. Moreover, these authorities have declared that the only method of counteracting these afflictions, virtually all of which are outgrowths of abuse of the advantages of our advanced civilization, is in changing the manner of living. 

They warn us solemnly that the next step in preventive medicine is that of individual hygiene.

Page 362

"Help yourself!" Physicians and surgeons raise their hands in despair and declare "If you are bent on suicide by riotous living, we cannot aid you, we cannot cure you. You will go to a sanitarium in the prime of life, to stay there. We may put new parts in you, and make a synthetic biped of you, but the years saved to you will be years of misery. The doors of the insane asylums are wide open for you, but the places are overcrowding and high-pressure capital will have to build additions to house the new patients. For desecrating nature, your best friend now is not one of us, but the undertaker. Don't fool yourself with the presumption that science can do the impossible. You must help yourself! You must change your manner of living! You must remove the cause! You must take up preventive medicine yourself!" 

Counteracting the Ageing Process

What do I mean when I refer to contra-ageing, deageing and rejuvenation? Or when I promise you a longer life in good health and happiness? I merely warn you against committing slow suicide by loose or fast living. I mean exactly what the scientists do.

I show you with scientific facts that the results can be produced by you and nature.

The modern diseases and afflictions which trustworthy physicians say can be prevented by individuals, but cannot be prevented or cured by science, are attributed to our complex civilization. Is that so? In other words, we blame civilization and exonerate ourselves. While bragging of the advantages which civilization has produced for labor-saving, leisure and comfortable living, we decry them by saying that they are Page 363

forcing us to kill ourselves. But is it not more likely that the fault is in ourselves rather than in our complex civilization? Is it not probable that we are misusing and disusing our advantages?

We may assume reasonably that civilization will not be simplified or de-complexed. None would acquiesce to social, economic and political devolution. The civilization which we believe to be suicidal to mankind doubtless will spread gradually throughout the world, for the peoples and races which are at present backward will be willing to risk their health, happiness and lives for the opportunities of convenience, wealth and freedom which we think we enjoy. For money and pleasure, property, influence and fame, mankind will gamble at absurd hazard. We may be confident that our method of slow suicide will be adopted all over the world. Even the banditry of the backward countries will be superseded by our gangism and commercialization of vice. Like us, they will have to learn by experience.

Civilization Ought to Include Nature 

It must be that we are misusing and disusing our advantages. Our advanced civilization is not ideal in all respects, yet it is more generous to man than any in the past, not excepting the simple. It has gotten away from us, but we can rectify and improve it. Meanwhile, is there a reason why we should not cease misusing and disusing our advantages? Is there a reason why we should not bring back nature into civilization, especially into our manner of living? Instead of adhering to slow, gradual self-destruction by antagonizing nature, it Page 364

would be advisable to adopt measures by which we would maintain normal health and prolong life.

My general plan, based on the concept which I have explained, is to oppose the prevailing suicide mode with maintenance of health at constant prime by following nature as closely as one's circumstances of living will allow. This is contra-ageing. It is the first and important effort in preventive medicine by the individual, in what the physicians call individual hygiene.

Contra-ageing is no more than maintenance of health. 

It is the same as offsetting ageing. As you would keep a building, a machine or tool in condition by never allowing it to run down, so you would keep the organism in condition. The depreciation would be overcome as it occurred, or might occur. Therefore, your health would always be sound. 

You Would Make Only Few Changes 

You would not have to change your mode of living much. You would continue to have the comforts, luxuries and conveniences to which you are accustomed, and you would continue having entertainment and recreation. But it would be positively necessary for you to abandon the habits which are destructive to health. 

Doing this might at first seem to be a sacrifice, for habit is second nature, although it may be injurious; but the change would be gradual and you would soon take greater pleasure with improved health. 

You would give up those excesses which cause the diseases which Dr. Crile mentioned, diseases which blight the nervous system, warp and break the heart, shrivel and deaden the brain and attack other organs mortally.

Page 365

To abandon these excesses is the first and most important step in individual hygiene. I take it that you would give them up if you seriously realized that they were destroying your body and mind, diminishing your efficiency, and shortening your years.

CHAPTER XII. 

For contra-ageing you will first have to think. You will have to study your habits impartially. You will have to find out what you are doing in work, pleasure, eating and so on that is gravely detrimental to health. 

I have no doubt that an unbiased self-analysis would convince you that you are living by a suicidal mode, for there is scarcely any one who has not run wild in misusing and disusing the advantages of civilization. 

Plan of Your Own from the Standard 

After you ascertain the health-destroying habits, you would alter them gradually until you worked out a system of your own for regulating your living mode to the laws of nature. You would work out a plan of your own for alternating exercise (including work) and rest, according to the suggestions which I have made to you, and taking the proper nutrition and diversion. 

You would thereby arrive soon at the constant prime of health which I have described, and thenceforth by living naturally you would preserve your health in perfect condition, maintain your efficiency, enjoy life, and prolong your span of years indefinitely.

De-ageing is a more serious matter. It is for those who have begun to notice the terrible consequences of reckless and careless living, and who are at the verge of Page 366

one or several of the organic or nerve diseases which permanently wreck health. Those who are in such a more or less desperate state would have to take a vacation of several weeks in a purely natural environment and follow a course of recuperation along the lines which I have recommended. They would restore their health by exercise and rest, diversion and dieting, or if necessary, by medical treatment under medical supervision, in a rural retreat. After restoring their health to normal they would each follow a system, conforming to my recommendations, adapted to his circumstances. 

Cavemen Sit at the Wheel

Most of us are cavemen, driving highpowered cars. 

Having developed a great civilization through our enterprise and ingenuity, with every imaginable facility for true culture, easy living and profitable employment, with intellectual, economic and social opportunities for the individual which were never before available, we squander our advantages, like savages, in a frenzy of licentiousness. We squander health, time, happiness and every possessed or procurable boon, repudiate every ethical and natural principle, and pack a lifetime, which should be one of health and happiness, into a few roisterous years of unconstrained dissipation. 

We want that "we live while we live," and "we'll get the most out of life," and "let tomorrow take care of itself; we'll have a fast time now." So, we dissipate in our work and our recreation and pleasure, we dissipate in our morals, we dissipate with our intellect, we dissipate our ideals and principles, we dissipate our honor and self-respect, we dissipate the body, we dissipate all of our advantages. And for this debauchery we pay the Page 367

inevitable penalty of physical or mental breakdown, or both, disappointment, misery and remorse.

Now, I leave it to your common sense to reason that science cannot mend the body or repair the mind wasted or shattered by years of such debauchery. As you meditate, when your health is ruined, on "we live while we live," your personal philosophy is changed. You then realize sorely that you did not live sanely or efficiently or happily, but in mad abandon. It is too late for you. 

I assume that the large majority is living at the suicidal pace. If my words do not apply to you, please accept my sincere congratulations, for you are a very rare exception.

At some critical point those who have lived like hyenas or moths may transmogrify themselves into intelligent human beings and regain their health. If they act in good time, they can restore physical and mental prime by the de-ageing process which I have recommended. They can rebuild their health in a recuperating vacation, and thereafter live naturally. 

I am very anxious to make it quite clear to you that there is no other means by which you may preserve sound health than the preventive. You cannot waste your health, yet have it, or get it back. You must protect it. The physicians will be unable to "fix you up.' 

You must utilize the precautionary measures which nature offers to you and not live like a fool, but reasonably and naturally. 

Again, I warn you that behind the physicians and surgeons are the insane asylums, sanitariums for incurables, poorhouses and cemeteries. Again, I remind you that the scientists have said this, cautioning you that

Page 368

they cannot reanimate or reinvigorate a parchmentcovered framework of bones, creaking and decaying inside. 

Think of the hero of Dickens' "A Tale of Two

Cities," Sydney Carton. He abused all of his extraordinary talents and debauched his health. The beautiful character of an admirable woman brought him to his senses; but it was too late, as he regretfully reasoned. He decided that his life was wasted and he would atone for his subversion of his advantages by one noble deed, a deed transcendently noble. The husband of the woman who inspired the grand ideal was sentenced to execution, and he made up his mind-his life having been wasted in idling and drinking, to take this man's place. 

This would be some atonement, and he would erase all the wrong done, which was to himself, by this single deed. Looking like the husband, it was easy for him to carry out his project, and he went to his death bravely. 

He was "twenty-three" for the guillotine of the French Revolution. To use a slang which has been current ever since, "A Tale of Two Cities" was published, Nature announces "twenty-three for you" to every desecrator of the cosmic principles. But there are very few among the modern wantons who have enough mind or strength left for performing a noble deed or thinking a noble thought when, in horror, nature "calls their bluff"; then, as before, the sole idea which they are capable of forming is sordid or selfish. 

CHAPTER XIII. 

You Have to Rely on Yourself 

Again, therefore, I advise you that you must rely entirely on preventive medicine or individual hygiene, Page 369

and that your health, your efficiency, your happiness, and your life-expectancy depend on your living naturally according to ordinary common sense. Health and all concomitant benefits are yours to get and yours to keep. 

Again, I say to you that only you yourself can keep or regain your health, and preserve it.

Elsewhere in this book I have broadly suggested a general formula for natural living. I suggest that you study this and adapt it to your idiosyncracies and circumstances. You should have constant prime of health by so doing.

The high intelligence which almost deifies man among the creatures and things of the universe attunes his whole economy to the beauty and cadence of the natural wonders in the universal cosmos. Alone, he understands the grand scheme as it stretches out from him to the antipodes of the earth and upward through the vastness of space to the blazing orb which is the last fringe of matter. His heart stirs with the meter of the cosmic rhythm. His spirit exults in the harmony pervading nature in the minuteness of next-to-nothing and through the variations of life and inanimate substance up to immensities which his calculating mind cannot so much as imagine. There are mysteries in the minuteness and immensity which he knows that he cannot solve, yet he understands and feels the symphony and order of the universal concord and the one-ness of himself and the all of nature.
